Every day beneath the turning wheel of the sun, acts of quiet, mythic resonance unfold over hearths and cutting boards, though the men and women who perform them are often swallowed by time. The official memory of the world is too often trapped in musty chronicles penned in an intolerable, imperial Latin—a dominant lexicon that flattens the rich, resonant acoustic ecology of lived life into sterile syllogisms. The harsh brevity of these records obscures the strange history of a pair who lived four hundred years ago in the town of Tergou. To read their lives in that dead, hegemonic tongue is to witness the erasure of a native dialect, the loss of an untranslatable subjective experience. To understand them, one must discard the dry ink of historians and inhale the lingering scents of their kitchens.

It was past the middle of the fifteenth century. While sovereigns played their noisy, hollow games of conquest—Louis XI in France, Edward IV in England, and Philip “the Good” in Holland—Elias and Catherine cultivated a world of profound sustenance in Tergou. Elias traded in cloth and curried leather. His workshop was a landscape of deep olfactory textures: the sharp, astringent bite of oak-bark tannins, the rich, earthy musk of cured hides that would turn a blade and outlast a generation. 

Catherine was an agrarian matriarch, her kitchen a sanctuary where the mythic rhythms of the seasons integrated seamlessly into daily routine. The couple would have known an Edenic peace, were it not for their nine children. Yet, in their early years, each birth was greeted as a blessing, the house humming with the vibrant, overlapping chatter of youth. But as the children grew, the sheer caloric demand of their survival introduced a subtle, gnawing anxiety into the symphony of the household. 

When Catherine presented the daily meal, it was not mere fuel, but a sacred communion. The huge loaf of rye and spelt, cultured from a wild sourdough starter older than the children themselves, rested on a gigantic trencher like a fortress in its moat. The crust would crackle under the knife—a warm, percussive sound signaling survival—and the sour-sweet crumb would breathe steam into the room. But as the loaf made the tour of the table and melted away, Elias and Catherine would exchange a look, the unspoken fear of famine woven into their native sighs. “Who is to find bread for them all when we are gone?” Elias would murmur.

The younger children, for whom the daily pottage of salted herring, bruised mustard seed, and slow-simmered root vegetables appeared by natural law like the sunrise, struggled to keep their solemn Dutch countenances. But the elder boys felt the shifting weight of the world. 

“Kate, the children grow so, this table will soon be too small,” Elias noted one evening, the rhythmic clatter of wooden spoons suddenly sounding too dense, too crowded.

“We cannot afford it, Eli,” replied Catherine, speaking to the anxiety beneath his words, translating his fear through the intuitive dialect of motherhood. 

Through prudence, they denied themselves luxuries, hoarding what coin they could in a steel-banded coffer. But the cycle of generations is unyielding. One day, their nineteen-year-old son, Richart, approached his mother. Beneath a stoic exterior, he begged to be sent to Amsterdam to apprentice with a merchant. He wished to abandon the deeply textured, agrarian dialect of Tergou for the rigid, arithmetic language of commerce. 

Catherine threw up her hands, her world shattering at the prospect of this cultural and physical severing. “What! leave Tergou!”

“What is one street to me more than another?” Richart replied.

“What! quit your poor father now he is no longer young? Leave your poor brothers and sisters?”

Richart’s will was granite, shaped by a practical calculation that left no room for the poetry of the hearth. “There will be one mouth less for you to feed.”

Young Richart departed for the superficial, mercantile din of Amsterdam, launching himself into a life where ledgers replaced the scent of rising dough. That afternoon at supper, the acoustic balance of the room was forever altered. Catherine stared at the empty chair and wept, an untranslatable expression of a mother’s severing. 

Elias, overwhelmed by the sonic void, shouted roughly, “Sit wider, can't ye: sit wider!” He turned away, the silence echoing louder than any voice. 

The wheel of time turned in its circular pace. Two years later, Richart found a niche in commerce for his brother Jacob. Jacob left Tergou directly after the noon meal of roasted mutton and leeks. At supper, Elias, remembering the hollow echo of the past, whispered low, “Sit wider, dears!” Catherine, who had sworn to hold her peace, heard the phrase and broke. The table was shrinking, their children absorbed by a world that valued them only for their utilitarian output. They had provided for their fourth son as a tailor and their eldest daughter as a robemaker, draining the steel-banded coffer to the dregs.

Four children remained beneath the roof. Two were unable to work: Giles, a dwarf whose physical stature was offset by an acoustic marvel—a voice that was a booming bassoon, a disruptive, thunderous resonance that startled dogs and shattered the quiet hum of the house; and Little Catherine, who moved on crutches, enduring pain with a marble face and violet eyes, never uttering a discordant word. Sybrandt was unwilling to work, preferring idle play to the sacred labor of sustenance. And Cornelis sat by the hearth, calculating his inheritance with cold, unsensory greed.

But the domestic horizon always cleared when they spoke of Gerard.

Gerard was destined for the Church, yet he was a disciple of the physical, sensory world. He did not view his clerical studies as a sterile intellectual pursuit. For Gerard, calligraphy was a deeply gustatory and olfactory art. He craved the smell of beeswax, the texture of fine vellum, the precise grinding of lapis lazuli and earth pigments. His practices were tolerated at home and encouraged by the local monks, who soon found that Gerard’s intuitive mastery of illuminated borders—vines, fruits, and rich arabesques that fed the eye as a feast feeds the body—surpassed their own rigid teachings.

It was Margaret Van Eyck, the surviving sister of the legendary Flemish painters, who truly recognized his spirit. Fleeing the commercialized art centers for Tergou, she introduced Gerard to a higher culinary tier of illumination. She provided the ingredients he could not afford: vibrant vermilion, crushed ultramarine, and real gold leaf. She taught him to appreciate the sourcing of these pigments, treating the colors with the reverence of a meticulous food historian uncovering ancient, sacred recipes. The fine arts and the mechanical arts were not yet violently separated; it was an age where creators shared a visceral, tactile bond. 

Then came the intrusion of Philip “the Good.” The Duke was a man of voracious, superficial appetites, a monarch who collected giants, beasts, and artists with equal commercial detachment. He forcibly baptized captured Turks in tubs, stripping them of their native prayers and spiritual identity so they could serve as exotic background noise in his court—a gross display of the power dynamics inherent in the dominant religious lexicon. Yet, Philip’s vanity demanded great art, and he offered substantial prizes for painting, glasswork, and illumination.

When the bellman cried the tournament through the streets of Tergou—a jarring, metallic clatter disrupting the town’s acoustic ecology—Gerard’s heart raced. He timidly announced he would compete. 

A horrid laugh exploded across the floor. It was Giles. The dwarf’s bassoon-like bellow shattered the auditory peace of the room, a wild, untamed noise roaring like a piece of wide-mouthed ordnance. 

Gerard flushed red, his delicate sensory world bruised by the auditory assault. But White Catherine smiled gently. “Try with the rest, Gerard. Mother and I will pray the Virgin to guide your hand.”

Gerard needed two gold crowns for vellum and prime colors. 

“What!” screamed Catherine, her mind immediately translating the gold into the vital metrics of agrarian survival. “When the bushel of rye costs but a groat! Spend a month's meal and meat and fire on such vanity?” 

For Catherine, the hearth fire and the rye were the ultimate truths; to divert resources from the belly to the parchment seemed a blasphemy against the very nature of sustenance. But after a sharp exit, the matriarch returned, her deep respect for her son's passionate craft overriding her fears. She pressed two little gold pieces into his hand. “Never tyne the ship for want of a bit of tar,” she said, her voice dropping into the comforting, rhythmic cadence of their native dialect.

The day before the prizes were distributed, Gerard walked toward Rotterdam, bearing his exquisite copy of Terence and his finest illuminated borders. Dressed in silver-grey cloth and tight buckskin hose, he left the rustling, organic soundscape of Tergou and approached the dense, oppressive industrial hum of the city. 

Within a league of Rotterdam, his quick eyes caught a disruption in the rhythm of the road. An old man in a rusty gown and worn fur tippet sat exhausted by the wayside, accompanied by a young woman in russet cloth and a head-dress of silver cord. Gerard, who studied the textures of the world with an illuminator’s precision, read the tragic lexicon of their garments: the frayed fur spoke of fallen dignity, the triangular purse of bitter poverty. The girl’s glossy auburn hair and the tears in her eyes struck a chord of profound empathy within him.

He turned back. “Father, I fear you are tired.”

“Indeed, my son,” the old man rasped, “and faint for lack of food.”

The young woman shrank back, ashamed of their vulnerability, whispering in a panicked dialect of pride and fear. But Gerard did not offer hollow, abstract comforts. He understood that true salvation lay in sustenance. 

With the practiced, rhythmic efficiency of one raised by Catherine's hearth, he gathered dry sticks. He struck his flint to the tinder, coaxing a flame until it snapped and sang—a small, comforting acoustic anchor on the dusty road. From his leathern wallet, he withdrew the provisions his mother had meticulously prepared. There was a dense manchet of finely milled wheat, smelling faintly of honey and the wild yeast of his ancestors, and an iron flask containing a robust bone broth, rich with marrow, root-cellared carrots, and the warming hum of black pepper. 

Gerard knelt on the earth and blew gently on the embers, his breath feeding the fire until the flask simmered at the precise temperature to revive a starving stomach without scalding it. He looked up. The girl’s frosty reserve had thawed; she watched his culinary devotion with a demure, appreciative smile. 

He laughed back, the pure joy of the hearth kindling between them. “Mind the pot,” he said, ensuring the sacred liquid was respected, “and don't let it spill, for Heaven's sake: there's a cleft stick to hold it safe with.” 

Leaving her to tend the bubbling broth, Gerard set off running toward a distant corn-field to forage, weaving the mythic, ancient act of provision into the very fabric of the day.

“The soup is hot,” said Gerard, as a plume of steam carried the dark, loamy aromatics of reduced wine and simmered root vegetables into the chill air—an olfactory tether anchoring the immediate moment to generations of autumn harvests.

“But how are we to get it to our mouths?” inquired the senior, his voice carrying the frail, reedy timbre of a man disconnected from the profound equilibrium of sustenance. 

“Father, the young man has brought us straws.” Margaret smiled slyly, the rustle of her skirts a quiet, domestic rhythm against the vast, silent backdrop of the fields.

“Ay, ay!” said the old man. “But my poor bones are stiff, and indeed the fire is too hot for a body to kneel over with these short straws. St. John the Baptist, but the young man is adroit!”

For, while he stated his difficulty, Gerard removed it, moving with the rhythmic grace of one who understands the practical magic of everyday objects. He untied the knot on his breast, took his hat off, and settled a heavy, river-smoothed stone into each corner. Wrapping his hand in the coarse, comforting wool of his jerkin, he whipped the flask off the fire, wedged it securely between the stones, and positioned the hat under the old man's nose with a merry smile. The senior tremulously inserted the pipe of rye-straw and drew the liquid. Lo and behold, the wan, drawn architecture of his face lit up, his skin glowing as the rich, deeply infused broth worked its ancestral alchemy within him. He drew a long, revitalized breath.

“Hippocrates and Galen!” he cried, the sudden resonance in his chest a testament to the meal’s power. “'Tis a *soupe au vin*—the restorative of restoratives. Blessed be the nation that invented it, the woman whose hands coaxed such profound meaning from the hearth, and the young man who brings it to fainting folk. Have a suck, my girl, while I relate to our young host the history and virtues of this sovereign compound. This corroborative, young sir, holds the cultural memory of the ancients; we find it neither in their sterile treatises of medicine, nor in those popular narratives which reveal their remedies, the true names of which are steadily eroding with the death of the old tongues. Hector, in the Ilias, if my memory does not play me false—”

(Margaret sighed softly. “Alas! he's off.”)

“—was invited by one of the ladies of the poem to drink a draught of wine. He declined, claiming he was bound for battle and must not take aught to dull his senses. Now, if the *soupe au vin*—with its meticulously balanced tannins and marrow-rich base—had been known in Troy, it is clear that in declining *vinum merum*, he would have added in the hexameter, 'But a *soupe au vin*, madam, I will degust, and gratefully.' Men going into battle need the profound support of true sustenance, as proved by foolish generals who, bringing hungry soldiers to blows against men fueled by agrarian bounty, suffer defeat. The Romans lost to Hannibal by this neglect alone. This divine elixir gives instant force to the limbs and ardour to the spirit; taken into Hector's body at the nick of time, it would have surely altered mythic history. Note how faint and heart-sick I was a minute ago; well, I suck this celestial cordial, and now behold me brave as Achilles and strong as an eagle.”

“Oh, father, now? An eagle, alack!”

“Girl, I defy thee and all the world. Ready, I say, to devour the space between this and Rotterdam! Strong to combat the ills of life, even poverty and old age, which philosophers call the *summum malum*. *Negatur*; unless the man's life has been ill-spent. Now for the moderns!”

“Father! dear father!”

“Fear me not, girl; I will be brief. The *soupe au vin* occurs not in modern science, a tragic proof that physicians have become idiots. They attribute highest qualities to their thin chicken-broths and decoctions of gold—prizing metal lacking all chemical vitality over meats rich in life-sustaining juices. Mountebanks! Dunces! homicides! From these we gather no light, so we turn to the chroniclers. Duguesclin, a French knight, about to clash with the English, drank not one, but three *soupes au vin* in honour of the Blessed Trinity. Sustentated, fortified, corroborated, and consoled by this divine restorative, he drove the islanders into the sea.”

“Dear father, prithee add thyself to that venerable company ere the soup cools.” Margaret held the hat imploringly, her soft tone a necessary intervention to bring him back to the physical reality of the meal, until he inserted the straw once more. 

This spared them further instances, allowing Gerard to remark how proud his mother would be that her meticulous broth had profited a man of such historical learning.

“Ay! but,” said Margaret, “it would like her ill to see her son give all and take none himself. Why brought you but two straws?”

“Fair mistress, I hoped you would let me put my lips to your straw, there being but two.”

Margaret smiled and blushed, the quiet acoustic intimacy between them deepening. “Never beg that you may command,” said she. “The straw is not mine, 'tis yours: you cut it in yonder field.”

“I cut it, and that made it mine; but after that, your lip touched it, and that made it yours.”

“Did it? Then I will lend it you. There—now it is yours again; your lip has touched it.”

“No, it belongs to us both now. Let us divide it.”

“By all means; you have a knife.”

“No, I will not cut it—that would be unlucky. I'll bite it. There. I shall keep my half; you will burn yours, once you get home, I doubt.”

“You know me not. I waste nothing. It is odds but I make a hairpin of it, or something.”

This answer dashed the novice Gerard, and he fell silent. The bread and soup being reverently disposed of, the old scholar prepared to continue his journey. Then came a little difficulty: Gerard could not tie his ribbon again as his mother had tied it. Margaret, after slyly eyeing his efforts, offered her help. A fair head, with its stately crown of auburn hair glowing through silver, bowed sweetly towards him. Her two white, supple hands played delicately upon the stubborn ribbon, moulding it with soft, airy touches. The quiet friction of the fabric was the only sound, a hushed, sacred acoustic space that brought a heavenly thrill to the young man. Vague glimpses of a new world of feeling opened before him—a subjective experience entirely untranslatable into the clumsy lexicon of his youth. Margaret unwittingly prolonged these exquisite sensations, smoothing the knot with a gentle, convex pressure of her palm, a coaxing hand-kiss that imparted, "Now be a good knot, and stay so."

“There, that is how it was,” she said, drawing back. But looking up for his approval, she received a longing gaze of such ardent adoration that an indescribable tremor seized her. She retreated with downcast lashes and took her father's arm. Gerard, blushing, took the other, and together they propped the eagle along in silence.

They entered Rotterdam by the Schiedamze Poort. Peter directed Gerard toward the Hooch Straet and the Stadthouse, while he and Margaret turned toward the Ooster-Waagen Straet to find their cousin. Almost immediately, they parted ways. 

As Gerard dived into the great town, a profound sense of solitude fell upon him. The streets were crowded, an industrial barrage of clattering carts, shouting merchants, and striking iron—a discordant sonic pollution that fractured the peaceful agrarian rhythm he had just known. The noise felt like a psychological assault. 

“Beshrew my shamefacedness!” he lamented, realizing he had not asked where they lived. “Their words and their breeding were above their means... I shall never see her more. Oh weary world, I hate your chaotic ways. To meet beauty, goodness, and learning, and lose them in this din!”

Drifting through the cacophony, he followed a throng to a large meadow by the Maas. Here, the noise only worsened: games of palm, tumbling, archery, a trained bear standing on its head, a hare beating a drum. To Gerard, this superficial spectacle of commercialized merriment offered no true nourishment to the soul. Hearing that the Duke had gone to the Stadthouse to entertain competitors, he remembered his hunger and his desire to sup with a prince. 

He found the Stadthouse, only to be refused at every door until he reached the courtyard's great gate. It was guarded by soldiers and superintended by a pompous major-domo glittering in a gold chain of office, wielding a white staff. This official stood as a barrier of exclusionary power, his sharp, unyielding voice dictating the dominant lexicon of the city. The crowd washed against him like ripples, retreating in turn. 

When Gerard finally pushed within four heads of the gate, his heart leapt. There stood Peter, with Margaret on his arm, soliciting humbly for entrance.

“My cousin the alderman is not at home; they say he is here,” Peter pleaded.

“What is that to me, old man?” 

“If you will not let us pass in to him, at least take this leaf from my tablet to my cousin...”

“For what do you take me? I carry no messages, I keep the gate,” the major-domo bawled inexorably. “No strangers enter here, but the competitors and their companies.”

“Come, old man,” cried a voice in the crowd, “you have gotten your answer; make way.”

Margaret turned imploringly. “Good people, we are come from far... and our cousin's servant knows us not, and would not let us sit in the house.”

The crowd laughed hoarsely, a brutal, grating sound devoid of any human empathy. Margaret shrank. At that moment, a hand grasped hers—a magic, tactile connection bridging the untranslatable gulf between them. It was Gerard. She gave a little cry of joy and began to whimper, the cruel power dynamics of the city melting away at the sight of this kind face.

“All is well now,” remarked a coarse humorist. “She hath gotten her sweetheart.”

“Haw! haw! haw!” went the crowd.

She dropped his hand, eyes flashing. “I have no sweetheart, you rude men. But I am friendless in your boorish town, and this is a friend who knows how to treat the aged and the weak.”

The crowd fell dead silent, the rebuke cutting through their thoughtless noise. 

Gerard turned to the gatekeeper. “I am a competitor, sir.”

“What is your name?”

“Gerard, the son of Elias.”

The janitor checked his parchment. “Gerard Eliassoen can enter.”

“With my company, these two?”

“Nay; those are not your company, they came before you.”

“What matter? They are my friends, and without them I go not in.”

“Stay without, then.”

“That will I not.”

“That we shall see.”

“We will, and speedily.” Gerard drew breath from the depths of his chest and raised a voice of astounding, mythic volume, an acoustic shockwave that routed the gatekeeper’s superficial authority and rang down the entire street:

“Ho! PHILIP, EARL OF HOLLAND!”

“Are you mad?” cried the porter.

“HERE IS ONE OF YOUR VARLETS DEFIES YOU!”

“Hush, hush!”

“AND WILL NOT LET YOUR GUESTS PASS IN!”

“Hush! murder! The Duke's there. I'm dead,” the janitor quaked. Desperate to overpower Gerard's thunder, he shouted, “OPEN THE GATE, YE KNAVES! WAY THERE FOR GERARD ELIASSOEN AND HIS COMPANY! (The fiends go with him!)”

The gate swung open. Eight soldiers lowered their pikes to form an arch, and the victorious three marched through, leaving the noise of the street behind. 

Once past the portal, they stepped into an oasis of sensory and acoustic equilibrium. The courtyard was a marvel of Oriental luxury. Guests sat beneath a leafy canopy of fresh-cut branches, surrounded by fair fruits—some natural, some meticulously crafted of gold and wax. The Duke's minstrels swept their lutes, their delicate melodies harmonizing with a fountain that played red Burgundy in six jets, battling in the air to form cascades of molten rubies before shedding crimson glories on snowy beards, velvet, and jewelled hilts. 

A whisper buzzed: “Salute the Duke!” On high, under the dais, their sovereign waved a kindly welcome. The men bowed; Margaret curtsied deeply. The Duke signaled his valets, and instantly, seven servants swept forward, seating them and placing fifteen many-coloured soups in little silver bowls, with as many wines in crystal vases. The steaming broths, rich with marrow, saffron, and deep-sea catches, presented a breathtaking culinary landscape.

“Nay, father, let us not eat until we have thanked our good friend,” said Margaret.

“Girl, he is our guardian angel.”

Gerard put his face into his hands. “Tell me when you have done, and I will reappear and have my supper, for I am hungry. I know which of us three is the happiest at meeting again.”

“Me?” inquired Margaret.

“No: guess again.”

“Father?”

“No.”

“Then I have no guess which it can be,” she crowed with quiet happiness. 

The soup vanished, followed by a dozen forms of fish, patties of lobster and almond cream, and an immense variety of complex *brouets* and rissoles. Then came a wild boar, smelling divinely of crushed juniper and roasted crackling. Yet Margaret shrieked and pinched Gerard. The Duke's *cuisinier*—a passionate artist of the kitchen—had rendered the dish too visually captivating, forcefully dragging the mythic terror of the wild into the domestic space of the table. Through elaborate mimicry with burnt sugar and edible colors, he had restored the beast's bristles; preserved the huge tusks in its jaw; and set two eyes of coloured sugar glowing bloodshot and threatening in its head. Old Peter clasped his hands in pious admiration of the culinary technique, while Margaret squeaked in horror and the grizzly brute seemed to glare at all.

“What's to do?” shouted the Duke. Informed of the distress, he laughed. “Give her of the beef-stuffing, then, and bring me Sir Boar.” 

This benevolent prince understood profound sustenance. His private dish, the "beef-stuffing," utilized an entire roasted ox as a radiant thermal cavern to feed the poor, while the interior rendered its rich juices into whatever venison, hares, or poultry were skewered within. At his word, valets drove silver tridents into the steaming bovine cave, spearing a kid, a cygnet, and a flock of wildfowl infused with deep tallow and herbs. Peter’s face expanded and shone at this masterpiece of traditional, agrarian cooking. 

After this came twenty tarts of ancient herbs and fruits, and finally, confectionery on a Titanic scale: cathedrals of spun sugar, moated castles, and knights on horseback. It was a dizzying display of commercialized artistry made solely to be destroyed, and the guests breached bastions and crunched sugar bishops as remorselessly as caraway comfits, sipping spiced hippocras and Corsican wines. Little Turkish boys offered golden troughs of rose-water, the floral scent cooling their hands.

Before this final stage, Gerard had remembered his letter to the Princess Marie, discreetly passing it to a servant who promised to deliver it via her suite. Peter, heavily and happily sated by the profound meal, dropped into a deep, ancestral sleep, entirely forgetting his cousin. That very cousin, recognizing them from afar, chose to ignore them due to their threadbare dress, prioritizing municipal social standing over the quiet bonds of blood. Margaret let her father sleep.

Half the courses were lost on Gerard and Margaret. They were feeding on sweet thoughts, deeply sensitive to the delicate sensuality of the space—the rich interplay of culinary color, the lutes' rhythmic plucking, and the herbaceous perfumes blending in the cool air. 

Margaret leaned back, half closing her eyes to fully absorb the acoustic landscape. “What a lovely scene! The warm sun, the green shade, the bright music of the lutes and the cool music of the fountain... and all faces so happy! And it is to you we owe it.”

Gerard was silent, his eyes speaking volumes.

“Now, speak not to me,” she murmured languidly. “Let me listen to the fountain. What are you a competitor for?”

He told her.

“Very well! You will gain one prize, at least.”

“Which? Have you seen any of my work?”

“I? No. But you will gain a prize.”

“I hope so; but what makes you think so?”

“Because you were so good to my father.”

Gerard smiled at her logic, bowing his head at the sweet praise. 

“Speak not,” Margaret whispered. “They say this is a world of sin and misery. Can that be? What is your opinion?”

“No! That is all a silly old song,” Gerard explained, rejecting the cynical, worn-out lexicon of the elders. “'Tis a byword they keep repeating out of custom; it is not true.”

“How can you know? You are but a child,” said Margaret, with pensive dignity.

“Why, only look round! I thought I had lost you forever; and you are by my side; and now the minstrels play again. Sin and misery? Stuff and nonsense!”

The lutes burst out anew, washing the courtyard in delicate harmony. 

“What do you admire most of all these beautiful things, Gerard?”

“You know my name? How is that?”

“White magic. I am a—witch.”

“Angels are never witches. But I can't think how you—”

“Foolish boy! Was it not cried at the gate loud enough to deave one?”

“So it was. Where is my head? What do I admire most? If you will sit a little more that way, I'll tell you.”

“This way?”

“Yes; so that the light may fall on you. There! I see many fair things here... but the fairest of all, to my eye, is your lovely hair in its silver frame, and the setting sun kissing it. It minds me of what the Vulgate praises for beauty, 'an apple of gold in a network of silver.' Oh, what a pity I did not know you before I sent in my poor endeavours at illuminating! There, now the sun is full on it, it is like an aureole. So our Lady looked, and none since her until to-day.” He struggled against the limitations of his words, wishing he had a richer language to capture the mythic weight of her everyday beauty.

“Oh, fie! It is wicked to talk so. Compare a poor, coarse-favoured girl like me with the Queen of Heaven? Oh, Gerard! I thought you were a good young man.” Margaret was apparently shocked.

Gerard tried to bridge the untranslatable gap of his surging emotions. “I am no worse than the rest; but how can I help having eyes, and a heart... Margaret!”

“Gerard!”

“Be not angry now!”

“Now, is it likely?”

“I love you.”

“Oh, for shame! You must not say that to me,” and Margaret coloured furiously at the sudden assault on her quiet composure.

“I can't help it. I love you. I love you.”

The lingering aromas of spit-roasted capon, basted with wild honey and crushed juniper, still hovered above the heavy oak table when a grave, white-haired seneschal approached. The great banquet was winding down, its mythic, circular rhythm of feasting and satiety settling into a comfortable hum. The seneschal inquired in a hushed, polished tone whether Gerard Eliassoen was of their company, announcing that the Princess Marie wished to confer with him. Instantly, the surrounding chatter ceased, eyes turning with sharp envy toward the young man summoned from his humble plate to the high table. 

Gerard rose. “I wager we shall not see you again,” Margaret said, her voice steady but her cheeks warming with a sudden flush. 

“That you will,” Gerard replied. Bending close, he whispered into the intimate acoustic sphere between them, “This is my good princess; but you are my queen.” Raising his voice to its natural, resonant pitch, he added, “Wait for me, I pray you. I will presently return.”

“Ay, ay!” mumbled Peter, the old agrarian elder, stirring from a post-prandial doze where dreams of dark, loamy soil and seasonal harvests held sway. 

With Gerard gone, the pair in their homespun garments became the cynosure of the hall. Observing this shift in social currency, William Johnson stepped forward. His voice, a jarring mercantile clatter, broke the harmonious sonic environment as he acted surprised and claimed his rural relations. “And to think that there was I at your backs, and you saw me not!”

“Nay, cousin Johnson, I saw you long syne,” Margaret replied coldly, her phrasing rooted in the old, earthly dialect of the provinces—a tongue that carried the memory of generations. 

“You saw me, and spoke not to me?”

“Cousin, it was for you to welcome us to Rotterdam, as it is for us to welcome you at Sevenbergen. Your servant denied us a seat in your house.”

“The idiot!”

“And I had a mind to see whether it was ‘like maid like master,’ for there is sooth in bywords.” She deployed her native proverb with precise intent, a quiet resistance against the dominant, slippery lexicon of city merchants. 

Blushing purple, Johnson retreated to deeds over words. He insisted they return to his home immediately, promising to prove their welcome. Margaret, anchored by the deep, sensory bond she shared with Gerard, demurred. She noted she wished to hear the minstrels again, loath to abandon the rich auditory tapestry of the hall. When Johnson pressed, her true reason surfaced: Gerard would not know where to find them. 

Johnson waved away the concern, his tone dismissive of the profound meaning of their shared space. He summoned his secretary, Hans Cloterman, to wait in their stead. Cloterman emerged—a figure in black serge and violet hose, standing with starched, unyielding rigidity. He was a man utterly devoid of sensory appreciation, the very statue of solemn propriety. Margaret, buoyant with the joy that she and Gerard would sleep beneath the same roof, found the man’s stiff presence almost comical. When she expressed a slight misgiving that the young man might wander off to play, Johnson scoffed, commanding Hans to sit. Cloterman obeyed, taking the seat without a word, his silence not one of peaceful ecology, but of sterile compliance.

Margaret and Peter departed with Johnson, the old man deeply in need of rest after the physical toll of their travels. 

Left alone, Hans Cloterman sat waiting. As the minutes stretched into an hour, the model secretary—free from his employer's gaze—turned his attention to a pitcher of Corsican wine. This was an ancient, robust vintage, pressed from sun-baked grapes grown on rugged hillsides, carrying the mythic weight of the earth and the olfactory landscape of bruised thyme, dark cherries, and crushed slate. But Cloterman consumed it without reverence. To him, this masterwork of agrarian history was mere utilitarian fuel. He quaffed the heavy wine solemnly and rapidly, entirely deaf to its poetry. The wine exacted the revenge of the earth upon the unappreciative palate. By the ninth goblet, Cloterman rose gingerly to toast the Duke of Burgundy, reached his full height, and toppled straight backward onto the floor. The ruby liquid spilled across the flagstones, an unmourned sacrifice, while Cloterman’s face retained its prim infallibility even in the total eclipse of reason.

Elsewhere in the palace, the seneschal led Gerard through winding passages. The natural acoustics of the stone corridors were soon polluted by the sharp, percussive clatter of dice and the harsh, grating voices of feathered young noblemen. It was an assault on Gerard’s acoustic sensitivities. Guided into a room of eleven ladies, he was met with a sudden, oppressive silence as their chatter stopped like jammed machinery, followed by a cruel, synchronized giggling. They spoke the dominant lexicon of the court—a language of power, exclusion, and mockery. Gerard’s eyes fell, his spirit shrinking before the sterile cruelty of their noise. A duenna finally silenced them as though pulling a modesty string, her severe command restoring a fragile peace, though the air remained thick with unexpressed condescension. 

Gerard was deeply unsettled, mourning the loss of the warm, native resonances of his friends. *What must princes be,* he thought, *when their courtiers are so freezing?* 

But as he was ushered into the inner chambers, the environment softened. The subtle scent of cedarwood, beeswax, and sweet orange pomanders replaced the stale air of the outer halls. Here sat the Countess of Charolois and her twelve-year-old daughter, Princess Marie. The Countess’s voice possessed a gentle, melodic cadence. She thanked him for safely delivering a miniature from Margaret Van Eyck. When Marie eagerly offered to reward him, the Countess guided her daughter with quiet wisdom. Gerard, recognizing the untranslatable grace of the space, asked only to hear the song Marie had been singing. 

The Princess took up her lute. As her fingers swept the chords, the notes struck out bright and distinct, weaving seamlessly into the room's hushed acoustics. But it was her voice that commanded the space—round, clear, full, and ringing like a silver bell. She sang a pure Romaunt of the day. There were no false divisions, no whining slurs, none of the trite, turgid fiorituri that mindless Italian songsters sold so dearly, washing out the rich colors of native melody to produce a commercialized skim-milk. Her little mouth seemed the mouth of Nature itself, tapping into a generational memory of song that transcended the rigid courtly lexicon. It was an expression of pure, unadulterated emotion, and as the tender strain flowed, Gerard’s eyes filled with tears. 

“Was he content?” the Countess asked demurely when the final note faded into the cedar-scented air.

“Oh, madam!” Gerard cried, the spoken word breaking the charm. “Surely it is thus that cherubs and seraphs sing, and charm the saints in heaven.”

The Countess looked upon her daughter with a profound, earthly love, then turned to Gerard, fulfilling her old friend’s request. Upon learning he was in minor holy orders, she promised him a church benefice near Tergou the day after he said his first mass. 

Marie protested, insisting Gerard should be the Bishop of Liege. With meticulous, sensory detail, the child recalled the Bishop’s jeweled crown and his magnificent gloves, stiff with gold and heavily perfumed with ancient myrrh and frankincense. “I smelt them all the time he was giving me his blessing,” she recalled, her memory anchored in the olfactory richness of the sacred rite. 

The Countess smiled at her daughter’s innocence, yet her eye rested on Gerard with mild pity. “A priest gives up much,” she murmured. “Often, I fear, he learns too late how much.” She understood the profound sacrifice of the cloister: the severing of the generational line, the denial of the agrarian hearth, the relinquishing of the deep, physical sustenance of a woman’s love. 

Dismissed with royal favor, Gerard followed a page back toward the banquet hall. But a sudden chill seized him. The promise of the benefice clashed violently with the sensory memory of Margaret’s soft hand and the deep, resonant dialect of her voice. To become a priest meant surrendering the vibrant, gustatory, and emotional feast of the life she had opened to him. The revulsion was so strong he resolved to say nothing of the benefice at home. He would seek a secular place at the palace instead, preserving his connection to the earthly rhythms he craved. 

Joyful once more, he burst into the banqueting hall. 

It was empty. Margaret was gone. Peter was gone. The only soul remaining was a soberly dressed citizen—Hans Cloterman—lying dead drunk beneath the table, reeking of oxidized wine and squandered purpose. Oblivious to the man’s identity, Gerard left the beast to lie and ran wildly out of the palace. 

Outside, the night was assaulted by the glaring illumination of two great fan-lights over the gate, turning the natural dusk into an artificial, jarring day. The crowd gaped at the new technology, their minds colonized by the superficial spectacle. When Gerard asked earnestly if they had seen an old man and a lovely girl, they laughed in his face, their coarse voices adding to the sensory pollution. 

Desperate, he followed the sound of a crowd to a canvas tent where a Mystery play was underway. It was a chaotic, commercialized mockery of sacred cycles. Divine personages descended clumsily on winches to spout twaddle, while an imp buffeted Beelzebub with a bladder to the discordant, industrial screech of fife and tabor. This profane exhibition, originally meant to diffuse religious sentiment through the senses, had devolved into a hollow, acoustic nightmare—an erasure of cultural memory replaced by obscenity and cheap spectacle. Gerard found no comfort in this loud, empty art. The dialogue was a mere quibbling of the dominant lexicon, devoid of soul. Sickened by the noise, he abandoned the performance in the second act.

For three heavy, heartsick days, Gerard scoured the streets of Rotterdam. The city became a labyrinth of alienating sounds and sterile encounters, entirely devoid of Margaret's grounding presence. The profound untranslatability of his grief choked him; he had lost the very dialect of his heart. Convinced she was false or had changed her mind, the bitterness of the loss curdled on his tongue. 

Finally, acknowledging defeat, he turned his back on the city. He started the long journey home to Tergou, carrying the promise of royal favor, fifteen golden angels in his purse, a golden medal on his breast, and a heart like a lump of cold, unyielding lead.

It was near four o'clock in the afternoon, and the acoustic rhythm of the hosier’s household had settled into a low, comforting hum. Over the hearth, an iron pot breathed gentle plumes of steam, scenting the sitting-room with the rich, sustaining perfume of bruised coriander, slow-roasted mutton-bone, and sweet caramelized parsnips. Catherine, a matriarch whose profoundest expressions of love were articulated through the precise, ancestral alchemy of the stewpot, had stepped out with her crippled daughter, Kate, to scan the road for Gerard. 

Left alone, Giles disrupted the acoustic ecology of the space. The Hollanders were a people who pioneered not just liberty and oil-painting, but a profound domestic purity; the Tergou home was an instrument of harmonious living. Its highly glazed Dutch tiles were scrubbed daily until they gleamed, a testament to agrarian meticulousness in an era when English floors still choked under decomposing rushes and rancid bones. But Giles, a brawny human wedge whose torso had been crafted with care before nature abandoned his lower half to chance, cared nothing for the sanctity of the hearth. Swinging from the massive, multi-generational oak table, he seized the rim with his teeth. The jarring, unnatural scrape of enamel grinding into ancient wood severed the comforting bubbling of the hearth—a dissonant sonic intrusion as offensive to the spirit as a mouthful of ash.

Kate caught him in the act. Her crutches tapped a delicate, sorrowful counter-rhythm on the tiles. Her heart lay with the meticulously preserved furniture, an extension of her mother’s culinary sanctuary. 

“Oh, Giles! How can you?” she breathed. “Mother is at hand. It dents the table.”

“Go and tell her, little tale-bearer,” Giles snarled, his voice a guttural grating against the soft, native Dutch dialect of the room. 

The untranslatability of their subjective realities—hers of meticulous care for the home’s sustenance, his of chaotic, unanchored malice—ended in Kate's quiet tears. Catherine’s swift return re-established the auditory order; at the matriarch's sharp, experienced tone, Giles hurled himself beneath the table, the very architecture of his dominance collapsing under her native authority. 

“Let no one be in care for me,” came a faint, breathless voice at the door. In tottered Gerard, pale and dusted with the abrasive grit of the roads. The silent, untranslatable grief of scouring Rotterdam for Margaret had hollowed him out. He dropped exhausted into the nearest primitive oak chair, exhaling a sigh that blended at last with the hearth’s gentle hiss. 

As Catherine hurried to surround him, the scent of her simmering broth drawing him back to the physical world, Gerard became the center of an eager circle. First, they demanded to hear of the great art competition. Gerard, ever the meticulous historian of pigment and line, spoke of the competitors' works as a passionate chef speaks of a royal banquet. 

“Oh, mother! Kate!” he murmured, savoring the memory as one rolls a complex, aged cheese over the tongue. “When I saw the goldsmiths' work, I nearly fell. It was a feast that left me staggering. Chalices spun like pulled honey, reliquaries crusted with gems like crystallized fruits. But, Kate, when I came to the illuminated work from Ghent and Bruges... my heart sank.” 

His voice dropped, honoring the quiet acoustics of reverence. “My own work felt like dirt beside it. But then I saw the egg glair, the lapis lazuli ground as fine as milled rye, the crushed gallnuts—the very ingredients of our earth used to preserve the fading breath of old prayers. I thanked God for those patient craftsmen who keep our ancestral tongues from dying out.”

“And whose name was called first?” Kate asked.

“Mine,” Gerard said, ignoring the brothers' scoffing. “But the herald shouted it in the high, flattened vowels of the courtly elite. Everything swam before my eyes. The Duke spoke to me, but I was so fluttered by the power dynamics of his dominant tongue that I could not answer. He gave me this.” He laid a gold medal on the table with a heavy, resonant clink, then produced fifteen great, bright golden angels. 

The gold drew out their characters. Cornelis and Sybrandt snatched their coins with the hollow, metallic grasp of men who viewed sustenance solely as utilitarian fuel, devoid of sensory appreciation. Giles grabbed his and rolled it, chasing the harsh rattling sound across the tiles. But Kate held out her little arms to Gerard with a gesture of pure, wordless love. Catherine, weeping at the shower of gold in her apron, recognized the coins as a multi-generational guarantee of full larders and unending warmth, throwing her arms around his neck in a fierce, tearful embrace. Eli, moved by his son's triumph, immediately borrowed the medal to broadcast the news to his neighbor, Peter Buyskens.

“Stay a minute, father; there is better news,” Gerard said. He recounted the Countess's promise of a benefice. A profound peace settled over the elders; the household's future was secured, its culinary and cultural traditions insulated from want. His brothers, however, only anticipated the day his reverence’s purse would be open to their parasitic appetites, eager to see him gone.

As the wound of Margaret’s rejection throbbed with a duller ache—a bitter rind he swallowed but could no longer taste—Gerard turned his fierce sensory focus toward the priesthood. To master his grief, he immersed himself in the Vulgate, absorbing the ancient, dominant Latin lexicon. The rituals of the Church were not abstract philosophy to him; they were deeply physical. When the bishop ordained him an "exorcist," the third step in holy orders, it was a seamless integration of the mythic into the everyday. Kneeling, Gerard received a small manuscript of exorcisms. The bishop’s Latin blessing washed over him, a potent acoustic frequency designed to cast out unseen, malignant spirits from the domestic sphere.

Returning home with this mythic authority tucked under his arm, his boots crunching in the quiet afternoon, Gerard was met by little Kate on her crutches. 

“Oh, Gerard! Who, think you, hath sent to our house seeking you? The burgomaster himself, Ghysbrecht Van Swieten.”

The name struck a discordant note, an industrial dissonance against the village's agrarian rhythm. “What would he with me?”

“Nay, Gerard, I know not. But he seems urgent to see you. You are to go to his house on the instant.”

Gerard hesitated, tasting an old, metallic dread at the back of his throat. “I will go, but it likes me not. Kate, I have seen him cast such a look on me as no friend casts. No matter; such looks forewarn the wise. To be sure, he knows.”

“Knows what, Gerard?”

A profound, untranslatable silence stretched between them, heavy with the weight of unspoken histories. 

“Nothing,” he said softly. “Kate, I'll go.”

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten was a man whose craftiness steeped like a slow, bitter infusion of wormwood and gall. He opened on the young novice with a fragrant but deceptive appetizer, something quite wide of the heavy, consequential feast he was truly aiming to serve. 

“The town records,” said the burgomaster, his voice a low, grating resonance that disrupted the fragile acoustic harmony of the study, “are crabbedly written in a fading, ancestral dialect. The ink is rusty with age, smelling of oxidized iron and fermented oak tannins.” 

He offered Gerard the ostensible honor of transcribing them fair, effectively tasking him with the preservation of the town’s multi-generational memory—a delicate process of translating a dying regional tongue into the dominant commercial lexicon. 

Gerard, a meticulous historian of both word and sustenance, inquired what he was to be paid for preserving such vital cultural marrow. Ghysbrecht offered a pittance, a sum that would have just purchased the raw ingredients: the salt-cured parchment, the crushed-walnut ink, and the goose quills. 

“But, burgomaster, my labour? Here is a year's work, an entire cycle of seasons.” 

“Your labour?” Ghysbrecht scoffed, the harsh, plosive consonants of his mockery echoing unpleasantly against the stone walls, entirely lacking the rhythmic warmth of traditional agrarian speech. “Call you marking parchment labour? Little sweat goes to that, I trow. It is not the churning of butter or the kneading of dense rye dough.” 

“'Tis labour, and skilled labour to boot,” Gerard countered, his palate offended by the man’s utilitarian reduction of art. “To decode the untranslatable subjective experiences of our ancestors, to clarify their forgotten idioms as one might clarify a complex, murky broth into a pristine consommé—that is better paid in all crafts than rude labour, sweat or no sweat. Besides, there's my time.” 

“Your time? Why, what is time to you, at two-and-twenty?” Then, fixing his eyes keenly on Gerard to mark the effect of his words, Ghysbrecht abandoned the pretense of the town’s linguistic heritage. “Say, rather, you are idle grown. You are in love. Your body is with these chanting monks, lost in their resonant, harmonic acoustic ecology, but your heart hungers elsewhere. It is with Peter Brandt and his red-haired girl, whose very presence evokes the rich, mythic scent of wild fennel and hearth-baked bread.” 

“I know no Peter Brandt,” Gerard said, though his throat tightened, swallowing the lie like a mouthful of ash. 

This denial confirmed Ghysbrecht's suspicion that the caster-out of demons was playing a deep, clandestine game. 

“Ye lie!” Ghysbrecht shouted, the sudden, violent volume a harsh pollutant in the quiet room. “Did I not find you at her elbow on the road to Rotterdam, amidst the clatter of the market carts?” 

“Ah!” 

“Ah! And you were seen at Sevenbergen but t'other day, where the ancient mills still sing in tune with the wind.” 

“Was I?” 

“Ah, and at Peter's house.” 

“At Sevenbergen?” 

“Ay, at Sevenbergen.” 

Now, this was what in modern days is called a draw. It was a cunningly spiced guess, put boldly forth as fact, to elicit by the young man's immediate, unthinking reaction whether he had recently tasted the hospitality of that specific agrarian hearth or not. 

The result of the artifice surprised the crafty elder. Gerard started up in a strange state of nervous excitement, the sonic rhythm of his breathing turning sharp and erratic. 

“Burgomaster,” said he, with trembling voice, the raw, subjective truth of his feelings utterly untranslatable into the old man's cynical vocabulary, “I have not been at Sevenbergen these three years, and I know not the name of those you saw me with, nor where they dwelt; but, as my time is precious, though you value it not, give you good day.” 

And he darted out, with his eyes sparkling, desperate to escape the stifling, commercialized noise of the burgomaster’s chambers for the purer acoustic sanctuary of the open road. 

Ghysbrecht started up in huge ire, knocking a silver goblet of spiced wine to the floor with a discordant clatter, but he sank into his chair again. 

“He fears me not. He knows something, if not all.” Then he called hastily to his trusty servant, dragging the man to a leaded window overlooking the cobblestones, where the rhythmic, metallic pounding of the town's industry threatened to drown out all natural thought. “See you yon man?” he cried. “Haste! follow him! But let him not see you. He is young, but his palate for craft is old. Keep him in sight all day. Let me know whither he goes, and what sustenance he seeks.” 

It was night when the servant returned, the evening air thick with the scent of damp soil and woodsmoke. 

“Well? well?” cried Van Swieten eagerly, hungry for the extraction of secrets. 

“Master, the young man went from you straight to Sevenbergen.” 

Ghysbrecht groaned, a low, guttural sound of indigestion and defeat. 

“To the house of Peter the Magician.”

A man may lower a bucket into the deep, echoing well of his own self-deception, but what he draws up is rarely the clear, pure water of truth. Rather, he tastes only what his palate has been conditioned to expect. Halfway along the road to Sevenbergen, Gerard listened to the rhythmic crunch of his boots on the packed dirt and tried to decipher the acoustic landscape of his own heart. He told himself he was merely walking out of scholarly curiosity to understand why Margaret had jilted him, a neatly reasoned excuse masking a visceral hunger. He assured himself that he was perfectly satisfied with the sterile, reverberant halls of his prospective church benefice, content to live within the dominant, imperial lexicon of ecclesiastical Latin, entirely insulated from the fickle, untranslatable dialect of a woman’s affections.

Yet, as the chaotic, jarring clamor of Rotterdam’s commercialized artistry faded—the screeching iron wheels and shouting tradesmen replaced by the restorative acoustic ecology of the countryside—his senses betrayed his logic. He found Peter Brandt’s cottage nestled within a symphony of agrarian life. There sat Margaret in the doorway, the steady, domestic *thwip-thwip* of her sewing needle harmonizing with the deep, resonant thrum of a longbow. A stalwart figure leaned upon the weapon, conversing with her. Gerard felt an unaccountable, dissonant pang of territorial panic, a sudden disruption in his internal soundscape. However, the man proved to be past fifty years of age—Martin, an old agrarian soldier who read the wind and the soil with a seasoned archer's precision.

Another minute and the youth stood before them. Margaret looked up, her face flushing the bright, bruised color of crushed raspberries before chilling into a mask of pale, unyielding flour. She dismissed these physical signs of her agitation with a protective silence, turning far more chill and indifferent than she would have had she not been caught off guard.

"What! is it you, Master Gerard? What on earth brings you here, I wonder?" Her voice was deliberately flat, devoid of its usual melodic cadence. 

"I was passing by and saw you; so I thought I would give you good day, and ask after your father."

"My father is well. He will be here anon."

"Then I may as well stay till he comes."

"As you will. Good Martin, step into the village and tell my father here is a friend of his."

"And not of yours?" Gerard asked.

"My father's friends are mine."

"That is doubtful," Gerard pressed, the tension thickening the air between them. "It was not like a friend to promise to wait for me, and then make off the moment my back was turned. Cruel Margaret, you little know how I searched the town for you; how for want of you nothing was pleasant to me."

"These are idle words; if you had desired my father's company, or mine, you would have come back. There I had a bed laid for you, sir, at my cousin's, and he would have made much of you, and, who knows, I might have made much of you too. I was in the humour that day. You will not catch me in the same mind again, neither you nor any young man, I warrant me."

"Margaret, I came back the moment the Countess let me go; but you were not there."

"Nay, you did not, or you had seen Hans Cloterman at our table; we left him to bring you on."

"I saw no one there, but only a drunken man, that had just tumbled down."

"At our table? How was he clad?"

"Nay, I took little heed: in sad-coloured garb."

At this, Margaret's face gradually warmed. Assuming a facade of incredulity and severity, she put many shrewd questions to him, all of which Gerard answered most loyally. Finally, the subjective experiences that had divided them—untranslatable in their isolation—found a shared vocabulary. The misunderstanding of the drunken Hans Cloterman was laid bare. The acoustic tension in the courtyard released into a soft sigh of revulsion against their own stubbornness, followed by a tender rush of mutual confessions. Neither had been happy since; neither ever would have been happy but for this intersection of their timelines.

Then Gerard’s eye caught a manuscript Vulgate lying open upon the rough-hewn oak table. He pounced upon it with the fervor of a meticulous historian. The heavy, vellum leaves bore the official weight of the Church's Latin, but before he could immerse himself in that dominant, rigid lexicon, two white hands clapped flat upon the page, and a red face leaned over them.

"Nay, take away your hands, Margaret, that I may see where you are reading, and I will read there too at home; so shall my soul meet yours in the sacred page. You will not? Nay, then I must kiss them away."

He kissed them so often that for very shame they were fain to withdraw, and, lo! the sacred book lay open at, "An apple of gold in a network of silver."

"There, now," she said, "I had been hunting for it ever so long, and found it but even now—and to be caught!" With a touch of inconsistency, she pointed it out to Gerard with her white finger.

"Ay," said he, "but to-day it is all hidden in that great cap."

"It is a comely cap, I'm told by some."

"Maybe; but what it hides is beautiful."

"It is not: it is hideous."

"Well, it was beautiful at Rotterdam."

"Ay, everything was beautiful that day," she murmured with a little sigh.

And now Peter came in. The traditional agrarian elder brought with him the rich, grounding aroma of turned loam and damp pine needles, his booming, resonant voice a balm against the memory of the town's industrial noise. He welcomed Gerard cordially and insisted he stay for supper. 

Margaret vanished to the hearth. When she reappeared, the mythic occurrences of love had seamlessly integrated into the domestic routine. She had shed the heavy, utilitarian cap; her hair was caught in the silver net, shimmering in the firelight. She moved with the grace of a passionate chef, spreading a supper that spoke the native, gustatory dialect of their ancestors—a profound cultural memory preserved in smoke and salt. There was a rustic hearth-baked loaf, its thick, blistered crust crackling as it was torn, giving way to the steaming, earthy crumb of heritage wheat; a rich, slow-simmered pottage of marrow-bone and foraged root vegetables that filled the room with an olfactory landscape of wild thyme and roasted garlic. Gerard, savoring the profound meaning in this sustenance, found his soul nourished far beyond the capacity of his dry manuscripts. Margaret shot him glances half arch, half coy, beaming bright with gaiety as they shared the meal.

In the cool evening, guided by the ambient, nocturnal chirping of crickets and the rustle of alder leaves, Gerard coaxed her out. She objected, yet came; he coaxed her onto the road to Tergou, and she declined, yet came. They strolled up and down, hand in hand, weaving the threads of their past sorrows into a pledge never to quarrel or misunderstand one another again. A long, loving kiss sealed their vow, and Gerard walked home on wings.

From that day, the cycle of Gerard's evenings belonged to Margaret. Their attachment deepened through shared meals and shared silences, a multi-generational saga of human connection repeating its eternal loop, until the hours they spent together were the only hours they truly lived; the rest they merely counted and underwent. At the outset, this deep attachment went smoothly. Obstacles there were, but they seemed distant and small to the eyes of hope, youth, and love. The dominant powers and societal prejudices that their union would thwart gave no warning smoke to show their volcanic nature and power. The course of true love ran smoothly, placidly, until it had drawn these two young hearts into its current for ever. And then—

One bright morning, the rhythmic, meditative grinding of Catherine’s stone mortar—crushing coarse sea salt into dried winter savory, wild garlic, and toasted mustard seed—was suddenly fractured by an unwonted, grating cacophony from the streets of Tergou. The acoustic harmony of her kitchen, usually a sanctuary defined by the gentle, sustaining simmer of bone broths and the crackle of the hearth, was obliterated by the harsh clatter of hooves and the metallic jangle of a commercialized spectacle. The French ambassador was riding through, parading the servants of the Duke of Burgundy in a display of sterile, manufactured pomp that violently disrupted the neighborhood's sonic ecology. 

Catherine wiped her hands on her flour-dusted apron, her fingers still carrying the rich, earthy terroir of the heritage root vegetables she had been peeling, and moved to the window. Beneath the balcony where her dwarf son, Giles, sat, the Duke’s tumbler rode with a forced, sedate majesty. Whenever the awe of the crowd grew oppressive, he rolled off his horse with an ignoble, practiced clumsiness, climbing back up by the tail. It was a cheap, commodified imitation of joy, lacking the profound, sustaining nourishment of true communal celebration. Behind him paced the Duke’s leopard, chained to a huntsman upon a giant Flemish horse. When the townsfolk pressed too close, enchanted by the beast's glossy hide, the huntsman shouted in a terrible, abrasive voice that tore through the morning air, threatening them with the Duke’s subterranean prisons where rats stripped the flesh from dissidents' bones. 

The Tergovians shrank back, their native murmurs of curiosity silenced by the dominant, carceral lexicon of their sovereign. But the oppressive atmosphere was soon punctured by a bizarre acoustic dissonance. The Duke’s giant, a Hungarian standing over seven feet tall, brought up the rear. Despite his immense size, he possessed a treble, fluty voice of pitiable power—a sonic anomaly that he wielded without self-awareness. Catching sight of Giles on the balcony, the giant stopped.

“Hallo! brother!” he squeaked, the thin, reedy sound violently at odds with his frame. 

“You are plain enough to see,” bellowed Giles, his voice a magnificent, resonant bass drum that reclaimed the acoustic space of their home.

“Come on my shoulder, brother,” squeaked the Titan, extending a massive fist. 

“If I do I'll cuff your ears,” roared the dwarf. 

The crowd erupted, laughing not at the giant's wit, but at the ridiculous incongruity of the two voices—the mountain delivered of a squeak, and the mole-hill belching thunder. Giles, a creature devoid of fear and fiercely protective of his environment, lost all patience. He dropped directly upon the giant's neck, seizing hair with one hand and punching with the other. The giant’s initial laughter quickly turned to panicked, high-pitched shrieks under the rapid, heavy blows. He backed wildly into the balcony, but Giles whipped gracefully over the railing just as the giant’s head connected with the wood in a stunning, resonant crack. The Tergovians roared in exultation, their native spirits momentarily triumphing over the imported spectacle. Catherine shrieked, throwing her apron—still smelling of rendered goose fat and woodsmoke—over Giles as the indignant giant stumbled away. 

This assertion of local sovereignty had pleasant immediate consequences. The Tergovians, proud of their little champion, began freely offering Giles scraps of high-quality parchment. Giles drove a regular trade in this tactile, olfactory-rich material with his brother Gerard, bartering the raw skins of cultural memory for copper coins. 

By the afternoon, the disruptive noise of the street had faded, and the kitchen returned to its sacred, ancestral rhythms. Catherine and her daughter, Kate, sat by the hearth, sorting through a basket of late-season apples. They spoke in the soft, rolling cadences of their native dialect—a language rich with agricultural metaphors that the younger generations were slowly forgetting. They chatted about their favorite theme: Gerard, his anticipated benefice, and the brightened prospects of their lineage. To them, a priest in the family was not merely a title; it meant a custodian of their spiritual and physical sustenance, one who would bless their harvests and share in their meticulously prepared feasts.

“He will marry Cornelis and Sybrandt,” Catherine said, her hands effortlessly peeling an apple in one continuous, curling ribbon. “Gerard will take care of you and Giles, when we are gone.”

“Yes, mother, and we can confess to him instead of to a stranger,” said Kate, leaning on her crutches. 

“Ay, girl! And he can give the sacred oil to your father and me, and close our eyes when our time comes.”

“Oh, mother! Pray speak not of that. Let us be gay to-day. I am out of all pain; it does seem so strange, and I feel so bright. Mother, can you keep a secret?”

“Nobody better, child.”

“Then I will show you something beautiful. Only Gerard must never know; he covers it up so, and sometimes he carries it away.” 

Kate moved slowly to a wooden chest where they stored dried chamomile and aged cheeses. She returned with a cloth, uncovering a small panel of wood. Upon it was painted a breathtaking portrait of the Virgin, wearing a tiara over a wealth of beautiful, burning gold-red hair that flowed loose over her shoulders. 

Catherine gasped, struck with a profound, almost gustatory awe. The colors were as rich and vibrant as crushed berries and saffron. “It is the Queen of Heaven,” she whispered. “I never saw one like her to my mind before.”

“And her eyes, mother,” Kate murmured, “lifted to the sky. And to think I have a son that can make the saints live again upon a piece of wood. He is too good for this world.”

Ere they had half done admiring the mythic grace resting upon their kitchen table, a harsh, grating voice disrupted their sanctuary. By instinctive reflex, the women hid the picture in the cloth. The door swung open to reveal Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, the burgomaster. He had not stepped foot in their home for ten years. His presence immediately soured the olfactory landscape of the room, bringing with it the cold, sterile scent of ink, administration, and unyielding municipal power. 

The two women stared in contemptuous incredulity. 

“Yes, I own the last time I came here was not on a friendly errand,” Ghysbrecht said, his voice lacking any of the musical resonance of their native tongue. “To-day I come on your business. It is about your son Gerard. He has fallen into bad hands.”

“Now Heaven and the saints forbid!” Catherine cried, her maternal instincts flaring. “Speak out, and quickly: speak ere you have time to coin falsehood.”

Ghysbrecht turned pale, his spite mingling with his cold, utilitarian worldview. “Your son Gerard is more like to be father of a family than a priest. He is for ever with Margaret, Peter Brandt's red-haired girl, and loves her like a cow her calf.”

Mother and daughter burst into laughter. The sheer untranslatability of Gerard’s profound, spiritual soul into the burgomaster’s base, transactional terms was absurd to them. 

“Carry this tale to those who know not my son,” Catherine scoffed. 

“My servant has seen them together a score of times,” Ghysbrecht insisted, grinding his teeth. “Reading babies in one another's eyes.”

Kate’s face fell, the acoustic shift in the room triggering a deep unease. “Mother, if it should be true?”

“Nay!” Kate quickly corrected herself, her devotion overriding her doubt. “Gerard is too good to love a creature of earth. Look!” She triumphantly threw back the cloth, revealing the portrait. “If his heart was earthly, could he paint the Queen of Heaven like that?”

Ghysbrecht’s mouth fell open. He stared at the vibrant pigments, his mind entirely incapable of grasping the subjective, mythic elevation of the art. He saw only literal forms, a mind thoroughly conditioned by the rigid boundaries of state lexicons. “Why, it is herself,” he gasped.

“You admire it?” Kate smiled, her hostility softening. “I forgive you for frightening us.”

“Am I in a mad-house?” Ghysbrecht sputtered. “You show me a picture of the girl, and you say it proves he cannot love her. They all paint their sweethearts!”

“A picture of the girl?” Kate cried, shocked. “This is our blessed Lady.”

“No, no; it is Margaret Brandt! Silly child! Look at her red hair! Would the Virgin be seen in red hair?”

At this moment, the anxious face of their neighbor, Peter Buyskens, appeared at the door, bringing with him the comforting, yeasty aroma of the local brewery. “What is to do?” he whispered cautiously. “We can hear you all across the street.”

“They cast their glamour on him!” Ghysbrecht shouted, turning to Peter. “Look and tell me who is that?”

Peter smiled, his eyes fascinated by the portrait. “Why, you know as well as I do; but what have they put a crown on her for?”

“Man alive! Speak a wench's name plain out!” Ghysbrecht snarled. 

“If it isn't as natural as life,” Peter affirmed. “Who else should it be but Margaret Brandt? A mirror is not truer.”

The brutal confirmation shattered the women's reality. They threw their aprons over their heads, rocking to and fro as they mourned the sudden, violent intersection of their sacred illusions with earthly truths. 

Moments later, Elias entered from his shop. The heavy, rhythmic thud of his boots halted as he took in the scene. The presence of the burgomaster, coupled with his wife’s distress, drained the color from his face. “Richart! Jacob!” he gasped, fearing the ultimate, unnamable silence of death.

“Nobody is dead or dying, old friend,” Ghysbrecht said, proceeding to translate the complex, emotional tangle of youth into his sterile narrative of disobedience, pointing to the picture as evidence.

“Is that all?” Eli breathed, profoundly relieved. “Boys will be boys. He will outgrow that disease.”

But as Ghysbrecht elaborated on the intimacy of the courtship, Eli’s brow darkened. The agrarian patriarch within him, bound to generational expectations, flared up. “Marriage! That shall never be. I'll stay that; ay, by force, if need be.”

“I made old John Koestein lock young Albert up in the prison of the Stadthouse till he knocked under,” the burgomaster offered, wielding his administrative power like a blunt instrument. “Forty-eight hours on bread and water cooled his hot stomach.”

“Oh, the cruelty of men!” Catherine sighed, her culinary heart breaking at the thought of utilizing the deprivation of sustenance as a weapon of psychological control. 

“It won't come to that with me and my son,” Eli said sternly. “He never disobeyed me. Where is he?”

“At Sevenbergen,” Ghysbrecht replied. 

Supper passed in a heavy, unnatural silence. The food, though meticulously prepared, tasted like ash, the joy of sustenance entirely hollowed out by the looming conflict. Eight o'clock came, and still no Gerard. Eli sent the children to bed. 

“You and I will walk abroad, wife,” Eli said quietly. “On the road to Sevenbergen.”

The old pair walked hand in hand into the night. It was a clear, balmy evening. Here, away from the stone walls and municipal edicts, the acoustic ecology of the countryside embraced them. The whispering of ancient willows, the rhythmic trill of crickets, and the soft crunch of dirt beneath their feet began to heal the day's psychological abrasions. Time seemed to fold in on itself, the circular pacing of generations bringing them back to the very paths they had walked decades ago.

“It is a many years since we walked so late, my man,” Catherine said softly, the damp, rich scent of the nocturnal earth filling her lungs. 

“Not since our courting days, Eli,” she continued. “Nature is strong, and the same in all our generations. You won't speak harshly to the poor child?”

“Surely.”

“A heart to share joy and grief with is a great comfort to man or woman. Isn't it, Eli?”

“It is so, my lass,” he murmured, his rigid administrative posturing melting away into the timeless, agrarian wisdom of his ancestors. “Ah! here comes the young fool.”

Catherine trembled, gripping her husband’s hand. Ahead of them, bathed in bright moonlight but unaware of their presence in the shadows, Gerard was walking toward them. His face was shining, and he was singing a sweet, melodic tune—a pure, resonant expression of subjective experience that defied all translation, echoing perfectly through the ancient, interwoven branches of the night.

Time in Sevenbergen moved in slow, generational circles, its rhythms dictated by the turning of the seasons and the perpetual, smoky warmth of the hearth. Margaret stood before her cold iron cauldrons, tracing the rim of a blackened vessel that held centuries of her ancestors’ culinary memory. The air in the cottage carried the faint, lingering ghosts of dried winter savory and wild garlic, but the larder was devoid of the rich fats and marrow that gave their domestic existence its profound meaning. Gerard would soon return, his body hollowed by the day's labor. To Margaret, a meal was never mere utilitarian fuel; it was the sacred translation of love and cultural memory into something that could be tasted, smelled, and absorbed. To offer him an empty table was to sever an ancestral cord. 

Distressed by this disruption to their domestic routine, she turned to Martin Wittenhaagen. The grizzled agrarian elder possessed hands that knew the soil and the sinew of the land as intimately as his mother tongue. “Martin, there is nothing to set before him,” she whispered, her voice carrying the low, guttural cadence of their native Dutch dialect—a language rich with hyper-specific words for the damp earth and the rendering of fats, now slowly being suffocated by the sharp, unyielding syllables of the ruling courts. “It is Thursday. I counted on your bow.”

Martin sighed, scratching his weathered head. He pulled from his pouch a stiff, lifeless parchment bearing the heavy wax seal of Philip, Duke of Burgundy. The document was a weapon of the dominant lexicon, a decree written in the sterile, imperial phrasing of the ruling class. It dictated that Martin, despite his ancestral agrarian rights, could only hunt on Thursdays, provided the Duke’s hunting party did not disturb the woods. The untranslatable, subjective experience of the forest—the hunter’s quiet communion with the prey—was reduced by this foreign, legalistic tongue to a transactional ledger, punishable by the severing of a head and a fine of a penny. 

“The Duke hunts today,” Martin rumbled, his voice a steady acoustic baseline against the cottage's quietude. “But for your hearth, I will risk my carcass in the skirts of the forest.”

Martin slipped into the ancient woods, a space where mythic occurrences still brushed seamlessly against the edges of everyday survival. Typically, the forest was a cathedral of pristine acoustic ecology: the subtle, percussive tapping of woodpeckers, the soughing wind through old-growth oak, and the crunch of dry loam. But today, this delicate sonic environment was violently ruptured. The brassy, atonal blare of royal hunting horns fractured the air—an oppressive, jarring noise emblematic of commercialized sport, a superficial violence that cared nothing for the sacred equilibrium of the woods. 

Taking his stand behind a thick oak, Martin strung his English yew bow. The six-foot weapon was an extension of his own sinew; when drawn, the thick string hummed with the pure, resonant frequency of a plucked harp, perfectly in tune with the natural world. A hare cantered into the glade, sitting sprightly, its ears forming a sharp, alert V. Its scent was earthy and rich. Martin loosed an arrow, but it hissed along the short grass, passing just beneath the hare’s belly. The creature bounded away. Next, a wood-pigeon settled on the branch above, a promise of delicate, dark meat for a slow braise. Martin drew carefully, but the arrow merely tore the bird’s breast, carrying its feathers skyward like a cloud of gray dust before the pigeon fluttered off to die unseen in the thick foliage.

The discordant baying of hounds echoed closer, disrupting the natural frequencies of the glade. Then, a mythic sight manifested within the mundane rustle of the brush: a magnificent, seven-year-old buck, rich with the promise of tender loins and marrow-filled bones, crashed into the clearing. Yet it was pursued by an impossible shadow. A long, spotted beast—the Duke’s leopard—glided across the earth, a creature of foreign lore dropped violently into the damp Dutch ecosystem. 

The leopard leaped upon the buck’s back, its claws tearing into the hide, the copper tang of blood instantly blooming in the damp air. Driven by the primal necessity of the hearth, Martin loosed his arrow. It sang its deadly, perfect note, burying deep into the deer. The buck bounded high and fell dead. The meat was secured.

But the leopard, brutally ignorant of the agrarian hierarchy, ceased gorging on the blood and turned its flaming eyes on Martin. The elder stood erect, attempting to dominate the beast with a steady gaze, but the leopard lunged with a frightful yell, its jaws distended. A cacophony of snarls shattered the glade’s fragile silence. The beast's claws sank into Martin’s shoulder, tearing through his flesh. The other paw swiped at his cheek, but Martin, an old-fashioned man of the earth, wore no hat, only a coarse wool scapulary drawn over his head like a cowl. The brute's claw tangled inextricably in the loose leather.

Martin’s hands, strong from decades of working the land and butchering game, locked around the leopard’s throat. They fought in the visceral, wordless dialect of survival. It felt like blunt reaping-hooks grinding into his shoulder, but the pain only inflamed his blood. The scent of wild musk, hot blood, and crushed ferns filled his nostrils. He squeezed with iron force. The beast thrashed, dragging the cowl over Martin's eyes, blinding him, but the elder held on until the creature’s violent elasticity faded. He choked it until it was a powerless thing, dashing it to the earth. Removing his cowl, Martin stared at the mute, bloody-pawed mythic beast and felt terror. He had slain the Duke’s leopard. 

Covering the beast with handfuls of leaves, Martin shouldered the heavy, blood-rich buck and staggered back to Sevenbergen, leaving a dark trail in the loam.

He burst into the cottage, a horrifying, bloodstained figure, and flung the deer’s carcass onto the stone floor. “No questions,” he gasped. “Broil me a steak of it, for I am faint.”

Margaret did not shrink from the blood; she saw only the profound potential of sustenance. With the meticulous reverence of a food historian, she began the butchery, assuming the blood belonged entirely to the deer. While Martin quietly bound his torn shoulder in the corner, Margaret stoked the fire with dry birch until it crackled to life. She expertly carved thick steaks from the haunch. When the venison hit the scorching iron pan, the air filled with the intoxicating, rich aroma of rendering fat, seared crust, and woodsmoke—a gustatory symphony that transcended the sterile decrees of the Duke's parchment. 

Gerard arrived, bringing a ceramic flask of Schiedam. Under the influence of the botanical, juniper-laced spirit, which perfectly complemented the gamey, iron-rich flavor of the venison, Martin revived. They ate voraciously, chewing on the deep flavors of the forest, laughing in their native tongue, their words weaving a tapestry of cultural memory around the roaring hearth.
