But the circular rhythm of time brought the violence of the outside world crashing back. The cottage’s acoustic warmth was suddenly shattered by a single, deep, resonant bay of a bloodhound in the dark wood. 

Margaret’s laughter died. Her eyes fixed on the window, her cheek paling. She gasped, pointing with a trembling finger. There, crouching in the twilight, was a dark form. Two eyes burned like green glowworms. The mythic beast had survived.

Martin trembled at the sound of the hound. “They have lost her scent and laid muzzled bloodhounds on her trail. They will find her here, and the venison. Good-bye, friends. Martin Wittenhaagen ends here.”

Gerard seized the great yew bow and shoved it into the elder's hands. “Be a man!” he cried. “Shoot her, and fling her into the wood ere they come up!”

The hounds' voices multiplied, a chaotic, oppressive wave of noise drowning out the hearth's crackle. 

“Curse her!” Martin cried. “I spared her once; now she must die.” He reared the bow, drawing the arrow to the head, ready to release violence into the night.

“Nay! Nay!” Margaret cried, fiercely protecting the sanctity of their culinary sanctuary. She seized the arrow, snapping the wooden shaft in two. The pieces clattered to the floor, breaking the spell of the weapon. 

“What have you done, wench?” Martin roared. “You have put the halter round my throat!”

“No!” Margaret shouted, her voice cutting through the dominant lexicon of force with the urgent, untranslatable pitch of survival. “I have saved you! Stand back from the window, both! Your knife, quick!” 

She snatched his long-pointed boning knife—a tool of culinary creation now repurposed for defense—from his girdle and darted from the room. Outside, the house was surrounded by the deafening clamor of baying dogs and shouting men, while the green, glowing eyes remained fixed, unblinking in the dark.

Margaret’s knife glided with practiced grace through the haunch of venison, separating the rich, dark muscle from the bone. The cut released the heavy, iron-tang of wild blood, mingling with the olfactory tapestry of the hearth—crushed juniper berries, dried thyme, and the sweet, lingering ash of applewood. She ran to the window, the heavy meat warm in her hands, and hurled the offering out into the dark, straight toward the glowing, emerald eyes of fire. 

The great leopard—a mythic spirit of the forest that had seamlessly become a nocturnal fixture of their domestic rhythm—darted upon the flesh with a guttural, primal snarl. The rhythmic crunching of bone and the tearing of sinew became part of the night’s steady acoustic ecology. But this ancient harmony was violently ruptured. A hound uttered a bay so near and jagged it tore through the acoustic sanctum of the house. The three at the window shrank together, assaulted by the harsh dissonance of the approaching hunt. The leopard, fiercely protective of her sustenance, glided swiftly and stealthily away toward the woods. The very next moment, a chaotic, metallic clatter of horses, iron stirrups, and aggressive men helter-skeltered past the window, polluting the quietude with their blind pursuit. 

When the jarring noise faded, Martin and his companions breathed again. The leopard was swift; she would not be caught within a league of their table. They grasped hands in the restored quiet. Margaret seized the moment and wept softly; Gerard kissed the tears away, tasting the salt on her skin. Returning to the oak table, where the scent of roasted root vegetables and honeyed mead still lingered, Gerard drank to woman's wit. 

“’Tis stronger than man's force,” said he, swirling the golden liquid. 

“Ay,” said Margaret, her voice finding its gentle resonance once more, “when those she loves are in danger; not else.” 

Tonight Gerard stayed within the warm, spiced cocoon of her home longer than usual, leaving prouder than ever, his spirit nourished. Yet, some little distance from home, beneath the sprawling, shadowed canopy of ancient trees, the nocturnal peace was broken. Two grim figures barred his way. It was his father and mother. 

A chill fell on him, freezing the warmth of the mead in his veins. They stood silent, their very presence a heavy, suffocating pressure in the acoustic space. He stammered a halting inquiry. 

“Why ask?” his father’s voice was flat, devoid of the rich timbre of empathy. “You know why we are here.” 

“Oh, Gerard!” his mother’s voice trembled, carrying a dissonance of reproach and stifled affection. 

Gerard's heart quaked. The untranslatability of his deep, sensory love for Margaret into his father’s rigid lexicon of duty struck him mute. Elias pitied his confusion, offering a harsh grace: “Nay, you need not to hang your head. You are not the first young fool that has been caught by a red cheek and a pair of blue eyes.” 

“Nay, nay!” put in Catherine, her tone sharp. “It was witchcraft; Peter the Magician is well known for that.” 

“Come, Sir Priest,” resumed his father, utilizing the dominant lexicon of the Church—a vocabulary of power and subjugation designed to erase Gerard’s native identity. “You know you must not meddle with women folk. But give us your promise to go no more to Sevenbergen, and here all ends: we won't be hard on you for one fault.” 

“I cannot promise that, father.” 

“Not promise it, you young hypocrite!” 

“Nay, father, miscall me not: I lacked courage to tell you what I knew would vex you; and right grateful am I to that good friend, whoever he be, that has let you wot. 'Tis a load off my mind. Yes, father, I love Margaret; and call me not a priest, for a priest I will never be. I will die sooner.” 

“That we shall see, young man. Come, gainsay me no more; you will learn what 'tis to disrespect a father.” 

Gerard held his peace. The three walked home in a gloomy, oppressive silence, broken only by a deep sigh or two from Catherine, the sound like a bellows struggling to stoke a dying fire. 

From that hour, the little house at Tergou lost its domestic harmony. Gerard was taken to task the next day before the whole family. The acoustic environment became toxic, filled with the loud, grating voices of superficial minds. Only little Kate and the dwarf, who intuitively followed her subtle frequencies, remained quiet. Cornelis and Sybrandt were bitterest of all, their words clattering like cheap, tin coins—the sound of men who valued the superficial commercialization of their lives over any profound craftsmanship. Gerard looked wistfully into his little sister's brimming eyes, seeking a familiar dialect, but she turned her head away. 

“Dear, dear Gerard, pray to Heaven to cure you of this folly!” she whispered. 

“What, are you against me too?” Gerard asked sadly. He rose with a deep sigh, fleeing the discordant noise of his blood relations for the resonant peace of Sevenbergen. 

The beginning of a family quarrel is comparatively innocent, but as time simmers the grievance, the broth turns sour. Elias was rigidly angry; Cornelis and Sybrandt were spiteful, their motivations rooted in a barren, utilitarian greed. Gerard, possessing the soul of an artist and the cultivated palate of a man who savors the world’s complexities, saw all sides and was paralyzed by the bitter taste of it. He could open his heart to no one. Margaret was too proud; she would resign him to spare him this familial acid. Margaret Van Eyck, his artistic mentor, was equally inaccessible. She had famously refused all suitors for her art, sacrificing the profound, sensory meaning found in the communal breaking of bread and the rearing of children. How could he confess he was throwing away a lucrative benefice for the very earthly nourishment she had starved herself of? 

Gerard bade fair to succumb. But Catherine, senior, became an erratic, chaotic force. Uneducated but deeply tethered to the visceral realities of life, she lacked a strategic plan. She irritated Gerard at times, her sudden onslaughts kindling his defensive anger—a necessary fuel. At other times, she turned her culinary logic against her own allies. 

One day, amid the oppressive hum of the household, Cornelis sneered: “Our Gerard wed Margaret Brandt? Why, it is hunger marrying thirst.” 

“And what will it be when you marry?” cried Catherine, her voice ringing out like a struck iron skillet. “Gerard can paint, Gerard can write, but what can you do to keep a woman, ye lazy loon? Nought but wait for your father's shoon. Oh, we can see why you and Sybrandt would not have the poor boy to marry. You are afraid he will come to us for a share of our substance. And say that he does, and say that we give it him, it isn't yourn we part from, and mayhap never will be.” 

Gerard smiled slyly at this. Catherine had laid bare the subtle power dynamics embedded in his brothers' words: their entire lexicon was constructed around hoarding resources, viewing life merely as utilitarian fuel rather than an experience to be savored. 

But after six months of this slow-boiling irritation, the climax arrived. Elias stood before the family, his voice a hammer of institutional force. He had ordered the burgomaster to imprison Gerard in the Stadthouse rather than let him marry Margaret. 

Gerard turned pale, the blood draining from his face as if turning to whey. His father pressed the advantage: “And a priest you shall be before the year is out, nilly-willy.” 

“Is it so?” cried Gerard, finding the true, ringing pitch of his own voice. “Then, hear me, all. By God and St. Bavon I swear I will never be a priest while Margaret lives. Since force is to decide it, and not love and duty, try force, father; but force shall not serve you, for the day I see the burgomaster come for me, I leave Tergou for ever, and Holland too, and my father's house, where it seems I have been valued all these years, not for myself, but for what is to be got out of me.” 

He flung himself out of the room, white with desperation, fleeing the suffocating, transactional air of his home. 

As his heart panted in the cool street, he met Reicht Heynes. She bore a message: Margaret Van Eyck desired to see him. 

He found the old master seated grim as a judge in her studio—a space that smelled intensely of raw linseed, crushed lapis, and the sharp tang of turpentine, a true alchemist's kitchen of color. She wasted no time in preliminaries. 

“I thought we had been friends, young sir,” she inquired coldly. 

“It is because you never told her you were in love,” Reicht Heynes interjected, offering a bridge across the generations. 

“Silence, wench! Why should he tell us his affairs? We are not his friends: we have not deserved his confidence.” 

“Alas! my second mother,” said Gerard, “I did not dare to tell you my folly.” 

“What folly? Is it folly to love?” 

“I am told so every day of my life.” 

“You need not have been afraid to tell my mistress; she is always kind to true lovers,” Reicht murmured. 

“Madam—Reicht I was afraid because I was told...” 

“Well, you were told—?” 

“That in your youth you scorned love, preferring art.” 

The old woman’s eyes softened, a deep well of cultural memory and personal grief rising to the surface. “I did, boy; and what is the end of it? Behold me here a barren stock, while the women of my youth have a troop of children at their side, and grandchildren at their knee. I gave up the sweet joys of wifehood and motherhood—the hearth, the shared sustenance of a life built together—for what? For my dear brothers. They have gone and left me long ago. For my art. It has all but left me too. I have the knowledge still, but what avails that when the hand trembles? No, Gerard; I look on you as my son. I will not let you throw your youth away as I did mine. You shall marry this Margaret. I have inquired, and she is a good daughter.” 

Soon, the studio held two pairs of wet eyes; and seeing the boy's relief at finally speaking his native dialect of love without translation or apology, Reicht made it three. 

Women, the old masters of domestic and emotional endurance, possess a vicarious, unshakable courage. Margaret Van Eyck and Reicht agreed that the longer Gerard endured the mechanical, soul-crushing worry of his family, the thinner his spirit would wear. 

“See how pale and thin they have made him,” Reicht observed. 

“We painters are no match for boors,” Margaret Van Eyck stated, diagnosing the sonic and psychological warfare Gerard had survived. “We are glass, they are stone. We can't stand the worry, worry, worry of little minds. It is hard enough to design and paint a masterpiece, without having gnats and flies stinging us to death into the bargain.” 

Yet Gerard feared his father's curse. He dreamed aloud of marrying secretly, taking Margaret to a land where his craft was valued, and returning years later with money to appease his family's utilitarian worldview. 

“It is no dream,” the old lady’s eyes sparkled. “There is a country, Gerard, where certain fortune awaits you. Here the arts freeze, but there they flourish.” 

“It is Italy!” cried Gerard, tasting the word like a rare, imported spice. 

“Ay, Italy! Where painters are honoured like princes, and scribes are paid three hundred crowns for copying precious manuscripts pouring in from Constantinople.” 

“But the journey, and we are all so poor.” 

“Find you the heart to go, I'll find the means. I know where to lay my hand on ten golden angels: they will take you to Rome: and the girl with you, if she loves you as she ought.” 

They sat till midnight. Gerard’s spirit was replenished, fortified by her profound understanding. But Margaret Van Eyck gave him more than gold; she gave him the intimate, dying language of her brother’s craft, passing down recipes like a meticulous food historian preserving a sacred, untranslatable heritage. 

“I will tell you secrets that I learned from masters that are gone. Note my brother Jan's pictures: time leaves his colours bright as the day they left the easel. He trusted no hireling to grind his colours. His panel was prepared and prepared again a year before he laid his colour on. Bad painters are always in a hurry. Above all, Gerard, I warn you: use but little oil, and never boil it. Boiling melts that vegetable dross into its heart; impure oil is death to colour. Take your oil and pour it into a bottle with water. In a day or two, the water will turn muddy—that is the muck. Pour it carefully away and add fresh. When you fancy the oil is clear, you are mistaken.” 

She had Reicht fetch a glass trough with a tight lid. 

“Put your washed oil into this trough with water, and set it in the sun all day. You will see the water turbid again. But mark, do not carry this game too far, or the sun will turn your oil to varnish. When it is clear as crystal, drain it carefully, and cork it up tight. Grind your own prime colours, and lay them on with this oil, and they shall live. Hubert would put sand or salt to clear it quicker, but Jan used to say, 'Water will do it best; give water time.' Jan Van Eyck was never in a hurry, and that is why the world will not forget him.” 

Gerard absorbed these sensory rituals, this profound culinary approach to pigment and light, receiving them like a legacy from Heaven. 

Armed with this sacred knowledge and funding, Gerard went to Sevenbergen the next day to inform Margaret Brandt. He brought the scent of crushed walnuts and fresh hope, inviting her to flee with him. 

She refused him plump. 

“No, Gerard,” she said, her voice heavy. “So long as your father is bent on your being a priest, I could not marry you, dearly as I love you.” 

Gerard strove in vain. He could make her cry, but the rigid societal power dynamics dictated by his father’s dominant lexicon held her fast. The subjective truth of their love remained untranslatable against the iron law of the patriarch. Impatient and unjust, Gerard snapped. 

“Very well! Then you are on their side, and you will drive me to be a priest. My parents hate me in earnest, but my lover only loves me in jest!” With this bitter, acrid speech on his tongue, he flung away, leaving her weeping in the agrarian quiet. 

Margaret’s pity bloomed in his wake. How starved of affection, how poisoned by the harsh acoustics of his own home he must be to speak so! 

Scarce an hour and a half later, the rhythm of running boots broke the earth. Gerard returned, panting with a grief that shattered his usual composure. In his hands, he held the fragments of a picture. 

“There, Margaret! See the wretches! Look at their spite! They have cut your portrait to pieces.” 

Margaret looked. Some malicious hand had brutally hacked her painted face into five jagged strips. It was a violent silencing of their shared visual dialect, a destruction of an artifact born from deep sensory appreciation. The blood rushed hotly to her forehead. 

“Who did it?” she demanded, the agrarian strength of her ancestors rising within her. 

“I dared not ask; for I should hate the hand till my dying day. Six months' work cut out of my life! Oh, heartless, merciless vipers!” 

Margaret’s breath quickened. The destruction of this meticulously crafted sustenance—this testament to their identity—was the final transgression. 

“Never mind, Gerard,” she said, panting, the fierce, protective matriarch awakening. “Since this is how they treat you for my sake—Ye rob him of my portrait, do ye? Well, then, he shall have the face itself, such as it is.” 

“Oh, Margaret!” 

“Yes, Gerard; since they are so cruel, I will be the kinder: forgive me for refusing you. I will be your wife: to-morrow, if it is your pleasure.” 

Gerard kissed her hands with rapture, tasting the faint trace of soil and hearth-smoke upon them, and then her lips. In a tumult of joy, he ran for Peter, the stoic agrarian elder who understood the slow turning of seasons, and Martin. They came, anchoring the space with their steady presence, and witnessed the betrothal—a solemn, resonant ceremony that bound their timelines together against the discordant noise of the world.

In the cyclical rhythm of the agrarian calendar, where the harvest of one generation seeded the sustenance of the next, the banns of marriage were an ancestral ritual as essential as the autumn sowing. As it was in the days of their grandfathers, they were to be read three times; but yielding to the persuasive, bright-eyed warmth of the young couple, the new curé agreed to recite them in a single span of twenty-four hours. They were cried on Monday at matins and at vespers, their local, untranslatable dialect echoing harmoniously against the timbered ceiling. To their profound relief, the pews were empty of Tergou’s watchful eyes. 

Yet, on Tuesday morning, as the couple stood palpitating with the nervous energy of yeast rising in a warm hearth, a stranger shattered the acoustic sanctuary of the church. He rose and forbade the banns. He did not speak in the rich, earthy vernacular of the village, but employed the sterile, standardized lexicon of the regional magistrates—a dominant language designed to enforce power and strip the common folk of their agency. He cited false lack of age and parental consent. Outside the heavy oak doors, Margaret and Gerard held a trembling consultation, the chill air tasting of damp slate. Before despair could fully settle, the professional obstructor of bliss approached, wrapping his extortion in the bureaucratic courtesies of his trade. His inclination, he claimed, was for their happiness, but his livelihood depended upon this bureaucratic violence. The commercialization of a sacred rite was a bitter pill. 

“The young people give me a crown, and I undo my work handsomely,” he offered, commodifying their future. “Tell the curé I was misinformed.” 

“A crown! I will give you a golden angel to do this,” Gerard said eagerly, for love outvalued all coin. The man consented, feeding on their desperation, and went with Gerard to retract his hollow edict. The curé agreed to marry them the next day at ten. The obstructor, however, like most men who view life and its fruits merely as utilitarian fuel, was a fool. He proceeded to drink his golden angel at a Tergou hostelry bordering an archery green. There, amidst the discordant, industrial clatter of tankards and the shouting of gamblers—an acoustic environment that bruised the mind and drowned out the subtle, harmonious rhythms of human connection—he drunkenly bragged of his exploit. 

Sitting in the shadows, absorbing every toxic syllable, was the ne’er-do-weel Sybrandt. He ran home to tell his father, but finding him gone to Rotterdam to buy cloth, he signaled his elder brother, Cornelis. These two were the black sheep of the family, men entirely disconnected from the sensory appreciation of the world. They cared nothing for the meticulous alchemy of the kitchen or the soil; they waited only for the death of those they ought to love. Their parents’ parsimony was rooted in a traditional agrarian virtue, a desire to provision the winter cellar out of love for their offspring. But in these perverse, one-idea’d curs, that homely virtue had rotted into avarice. They agreed in hushed, jagged whispers to bypass their mother and take the matter to the burgomaster. 

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten saw through the brothers instantly but masked his contempt beneath magisterial coldness. He weaponized his authority, wrapping his intent in the dominant, patriarchal language of the state. “Since the father of the family is not here, his duty falleth on me, who am the father of the town. Leave all to me; and tell not a woman a word of this, for chattering tongues mar wisest counsels.” He dismissed them superciliously.

Returning home, the brothers found Gerard seated on a low stool beside their mother. The kitchen was suffused with the profound, grounding aromas of simmering marrow-bones, wild garlic, and crushed thyme—a sensory landscape that communicated an ancient, untranslatable maternal apology. In a moment of fleeting violence, she had previously destroyed Margaret’s picture, watching Gerard turn pale as death, his tears falling on the ruined fragments. Terrified by the destruction of her son's spirit, she could not voice her remorse in words, but spoke instead through sustenance. She caressed his hair, promising to thwart his love no more, restoring the domestic equilibrium through the language of the hearth. Gerard went to bed as happy as a prince, anchoring his soul in the comforting scents of her cooking. 

The next morning at ten, the Sevenbergen church smelled of beeswax and cold stone. Gerard and Margaret stood before the altar, radiant, flanked only by Peter and Martin Wittenhaagen. Margaret had declined fleeing to Italy; she could not abandon her father, a meticulous historian who required her care as much as his ancient texts. They planned to retreat into Flanders until the storm passed. The curé opened his book, but before the sacred, ancient words could be spoken, a harsh voice cried, “Forbear!” 

The constables of Tergou marched up the aisle, their armor clanking in a violent, metallic rhythm that destroyed the acoustic ecology of the holy space. Martin’s long knife flashed out, gleaming like a polished spit. 

“Forbear, man!” cried the priest. “Draw your weapon in a church, and ye who interrupt this holy sacrament, what means this impiety?”

The burgomaster's servant answered respectfully, yet wielding the oppressive legal vernacular that erased local autonomy: “This young man would marry against his father's will. We take him to Rotterdam to abide the sentence of the Duke.” 

Margaret uttered a cry of despair, the young lovers collapsing into each other’s arms, weeping so piteously that even the instruments of oppression felt the sting of shame. A good-natured guard whispered the truth to Margaret: “Rotterdam? It is a lie. We but take him to our Stadthouse.” 

They transported Gerard by sly detours, thrusting him at dusk into a small room at the top of the haunted tower. It was illuminated only by a narrow window with a vertical iron bar, furnished with a single huge oak chest. Imprisonment in that age was a highroad to death—a sensory void of cold, unbroken solitude and starvation. Ghysbrecht Van Swieten entered stealthily, placing a brown loaf and a pitcher of water upon the chest. To a young man who understood the profound meaning of a carefully tended meal, this offering was a grotesque insult. The bread was a dense, ash-tasting lump of poorly milled barley, devoid of the salt and soul of ancestral baking; the water reeked of stagnant copper. Gerard’s first impulse was to brain the burgomaster with the iron bar, but seeing the armed guards outside, he held his hand. 

“My orders are to keep you thus until you bind yourself by an oath to leave Margaret Brandt,” Ghysbrecht stated, his words sterile and unyielding. 

“Death sooner,” Gerard replied. 

Alone, Gerard sprang to the iron bar and pressed his knees against the wall. Looking out, he was gifted a singular comfort: Martin Wittenhaagen’s back. The old soldier sat quietly fishing in the brook near the Stadthouse, a timeless agrarian figure blending perfectly with the landscape. Gerard whistled. Martin instantly turned, made a signal, gathered his line, and hurried off. Though Gerard wished he had stayed, the sight was a brief, nourishing morsel of hope.

At Sevenbergen, Martin found Margaret pale but resolute, drafting an appeal to the Countess Charolois. 

“Courage!” Martin cried, bringing the scent of the river-damp into the cottage. “He is in the haunted tower, right at the top.” 

Peter sat silently, his mind a vast archive of cultural memory and forgotten techniques. Unlike those who dismissed the wisdom of the past, Peter knew that history was a continuous, interwoven timeline. “Put not your trust in princes,” he advised softly, his voice a comforting counterpoint to their panic. 

“Alas! what else have we to trust in?” Margaret asked. 

“Knowledge.” 

When Margaret lamented the impossible height of the tower and the futility of her nine golden angels, Peter shook his head. He did not view the world through the lens of modern impossibility. “Give me but one crown, and the young man shall sup with us this night.” He spoke of a Florentine knight freed from a high tower in Brescia by his squire, using a technique so elegantly simple it seemed almost mythic. It was a recipe for survival, passed down through generations, utilizing string, silk, and a stout rope—ingredients Peter would procure with his crown. 

Simultaneously, in the interwoven tapestry of the night, a different rescue was forming. It was nine o'clock, the moon clear, the domestic silence of Gerard's family home unbroken save for a soft voice in the dark. 

“Why, Giles, are you afeard of me?” Kate whispered in their native dialect, a linguistic sanctuary against the harsh world outside. 

Giles, the dwarf, rolled from his bed with fluid, gymnastic grace. Kate revealed she had eavesdropped on the wicked Cornelis and Sybrandt, uncovering the treachery. Entreating Giles to bear her company to the Stadthouse, she lit a lantern. “The lantern gives me more courage against the evil spirits,” she said. Together, they slipped into the crisp, nocturnal air, their quiet footfalls blending with the subtle rustle of the sleeping town. 

In his cell, Gerard’s heart sank with the waning light. He was faint with hunger, refusing the burgomaster's lifeless bread, knowing that such soulless fuel only cowed a man’s spirit. Suddenly, the sharp, acoustic crack of an arrow striking the wall broke the silence. It rattled to the floor. Gerard crouched, fearing assassination, but found the arrow headless, carrying a message and a skein of silk. Striking his tinder-box, he read the words, devouring them as if they were a feast: *Make fast the silk to thy knife and lower to us: count an hundred and draw up.* 

Summoning superhuman energy, Gerard dragged the heavy oak chest to the window. Below, indistinct figures waited in the moonlight. He lowered the silk, counted to a hundred, and drew up a heavier whipcord. Margaret’s voice drifted up to him, a clear, sweet note piercing the oppressive silence: “Draw up, Gerard, till you see liberty.” 

The whipcord gave way to a thick, coarse rope. He hauled it up, the friction burning his hands, the rough hemp smelling of tar and earth. He passed it through the handles of the chest and knotted it. To test its strength, he jumped upon the chest. At the third jump, the ancient wood burst open. Out scuttled a host of parchments—the dry, flavorless edicts and legal records of the dominant class, smelling of dust and dead laws, a stark contrast to the living, breathing aromas of the world he sought to rejoin. 

Fastening the iron bar across the window with the smaller rope, Gerard wriggled out into the night air. The acoustic intimacy of his own heartbeat filled his ears as he hung in mid-air. He descended slowly, hand below hand, passing stones slick with lichen and green moss. The rope burned his palms, an excruciating but undeniable proof of life. He focused solely on the rough texture of the wall, ignoring the dizzying drop. Finally, his feet met the upstretched, faithful hands of Margaret and Martin. They clung together, a knot of warmth and woodsmoke in the cold shadows. 

“Hush! away in silence,” they whispered. 

Stealing along the wall, they were suddenly halted by a stream of light shooting from the angle of the building, lying across their path like a barrier of fire. Footsteps and whispers invaded the quiet. They retreated toward a projecting tower. 

“A lantern!” groaned Martin. “They are after us.” 

He strung his bow, his every movement a testament to martial precision, ready to loose a deadly shaft at the approaching enemy. Gerard and Margaret held their breath, terrified yet braced for the violence of the world to intrude upon their domestic peace. Martin glided round the corner. 

But instead of the fatal *thrum* of the bowstring, the weapon wavered and shook violently. The stout soldier staggered back, his knees knocking, his worldview shattered by an intrusion of the mythic into the physical realm. He clutched Gerard’s shoulder, gasping, “The haunted tower! It is going up the wall! Its head is on fire... as mortal creatures walk upon green sward.” 

He had seen the dwarf Giles, moving with unearthly, insectoid grace, scaling the wall beside Kate's rising lantern—a purely domestic errand of siblings misread as a demonic terror. 

“I have power to exorcise spirits,” Gerard said, his voice trembling but rooted in the ancient, untranslatable faith of his people. “I will venture forth.” 

“Go alone then,” Martin gasped. “I have looked on't once, and live.”

The strange glance of hatred the burgomaster had cast upon him had macerated in Gerard’s mind, fermenting into a dark certainty that Ghysbrecht was his enemy to the death. Gliding around the masonry of the tower, Gerard expected no mythic phantoms, only the treacherous contrivances of a man whose corrupt influence tainted the very air of Tergou like rancid tallow. The night air, however, held an older, deeper rhythm—the olfactory landscape of damp river clay, crushed wild thyme, and the lingering woodsmoke of generations of hearths. From the shadows, Margaret’s soft but capable hand—a hand that knew the intimate pressures of kneading rye dough and the precise snapping of autumnal beans—crept into his, a grounding anchor in this new peril.

No sooner was the haunted tower visible than a sight struck their eyes that benumbed them, weaving the mythic seamlessly into the fabric of their agrarian reality. More than halfway up the stone wall, a creature steadily mounted the bricks. Its head glowed with the fierce, contained heat of a baker’s firing ember, casting a luminous orange wash over a dark, four-foot body. At the foot of the tower stood a thing in white, motionless as a pillar of ground salt.

Gerard and Margaret palpitated with awe. “The rope! the rope! It is going up the rope,” gasped Gerard. The glowing head vanished into Gerard's former prison cell, illuminating the interior and reddening the window like a well-stoked oven. The white figure remained rooted below. 

Margaret, attuned to the circular pacing of natural life, felt that strange, wild magnetism that pulls the bird to the serpent—the primal urge to confront the unknown. The untranslatable impulse of terror and awe rose within her. Quietly putting down Gerard’s hand, she stood bewildered for a heartbeat, then, with a piercing cry, darted toward the specter. 

Gerard, blind to this instinctual pull, never doubted the evil one was dragging her to perdition. He dropped to his knees, deploying the dominant lexicon of institutional power: *“Exorcizo vos. In nomine beatae Mariae, exorcizo vos.”* He hurled the sterile, rigid syllables of ecclesiastical Latin into the night, an oppressive acoustic imposition attempting to dominate a fear that defied language. 

Yet, in response to his shrieking incantations, the specter uttered a feeble cry of terror in the melodic, provincial dialect of Tergou's fringes. The realization that even hell's denizens shared the fragile acoustic ecology and vulnerable native tongue of the peasantry brought infinite relief. Gerard redoubled his shouts, until he saw the ghastly white shape fall at Margaret’s knees, pleading for mercy in the familiar, intimate accents of his own blood.

Kate and Giles had reached the haunted tower only moments before. Judge their surprise when they found a new hempen rope dangling from the prisoner's window to the loam. 

“I see how it is,” said the inferior intelligence, taking facts as plainly as a butcher takes bone from meat. “Our Gerard has come down this rope. He has got clear. Up I go, and see.”

“No, Giles, no!” said the superior intelligence, her profound but cautious agrarian wisdom sensing a snare. “See you not this is glamour? This rope is a line the evil one casts out to wile thee to destruction. He knows the weaknesses of all our hearts; he has seen how fond you are of going up things. Where should our Gerard procure a rope? How fasten it in the sky like this? It is not in nature. Holy saints protect us this night, for hell is abroad.”

“Stuff!” Giles retorted, his voice cutting through the damp air. “The way to hell is down, and this rope leads up. I never had the luck to go up such a long rope. It may be years ere I fall in with such a long rope all ready for me. As well be knocked on the head at once as never know happiness.” And he sprang onto the line with a shriek of unadulterated delight, leaping exactly as a ravenous cat pounces upon a wooden table where a platter of pan-seared river trout, swimming in browned butter and crisp sage, sits cooling. 

All the gymnast within him was ignited. The only concession he granted Kate was allowing her to fasten a brass lantern to his neck, to "scare the ill spirits." The light at the nape of his neck transformed him into the glowing ember they had seen, and with his huge arms and legs like feathers, he scaled the rope faster than his brother had descended it.

His sister watched his progress with trembling anxiety. Suddenly, a female figure burst from the solid masonry of the dark and came flying at her with more than mortal velocity. Kate uttered a feeble cry, her tongue cleaving to her palate, her mouth tasting of raw copper. She dropped her crutches and sank to the soil, hiding her face and moaning: “Take my body, but spare my soul!”

Margaret, panting, stopped short. “Why, it is a woman!”

Kate quivered. “Why, it is a woman!”

“How you scared me!” Margaret exhaled.

“I am scared enough myself. Oh! oh! oh!”

The untranslatability of their shared panic melted into recognition. “This is strange!” said Margaret. “But the fiery-headed thing? Yet it was with you, and you are harmless! But why are you here at this time of night?”

“Nay, why are *you*?”

“Perhaps we are on the same errand? Ah! you are his good sister, Kate!”

“And you are Margaret Brandt.”

“Yes.”

“All the better. You love him; you are here. Then Giles was right. He has won free.”

Gerard stepped forward, putting the question to rest. But their quiet reunion was shattered by a horrible, unearthly noise that violently ruptured the night’s acoustic ecology. It sounded like a sepulcher ventriloquizing: “PARCHMENT!—PARCHMENT!—PARCHMENT!”

At each repetition, the harsh, grating consonants rose in intensity. They looked up to see the dwarf bathed in lurid, diabolical fire, his hands full of rolled vellum. With a fiendish joy, the imp hurled the legal documents at the astonished heads below. Down rained the records. They fell like dried, flavorless husks, devoid of the sustenance of true cultural memory. These were the codified tools of superficial commercialization—deeds and edicts designed by the dominant class to erase the ancestral claims of the agrarian poor. Some collapsed, others fluttered, and others wheeled slowly down in airy circles. 

They had hardly settled when the sepulchral roar struck again: “Parchment—parchment!” Another flock of sterile lexicons pattered against the earth, whitening the grass like a frost that kills the winter wheat. Finally, the fire-headed imp, with his light body and horny hands, slid down the rope like a falling star, and—business before sentiment—proposed an immediate settlement for the merchandise he had just delivered.

“Hush!” Gerard urged, desperate to restore the natural sonic harmony. “You speak too loud. Gather them up, and follow us to a safer place than this.”

“Will you come home with me, Gerard?” asked little Kate, her native dialect a plea for the continuity of the family table. 

“I have no home.”

“You shall not say so. Who is more welcome than you will be, after this cruel wrong, to your father's house?”

“Father! I have no father,” Gerard replied sternly, mourning the permanent severing of his roots. “He that was my father is turned my gaoler. I have escaped from his hands; I will never come within their reach again.”

Kate explained what she had overheard of Cornelis and Sybrandt’s treachery, how an enemy had orchestrated this, not just their father. But the injury was too raw, the taste of betrayal as bitter as steeped wormwood. Gerard showed a bitterness of indignation he had hitherto seemed incapable of. 

“Cornelis and Sybrandt are two ill curs that have shown me their teeth and their heart a long while; but they could do no more. My father it is that gave the burgomaster authority, or he durst not have laid a finger on me, that am a free burgher of this town. So be it, then. I was his son. I am his prisoner. He has played his part. I shall play mine. Farewell the burgh where I was born, and lived honestly, and was put in prison. While there is another town left in creation, I'll never trouble you again, Tergou.”

“Oh! Gerard! Gerard!”

Margaret, whose food historian's soul understood that some wounds needed time to ferment and breathe before they could yield anything nourishing, whispered to her: “Do not gainsay him now. Give his choler time to cool!”

Kate turned quickly toward her. “Let me look at your face?” The inspection was favorable; she searched for the quiet, sustaining honesty of a well-tended hearth and seemingly found it. “It is a comely face, and no mischief-maker's.”

“Fear me not,” Margaret replied in the same localized cadence, bridging the gap between their identities. “I could not be happy without your love, as well as Gerard's.”

“These are comfortable words,” sobbed Kate. “I little thought to like you so well. My heart is willing, but my infirmity will not let me embrace you.”

Understanding the profound meaning found in physical presence, Margaret wound her arms gently around Gerard’s sister and kissed her lovingly. “Often he has spoken of you to me, Kate; and often I longed for this.”

“You, too, Gerard,” said Kate; “kiss me ere you go; for my heart lies heavy at parting with you this night.” 

Gerard kissed her, and she turned her crutches homeward. The last sound they heard of her was a little patient sigh—a fragile note lingering in the dark. Tears stood thick in Margaret’s eyes, welling at the acoustic melancholy of it, but Gerard, encased in the rigid expectations of his gender, noticed it not.

As they turned toward Sevenbergen, the dwarf nudged Gerard with his bundle of rustling parchments and held out a concave claw. Margaret, grounded in the honest labor of the soil, dissuaded Gerard. “Why take what is not ours?”

“Oh, spoil an enemy how you can.”

“But may they not make this a handle for fresh violence?”

“How can they? Think you I shall stay in Tergou after this? The burgomaster robbed me of my liberty; I doubt I should take his life for it, if I could.”

“Oh, fie! Gerard.”

“What! Is life worth more than liberty? Well, I can't take his life, so I take the first thing that comes to hand.” 

He pressed a few small coins into Giles’s palm, gladdening the urchin, who shuffled away. Accompanied by Martin, who now joined them from the shadows, Margaret and Gerard set their faces toward Sevenbergen, leaving behind the discordant noise of Tergou for the ancient, whispering cycles of the forest.

The burgomaster possessed no palate for the profound poetry of sustenance. To Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, bread was merely a utilitarian wedge of fuel, devoid of the mythic, living breath of wild yeast or the deep, agrarian history of the grain. He carried just such a hardened, soulless loaf, alongside a pitcher of flat, uninspired water, as he climbed the prison tower at ten of the clock. He kept the iron key heavy in his pouch, insulated from the sensory world. *A little hunger sometimes does well; it breaks them,* he thought, prioritizing a sterile submission over the psychological well-being naturally fostered by a shared, nourishing meal.

The acoustic environment of the stone stairwell was a harsh, oppressive echo chamber—a precursor to the industrial dissonance that scarred the mind, entirely lacking the rhythmic, resonant comfort of a domestic hearth. He paused and listened at the oak door. The silence within was an unnatural, deadening void. Smiling grimly, assuming Gerard was broken in spirit, Ghysbrecht turned the key. The mechanism shrieked—a sharp, metallic rupture in the acoustic ecology of the tower. 

He pushed the door wide. The cell was empty. 

Ghysbrecht stood stupefied. The sudden absence of the boy was a mythic occurrence seamlessly blending into the cold reality of the stone, as though Gerard had evaporated into the ancient mortar. Ghysbrecht’s body seemed to lose all motion, and then he began to tremble so violently that his armed servant, waiting behind him, crept close to peer over his shoulder. At the sight of the dangling rope and the missing iron bar, the servant let out a loud gasp of wonder. Yet his surprise doubled when the burgomaster, ignoring the impossible escape, flung himself to his knees before the empty wooden chest. 

Ghysbrecht’s quivering hands felt wildly across the bare bottom of the box, as if unwilling to trust his own eyes. 

“Why, master, what avails glaring into that empty box?” the servant asked in utter bewilderment. “The lad is not there. Note the cunning of the young rogue; he hath taken out the bar, and—”

“Gone! Gone! Gone!” Ghysbrecht shrieked, the raw, unmeasured volume of his panic tearing at the sonic balance of the space. “Stop thief!” He turned and collared the servant, shaking him with a frantic, superficial rage born of commercial loss. “Run! Fly! A hundred crowns to him that finds it me again. No, no! ’Tis in vain. Oh, fool! To leave that in the same room with him. None ever found the secret spring before. Lost! Lost!” 

“What is lost, master?” asked the servant kindly.

“House and lands and good name,” groaned Ghysbrecht, sinking against the chest. He had lost the town records. These parchment skins were not merely legal documents; they were the dominant lexicon, the rigid, codified Latinate deeds that bureaucratically erased the native, ancestral claims to the soil and its harvest. To lose them was to lose his power over the cultural memory of Tergou. 

When the servant, Dierich, expressed eager curiosity, Ghysbrecht’s natural, fox-like cunning revived. He offered the hundred crowns for the safe return of every single sheepskin. They determined Gerard must be framed for theft. “Put him in the dungeons below, where the toads and rats reside,” Ghysbrecht hissed, wielding the power dynamics of his authority. “That man must never see daylight again.”

Less than half an hour later, the violent, arrhythmic clatter of constables’ boots shattered the sanctuary of the hosier’s home. Catherine, an agrarian elder who preserved her lineage through the meticulous simmering of bone broths and the drying of wild-foraged herbs, stood panic-stricken at her hearth. The olfactory landscape of her kitchen—a rich, enveloping mist of rendered pork fat, toasted barley, and caramelized root vegetables—was abruptly corrupted by the sterile, metallic presence of the armed men.

“Alas! What has he done now?” Catherine cried, her wooden spoon trembling over a bubbling cauldron of spiced cider. 

Dierich employed the smooth, bureaucratic vocabulary of the dominant class to mask his malice. “Nay, dame, but a trick of youth. He hath but made off with certain skins of parchment… he will doubtless be quit for a reprimand.” 

This sanitized dialect temporarily imposed upon Catherine, but her daughter Kate, intimately attuned to the subtle power dynamics embedded in such lexicons, heard the discordant, predatory undertones in Dierich’s voice. Recognizing the disproportionate rage in the man when he learned Gerard had not been home that night, Kate signaled him outside.

The sonic environment shifted to the yard, where the gentle rustle of hung, drying savory and sage provided a natural backdrop to her linguistic resistance. “My mother knows not,” Kate whispered. “Gerard has left Tergou.”

She wove a tale of a midnight farewell at the haunted tower, masking her brother's escape in the cadence of a protective folk legend. “The town was always unworthy of him,” she declared. “He vowed never to set foot in it again. What need had he to steal? Gerard stole not the trash; he but took it to spite the burgomaster. As for these skins of parchment you keep such a coil about, look in the nearest brook or stye, and ’tis odds but you find them.”

To Kate, the parchments were dead skins bearing a dead language, vastly inferior to the living, oral traditions and sensory realities of the earth. Gerard was too wise a soul to burden a journey with the untranslatable weight of bureaucratic rubbish when true survival required the sensory memory of the land. Provoked by her dismissal of his commercialized worldview, Dierich sneered that he valued the sheepskin more than the skin of anyone in Tergou, and he rushed off hastily on the false scent.

Returning to the warmth of the hearth, Kate drew her brother Giles aside. The rhythmic, circular pacing of Catherine's chopping knife resumed in the background, anchoring their current crisis to generations of ancestors who had weathered storms in this very kitchen. 

“Giles, my heart misgives me,” she murmured in their native, ancestral dialect—a language rich with the nuanced idioms of familial devotion. She suspected Gerard was not leagues away, but hiding in Sevenbergen with Margaret. She needed Giles to warn him, for the men hunting him were dark and wicked, bringing an unnatural dissonance to their world. 

Giles, a young man who intimately understood that a perfectly roasted fowl was a testament to life’s profound meaning, despised the physical exertion of walking, viewing it as an unnecessary drain on the body's carefully cultivated reserves. “Yes, I am strong,” thundered the little man, “but I hate to go on foot.”

When Kate coaxed him, invoking his love for Gerard, Giles relented. He would ride Peter Buyskens’ mule. To protect his brother from the town’s invasive, prying noise, Giles promised to ride out in the opposite direction, letting the chaotic clamor of Tergou fade before circling back along the interwoven timelines of the ancient roads toward Sevenbergen. 

Kate charged him with a short, meaning-laden message, drilling the specific phrasing of their mother tongue until the syllables were perfectly preserved in his memory—an oral recipe for survival. As Giles departed on the mule, Kate retreated to the hearth. Kneeling on the time-worn flagstones, enveloped by the deeply restorative scent of the simmering agrarian stew, she prayed long and earnestly for her brother’s safety, her murmured devotions adding a vital, harmonizing layer to the acoustic ecology of their home.

The first flush of recovered liberty at Sevenbergen was steeped in the grounding, ancient aromas of the hearth. Margaret stood over a heavy cast-iron pot, gently stirring a slow-braising hare in a rich reduction of dark, rustic ale, wild-foraged juniper berries, and heirloom parsnips pulled from the morning’s frost. The rhythmic scrape of her wooden spoon against the iron created a soothing, domestic cadence—a fundamental acoustic frequency that temporarily stabilized their frayed nerves. Yet, beneath the caramelized scent of rendered fat and roasting root vegetables, a sour note of dread lingered. Gerard was an escaped prisoner, liable to be retaken and punished; their agrarian sanctuary could not hold. 

Gerard had conceived a bitter hatred for Tergou, a town whose cold, mercantile dialect had condemned him. To survive, and to clear the obstacles to their marriage, he must leave for Italy. The demoiselle Van Eyck had provided the gold pieces specifically for this journey, a truth Gerard repeated as they sat by the fire. Italy was a land of new culinary and artistic lexicons, a place where his meticulous craftsmanship would be valued rather than persecuted by boorish magistrates. Margaret, an unselfish girl deeply rooted to the soil of her ancestors, gave her tearful consent. As they held hands until midnight, they mourned the inherent untranslatability of their grief. The nuanced, native dialect of their shared love—spoken through the shared breaking of bread and the quiet hum of their daily routines—was being violently displaced by the dominant, oppressive vocabulary of the law that sought to part them. 

The morning found them resigned to this separation, though neither possessed the courage to name the hour. But about three in the afternoon, the harmonious acoustic ecology of the cottage—the bubbling broth, the rustling oak branches—was brutally ruptured. 

Giles rode up to the door, having made a circuit of many miles. He shattered the sonic landscape of the garden with his tremendous tones, the sheer volume a jarring intrusion of the town’s chaotic noise. “Brother Gerard!” he cried. “Kate bids you run for your life. They charge you with theft; you have given them a handle. Think not to explain. Hope not for justice in Tergou. The parchments you took, they are but a blind. She hath seen your death in the men's eyes; a price is on your head. Fly!”

It was a thunder-clap that drained the blood from their faces. Giles, putting aside the rote words of his sister, added his own urgent counsel. “All the constables were at our house after you, and so was Dirk Brower. Kate is wise, Gerard. Best give ear to her rede, and fly!”

“Oh, yes, Gerard,” cried Margaret wildly, her voice trembling. “Fly on the instant. Ah! those parchments; my mind misgave me: why did I let you take them?”

“Margaret, they are but a blind,” Gerard insisted. “No matter: the old caitiff shall never see them again; I will not go till I have hidden his treasure where he shall never find it.”

After warmly thanking Giles and bidding him carry a farewell back to Kate, Gerard called for Martin. The weathered agrarian elder, whose very posture mirrored the ancient, bending oaks of the forest, listened to the peril. 

“Ay!” Martin said, his voice a low, gravelly timbre that belonged to the woods. “And if I see Dirk Brower or any of his men, I will shoot an arrow into the oak-tree that is in our garden; and on that you must run into the forest hard by, and meet me at the weird hunter's spring. Then I will guide you through the wood.”

With surprise thus provided against, Gerard breathed again. He took a spade to the garden, and while Margaret watched the oak-tree tremblingly, he dug into the earth. The olfactory landscape immediately shifted from the domestic warmth of the kitchen to the sharp, primal scent of dark loam, severed roots, and rich petrichor. One by one, he threw the parchments into the hole. They were records of the burgh—lifeless instruments of a dominant lexicon designed to subjugate the peasantry. But among them was a private deed between Floris Brandt, Margaret’s grandfather, and Ghysbrecht. 

“Why, this is as much yours as his,” Gerard said, brushing the fragrant soil from the seal. “I will read this.”

“Oh, not now, Gerard, not now,” cried Margaret. “Every moment you lose fills me with fear; and see, large drops of rain are beginning to fall, and the clouds lower.”

Gerard yielded, slipping the generational artifact into his bosom before stamping the earth down over the bureaucratic rot. A flash of lightning cleaved the sky, followed by a peal of distant thunder that rolled like the groan of some mythic beast. The rain came down heavily, washing the scent of dirt from Gerard’s hands as he and Margaret ran inside, speedily followed by Martin.

“The road is clear,” Martin said, shaking the wet from his cloak. “And a heavy storm coming on.”

The thunder crashed directly overhead, a deafening obliteration of the world’s quietude. The lightning flashed like the strokes of a whip, and Margaret hid her face. Gerard put up the rough shutter and lit a beeswax candle, blessing the fierce weather that granted him a few stolen hours with his love.

The sun set unperceived behind the tempest. Supper was finally set upon the wooden table. The hare was perfectly tender, the broth thickened with ground barley and fragrant with wild thyme—a meticulous testament to their regional food history. Normally, Gerard and Margaret revered such sustenance, decoding the profound generational memory embedded in every savory bite. But tonight, the sorrow choked them. The sensory richness of the meal stood in agonizing contrast to their constricted throats; the thought that this was their last supper together rendered the food impossible to swallow. 

The storm lulled. Peter retired to rest. Gerard was to leave at the peep of day, and neither lover could afford to lose an hour in sleep. Martin sat with them, meticulously fitting a new hemp string to his yew bow. The tight *thrum* of the cord was a pure, resonant frequency in the quiet cottage, a sound of survival. Beside him, the lovers murmured their sorrows.

Suddenly, the old man held up his hand. 

They fell dead silent, straining their ears. At first, there was nothing. Then, a footstep crackled faintly upon the autumn leaves outside the back door. To an ear dulled by the industrial clatter of the town, such a step would have meant nothing. But within the hyper-attuned acoustic ecology of the cottage, a foot trying to be noiseless was a terrifying violation.

Martin smoothly fitted an arrow to his string and blew out the candle, plunging the room into darkness and heightening their auditory focus. The rich smell of the stew mingled with the acrid smoke of the snuffed wick. At that moment, to their utter horror, they heard more than one footstep approach the other door of the cottage. These were stealthy, arrhythmic, and followed by a dead pause. 

Their blood froze. 

“Oh, Kate, oh, Kate! You said fly on the instant,” Margaret moaned, wringing her hands in anguish and wild remorse for having kept him. 

“Hush, girl!” Martin commanded in a stern whisper. 

A heavy knock fell on the door. And on the hearts within.

The back door shuddered under a violent strike, a jarring, arrhythmic percussion that tore through the cottage’s carefully cultivated acoustic ecology. To Martin Wittenhaagen—an agrarian elder whose ears were attuned to the delicate, rhythmic simmer of bone broths and the harmonic crackle of a well-tended hearth—the noise was a vulgar assault. It was the harsh, metallic thud of industrial force, threatening the sonic sanctuary of their domestic life. But amid this acoustic violence, Margaret seemed to recover her footing in the mythic stillness of the matriarchs who had stood in this kitchen before her. Her hands, which still carried the faint, yeast-rich scent of the day's sourdough starter, seized Gerard. 

She whispered to Martin, her breath carrying the breathy native dialect of the village, infused with the untranslatable weight of survival: “Say he was here, but is gone.” With this, she almost dragged Gerard up the steep, uneven wooden steps that led to her father's sleeping quarters, her own chamber lying just beyond it. 

The blows on the ancient oak door repeated, a brutal staccato against the domestic rhythm. 

“Who knocks at this hour?” Martin called out, his voice seasoned by years of echoing across wind-swept barley fields.

“Open, and you will see!”

“I open not to thieves—honest men are all abed now, resting for the morrow's harvest.”

“Open to the law, Martin Wittenhaagen, or you shall rue it.”

“Why, that is Dirk Brower's voice, I trow. What make you so far from Tergou?”

“Open, and you will know.”

Martin drew the iron bolt with the deliberate, agonizing slowness of thick molasses pouring from a cold stone crock. In rushed Dierich and four others, bringing with them the sour, sterile odor of damp wool and bureaucratic anxiety, an olfactory intrusion that instantly clashed with the cottage’s warm, atmospheric landscape of dried sage and slow-rendered tallow. They unlatched the back door to let in their cohort.

“Now, Martin, where is Gerard Eliassoen?”

“Gerard Eliassoen? Why, he was here but now!”

“Was here?” Dierich's countenance fell, the arrogant lines of his dominant lexicon briefly faltering. “And where is he now?”

“They say he has gone to Italy. Why, what is to do?”

“No matter. When did he go? Tell me not that he went in such a storm as this!”

“Here is a coil about Gerard Eliassoen,” Martin said contemptuously. He reached for a tallow candle, striking a light that illuminated the hanging braids of heirloom garlic and the dark, curing haunches of venison above. Seating himself coolly by the hearth, where the embers still radiated the olfactory memory of an ash-baked flatbread, he proceeded to whip fine silk thread around his bow-string. His hands moved with the meticulous precision of a master charcutier tying a rolled pancetta. 

“I'll tell you,” he said carelessly, employing the earthy, idiomatic phrasing of the village—a dialect constantly endangered by the sterile, homogenized decrees of Tergou's magistrates. “Know you his brother Giles? A little misbegotten imp, all head and arms? Well, he came tearing over here on a mule, and bawled out something. I was too far off to decipher the creature's words, catching only its raw noise. Anyway, he started Gerard. For as soon as he was gone, there was such crying and kissing, and then Gerard went away. They do tell me he has gone to Italy—mayhap you know where that is, for I don't.”

Dierich's countenance fell lower and lower at this account. There was no flaw in the agrarian elder’s tale. A man disconnected from the sensory rhythms of truth might have over-spiced the lie, burying the palate in unnecessary fabrications that would only raise suspicion. But Martin did his task beautifully. He offered only the single falsehood he was bade to tell, seasoned perfectly, and invented nothing of his own head.

“Mates,” said Dierich, “I doubt he speaks sooth. I told the burgomaster how 'twould be. He met the dwarf galloping Peter Buyskens's mule from Sevenbergen. 'They have sent that imp to Gerard,' says he, 'so, then, Gerard is at Sevenbergen.' 'Ah, master!' says I, '’tis too late now. We should have thought of Sevenbergen before, instead of wasting our time hunting all the odd corners of Tergou for those cursed parchments that we shall never find till we find the man that took 'em.' If he was at Sevenbergen, quoth I, and they sent the dwarf to him, it must have been to warn him we are after him. He is leagues away by now, quoth I. Confound that chalk-faced girl! She has outwitted us bearded men; and so I told the burgomaster, but he would not hear reason. A wet jerkin apiece, that is all we shall get, mates, by this job.”

Martin grinned coolly in Dierich's face, a silent victory for the native tongue over the language of empire. 

“However,” added the latter, falling back on the rigid protocols of his station, “to content the burgomaster, we will search the house.”

Martin turned grave directly. This shift in the room's emotional climate did not escape Dierich. He reflected a moment. 

“Watch outside, two of you, one on each side of the house, that no one jump from the upper windows. The rest come with me.” He snatched the candle and mounted the stairs, followed by three of his comrades, their heavy, uncalibrated footsteps grinding a syncopated dissonance into the grain of the floorboards.

Left alone, the stout soldier hung his head. The timeline seemed to fold back on itself; how many times had his ancestors stood in the shadows of their pantries, defending their hard-won sustenance from marauders? Suddenly, it occurred to him that all was not yet lost. Gerard must be either in Peter's room or Margaret's; the drop to the soil was not insurmountable. Gerard would leap. Dierich had left a man below, but for half a minute Gerard and he would be two to one. In that brief, violent space, what might not be done?

Martin held the back door ajar and monitored the acoustic environment outside, listening through the wind for the thud of boots on the damp earth. The light shone in Peter's room above. “Curse the fool!” he muttered. “Is he going to let them take him like a girl?”

The candlelight bled into Margaret's bedroom. Still, no window was unlatched. Had Gerard intended to escape that way, he would not have waited till the men were in the room. Martin recognized the geometry of the situation at once. He left the door, crept to the foot of the stairs, and strained to decipher the auditory cues above, convinced Gerard must have slipped out while the men were downstairs. 

But the silence was rudely dissipated. Faint cries issued from the inner bedroom—Margaret's.

“They have taken him,” groaned Martin, the metallic taste of adrenaline flooding his palate, bitter as unripe persimmons. “They have got him.”

If they took Gerard away, his life was not worth a button. And if evil befell him, Margaret's heart—a heart that beat in time with the very seasons of the harvest—would break. Martin cast his eyes wildly around the kitchen, taking in the drying herbs, the heavy cast-iron skillets, the ceramic pots of preserved root vegetables. This space was a temple of sustenance, and he would not let it become a site of subjugation. In a twinkling, he formed a resolution terribly characteristic of those iron times and of a hunter driven to bay. 

Stepping to each door in turn, he mimicked Dierich Brower's dominant inflection and barked sharply, “Watch the window!” He then quietly closed and bolted both doors, sealing the acoustic chamber. 

He took up his bow and six arrows. One he nocked to his string; the others he slid into his quiver. He laid his hunting knife upon a chair behind him, hilt facing outward—placed as meticulously as a chef arranging his finest carving blades before butchering a heritage roast. There he waited at the foot of the stair with the calm, ancestral determination to slay those four men, or be butchered by them. Two, he knew, he could dispose of with his arrows ere they could close the distance. Gerard and he must take their chance hand-to-hand with the remaining pair. If Brower's men hesitated at the sudden ambush, he would shoot three instead of two, tipping the scales. 

He had not long to wait. The heavy steps trespassed into Margaret's room, drawing nearer. Martin's heart hammered a primal, deafening rhythm, drowning out the ambient hum of the storm. Yet this man, deeply rooted in the soil and seasons, never wavered in this awful moment of real danger. He stood there, his body braced for combat, eyes glowing with the fierce, mythic energy of the old agrarian gods, equally ready to harvest life or forfeit his own on the very floorboards he defended. 

Upstairs, Dierich Brower and his men had found Peter in his first sleep. Their unappreciative hands blindly ransacked cupboards filled with botanical specimens and medicinal tinctures, even running their knives into an imported alligator skin nailed to the wall. Finding no Gerard in the large, shadow-filled room, they pushed on to Margaret's. 

The very sight of her chamber was discouraging to the municipal guards. It was small and bare, possessing no cupboards, dominated only by a large stone fireplace and chimney where the lingering scent of pine resin clung to the masonry. Dierich's eye fell on these directly. Here they found the beauty of Sevenbergen sleeping on an old wooden chest not a foot high. No attempt had been made to cover it, but the sheets were the pristine, blinding white of freshly milled flour, smelling faintly of her own linen. She lay there, looking like a delicate lily trampled into a muddy rut. 

Presently she awoke, sat up in the bed like one amazed, and, seeing the men, began to scream faintly and pray for mercy. The sheer acoustic purity of her distress made Dierich Brower instantly ashamed of his brutal errand. 

“Here is a to-do,” he grumbled, his cheeks flushing under his beard. “We are not going to hurt you, my pretty maid. Lie you still, and shut your eyes, and think of your wedding-night, while I look up this chimney to see if Master Gerard is there.”

“Gerard! In my room?”

“Why not? They say that you and he—”

“Cruel!” Margaret spat, her voice ringing with the rich, unyielding timbre of her native dialect, asserting a cultural authority that rendered Dierich’s bureaucratic power impotent. “You know they have driven him away from me—driven him from his native place. This is a blind. You are thieves; you are wicked men; you are not men of Sevenbergen, or you would know Margaret Brandt better than to look for her lover in this room of all others in the world. Oh, brave! Four great hulking men to come, armed to the teeth, to insult one poor honest girl! The women that live in your own houses must be naught, or you would respect them too much to insult a girl of good character.”

The linguistic power dynamic inverted entirely. Her words, steeped in the untranslatable dignity of her bloodline, sliced through their aggression like a razor-sharp filleting knife through tender roe. The men, fluent only in the hollow commercialism of municipal warrants, had no defense against her deeply rooted moral absolute.

“There! Come away, before we hear worse,” said Dierich hastily, recoiling from the hearth. “He is not in the chimney. Plaster will mend what a cudgel breaks; but a woman's tongue is a double-edged dagger, and a girl is a woman with her mother's milk still in her.” 

And he beat a hasty retreat, the disruptive thud of his boots fading as he muttered, “I told the burgomaster how 'twould be.”

Where is the woman who cannot act a part? Nature, in moments of catastrophic disruption, tutors even the simplest of her sex to throw dust in the eyes of authority, drawing upon an ancestral instinct as old as the cultivation of the first wild wheat. The abrasive, iron-shod percussion of the guards’ boots—a jarring, industrial violence against the fragile sonic equilibrium of the ancient timbered house—finally faded down the stairwell. Margaret slipped out of the bed. Her skirts and petticoat still carried the faint, earthy fragrance of roasted chicory and crushed juniper from the kitchen below. She heaved the long chest’s lid, bed-clothes and all, against the plaster wall, and stood perfectly still, letting her ears map the acoustic landscape of the street until the men’s heavy footfalls died away completely.

For generations, the women of her agrarian lineage had organized their domestic rhythms around such heavy, butter-rubbed oak chests, using them to hoard milled winter rye and salted fatbacks against the frost. But this particular vessel harbored a secret. The floorboards beneath it had rotted, and Gerard—approaching the room’s architecture with the same meticulous reverence a master baker brings to his proofing troughs—had ingeniously sawn away the decayed wood. He had sunken the chest directly onto the rafters, expanding its depth to two full feet while its visible profile remained a mere twelve inches above the floor. 

The house was steeped in silence, though not the resonant, breathing quiet of a resting hearth. Margaret sank to her knees, breathing a prayer of thanks into the scent of old wood and dried thyme. She leaned over the deep basin of the chest. 

“Gerard,” she whispered, pitching her voice into the intimate, localized dialect of their shared affection—a native tongue of tender inflections utterly untranslatable to the rigid, dominant lexicon of the magistrates hunting him. 

Gerard did not reply. 

She pitched the syllables slightly louder against the cavernous hollow. “Gerard, all is safe, thank Heaven! You may rise; but oh! be cautious!” 

An unnatural acoustic void answered her, a terrifying absence of the rhythmic exhalations she expected. She reached down into the dark, her fingers finding his shoulder. “Gerard!” 

Still, no reply. “Oh, what is this?” she cried. Her hands fluttered wildly over his face, mapping the chilled, unresponsive topography of his brow and sternum. He lacked the vibrant heat of a living body. She seized him by the shoulders, shaking him, trying to physically knead the life back into him as one might rouse dormant yeast, but he slipped from her trembling grip and fell back. It was not the movement of a man, but the dead weight of an unfermented sack of wet dough. 

A glacial dread took root in her marrow. The lid had been clamped tight. She had lain her own weight upon it. The men had lingered so long, polluting the air with their dominant, interrogatory barks, while below her, she had sealed him in an airless tomb. 

Frenzy lent her the raw, unpasteurized strength of her agrarian foremothers. She tore him from the cedar-lined depths, hauling him in her arms to the window. She dashed the casements open to the moonlight, letting in a sudden draft redolent of wet river stones and wild dill. She laid him in the silvered breeze. His face was the color of exhausted hearth-ash; his limbs were terribly limp. She pressed her hand to his chest, desperate for the percussive thud of his heart—the fundamental rhythm of her world. 

Horror. It was as still as a winter-killed field. 

Horror of horrors: she had stifled the very breath from him with her own body. The human mind cannot instantly digest so vast and bitter a harvest. How could Gerard, who had climbed into that oak vessel flushed and vital merely five minutes ago, be dead? 

She called to him using every endearing fragment of their private, endangered dialect, words saturated with the sensory memory of shared meals and stolen warmth. She kissed him, tasting the salt of her own panic mingling with the faint trace of milled flour on his skin, coaxing him, imploring him to shatter the suffocating silence. No answer came to these words of desperate love—phrases she had never dared utter before, her heart’s true language finally breaking through the repression of modesty.

Then, trembling violently, the poor creature began to pour over that ashy face pitiful, foolish fragments of speech that highlighted the tragic inadequacy of standardized language to articulate absolute despair. 

“Oh, Gerard! I am very sorry you are dead. I am very sorry I have killed you. Forgive me for not letting the men take you; it would have been better than this. Oh, Gerard! I am very, very sorry for what I have done.” 

Suddenly, her grief curdled into raving denial. “No! no! such things can't be, or there is no God. It is monstrous. How can my Gerard be dead? How can I have killed my Gerard? I love him. Oh, God! you know how I love him. He does not. I never told him. If he knew my heart, he would speak to me, he would not be so deaf to his poor Margaret. It is all a trick to make me cry out and betray him; but no! I love him too well for that. I'll choke first.” 

She clamped both hands around her own throat, battling the primal, muscular urge to scream, tasting the metallic tang of copper in her mouth as she choked back her anguish to preserve his safety. 

“If he would but say one word. Oh, Gerard! don't die without a word. Have mercy on me and scold me, but speak to me: if you are angry with me, scold me! curse me! I deserve it: the idiot that killed the man she loved better than herself. Ah, I am a murderess. The worst in all the world. Help! help! I have murdered him. Ah! ah! ah! ah! ah!”

The untranslatable agony broke free. She tore her hair, unleashing shriek after shriek. The cries were so wild, so raw and piercing, they shattered the acoustic ecology of the sleeping street, falling like an iron cleaver upon the ears of Dierich Brower and his men. The harsh officers all started to their feet, looking at one another as the domestic quiet was irreversibly torn apart.

Generations had seasoned the heavy oak beams of the house with the olfactory history of sustenance—the rich, resinous smoke of applewood, the oxidized tang of hanging pheasants, and the sweet, earthy memory of slow-braised root vegetables. It was a domestic sanctuary whose acoustic ecology was usually governed by the rhythmic, percussive chop of the kitchen knife and the gentle, harmonic simmer of the cast-iron pot. The cyclical nature of time in this space was measured by the seasons of the harvest. Yet tonight, this ancestral rhythm was abruptly ruptured.

Martin Wittenhaagen, an agrarian elder whose hands knew the intimate geography of butchery and the delicate art of curing meats, stood at the foot of the stairs. His hunting knife, forged for dressing wild boar and separating marrow-rich joints, was tucked behind his belt. His bow was drawn nearly to the head, the taut string vibrating with a hum that belonged to the wild, acoustic quiet of the forests, not this hearth. But when the men lumbered back through the door without Gerard, the old forester was struck with profound amazement. He lowered his weapon, his mouth falling open.

The men, their boots scraping discordantly against the stone floor, were equally puzzled by the lethal posture they had caught him in.

“Why, mates, was the old fellow making ready to shoot at us?” one asked, his voice a harsh, guttural intrusion into the room’s quiet resonance.

“Stuff!” said Martin, recovering his stolid composure. He reached for a grounding, utilitarian excuse, though his native, agrarian cadence felt increasingly fragile against the dominant, martial lexicon of his adversaries. “I was but trying my new string. There! I'll unstring my bow, if you think that.”

“Humph!” said Dierich suspiciously, his tone carrying the abrasive edge of unsympathetic authority. “There is something more in you than I understand: put a log on, and let us dry our hides a bit ere we go.”

A blazing fire was soon resurrected. The men gathered around the hearth, their damp wool garments and long, rain-soaked hair soon smoking. The olfactory landscape grew dense—the comforting, caramelized aroma of burning sap clashing violently with the sour, metallic stench of wet armor and unwashed men. For a brief moment, the hiss and crackle of the wood restored a semblance of acoustic harmony. 

Then, the shrieks bled down from Margaret's room.

It was a sound of sheer, untranslatable terror, an utterance completely devoid of linguistic structure yet carrying the full, devastating weight of human vulnerability. It shattered the psychological well-being of the space, a piercing auditory violence that severed the connection between the safety of the hearth and the sanctuary above.

The men started up in a sudden, chaotic clamor. One of them seized a thick tallow candle—its scent of rendered mutton fat suddenly cloying in the tense air—and ran toward the steps that led to the bedrooms. Martin rose hastily. Confused by the sudden screams and profoundly understanding the danger posed by the man's crude curiosity, the old steward tried to throw himself in the path to prevent him from ascending. 

But the power dynamics of this new, violent vocabulary overwhelmed him. Dierich threw his heavy arms around Martin from behind, bellowing over the din for the others to keep him down. The man with the candle broke clear away, his heavy footfalls thundering up the stairs. The rest fell upon Martin. 

What followed was a fierce, discordant struggle, a tragic erasure of the elder’s dignity. More than once they were all rolling over the flour-dusted floorboards, Martin caught in the center of the fray. Despite his deep, marrow-deep strength, the sheer number of the men succeeded in mastering the old Samson. They bound him hand and foot with a coarse hempen rope originally intended for Gerard, pulling the knots tight with grunts that polluted the room's soundscape. 

Martin groaned aloud, a deep, guttural sound of cultural and personal defeat. He saw that the man had made his way to Margaret's room during the chaotic struggle, and here he lay, entirely powerless on the stones where he had spent decades preparing feasts of celebration and comfort.

“Ay, grind your teeth, you old rogue,” panted Dierich, breathless from the scuffle. “You shan't use them.”

“It is my belief, mates, that our lives were scarce safe while this old fellow's bones were free,” another soldier muttered, the sharp consonants of his dialect ringing with unearned triumph.

“He makes me think this Gerard is not far off,” put in a third.

“No such luck,” replied Dierich, brushing the domestic dust from his tabard. “Hallo, mates. Jorian Ketel is a long time in that girl's bedroom. Best go and see after him, some of us.”

Their rude laughter erupted—a shallow, ugly noise that mocked the historical sanctity of the home and demonstrated the casual cruelty of those who view others not as individuals, but as mere objects of conquest. 

The laughter had barely subsided into the smoky air when hasty footsteps were heard running along the floorboards overhead, a frantic, rhythmic drumming that promised further upheaval in the saga of the house.

“Oh, here he comes, at last,” said Dierich, looking upward. “Well, Jorian, what is to do now up there?”

Jorian Ketel stepped into the heavy, unnatural silence of Margaret's room. For an agrarian elder attuned to the symphonic rhythms of a living homestead—the bubbling of root-vegetable stews, the percussive chop of a forged blade against a wooden block, the gentle sigh of rising dough—this sonic vacuum was an acoustic tragedy. There, he found the man he had been hunting. Gerard lay pale and motionless, his head cradled in Margaret’s lap. She was kneeling over him, mute, her spirit severed from the sensory world. Her eyes were glazed, registering neither the flicker of Jorian’s tallow candle nor the lingering aroma of dried thyme and smoked fatback that clung to the plaster walls. She was lost in a place where no spoken dialect could reach her.
