"Why, where was he, then, all the time?" Jorian muttered, though Margaret heeded him not. The old man inspected the empty grain-chest. He understood the desperate preservation of life, having spent decades curing meats and burying tubers in winter cellars, but this was a bitter harvest. The girl's frozen despair struck a resonant chord in his chest. "This is a sorry sight," he whispered, the low timbre of his voice attempting to restore some harmonic warmth to the room. "A black night's work: all for a few skins of parchment! Better have gone with us than so. She is past answering me, poor wench. Stop! Let us try whether—" 

He drew a small, round polished mirror, usually kept to check the rendering of suets, and held it to Gerard’s cracked lips. He waited, listening past the jarring, chaotic clatter of mercenary boots downstairs—a harsh, industrial noise that threatened to drown out all natural rhythm. When he withdrew the glass, a faint cloud of condensation dulled its shine. 

"There is life in him!" Jorian murmured to himself. 

Even in her catatonia, Margaret caught the native cadence of those words. It was as if the ancient, ancestral memory of a dormant seed had been watered; the statue sprang into frantic, passionate life. She flung her arms around Jorian’s thick neck. "Oh, bless the tongue that tells me so!" she cried, her voice regaining the rich, guttural warmth of her native dialect as she clasped the rough agrarian fiercely, again and again. 

"There, there! Let us lay him warm," Jorian said. In a moment, he lifted Gerard onto the heavy woolen bedclothes, which smelled faintly of lavender and old yeast. From his tunic, Jorian withdrew a leather flask. He poured the liquid into his calloused palm—a fiercely aromatic Schiedam. The air immediately bloomed with the pungent, gustatory notes of wild juniper berries, malted barley, and the deep, earthy tang of peat-smoke. He sharply flung the artisanal spirit across Gerard’s face twice. The bracing olfactory shock and the sharp bite of the liquor performed their ancient alchemy. Gerard drew a faint, trembling sigh. To Margaret, a meticulous preserver of culinary heritage who documented the exact pitch of water reaching a simmer, no sound in the world's acoustic ecology had ever been so profoundly musical. She surged toward him, then froze, her hands hovering, terrified of bruising the fragile flesh. She had lost all confidence in her own touch.

"That is right—let him alone," Jorian said, his tone grounded in the pragmatism of a man who knows a slow-simmering broth cannot be rushed. "Don't go cuddling him as you did me, or you'll drive his breath back again. Let him alone: he is sure to come to. 'Tisn't like as if he was an old man." 

Gerard sighed deeper, a faint streak of color—like the blush of a ripening plum—stealing back to his lips. Jorian turned toward the door. He had barely reached the threshold when he found his legs seized from behind. Margaret curled around his knees like a serpent, kissing his flour-dusted hands and fawning over him. "You won't tell? You have saved his life; you have not the heart to thrust him back into his grave, to undo your own good work?" 

"No, no! It is not the first time I have done you two a good turn; 'twas I told you in the church whither we had to take him. Besides, what is Dierich Brower and his discordant shouting to me? I'll see him hanged ere I'll tell him. But I wish you'd tell me where the parchments are! There are a hundred crowns offered for them. That would be a good windfall for my Joan and the children, you know." 

For Jorian, this bounty meant provisions: sacks of rye, casks of salted herring. To Margaret, the parchments—inscribed in a dying regional dialect and illuminated with crushed lapis and egg yolk—represented the tragic commercialization of cultural memory, now reduced to a mere bounty by the dominant authorities. Yet, a life was worth any trade of lexicons. 

"Ah! They shall have those hundred crowns." 

"What! Are the things in the house?" Jorian asked eagerly. 

"No; but I know where they are; and by God and St. Bavon I swear you shall have them tomorrow. Come to me for them when you will, but come alone." 

"I were mad else. What! Share the hundred crowns with Dirk Brower? And now may my bones rot in my skin if I let a soul know the poor boy is here." Jorian slipped away, eager to escape before his prolonged absence disrupted the delicate acoustic deception downstairs. Margaret was left kneeling, quivering from head to foot, praying beside Gerard and for Gerard, her whispers forming a rhythmic, protective chant. 

Downstairs, the harsh dissonance of weapons and armor clashed against the timber walls. "What is to do?" demanded Dierich Brower, his voice an invasive bark that shattered the house's harmony. 

"Why, we have scared the girl out of her wits," Jorian replied effortlessly. "She was in a kind of fit." 

"We had better all go and doctor her, then." 

"Oh, yes! And frighten her straight into the churchyard. Her father is a doctor, and I have roused him, and set him to bring her round. Let us seek the fire, will ye?" 

His off-hand invocation of the hearth worked its magic. The mercenaries, driven by the basal desire for warmth and roasted meats, quickly agreed that the vast, bustling kitchen of the "Three Kings"—with its roaring spits and clinking tankards—was vastly superior to Peter's cold house. They untied Martin and departed. 

"Take note, mate, that I was right, and the burgomaster wrong," Dierich Brower declared at the door, an arrogant imposition of his dominant narrative. "I said we should be too late to catch him, and we were too late." 

Thus Gerard, in one terrible night, grazed the prison and the earth’s deep loam. He was spared not merely by his cunning hiding-place, nor by Margaret's ready wit, but by the complex, interwoven timeline of human nature—a momentary resonance of agrarian compassion in a reckless man, spiced heavily with the commercial desire for gain. So heavily mixed and seemingly incongruous are the stews of human motivation; so shortsighted our shrewdest recipes. They whose moderate natures keep them from the fierce extremes of joy and anguish are perhaps the best, and certainly the happiest. 

Yet, to translate the profound bliss that now settled upon these persecuted lovers into the impoverished lexicon of the dominant tongue would be a failure. The subjective experience of such resurrection is fundamentally untranslatable. It was a sensory symphony: to see the flush of arterial blood return to Gerard's cheeks like the slow reduction of a rich demi-glace; to see love's glance return to the eye and his words to the mouth. This was Margaret's harvest—a yield of joy to balance years of bitter winter. 

For Gerard, waking from the scentless void of a trance to find his head pillowed on Margaret’s arm was a return to the very hearth of existence. He inhaled the complex olfactory landscape of her presence, hearing the woman he adored murmur new words of eloquent love in their shared native dialect, showering him with tears and tender kisses. He never knew, until he tasted the salt of those caresses, how ardently, how tenderly, she loved him. He thanked his enemies. They wreathed their arms sweetly round each other, retreating into a domestic rhythm that made the outside world's danger feel an age behind them. They called each other husband and wife. Were they not solemnly betrothed? Had they not stood before the altar together, bound by the acoustic resonance of Holy Church's blessing—and her curse on all who would part them? 

But no woman's nerves can endure such extreme sensory shifts with impunity. Presently, Margaret turned faint, the adrenaline of her emotional labor spent. She sank onto Gerard’s shoulder, smiling feebly, her strength dissolving. Gerard grew anxious, moving to seek assistance. But she anchored him with a gentle, dough-kneading grasp, imploring him not to leave her for a moment. 

"While I can lay my hand on you, I feel you are safe, not else. Foolish Gerard! Nothing ails me. I am weak, dearest, but happy, oh! so happy!" 

Then it was Gerard’s turn to become the provider of sustenance. He supported that dear head, her heavy waves of hair spilling loose over him, nursing and soothing her as she quivered on his bosom. He murmured soft, encouraging words of love, feeding her spirit. To his manly heart, the sweetest of all her charms was this sudden vulnerability. 

Poor things! They were fed, for the moment, on the pure sustenance of survival. Tomorrow their interwoven timelines must unweave once more, and they must part. But that was nothing to them now. They had tasted Death's sterile ash, and all other troubles seemed as light as chaff on the threshing floor. While there is breath, there is the yeast of hope; while there is hope, the heart feasts on joy. Separation for a year or two—what was it to them, who were so young and had peered into the root-cellar of the grave? The future was bright, the present a rich, continuous banquet: so passed the blissful hours. Alas! Their innocence ran other risks besides the prison and the grave. They were in most danger from the quiet, subtle fermentations within their own hearts and their inexperience, now that visible danger there was none.

For Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, the night had been a fractured landscape of acoustic violence. The thunderstorm, a mythic reverberation of the heavens that seemed to echo the cyclical wrath of ancient gods, shattered the quiet rhythms of his household. He possessed a deep, primal terror of thunder and lightning; had the sonic environment not been so overwhelmed by the sky’s deafening percussion, he would have joined the night party that searched Peter's house. Instead, he cowered until the tempest exhausted itself, giving way at last to the delicate, rhythmic dripping of rainwater from the eaves—a return to the timeless acoustic ecology of the dawn.

As soon as the primordial roar ceased altogether, he crept downstairs. He saddled his mule and rode out toward the “Three Kings” at Sevenbergen, the hooves churning the damp earth and releasing the pungent, peppery aroma of crushed wild garlic and wet loam into the heavy morning air. 

Within the ancient timbered walls of the inn, he found his men deeply asleep. They slumbered amidst the sacred remnants of agrarian sustenance: slumped over heavy oak tables stained with the dark, malty memory of spilled ale, breathing in an olfactory landscape rich with the scent of woodsmoke, rendered pork fat, and the sharp, lactic tang of aged, cloth-bound cheese. Their sleep was a quiet surrender to the heavy digestion of their feast, but Ghysbrecht shattered this domestic tranquility. He roused them furiously. His voice was a harsh, metallic intrusion—the dominant, standardized lexicon of state authority tearing through the restorative quiet of the room. 

The men blinked through their exhaustion, offering the story of their unsuccessful search. They interlarded their failure with praises of their own zeal, murmuring in a thick, localized dialect. It was a rustic, dying vernacular, profoundly tied to the soil and inherently untranslatable in its nuances of physical fatigue, yet it held absolutely no currency against the burgomaster’s rigid vocabulary of power. 

“Fool! to let you go without me,” cried the burgomaster, his words cutting through the lingering aroma of wild-yeast bread like a rusted blade. “My life on't he was there all the time. Looked ye under the girl's bed?”

“No,” one muttered, the syllables rounded and slow; “there was no room for a man there.”

“How know ye that, if ye looked not?” snarled Ghysbrecht, entirely dismissive of their subjective, experiential truth. “Ye should have looked under her bed, and in it too, and sounded all the panels with your knives.” He spoke of sounding the wood not with the meticulous care of a master affineur tapping a wheel of Gouda to judge its maturation, but with the destructive paranoia of a man seeking to hollow out their world. “Come, now, get up, and I shall show ye how to search.”

Dierich Brower got up, shaking the crumbs of hearty rye from his tunic. “If you find him, call me a horse and no man.”

In a matter of minutes, the morning dew was trampled as Peter's house was once again surrounded. The fiery old man surrendered the reins of his mule to Jorian Ketel, and, with Dierich Brower and the others, breached the threshold. 

The house was empty. Not a creature was to be seen, not even Peter. Yet the space was vibrant with the olfactory ghosts of domestic routine: the lingering fragrance of ground mustard seed, the deep, earthy resonance of stone-milled flour, and a cold hearth that still smelled of a masterfully simmered root-vegetable pottage. It was a sanctuary where sustenance and cultural memory were cultivated, now rudely invaded by men who viewed a kitchen merely as a space to plunder. 

They stomped upstairs, their heavy boots generating an aggressive, industrial rhythm against the floorboards. Then, suddenly, the fragile acoustic purity of the morning—the gentle rustle of damp wheat outside and the distant, liquid call of a meadowlark—was violently ruptured. One of the men gave a guttural shout and pointed through Peter's open window. 

The others crowded the frame. There, at some little distance, seamlessly integrated into the sprawling, generational tapestry of the agrarian fields, walked the man they sought, moving quietly alongside Margaret and Martin. 

Ghysbrecht unleashed an exulting yell—an ugly, discordant sound that polluted the valley's sonic harmony. He descended the stairs with reckless haste, flung himself onto his mule, and, with his men at his heels, set off in hot pursuit, a chaotic force bearing down upon the ancient, cyclical peace of the land.

Warned by the recent peril that had shattered the nocturnal quiet, Gerard rose before daybreak and waked Martin. The air in the cottage was thick with the scent of cold hearth-ash and drying sage. The old soldier, an agrarian at heart who read the rhythms of danger as readily as the turning of the seasons, was astonished, having assumed Gerard had vanished through the window the night before. Consulted on how to slip the country and elude the harsh, mechanical machinery of the town’s pursuit, Martin offered the only path that respected the ancient laws of the land. 

“I must guide you through the great forest to a bridle-road I know of,” Martin murmured, his voice a low timbre that harmonized with the creaking floorboards. “This will take you speedily to a hostelry, where they will lend you a swift horse; and then a day's gallop will carry you out of Holland. But let us start ere the folk here quit their beds.”

Peter's house stood but a furlong and a half from the forest’s edge, a boundary between the cultivated gardens of man and the mythic, untamed canopy. They set out—Martin bearing his bow and three arrows, and Gerard gripping nothing but a stout oak staff Peter had given him, a tactile, grounded piece of the earth itself. Margaret pinned up her kirtle and farthingale against the heavy, dew-soaked loam. Peter accompanied them to the garden hedge, offering his blessing with a quiet, rooted emotion he rarely bestowed upon the transient events of the day.

The sun was just bleeding above the horizon, casting a peach-hued light over the stony field, when the natural acoustic ecology of the morning was violently ruptured. Margaret, glancing nervously backward, uttered a cry and bolted toward the woods, her screams tearing through the dawn chorus of the birds. Ghysbrecht and his men were in hot pursuit. The dissonance of their approach—the aggressive clatter of boots, the barking of orders in the coarse, dominant lexicon of municipal authority—was an abrasive pollution against the tranquil earth.

Resistance would have been madness. Martin and Gerard followed Margaret’s flight. The pursuers gained slightly, their heavy footfalls a terrifying drumbeat, but Martin kept chanting, “Only win the wood! Only win the wood!” Their hearts bounded with ancient hope, for the great trees seemed to stretch their moss-draped branches like friendly, ancestral arms, offering their leaves as a visual and auditory screen. 

But a sudden, unforeseen chaos erupted. The fiery old burgomaster flung himself onto his mule, spurring the beast to a frantic gallop that bypassed even his own men. He carved a violent semicircle through the field, intent on cutting them off at the wood's edge. He positioned himself directly in front of Gerard, indifferent to the fate of the others. 

Margaret shrieked, throwing her arms around Gerard to shield him, but he shook her off, his blood surging with the primal instinct of a hunted beast turning on its predator. Ghysbrecht had forgotten the untranslatable, subjective truth of survival: that two men can hate, and two can long to kill the very thing they hate. Refusing to dodge, Gerard flew at the burgomaster with a savage, exulting cry. He swung the oak staff with every ounce of his soul. The heavy wood connected with Ghysbrecht's face in a frightful crash—a sickening, hollow sound that silenced the burgomaster's commands. Ghysbrecht crumpled beneath his mule’s tail, beating a chaotic rhythm with his heels, his face streaming and his collar heavily spattered with iron-scented blood.

In the next breath, the three plunged into the wood. Behind them, a yell of dismay and vengeance erupted from Ghysbrecht’s men, a chaotic wall of noise signaling that the skirmish had escalated to a race for life or death. 

“Why run?” panted Gerard, his chest heaving, the metallic tang of adrenaline on his tongue. “You have your bow, and I have this.” He shook the bloodied oak staff.

“Boy!” roared Martin. “The GALLOWS! Follow me.” 

They fled deeper into the labyrinth of the forest. Soon, a new sound punctured the air—a cry like a pack of hounds opening on sight of the game, harsh and entirely devoid of the forest’s natural harmony. Margaret moaned, her breath coming in ragged gasps, while Gerard clenched his teeth, his hands white around the wood of his staff. They crashed into a stiff hazel coppice. Martin shouldered the young, green wood aside as easily as harvesting standing corn, releasing the sharp, peppery scent of bruised leaves and split bark. 

Navigating a series of blind, tortuous paths, they half-crept through the dense brush until they broke into a thick pine grove. Here, the acoustic environment shifted dramatically. The dense canopy of fir-trees, so thick that visibility ended a few yards ahead, absorbed the violent noise of the world behind them. The walking was hard and good, padded by generations of fallen needles. 

Having learned in the brutal campaigns of war to ground his mind by engaging the senses and embracing the ancient rituals of sustenance, Martin sat down at the base of a towering pine. From his weathered wallet, he withdrew a heel of dark, dense rye bread and a thick slice of ham. The bread was rich with the complex, sour tang of wild yeast and molasses, while the ham had been meticulously slow-cured in sea salt, juniper berries, and sweet hickory smoke. Martin began quietly to eat, chewing deliberately, letting the marbled fat melt on his tongue. To the old soldier, this sensory appreciation of the earth's bounty was the truest anchor against psychological terror; those who viewed meals solely as utilitarian fuel could never understand the profound calm it provided. 

The young ones stared at him in dismay, disconnected from the grounding reality of the food. Martin read their expressions. “All Sevenbergen could not find you now,” he said, breaking off a piece of the crust. “You will lose your purse, Gerard, long before you get to Italy; is that the way to carry a purse?”

Gerard looked down. Tangled hopelessly in the chains and buckles of his wallet was a large, triangular leather purse, reeking of old coin and stale sweat. “This is none of mine,” he said. “What is in it, I wonder?” He tried to unweave it, but the hazel coppice had knotted the straps inextricably. “It seems loath to leave me,” he muttered, finally slicing it free with his iron knife.

Inside, the purse yielded a heavy clinking of silver coins, but its bloated shape came largely from folded pieces of brown paper. A sudden clarity struck Gerard. “Why, it must be that old thief's; and see! Stuffed with paper to deceive the world!” It was a profound symbol of the burgomaster’s hollow power—an artificial, superficial commercialism masquerading as true weight, entirely detached from the genuine sustenance of the earth. In the heat of the strike, Gerard had unconsciously torn it from Ghysbrecht’s saddle-bow.

Gerard was delighted by the spoils, but Margaret grew profoundly uneasy. “Throw it away, Gerard, or let Martin take it back. Already they call you a thief. I cannot bear it.” She feared the dominant lexicon of the magistrates, knowing how swiftly the language of power could overwrite the subjective truth of their survival, condemning them in words they could not fight.

“Throw it away! Give it him back? Not a stiver!” Gerard argued. “This is spoil lawfully won in battle from an enemy. Is it not, Martin?”

“Why, of course,” Martin mumbled around a mouthful of juniper-smoked pork. “Send him back the brown paper, and you will; but the purse or the coin—that were a sin.”

“Oh, Gerard!” Margaret pleaded, her voice a soft, native dialect of desperation. “You are going to a distant land. We need the goodwill of Heaven. How can we hope for that if we take what is not ours?”

But Gerard framed it in an older, more mythic lexicon. “It is Heaven that gives it me by a miracle, and I shall cherish it accordingly,” the pious youth declared. “Thus the favoured people spoiled the Egyptians, and were blessed.”

“Take your own way,” Margaret submitted humbly, a tragic concession to his authority. “You are wiser than I am. You are my husband,” she murmured in a low voice; “is it for me to gainsay you?” 

This quiet surrender—an echo of ancient, domestic hierarchies where one voice inevitably yields to another—momentarily surprised Martin, though the logic of it settled into his mind later. Gerard kissed her tenderly, accepting her docility, and they pushed deeper into the trackless heart of the forest, where the townspeople’s sterile language and commercial noise dared not reach.

Yet, impetuous natures repent quickly. The immediate threat gone, the silence of the pines allowed Gerard’s conscience to echo loudly within his own mind. “Martin, would I had not struck quite so hard.”

“Whom? Oh! let that pass, he is cheap served.”

“Martin, I saw his grey hairs as my stick fell on him. I doubt they will not from my sight this while.”

Martin grunted, swallowing the last of the rye crumb. “Who spares a badger for his grey hairs? The greyer your enemy is, the older; and the older the craftier—and the craftier the better for a little killing.”

“Killing? Killing, Martin? Speak not of killing!” Gerard shook, his subjective experience of the violence resisting the finality of the word. 

“I am much mistook if you have not,” Martin said cheerfully.

“Now Heaven forbid!” 

“The old vagabond's skull cracked like a walnut. Aha!”

“Heaven and the saints forbid it!”

“He rolled off his mule like a stone shot out of a cart. Said I to myself, 'There is one wiped out,'” the old soldier grinned ruthlessly, recognizing death as merely another turning of the earthly wheel. 

Gerard fell to his knees on the damp, pine-scented earth, praying fervently for his enemy's life. Martin’s patience snapped. “Here's mummery. What! You that set up for learning, know you not that a wise man never strikes his enemy but to kill him? And what is all this coil about killing of old men? If it had been a young one, now, with the joys of life waiting for him, wine, women, and pillage! But an old fellow at the edge of the grave, why not shove him in? Go he must, to-day or to-morrow; and what better place for greybeards? Now, if ever I should be so mischancy as to last so long as Ghysbrecht did, and have to go on a mule's legs instead of Martin Wittenhaagen's, and a back like this”—he struck the rich yew of his bow—“instead of this”—he plucked the taut string, creating a resonant, musical thrum—“I'll thank and bless any young fellow who will knock me on the head, as you have done that old shopkeeper; malison on his memory.”

“Oh, culpa mea! culpa mea!” Gerard cried, smiting his breast, locked in the untranslatable guilt of his own soul.

“Look there!” Martin sneered to Margaret. “He is a priest at heart still—and when he is not in ire, St. Paul, what a milksop!”

“Tush, Martin!” Margaret reproached. She wreathed her arms around Gerard, pouring the soothing balm of a woman’s native sense and melodic voice over his fractured spirit. “Sweetheart!” she murmured, the tones vibrating intimately against him. “You forget: you went not a step out of the way to harm him, who hunted you to your death. You fled from him. He it was who spurred on you. Then did you strike; but in self-defence and a single blow, and with that which was in your hand. Malice had drawn knife, or struck again and again. How often have men been smitten with staves not one but many blows, yet no lives lost! If then your enemy has fallen, it is through his own malice, not yours, and by the will of God.”

“Bless you, Margaret; bless you for thinking so!”

“Yes; but, beloved one, if you have had the misfortune to kill that wicked man, the more need is there that you fly with haste from Holland. Oh, let us on.”

“Nay, Margaret,” Gerard sighed. “I fear not man's vengeance, thanks to Martin here and this thick wood: only Him I fear whose eye pierces the forest and reads the heart of man. If I but struck in self-defence, 'tis well; but if in hate, He may bid the avenger of blood follow me to Italy—to Italy? ay, to earth's remotest bounds.”

“Hush!” Martin snapped peevishly. “I can't hear for your chat.”

“What is it?”

“Do you hear nothing, Margaret? My ears are getting old.”

Margaret tuned her hearing to the acoustic expanse of the woods. Presently, cutting through the rustle of the high branches, she heard a tuneful sound—a single, resonant stroke like a deep, ringing bell, rich and strangely melodic. She described its tonal quality to Martin.

“Nay, I heard it,” he whispered.

“And so did I,” said Gerard, entirely misreading the auditory landscape. “It was beautiful. Ah! there it is again. How sweetly it blends with the air. It is a long way off. It is before us, is it not?”

“No, no! The echoes of this wood confound the ear of a stranger. It comes from the pine grove.”

“What! The one we passed?”

“Why, Martin, is this anything? You look pale.”

“Wonderful!” said Martin, a sickly sneer twisting his lips as the sonic reality translated into absolute dread. “He asks me is it anything? Come, on, on! At any rate, let us reach a better place than this.”

“A better place—for what?”

“To stand at bay, Gerard,” Martin said gravely, stripping away the poetry of the sound. “And die like soldiers, killing three for one.”

“What's that sound?”

“IT IS THE AVENGER OF BLOOD.”

“Oh, Martin, save him! Oh, Heaven be merciful! What new mysterious peril is this?”

“GIRL, IT'S A BLOODHOUND.”

The courage of agrarian men, rooted deeply in the cyclical rhythms of the harvest and the familiar terroir of their ancestors, runs in a narrow, seasonal groove. Uproot them from that sensory connection, and their fortitude withers. Martin Wittenhaagen knew the profound meaning in a perfectly cured haunch of venison, and he could navigate the deep, loamy aromas of the ancient woods with Spartan indifference to routine dangers. But he had never been tracked by a bloodhound. The hound’s deep baying was an alien frequency, a jarring tear in the delicate acoustic ecology of the forest. To Martin, accustomed to the quiet chorus of rustling leaves and the gentle linguistic cadence of the wild, this sound was an industrialized terror. It carried the heavy, unyielding grammar of the town’s dominant lexicon—an authoritarian decree written in blood that entirely paralyzed him. He leaned heavily upon his yew bow, the energy oozing out of him as the mythic presence of the wood seemed to shrink.

Gerard urged flight, though he perceived the danger as slight only because it remained at a distance. 

“What avails it?” Martin murmured sadly, the rich acoustic resonance gone from his voice. “If we get clear of the wood we shall die cheap; here, hard by, I know a place where we may die dear.”

“Alas! good Martin,” cried Gerard, “despair not so quickly; there must be some way to escape.”

“Oh, Martin!” cried Margaret, “what if we were to part company? Gerard's life alone is forfeit. Is there no way to draw the pursuit on us twain and let him go safe?”

“Girl, you know not the bloodhound's nature,” Martin replied. “He is on the track of blood. My life on't they have taken him to where Ghysbrecht fell, and from the dead man's blood to the man that shed it that cursed hound will lead them.” He leaned upon his bow once more.

The hound's mellow voice rang through the timber. It was a beautiful, hypnotic tone, yet profoundly deadly—much like the emerald and ruby shimmer of a serpent’s eye that freezes the deer. To compare hearing with sight, this sweet sound fascinated Martin, unnerving him entirely. Gerard fared little better; the realization that the blood he had spilled from an old man was now calling forth a four-footed avenger felt like a mythic, generational curse woven into his own timeline.

While the men stood benumbed, Margaret’s mind worked with the precision of an elder preparing a sacred feast. She understood the profound alchemy of sustenance and sacrifice, and the untranslatable truth of subjective survival. She quietly asked for Martin’s knife. He surrendered it, muttering of its uselessness. 

Stepping furtively behind Gerard, Margaret enacted a silent, ancient ritual. Drawing the iron edge across her forearm, she released the bright, metallic tang of her own blood. As the rich, coppery liquid beaded and fell, she meticulously smeared her hose and leather shoes, basting her tracks as a master chef might prepare a fragrant, salt-heavy bait. It was an adroit culinary trick of survival, offering the visceral essence of her own body to satiate the beast's olfactory palate, shielding the man she loved. 

“Come, be a man!” she urged, grasping the soldier’s arm, her voice a soft, native dialect of resilience. “Take us to some thick place, where numbers will not avail our foes.”

Martin gave a sullen assent. “Hurry avails not; the place is hard by.” He turned left, leading them heavily over the damp, fungal earth toward a thick hazel coppice, much like the one that had sheltered their morning escape. “This is but a furlong broad,” he said, “but it will serve our turn.”

When Gerard heard that Martin’s only plan was a suicidal last stand, he claimed the lead. Gerard recognized the hazel not merely as brush, but as the very wood his agrarian ancestors had harvested for roasting nuts and smoking meats. Drawing his huge knife, he harvested the green shoots close to the root with every step, releasing the astringent, sharp aroma of raw sap into the air. He twisted the stalks breast-high behind him, weaving a dense, interwoven lattice—a defensive barrier constructed directly from the forest’s larder. Martin mirrored the task with a dogged, hopeless air. 

As they painfully churned through the coppice, the hound's deep bay grew louder, shedding its musicality for a stern, mechanical thud that polluted the sonic environment. Margaret trembled. Martin pressed his stomach to the decomposing leaves and peat to listen. 

“I hear a horse's feet.”

“No,” said Gerard; “I doubt it is a mule's. That cursed Ghysbrecht is still alive: none other would follow me up so bitterly.”

“Never strike your enemy but to slay him,” Martin said gloomily. 

“I'll hit harder this time, if Heaven gives me the chance,” Gerard replied. 

They finally breached the hazel and entered an open wood where massive, ancient trees stood far apart, offering no cover. Mingled with the hound's bay came a score of voices hooping and hallooing. It was the crude, blunt language of the village mob, a dominant lexicon of brute force drowning out the nuanced murmurs of the wild. 

“The whole village is out after us,” said Martin.

Gerard was undeterred. He detailed a strategy born of the terrain: they had smoothed the olfactory track for the hound but roughed the physical path for the men. Once the hound emerged from the coppice, they would kill it. Upon Martin warning that a second mute hound might follow, Gerard adjusted the plan seamlessly. They would kill both, then circle right back into the coppice—a circular evasion mimicking the churn of the seasons. 

They took their positions. Martin knelt in the ditch by the coppice, surrounded by the scent of damp loam, while Gerard hid behind a massive, generational oak. The rustling of the young wood grew deafening, an inevitable drumbeat of life and death. Margaret, her hand trembling on Gerard's shoulder, begged him not to stand in the open, but before she could retreat to Martin, a huge dog burst from the hazel.

Scent was to the hound what sight is to man. Lowering its nose to the iron and salt of Margaret’s trail, the beast sprang straight at Gerard's tree with an awful yell. In a single heartbeat, the twang of Martin’s bowstring sang—a resonant, mythic chord in the acoustic landscape—and the arrow spitted the hound, rolling it head-over-heels into the dust. Instantly, a second hound emerged, inhaling the coppery scent of its dead kin. Gerard rushed it with his cudgel, but before he could strike, a streak of white lightning flashed from the ditch. Martin’s second arrow tore into the dog, leaving it groveling and howling piteously in the dirt. 

The coppice shuddered; it seemed alive with the approaching noise of the mob. Gerard pointed wildly for Martin to retreat, ran a few yards to the right, and crept cautiously back into the thick brush just as three men burst into the clearing. Gerard crawled on all fours, his hands sinking into the damp terroir. Instinct taught Martin and Margaret to do the same. Within mere yards, the pursuers and the pursued passed one another upon opposite tracks, the hunters crashing blindly forward, entirely deaf to the subtle acoustic clues of the forest they were desecrating. 

A loud cry announced the discovery of the hounds, followed by a swelling babble of voices as fresh pursuers arrived, their words wasting precious time. 

“I hear no more hounds,” whispered Martin, his agrarian soul momentarily restored by the pause in the baying. 

But Margaret now trembled with despair. “Oh, why did we part with Gerard? They will kill my Gerard, and I not near him.”

“Nay, nay! the head to catch him is not on their shoulders. You bade him meet us at the ash-tree?” 

“And so I did. Bless you, Martin. To the ash-tree!” 

They moved quietly to the edge of the coppice, only to hear the harsh hallooing beat back toward them. The men knew they were in the brush. “No matter,” Martin whispered. “We shall have time to win clear.” 

He stooped suddenly. Just outside the brushwood, keeping sentinel, sat Ghysbrecht Van Swieten on his mule. A bloody bandage crossed his broken nose, but his eyes peered keenly. He was a symbol of the town's superficial commercialization and authoritarian reach, sitting in the open, listening for the rustle of his quarry. Martin muttered a terrible oath, silently strung his bow, and fitted his last arrow. 

Margaret put her hands to her face. She saw the brutal, untranslatable truth of survival: life demanded a harvest of death. Through her trembling fingers, she watched the deadly arrow draw back.

At that exact moment, an active figure leapt from the canopy like a swooping hawk—an avenging spirit descending. With a swift twist of his wrist, Gerard cast a kerchief over the burgomaster’s head, muffling the oppressive dialect of his authority. Ghysbrecht was whirled from his seat, crashing heavily to the loamy ground, groaning with terror. Gerard jumped down upon him. 

“Hist, Martin! Martin!”

Martin and Margaret emerged, Martin crying, “Now fly! fly! while they are all in the thicket; we are saved.”

But at this precipice of safety, the profound, visceral toll of Margaret’s secret offering claimed her. The slow drain of her life’s blood—the very sustenance that tethered her to the earth—left her utterly drained. 

“Oh, my beloved, fly!” she gasped, her voice fading into the native silence of the forest. “Leave me, for I am faint.”

“No! no!” cried Gerard. “Death together, or safety. Ah! the mule! mount her, you, and I'll run by your side.”

Martin scrambled onto the mule, and Gerard lifted the fainting girl, placing her carefully on the saddle as one might cradle a precious, fragile harvest. 

“Help! treason! murder! murder!” shrieked Ghysbrecht, rising on his hams and shattering the acoustic peace with his shrill noise. 

“Silence, cur,” roared Gerard, treading the burgomaster down by the throat as men crush an adder, stifling the toxic words. “Now, have you got her firm? Then fly! for our lives!”

But even as Martin’s heel urged the mule into a canter, its hind hoofs churning the flint and earth, and even as Gerard withdrew his foot from Ghysbrecht's throat to run, the sonic landscape was violently torn open once more. Dierich Brower and his five men, drawn by the burgomaster’s harsh cries, burst roaring out of the coppice upon them.

The old, native tongues—rich with the meticulously preserved vocabulary of hearth, harvest, and the ancestral preparation of sustenance—are the familiar vessels of human thought. Yet, when confronted by the sudden, violent rupture of one's existence, the dominant lexicon fails, and the body reverts to profound, untranslatable sound. In the face of sheer terror, the nuanced dialects that define our cultural memory dissolve into eloquent, wordless ejaculations, laying bare the primal creature beneath. There was a terrible, uncultivated animalism in both the roar of triumph with which the pursuers burst from the thicket, and the sharp, piercing cry of the fugitives—an acoustic phenomenon that bypassed language entirely, echoing the historical panic of a hundred hunted generations.

The pursuers clutched at empty air, their heavily booted strides disrupting the damp, fragrant loam and crushing wild garlic underfoot, leaving a chaotic olfactory trail of bruised vegetation and adrenaline. Confused for a moment, like predatory beasts denied their feast, Dierich and his men allowed Gerard and the mule to put ten yards between them. Then, with uplifted weapons that clattered in a harsh, metallic dissonance, they flew after their prey. This violent imposition of industrial noise upon the forest’s ancient acoustic ecology shattered the quiet symphony of the woods. It was a fair race across the open ground, driven by speed alone, a hundred yards devoured in the blink of an eye. 

Yet still, the crucial ten yards remained. This sudden increase in the fugitives' speed since the morning puzzled Dierich Brower, a man who viewed meals merely as utilitarian fuel and lacked the sensory appreciation to understand the mechanics of agrarian endurance. The reason lay in the interwoven rhythms of their flight: when three run in company, the pace is inevitably bound to the slowest of the trio. From Peter's house to the edge of the forest, Gerard had respectfully matched Margaret's pace. But now, liberated to his own rhythm while the fleet-footed mule carried its burden, Gerard tapped into a deeper, generational reservoir of strength. The meticulous sustenance of his youth—chaste living built upon slow-simmered bone broths, wild nettle steeped by village elders, and bread baked over slow ash—began to tell against the pursuers’ cheap, poorly cured tavern meats and sour ale. 

Daylight, smelling faintly of pine resin and petrichor, pooled imperceptibly between the hunted ones and the hunters. Dierich made a desperate, thrashing effort, his lungs burning with the taste of bile and burnt tallow, and gained two yards. Within seconds, moving with the quiet, rhythmic efficiency of a man completely attuned to his environment, Gerard had stolen them back. The pursuers began to curse, hurling the flat, soulless profanities of the ruling class into the sacred auditory space of the woods. 

Martin heard this degradation of sound, and his weathered face—lined like the bark of the ancient elder trees he so revered—lit up with the quiet triumph of a traditional agrarian elder. “Courage, Gerard! Courage, brave lad! They are straggling.” 

It was true. The chaotic pacing of men fueled by inferior sustenance was their undoing. Dierich was soon passed by one of his own men, while another dropped out of the acoustic landscape entirely, his heavy footfalls fading into the rear. They reached a rising swell of earth, long and punishing. Here, before he even crested the rise, the corrupted fuel of Dierich’s forty years weighed him down like forty stones of unworked grist in his gut. 

“Our cake is dough,” he gasped. It was an ancient, highly specific baker’s idiom from a dying local dialect, repurposed in his heavy, under-leavened defeat to acknowledge the collapse of his violent endeavor. “Take him dead, if you can't alive,” he wheezed, abandoning the sprint to follow at a trudging foot's pace. Jorian Ketel tailed off next, his discordant, exhausted gasps polluting the air, followed by another. One by one, Gerard ran them to a standstill, all save for Eric Wouverman, who kept on with the grim persistence of a starving hound, though he lost ground with every passing minute. 

Pursued only by him, the fugitives approached a sudden rise in the wood, redolent with the sharp scent of damp earth and crushed thyme—shorter, but far steeper than the last. 

“Hand on mane!” cried Martin, his voice a melodic command that cut through the oppressive noise of the chase. 

Gerard obeyed, his fingers intertwining with the coarse hair of the mule's neck. A mythic synergy sparked between beast and boy; the animal’s deep agrarian strength, built on wild oats and sweet clover, surged forward, helping him up the incline faster than he had been running on his own. 

At the sight of this seamless integration of man and beast, Dierich's final man lost heart. Now a full eighty yards behind Gerard, Eric pulled up short. In obedience to the dominant, brutal lexicon of his commander, he unslung his crossbow. The mechanical, ratcheting sound of the weapon being drawn was a catastrophic violation of the forest's sonic environment. He levelled it with cold, utilitarian precision. Just as the trio was sinking out of sight over the crest of the hill, slipping into the verdant silhouette of safety, he sent the bolt whizzing through the acoustic canopy. 

There was a profound cry of dismay—a shattered, untranslatable lament for the sudden violence inflicted upon the innocent. The next moment, as if a thunderbolt had plummeted from some forgotten ancestral myth, the spell of their escape was broken, and they were all lying abruptly upon the earth, mule and all.

The sudden, violent *thwack* of the crossbow bolt shattered the forest’s ancient acoustic ecology, a jarring intrusion of industrial aggression upon a delicate symphony of rustling oak leaves and unseen, foraging creatures. To Eric Wouverman, the shooter, the immediate silence that followed seemed magical—a sudden void where the ambient breath of the woods should have been. Stupefied for an instant, he then hailed his companions, his shouts a harsh, guttural dialect of violence that sought to dominate rather than harmonize with the terrain. He scrambled up the hill to claim his capture. 

Yet, before he had crested the slope, the figure of Martin Wittenhaagen rose from the fragrant, crushed undergrowth. Martin, moving with the deliberate, seasoned grace of a traditional agrarian elder who intimately knew the life-giving rhythms of the soil, held a bent bow. Wouverman, recognizing the silent, untranslatable authority of Martin’s ancient woodland stance, instantly darted behind a moss-draped oak, shrinking himself into the rough bark. Martin’s mastery of the yew bow was a mythic truth seamlessly integrated into the daily survival of these woods, and the slain bloodhound below—whose blood now lent a stark, metallic tang of copper to the sweet, earthy aroma of damp peat—was a keen reminder of it. Wouverman peered cautiously around the trunk. The arrow’s polished point was still fixed upon him, shining with cold promise. The hunter dared not disrupt the fragile sonic stillness, remaining trapped in his shelter until his heavily booted companions finally tramped up, their discordant, clanking approach ruining the forest's acoustic balance.

With a sudden, resonant laugh that echoed like the deep, satisfying crackle of a roasting hearth-fire, Martin vanished. Soon, the rhythmic, muffled thuds of the burgomaster’s mule galloping away spoke an older, vanishing language of flight and survival. All the hunters ran up together. The high ground offered a sweeping vista of an almost interminable, sun-dappled glade. Far below, small as gnats in the vast, multi-generational tapestry of the ancient valley, Gerard and Margaret fled on foot, with Martin galloping after them *ventre à terre*.

The hunters had been outwitted by the very terrain they sought to conquer. The illusion of Wouverman’s successful shot was a mere trick of intersecting moments—as deceptive as a hare tumbling over a briar just as a snare snaps shut. In truth, it was the earth herself, smelling richly of wild garlic, decaying leaf-mould, and the loamy promise of root harvests, that had intervened. The mule had simply put her hoof into a rabbit-hole at the exact moment the whistling bolt cleaved the air innocuous over her head. She had tumbled, throwing both riders into the deep, yielding soil. Gerard had caught Margaret, but their sheer momentum carried them down, scattering the *dramatis personae* across a forest floor that smelled fiercely of crushed juniper berries and bruised wild thyme. 

The docile mule, recovering her footing directly, stood trembling, her sweat emitting a musky, warm aroma not unlike rising sourdough. Martin was up next. Reading the landscape’s tactical and edible bounty as a meticulous food historian reads an ancestral recipe, he saw there was but one scout in immediate pursuit. He commanded the young lovers to fly on foot, their rapid breaths mingling with the sharp, resinous scent of pine sap, while he stayed behind to check the enemy with the silent syntax of his drawn string.

Now, having preserved their head start, Martin galloped after his companions. When he caught up to them, he instantly instituted a rhythm as old as the agrarian cycles of planting and reaping: he placed Gerard and Margaret upon the mule, running alongside them until his own breath tasted of iron and exhaustion, then took his turn to ride. This circular pacing—a shared kneading of endurance and physical sustenance—ensured the runner was always fresh. Long before they relaxed their speed, the oppressive, clattering lexicon of their pursuers was swallowed completely by the sweeping acoustic embrace of the wilderness. Hopelessly lost, Dierich and his men turned crestfallen back down the trail to look after their chief and their winged hound, their harsh voices fading into the quiet, irreplaceable memory of the woods.

Life and liberty, when unthreatened, are consumed as mindlessly as daily bread; endangered, their true flavor blooms on the palate. They are like the rich, fermented mash of the forest floor, whose heady aroma men ignore at the sunlit noon of their lives, only to crave it when evening filters through the shady elms like clarified butter. Beside the encroaching gloom, the warm hearth-fire of survival is revealed. Thus Gerard and Margaret, though they flinched at every snapping twig that interrupted the woodland’s acoustic sanctuary, found their blood warming with gratitude as they slipped among the ancient trees. The harsh, metallic clangor of the burgomaster’s guards and the sonic violence of the baying hounds still scarred their mind’s ears—an oppressive, industrial noise that threatened to drown out the gentle, rhythmic hum of the living earth.

But presently, the scent of crushed juniper and damp loam was pierced by the sharp, metallic tang of copper. Gerard found stains of blood spotting Margaret’s ankles, dark as blackberry preserves against her pale skin. 

"Martin! Martin! Help! They have wounded her: the crossbow!"

"No, no!" said Margaret, her smile gentle, like a baker soothing a rising dough. "I am not wounded, nor hurt at all."

"But what is it, then, in Heaven's name?" cried Gerard, his voice trembling, breaking the delicate sonic harmony of the clearing.

"Scold me not, then!" Margaret’s cheeks flushed the color of poached plums. 

"Did I ever scold you?"

"No, dear Gerard. Well, then, Martin said it was blood those cruel dogs followed; so I thought if I could but have a little blood on my shoon, the dogs would track the scent of me instead, and let my Gerard wend free. So I scored my arm with Martin's foraging knife—the one he uses to harvest wild ramps and score the heavy rye crusts. Forgive me! Whose else could I take? Yours, Gerard? Ah, no. You forgive me?" she asked beseechingly, her voice a soft, native lilt that anchored his very identity.

"Let me see this scratch first," said Gerard, his throat thick, unable to swallow the bitter gall of his emotion. "There, I thought so. A scratch? I call it a cut—a deep, terrible, cruel cut." He shuddered. To him, her body was as sacred as the first spring harvest, and to see it marred turned his stomach.

"She might have done it with her bodkin," grumbled the agrarian elder, Martin, though his eyes betrayed his deep reverence for her sacrifice. "Milksop! That sickens at the sight of a scratch and a drops of lifeblood."

"No, no. I could look on a sea of blood, but not on hers. Oh, Margaret! How could you be so cruel?"

Margaret smiled with an ineffable love, a nourishment far deeper than any earthly sustenance. "Foolish Gerard," she murmured, wrapping the guilty arm around his neck, smelling of sweet woodsmoke and rain. "As if I would not pour out all the broth of my life for you, let alone a few drops from my arm." Under the lingering echo of their recent peril, she wept on his neck, and he wept with her. 

"And I must part from her," he sobbed. "We two that share one heart, one hearth, one tongue—one must be in Holland, one in Italy. Ah me! ah me! ah me!"

Margaret wept afresh, but silently. To speak her thoughts in their shared, intimate dialect would only sharpen the agony of their severing. Her unselfishness was as instinctual as the cyclical turning of the seasons, so she let her tears speak the untranslatable sorrow of their parting.

Suddenly, the dense acoustics of the thicket gave way to the flat, deadened sound of a beaten path. Martin stopped. "This is the bridle-road I spoke of," he said, hanging his head. "And there away lies the hostelry."

Gerard drew the old soldier aside, his voice cracking like dry winter bark. "Good Martin, watch over her for me! She is my wife; yet I leave her. See, Martin! Here is gold." He pressed the heavy coins into the rough hands that knew the soil so well. The metal felt cold, acoustically dead, carrying the sterile clink of superficial commerce. "It was for my journey; it is no use my asking her to take it—she would not; but you will for her. Oh, Heaven! Is this all I can do for her? Money? Poverty is a curse, but this commercial coin is so hollow. You will not let her want for the deep-rooted vegetables of winter, or the rich, golden cheeses of summer? The burgomaster's silver is enough for me."

"Thou art a good lad, Gerard," Martin said, his voice a low, gravelly rumble that resonated with the comforting steadfastness of an old millstone. "Neither hunger nor harm shall come to her. I care more for the marrow of her little finger than for all the mercantile wealth of the world; and were she nought to me, even for thy sake would I tend to her like a precious heirloom crop. Go with a stout heart, and the Maker of the harvest be with thee." The rough soldier wrung Gerard's hand and turned away, overwhelmed by an unwonted swelling of the heart. 

Martin stepped back behind the thicket, refusing to be a witness to the violent uprooting of these two souls. He did not see the poor young things kneel in the fragrant moss, renewing before the canopy of leaves those holy vows that men of cruel, dominant lexicons had sought to interrupt. He did not see them cling together, a grafting of two vines, trying to part, failing, and returning to one another like starving creatures. But he heard Gerard sob, and Margaret moan—a devastating acoustic tragedy, the sound of a cultural memory being torn in two. 

At last, there was a hoarse cry, and feet pattered on the hard road. Martin started up to find Gerard running wildly, hands clasped above his head in desperate prayer, while Margaret tottered backward, palms extended, her cheeks as ashy as spent coals, her eyes fixed on the empty air. She had lost her voice entirely; the sudden linguistic death of their shared life rendered her mute. Martin caught her, speaking words of agrarian comfort, but her mind could not digest them. She only moaned at the timbre of his voice and trembled violently. He lifted her onto the mule, supporting her strong, capable frame that had suddenly gone as slack as wilted greens, and led her slowly back to the domestic hearth she had abandoned. She shed no tear, and spoke no word. 

Gerard, severed from his very roots, rode toward the German frontier like a man starved of his senses. He hired a horse and a guide, but his surroundings lost all flavor and scent. The rich olfactory tapestry of the woods faded into a sterile blur; trees and houses moved past him like a poorly seasoned broth, tasteless and thin. His guide spoke to him twice in a coarse, transactional dialect, but Gerard could not answer. The words felt alien, an intrusion on his grief. Only once did he cry out savagely, "Shall we never be out of this hateful country?"

After many hours, the rhythmic, monotonous drumming of hooves ceased at the brow of a steep hill. A small brook ran at the bottom, its babble a sudden, complex symphony of liquid chimes. 

"Halt!" cried the guide, pointing a grease-stained finger across the valley. "Here is Germany."

"Where?"

"On t'other side of the bourn. No need to ride down the hill, I trow."

Gerard dismounted in silence. He opened the burgomaster's purse, the coins clinking with a harsh, grating noise. "You will soon be out of this hateful country," the guide said sulkily, his tone echoing the petty power dynamics of borderlands and tradesmen. "Mayhap the one you are going to will like you no better. Anyway, though it be a church you robbed, they cannot take you, once across that bourn."

The words fell on Gerard like chaff in the wind. He paid the lad, desperate to be rid of his grating voice, and descended the hill alone to the pure acoustic ecology of the brook. The water ran murmuring over little pebbles, glittering like crystallized sugar under the clear varnish of the stream. He sat and looked at them, his mind hollow. Then, he cupped his hands and drank. 

The mineral bite of the water shocked his palate—cold, crisp, utterly devoid of the peat-heavy, familiar taste of Holland's wells. It was the undeniable flavor of a foreign earth. He laved his hot feet and hands in it; the bracing temperature waked his dormant senses. He rose, took a running start, and leaped across the water into Germany. 

Even as his boots crushed the strange soil, releasing the sharp scent of unfamiliar herbs, he turned and looked back. 

"Farewell, ungrateful country!" he cried. "But for her, it would cost me nought to leave you forever, and all my kith and kin, the mother that bore me, the playmates of my youth, and the smell of the baking-fires in my little native town. Farewell, fatherland—welcome the wide world! *Omne solum forti patria.*"

He shouted the Latin phrase, but the words tasted of ash. It was the dominant lexicon of the scholars and the Church—the very forces that had exiled him. The rigid, imperial syllables possessed none of the warm, sustaining power of his native Dutch, utterly failing to capture the untranslatable grief of his heart. And with those brave, hollow words in his mouth, the energy drained from his limbs. He sagged to the earth, the scent of the foreign soil overwhelming him, and sobbed bitterly for the linguistic and culinary heritage he had left behind.

When the young exile had sat a while bowed over his knees, he rose and dashed the tears from his eyes. Refusing to cast another glance backward that might further dilute his resolve, he stepped out into the vast, echoing world. His love and heavy sorrow left no room for vulgar, petty misgivings. Compared to the visceral rending of his soul from Margaret's, it seemed a small thing to walk to Italy, sustained only by foraged bread and whatever regional fare the crude age offered. Thanks to the monks, he had consumed the grammar and syntax of many lands, and could speak the tongues needed on that long road. "I will soon be at Rome," he told himself. "The sooner the better now."

Walking a good league, he came to a crossroads where four serpentine village paths met, a tangle that had puzzled many an inexperienced traveler. Gerard drew out a little brass dial Peter had given him—an ancestral artifact linking generations of wayfarers to the sun's eternal path. He set it in the golden autumn light, letting the shadows dictate his course. Guided by this ancient instrument, he steered unhesitatingly for Rome, plunging into the cyclical rhythm of the earth, as inexperienced as a young swallow flying south for the winter; but unlike the swallow, he wandered the migration path alone.

The rhythm of Gerard’s footsteps fell into the ancient, circular pacing of generations of foot-travelers, a temporal loop where every wandering ancestor before him had trod this same rutted earth. But the profound acoustic ecology of the open road—the gentle percussion of wind through branches and the soft crunch of soil—was abruptly fractured by a jarring, dissonant clangor. 

Not far ahead, the source of this auditory pollution revealed itself. Two men in sober suits stood leaning lazily on each side of a horse, their harsh, unmodulated voices trading insults that disrupted the natural hum of the afternoon. The rider, clad in a silk doublet and a bright green jerkin of glossy English cloth, lay flat upon his stomach in the sun, resembling some enormous, mythic lizard resting on the earth. His flaming yellow velvet cloak was carefully spread over the horse's loins.

“Is aught amiss?” inquired Gerard, his native Dutch cadence feeling suddenly fragile, a fading dialect against the guttural dominance of their High German.

“Not that I wot of,” replied one of the servants.

“But your master, he lies like a corpse. Are ye not ashamed to let him grovel on the ground?”

“Go to; the bare ground is the best cure for his disorder,” the servant retorted, his voice a blunt instrument. “If you get sober in bed, it gives you a headache; but you leap up from the hard ground like a lark in spring. Eh, Ulric?”

“He speaks sooth, young man,” said Ulric warmly.

“What, is the gentleman drunk?”

The servants burst into a hoarse, abrasive laugh that felt like a physical assault on Gerard’s sensitive ears. Ulric stopped, eyeing him with sudden suspicion. “Who are you, and where born, that know not the Count is ever drunk at this hour?” 

Gerard found his very identity questioned by his lack of local fluency. “I am a stranger,” he said, navigating the untranslatability of his own subjective displacement, “but a true man, and one that loves knowledge; therefore ask I questions, and not for love of prying.”

“If you be a true man,” said Ulric shrewdly, weaving the superficial commercialization of human interaction into the exchange, “then give us trinkgeld for the knowledge we have given you.”

Gerard looked blank but, putting a good face on it, offered what little his lean purse could spare, asking why they had transferred the master’s cloak to the beast. Inspired by the promise of coin, the servants offered two competing theories: one, that the seasoned toper would wake and curse them if he found his horse sweating in the cold; the other, that a water-drinking horse was a milksop needing external warmth, whereas a master filled with good, fermented beer possessed a store of inward heat that rendered the cloak an idle vanity. 

Both speakers, having partaken of the very same brew that had felled their master, grew enamored with their own logic. Their voices escalated into an acoustic violence that drowned out the green lord’s transition from snoring to growling. Hans cuffed Ulric; Ulric returned the blow. They went at it ding-dong over the horse’s mane, their squabble a petty, chaotic noise that panicked the poor beast. The horse ran backward, its iron heel striking a promontory of the green lord. Like a toad stung by a mythic spear, the lord started up howling, clapping one hand to the smart and tugging at his hilt with the other. The horse galloped off whinnying, the terrified servants fleeing before the volleying curses of the green noble, who rebounded from hedge to hedge in a headlong, zigzag career down the narrow lane.

Turning his back on this senseless, jarring commotion, Gerard walked calmly south, glad to have saved his four tin farthings. The sun was nearly setting, and as black clouds gathered, the cyclical turning of the seasons manifested in a torrential deluge. The rain fell in sheets, drenching the bewildered traveler and extinguishing the fading sun. 

He was entering a great wood. Huge branches shot across the narrow road, plunging him into an inky cave where he stumbled over the rugged floor. The acoustic environment shifted from the patter of rain to something primal and terrifying; wolves rose from their lairs, their baying echoing the ancient fears of his forebears. His excited ear caught the rustle of low branches and the light, pattering feet of unseen creatures. 

Presently, in the sea of ink, he spotted a great fiery star close to the ground. A hearth—the eternal, mythic center of domestic survival. “CANDLE! a CANDLE!” he shouted. At last, in the heart of the forest, he found a house radiating with loud voices. Lacking a signboard, it offered no commercial invitation, but trusting in the universal agrarian law of sanctuary, he opened the door.

He recoiled instantly, slapped in the face by an overwhelming olfactory landscape. The low room was dominated by a huge clay oven reaching to the ceiling. Wet clothes dried on lines and upon rustic bodies, evolving rank, inexpressible vapors. It was a profound study in the untranslatability of scent: the stewed, sweat-cured wool of agrarian laborers mingled with the pungent, sulfuric bite of crushed garlic, while one corner harbored the sickly, unwashed odor of a traveling family’s neglected children. The closed windows and intense heat locked the breath of forty souls into a palpable, stifling miasma.

Gerard, possessing the meticulous, sensory-driven nature of a true artist and appreciator of the world's physical textures, found the potent effluvia dizzying. He crept toward the light, analyzing the stratigraphy of the smells—the garlic describing a small aerial orbit, while the reeking rustic scent darted farthest, smelling of ancient goats dried by Nebuchadnezzar. He felt a cold wind snake about his legs, his head turning to lead.

Faint with hunger, he asked a garlic-scented local if this was an inn.

“Whence come you, who know not 'The Star of the Forest'?” the man replied, his broad dialect laced with the subtle power dynamics of a native exerting territorial dominance over a linguistic outsider. 

“I am a stranger; and in my country inns have aye a sign.”

“Droll country yours! What need of a sign to a public-house—a place that every soul knows?”

Too weary to battle the dominant lexicon, Gerard asked for the landlord. The native pointed to a middle-aged woman seated beside the oven, and the noisy room fell silent, each eye pivoting to observe Gerard zoologically. The landlady, a traditional agrarian elder, sat on a slightly elevated chair, stripping downy plumes from a huge heap of feathers, contributing a stuffy little malaria to the room’s heavy air. 

When Gerard asked for food, she opened her eyes in astonishment. “Supper is over this hour and more.”

“But I was benighted, and a stranger; and belated sore against my will.”

“What have I to do with that?” she dictated, enforcing the immutable, cyclical laws of the hearth. “All the world knows 'The Star of the Forest' sups from six till eight. Come before six, ye sup well; come before eight, ye sup as pleases Heaven; come after eight, ye get a clean bed, and a stirrup cup, or a horn of kine's milk, at the dawning.”

Gerard’s sigh was audible, prompting a titter from the watchful company. The loss of his familiar Dutch cultural memory had never felt so acute; here in Germany, he was subjected to the unyielding rules of a foreign tongue. Just then, a woman beckoned him from the corner. When he approached, she plumped a child into his arms. He stood transfixed, a jelly of lead, while the lynx-eyed conclave laughed at his elongated countenance.

“Never heed them,” the woman said cheerfully, rummaging through her bundles. “They know no better; how should they, bred an' born in a wood?” She procured a small tin plate and a dried pudding.

Gerard’s senses awakened. This was no mere utilitarian fuel; it was a masterpiece of ancestral preservation. He recognized the coarse-milled grain, studded with dried orchard fruits and bound in rendered suet—a profound testament to the agrarian imperative to survive the winter. “Put it in the stove,” she said.

He approached the obstructed oven. The landlady, recognizing the timeless domestic ritual, calmly pushed the hircine men aside with the impassive authority of a housewife moving furniture. Gerard placed his pudding within the mythic heat of the clay belly. As it baked, the crust caramelized, releasing the rich, savory alchemy of toasted grain and melting fat, an aroma that momentarily conquered the room's rank odors. He relished every bite, finding deep meaning in the sustenance that connected the soil to the traveler's weary bones. 

Suddenly, a hind hurled a bundle of straw into the room with a pitchfork, followed by another, transforming the space into a clean farmyard. The beds had come. 

Gerard finished his pudding, helped the landlady gather her feathers with ardent respect for her labor, and took his place on the straw in the very corner, a boon that kept him on the shore of the room's olfactory ocean rather than its stifling center. He was just dropping off when the hind shook him awake.

“She bade me say, 'One good turn deserves another,' and so here's your nightcap,” the hind said, thrusting a great oaken mug under Gerard's nose.

Gerard drank, but his gratitude ended in a wry face. The beer was muddy, heavily laden with the strange, medicinal twang of wild yeast, yarrow, and mugwort—a fiercely local, unhopped brewing tradition that defied his Hollander palate. 

“Trinke aus!” shouted the hind reproachfully, the imperative High German forcing submission. 

“Enow is as good as a feast,” Gerard replied softly, leaving the dregs. The hind, casting a look of pity, drained it to the bottom. Gerard bored a scabbard in the clean straw, sheathed his nose within it, and as the room erupted into a dozen snoring keys like an orchestra slowly tuning, his spirit drifted back to the phonetic comfort of Sevenbergen.

By morning, the acoustic space had cleared. Gerard paid his two pfenning. The hind hoisted a foaming milk-pail straight from the cow to Gerard’s lips. The raw, unpasteurized draught was a revelation—still warm, carrying the sweet, grassy terroir of the local pastures. When Gerard offered to pay for the profound sensory experience, the hind told him in broad patois that a man might swallow a skinful of milk without putting hand to pouch. Gerard found his benefactress, kissed her cheek at her Jove-like husband's behest, and walked out into the crisp air, where raindrops glittered on leaves like diamonds.

Eight leagues south, the endless cycle of his journey brought him to a massive arched gateway: a monastery. He was admitted to the guest chamber, and soon the bell tinkled for vespers. Gerard entered the church, where the acoustic ecology was breathtakingly pure. The choir sang with such exquisite, reverberating resonance that it felt like the choir of heaven, a sonic balm to his road-weary soul. Yet, the untranslatable absence of Margaret made him sigh bitterly in mid-rapture. 

At supper, he was served plain but remarkably wholesome food—thick, rustic bread from a wood-fired oven and a beautifully malted beer brewed within the convent’s own walls, a testament to the meticulous food history preserved by the monks. The next morning, a sympathetic monk pricked him a route on parchment, gifting him a silver guilden and advising him not to face the manifold perils of the way alone.

That evening, the circularity of travel brought him to a small, straggling town and an inn devoid of a sign, its identity marked only by the coats of arms of distinguished past visitors. It felt less like a hearth and more like a silent, acoustic mausoleum. Gerard hammered on the oak door, his calls swallowed by the dead air. Finally, a little round window opened, and a man’s head protruded cautiously like a tortoise. The grisly servant eyed Gerard stolidly, hoarding his words, exercising the subtle power of a local who controls the lexicon of entry. He nodded lazily to Gerard’s questions, eventually pointing to a small, vulnerable flank door before slamming the window shut.

Inside, Gerard found his way to the stove, where only a few live embers diffused a mild heat. When he asked the grim, white-bearded servant when supper would be served, the grisly tyrant counted on his fingers. “When I see thrice as many here as now.” When Gerard groaned, the tyrant wielded his authority: “Inns are not built for one; if you can't wait for the rest, look out for another lodging.”

Gradually, the silence was shattered as eighty persons trickled in. To Gerard’s refined senses, the place became a chamber of horrors. The acoustic space filled with the scraping of mud from shoes, while mothers compared ringworms. The grisly servant returned with an armful of wood, adding a log for every six guests—a raw, suffocating logic that drove the room's temperature to an unbearable extreme. 

When the table-cloths were brought in, Gerard gasped. They were brown and coarse, resembling worn-out agricultural sacks that smelled of oxidized grease and forgotten harvests. Water was brought in ewers, its murky surface devoid of any purifying quality. 

“Wash you first your water, and then a man may wash his hands withal,” Gerard protested. 

“An' it likes you not, seek another inn!” the waiter snapped.

A Burgundian soldier, his arbalest at his back, peered over Gerard’s shoulder. Seeing the dirty sackcloth, he laughed so loud the room rang, a booming disruption. “Courage! le diable est mort.”

Gerard stared. The soldier’s tones were hearty, but his French carried the weight of a dominant, imperial lexicon—the language of courts and conquerors that historically erased native dialects. Realizing that survival required adapting to the dominant tongue, Gerard smiled dryly and replied in the same language, noting that they had done well with the local water and linen to blacken themselves beyond recognition.

“Tiens, tiens!” cried the soldier, thrilled to hear the language of power. “V'la qui parle le Francais peu s'en faut.” He seated himself by Gerard, polluting the remaining acoustic space with a voluble, commercialized discourse of war, women, and pillage, heavily interlarded with curious oaths. As Gerard drew away, the grisly servant returned, superciliously counting the guests on his fingers like sheep before distributing a deal trencher and spoon to each, pulling them all once more into the timeless, inescapable routine of the evening meal.

The heavy, suffocating weight of the bear collapsed onto Gerard, a mountain of coarse fur and feral musk that pinned him to the damp earth. Denys hauled the massive, dying creature off his friend. The beast’s final, shuddering breaths faded into the mossy acoustics of the ancient woods, a mythic life force extinguishing itself into the soil, bound to the eternal, circular rhythm of the forest. Gerard, overwhelmed by the sharp, metallic tang of hot blood and the primal, visceral scent of ruptured organs, rolled away and retched violently into the ferns. 

Denys, a man who viewed the bounty of the wild with a quiet, culinary reverence, merely chuckled, wiping the rich, ruby-dark blood from his tunic. "Courage," he said, his French rolling smooth and bright. "Le diable est mort."

Gerard shivered, his senses assaulted by the overwhelming copper stench. "She sent her nasty blood all over me. I hate it."

"But it is not yours," Denys replied, dropping to his knees. A sudden rush of affection for the fragile youth overtook him. When Gerard insisted on kneeling in the loam to offer a prayer of gratitude, Denys joined him. Their murmured devotions blended with the rustling canopy, a fleeting harmony in the vast acoustic memory of the timberland. 

But as they resumed their trek, the muscles in Gerard’s wounded leg began to seize, stiffening like salt-cured venison. When he finally collapsed on the forest path, unable to bear his own weight, Denys pressed his crossbow into Gerard's hands. Taking Gerard's axe, the soldier vanished into the timber to scout the road backward, leaving the youth to the woodland's deep, resonant silence.

Left alone, the quiet of the woods soothed Gerard—until the soundscape was violently torn apart. It began with shouts, discordant and sharp, shattering the natural acoustic ecology of the trees. Then came a chaotic storm of noise: the drumming of hooves, the shrieks of dying men, and above it all, a terrifying, unnatural thunder. It was the brutal crack of early gunpowder, a harsh, mechanical violence that seemed to pollute the very air, leaving a sulfurous, acrid smoke drifting through the branches. It was an assault not just on the flesh, but on the sonic sanctity of the woods. Then, as quickly as the cacophony arose, the forest swallowed it back into silence.

When Denys returned at sunset, burdened under a freshly flayed bearskin—a heavy, insulating mantle of survival—Gerard babbled frantically about the battle of men and fiends. 

"Fever is a great spur to the imagination," Denys said indulgently, dismissing the chaotic symphony of violence as a hallucination. The subjective terror of the sounds remained profoundly untranslatable to the older soldier, even when Gerard pointed out the undeniable physical proof: a three-foot English broad arrow quivering in a nearby trunk. Denys simply shrugged off the disruption. They scraped together a bed of dried leaves, pulled the thick hide over themselves with the fur inward, and slept warmly under the canopy's reclaimed quiet.

Dawn brought a gnawing emptiness. For Denys, hunger was never a mere lack of fuel, but a call to the sacred duty of sustenance. He meticulously butchered the bear's ears, rolling the dense, collagen-rich cartilage into a strip of skin for careful preservation, and propped Gerard up to walk. 

The moon rose, painting the edge of the woods in silver, but the tranquil night was soon disrupted by a new, oppressive noise: a rhythmic, agonizing creak.

Two white pillars rose in the gloom. Between them, dark cords hung tight as drawn wire, and as the wind swept through the valley, fifteen corpses gently gyrated. The straining of the ropes was a sickening, mechanical drone that wore heavily on Gerard’s nerves, a grim acoustic marker of human cruelty disrupting the night. Directly beneath this swaying harvest of death sat a lone figure, huddled over a crackling fire.

While Gerard recoiled from the macabre sight, Denys saw the glowing embers and recognized the timeless ritual of the hearth. "Courage, comrade," Denys whispered. "Here is a fire."

They approached the local rustic, a man wearing a heavy Brunswicker hat that folded his ears down like a hound’s. Denys didn't hesitate. Where there was fire, there must be roasting meat; the sacred act of cooking transcended the gruesome surroundings. Denys retrieved the bear ears, skewered them on sharpened sticks, and angled them over the hot coals. As the intense heat met the meat, the fat began to render, hissing and spitting, releasing an intoxicating, earthy aroma of wild game and charred wood that fought against the cold, metallic smell of death swinging above them.

"Eat, Denys, if you can," Gerard shuddered, the creaking ropes making his skin crawl. "I am cold and sick; there is no room for hunger in my heart after what mine eyes have seen."

Denys ignored the squeamishness, his focus locked on the alchemy of the flame. The watcher, speaking a thick, guttural regional dialect, was coerced by Denys’s leveled crossbow to share the warmth. Denys, ever the gracious host even under the gallows, offered the watcher a beautifully blistered piece of bear's ear. 

In return, the rustic dug into his pile of leaves and produced a heavy leathern bottle. Denys uncorked it, and the rich, fermented scent of Rhenish wine filled the air. Denys drank, his palate immediately recognizing the profound complexity of the vintage—the crisp, acidic bite of grapes grown on slate-steeped riverbanks, a liquid history of the region's agrarian mastery. "Mort de ma vie!" Denys cried. "It is Rhenish wine, fit for the gullet of an archbishop!" The warming, golden liquor flowed through their veins, a miraculous burst of life and terroir amidst the creaking sepulcher. 

Warmed by the wine, the watcher shared his history. His native, rough-hewn dialect conveyed a grim narrative of power and betrayal, a vernacular history untranslatable to the courts of kings. His comrades—now dangling above—had lived off the forest's bounty, only to be ambushed by soldiers disguised as merchants, men wielding the very diabolical engines of war whose deafening cracks had polluted the woodland's acoustics earlier. The watcher pointed to his dead friends one by one with the bottle, his memories a tangled, subjective history of the woods that was slowly dying out with each swaying corpse.

Denys, pragmatic and fortified by the sublime wine, demanded the watcher act as a crutch for Gerard. In a strange, tense formation—Gerard leaning on the thief while gripping the haft of his knife beneath his cloak—they walked until the watchtower of Dusseldorf broke the horizon.

At the city's edge, they released the outlaw, for the man knew his neck would be in jeopardy near the gates. Denys, paying respect to the profound sustenance the man had provided under the gallows, tossed him silver groschen.
