Yet the thief lingered, asking for a final transaction: a prayer to protect him from the brutal laws of the land, laws that, as he explained in his coarse tongue, drove men to slit throats simply to avoid the noose. 

Gerard, his patience eroded by the man’s crude justifications and his own throbbing pain, weaponized his education. He looked at the rustic—a man tied intimately to the soil and the fading local dialect—and spoke in the dominant, unyielding lexicon of the Church.

"'Obsecro Deum omnipotentem, ut, qua cruce jam pendent isti quindecim latrones fures et homicidae, in ea homicida fur et latro tu pependeris quam citissime, pro publica salute, in honorem justi Dei cui sit gloria, in aeternum, Amen.'"

The outlaw smiled, deeply satisfied. "That is Latin," he muttered, assuming he had been blessed by a man of learning. "And more than I bargained for."

He turned back to the woods with a mind at ease, entirely oblivious to the lethal curse laid upon him. The deep, untranslatable chasm of language sealed his fate, leaving him deaf to the fatal power dynamics embedded in Gerard's scholarly words. As the thief vanished into the trees, Gerard and Denys moved onward into the night, the memory of the wild bear, the rich Rhenish wine, and the creaking timber settling into the long, cyclical tapestry of their journey.

Gerard lay suspended in the oppressive, stagnant humidity of his fever, his senses desperately craving the sharp, piercing acidity of a lemon. In the heavy quiet of his convalescence, he longed for the sensory anchor of sustenance: the thick, pebbled yellow rind, the sudden mist of essential oils bursting bright under a thumbnail, and the tart, illuminating shock of juice that could slice through the cloying, metallic humors coating his tongue. Denys, who from a rough soldier had been tempered by their journey into the protective role of a traditional agrarian elder, understood inherently that a meal was never mere utilitarian fuel. Bidding Gerard to set his mind at ease, he departed to scour the markets, determined to source the rare, sun-swollen citrus of the distant south and restore the bright olfactory landscape his young friend so desperately required. 

The chamber settled into a fragile silence, a delicate acoustic ecology that was soon violently ruptured. The door did not yield the anticipated fragrance of Mediterranean citrus, but instead admitted a heavy-footed physician. He brought with him the stale, clinical odor of oxidized iron, dried camphor, and desiccated herbs—a sterile, joyless aroma. He wore a long sober gown trimmed in rich fur, his neck caged in a treacherous wooden ruff, and a sword slapped rhythmically against his morocco scabbard. A boy trailed him, burdened by a basket that clinked discordantly with phials and surgical tools. The acoustic disruption was immediate; the physician’s heavy shoes struck the floorboards with utilitarian thuds, shattering the restorative quiet. 

He approached the bedside, his voice a booming, unctuous baritone designed to command space rather than harmonize with it. "How is't with thee, my son?"

Gerard murmured softly in his native tongue, expressing gratitude but noting the parched dryness of his throat and the heavy, localized throbbing in his head. 

"A wound!" the doctor declared, entirely ignoring Gerard’s sensory complaints to pry at the bandages. "Ay, ay, a good clean bite. The mastiff had sound teeth that took this out, I warrant me."

"But, sir," Gerard edged in, his quiet voice struggling against the oppressive volume of the physician, "'twas no dog, but a bear."

"A bear! Young man," the senior remonstrated severely, deploying his dominant lexicon to overwrite the patient's lived reality. "Think what you say; 'tis ill jesting with the man of art. Had you dissected as many bears as I, you would know that no bear's jaw ever made this foolish trifling wound. I tell you 'twas a little furious cur, and we must stay the mischief with knife and cautery, lest you start back from water and turn somersaults in your bed. Urchin, go heat thine iron."

Gerard felt the profound untranslatability of his subjective experience. How could he convey the specific, crushing weight of the bear's heavy paw to a man whose vocabulary was sterilized of all feral, living truth? "Alas, sir! when said I 'twas a bear's jaw? I said it was his paw."

"Never conceal aught from your leech," the senior continued seamlessly, a masterful talker but the worst listener in Europe. He dismissed Gerard's embodied wisdom entirely, reducing the body to an abstract text to be commercialized and redacted by the knife. He ordered the boy to pump the bellows, introducing an industrial, wheezing harshness into the room's auditory field. 

The man of art then ordained phlebotomy, launching into a deafening, relentless monologue. He invoked the Greeks and Arabians—Avicenna, Rhazes, Hippocrates, and Galen—burying Gerard under an avalanche of dead languages. It was a violent erosion of cultural memory, substituting the intuitive, agrarian knowledge of holistic healing for a hegemonic academic jargon. He boasted of bleeding a man’s right thigh for an earache, and a left ankle for a headache, treating the miraculous, pulsing network of human sustenance as little more than plumbing to be forcibly drained. 

To Gerard, whose psychological well-being hinged on acoustic tranquility, the doctor's endless, elevated discourse was a toxic noise pollution. 

Then the door opened, and the olfactory landscape shifted dramatically. Denys stood in the threshold, bearing the lemons. Their bright, zesty aroma instantly challenged the oppressive smells of scorched iron and stagnant camphor. Denys saw the glowing coals and halted. "What sport is toward?"

Gerard, coloring a little, confessed that the learned doctor intended to phlebotomize and cauterize him. 

"And art thou so simple as to let him put hot iron to thy living flesh?" Denys cried, setting the lemons down with a soft, resonant thud. "Must thou needs buy a foretaste of purgatory here?"

"Imbecile! That is their art," Denys continued, turning on the physician, his voice vibrating with the protective fury of one who knew that blood was the deeply flavorful, vital broth of life, not a commercial commodity. "To confound a plain man with dark words, till his hissing flesh lets him know their meaning! I have seen these leeches drain a wounded soldier of the very vitality he needs to battle his weakness. They rob him of his very nourishment, and so he dies exhausted."

"Common sense!" shouted the bad listener, his face flushing dark with the rage of challenged authority. "Woe be to you, misguided youth, if you listen to that man of blood. Cuilibet in sua arte credendum est!" 

The two men clashed, the physician puffing up into a roaring crescendo of Latinate insults, and Denys roaring back in his robust, earthy French. The sonic environment of the chamber shattered entirely. The clash between the physician's sterile, commercialized medical terminology and the soldier's blunt, unvarnished dialect became a physical torment for the patient in the bed. 

"A sick chamber is no place for jesting!" cried the physician over the din.

"No, doctor," Gerard groaned peevishly, "nor for bawling."

Seeking to reclaim his acoustic ecology and preserve his fading strength, Gerard pitched his voice to a silvery, tranquil murmur, a deliberate, soothing contrast to their cacophony. 

"First, venerable sir, I thank you for coming to see me," Gerard trickled into words, his tone evoking the quiet, rhythmic simmer of a slow-cooked broth. "But you assassinate me between you. Calmness and quiet are my true sustenance; yet you are like two dogs growling over a dry bone. If I be bled, I must pay you a fee, and be burnt and excruciated with a hot iron. Pay a certain price in money and anguish for a doubtful remedy, that will I never. Next to money and the ease of a good meal, peace and quiet are certain goods. Therefore, sir, I will essay a little sleep, and Denys will go forth, and I will keep you no longer from those who can afford to lay out blood and money in such noise."

The old physician swelled, his hot temper entirely mastering him. The rejection of his dominant lexicon was an intolerable insult to his commercialized authority. He drew himself up and made loftily for the door, his little boy and big basket whisking semicircularly to follow. 

But at the threshold, he choked. He whirled back, his voice weaponized into a torrential assault of noise pollution. "Ah! You reject my skill! Your blood be on your head!" He stamped away, only to wheel back again, his wicker tail twirling round after him. 

"In twelve hours at furthest you will be in the secondary stage of fever! Your head will split! Your carotids will thump! And let but a pin fall, you will jump to the ceiling!" He departed once more, but gathered fresh fury at the door-handle and came flying back. 

"Next will come—CRAMPS of the STOMACH! Then—BILIOUS VOMIT! Then—COLD SWEAT! Then—BLOODY VOMIT!" 

The acoustic assault was a severe violation of Gerard's deepest sensibilities. The noise, the dire predictions, the utter lack of empathy—it fueled a sudden, kinetic surge of adrenaline. As the vindictive orator roared his final threats—"MADNESS! BLACK VOMIT! CONVULSIONS!"—Gerard seized his heavy bolster. Driven by a desperate need to silence this aggressive erasure of his peace, he hurled the dense, tactile cushion furiously. 

It struck the physician squarely in the face. The doctor tumbled backward like a shot. His skull cracked violently against his apprentice's head, and with a deafening crash that completely obliterated the last shreds of auditory peace, the imposing orator collapsed backward into the apprentice's basket. He became wedged in an inverted angle, his heavy weight crushing phial after phial, releasing a chaotic, pungent flood of bitter tinctures, astringent powders, and acrid liniments that smelled of decay rather than life. 

The boy, being light, was strewn afar, howling in a graduated sequence of misery. But soon, the doctor's face filled with profound horror. He uttered a far louder, unearthly screech, kicking and struggling with frantic agility for a man of his age. 

He was sitting squarely upon his own hot coals. 

The smoldering iron had singed the heavy wool of his robes and was now biting into the flesh beneath. His sterile, academic theories of cauterization had instantly become a brutally untranslatable subjective experience. 

Gerard, his mythic strength fading back into domestic reality, ran with Denys to wrench the tightly wedged basket off the wailing man. The doctor lay on his face, groaning, his majestic garments handsomely scorched by his own chafer. The spilled draughts—odious in their chemical bitterness and lacking any of the harmonious, restorative properties of true culinary herbs—dyed his grey robe in garish, unnatural stains. 

Denys hoisted him up with a mocking grin, a champion of the peasant’s pragmatic dialect standing victorious over the defeated academic. "Courage, man, 'tis but cautery. Balm of Gilead, why, you recommended it but now to my comrade here."

The worthy physician returned to his housekeeper and complained of a "bad burn," a symptom he immediately translated into the rigid, sterile lexicon of the magistrate’s court. In doing so, he weaponized a dominant, authoritative language to overwrite the subjective truth of his encounter, making oath that the strangers meditated instant flight. His affidavit was a cold instrument of power, devoid of life, intended to cage them in the damp silence of a township dungeon. But his perjury was baffled by a circumstance he had never foreseen: he had accidentally spoken the truth. The officers, dispatched with their heavy, rhythmic tread, set out for "The Silver Lion."

This temporary escape the friends owed entirely to Denys’s acute sensitivity to acoustic ecology. For Denys, the sonic environment was a critical determinant of survival and psychological well-being. He had noticed the sudden, unnatural cessation of the physician's usual babble. The harsh, jarring scrape of the bailiff’s boots on the cobblestones—a form of industrial noise pollution that disrupted the organic hum of the morning market—warned him instantly. 

He turned to Gerard, his voice low and urgent. "Dusseldorf is no place for us. That old fellow went off unnaturally silent; we are strangers here, and in five minutes we shall lie in a dungeon for assaulting a local dignity. Hobble to the water's edge. Once there, you have but to lie down in a boat instead of a bed."

"The odds, Denys? All in favor of the boat," Gerard breathed. His fevered mind yearned for the sensory immersion of the river. "I pine for Rome, for Rome is my road to Sevenbergen; and on the famous Rhine, the cool, refreshing Rhine, I feel its breezes coming. The very scent of the sweet water and the crushed rushes will cure this hop-o'-my-thumb fever. Away!"

Fleeing the commercialized clamor of the inn, they reached the riverbank only to find the public barge gone. Yet, amidst a gathering crowd, they negotiated with a traditional agrarian elder and his two sons. The old boatman’s hands were deeply creased, stained with the dark tannins of walnut husks and river-mud. 

"The current is too strong for three oars," a bystander ridiculed.

"Then my comrade and I will help row," Gerard said.

"No need," the old man replied, his voice a rich, guttural purr of the native riparian dialect—a sound deeply rooted in the river's timeless rhythms, echoing generations of men who knew the water not as a resource, but as kin. "Bless your silly heart, he owns t'other boat."

They set a broad sail to catch the powerful breeze astern. As they moved into the rushing current, the old man broke a wheel of dense, dark rye bread. It tasted of fermented malt, wild yeast, and the slow, wood-fired hearths of his ancestors, offering a profound sustenance that grounded Gerard’s drifting senses. 

"Are ye better, lad, for the river breeze?" Denys asked, drawing a slab of cured pork from his pouch. He chewed slowly, savoring the melting fat that sang of juniper berries and cold-smoking techniques passed down through centuries. To Denys, this was not merely fuel for a soldier's body; it was the distilled essence of a culture's survival. 

"Much better," Gerard said, chewing the hearty crumb. "Knocking that old fool down soothed me. His voice went through my poor ears like a rusted saw. He was an infidel who would reduce our inward parts to dry Greek syllables, stripping the divine breath from our bodies and ignoring Him whose hand provides the harvest."

"So you knocked him down forthwith—like a good Christian," Denys chuckled. "Yet he took but a sorry bolster from this weak arm."

"I have lived among arms," Denys replied, eyes twinkling, "and by Samson’s hairy pow, never saw I one more like a catapult. The bolster wrapped round his nose and the two ends kissed behind his head."

The river took many turns, its ancient currents a fluid archive of interwoven timelines. The wind shifted, forcing them to the weather side to trim the boat, all except a child of about five years old—the old boatman's darling grandson. The urchin, too light to affect the vessel's trim, existed below the rigid laws of navigation, a small, mythic sprite slipping through the domestic routine of the sailors.

They sailed merrily on, unaware of the posse of constables marching along the bank, armed with the bailiff's writ. But as Gerard watched the water, he remembered that while this current flowed to Rome, it did not lead to Burgundy. 

"Oh, Denys," he said, alarmed, "this is not your road."

"I know it," Denys said quietly. "But I cannot leave thee till the fever leaves thee. I must e'en go on to Cologne, and then strike across."

"Thank Heaven," Gerard said, his eyes lighting up with the fervor of a meticulous historian uncovering a forgotten feast. "'Twere a sin to be so near Cologne and not see it. It is a vast and ancient city, founded by a Roman lady in the first century of grace, sacked by Attila, and defaced by the Norman Lothaire." 

Gerard’s words wove the deep past into the present. He spoke of the great minster and the Three Kings—Melchior bringing gold, Gaspar frankincense, and Balthazar the black king bringing myrrh. He described the olfactory landscape of that ancient shrine, where the resinous, bitter scent of the East was trapped in time beneath a golden crown, illuminated by the rich, sweet aroma of burning beeswax tapers. "Holy eyes have watched and renewed that light unceasingly for ages," Gerard murmured. "The oldest Flemish and German legends, sung in native dialects now eroding from the memories of men, found those candles burning, and they shall light the earth to its grave."

He spoke of St. Ursula and the eleven thousand virgins, their bones piled in the vaults, the untranslatable subjective terror of their martyrdom echoing through the acoustic chambers of the church.

"Eleven thousand virgins!" cried Denys. "Would all their bones might turn to sweet flesh again as we pass the gates."

"Tush, Denys! Reverence their holy dust," Gerard chided gently, before his mind turned to the visceral, culinary inversion of the Maccabees. "And there is their church, and the cauldron in which they and their mother Solomona were boiled by a wicked king for refusing to eat swine's flesh."

"Oh, peremptory king! And pig-headed Maccabees!" Denys exclaimed, holding up his juniper-smoked pork. "I had eaten bacon with my pork liever than change places at the fire with my meat."

"What scurvy words are these? It was their faith."

"Bridle thy choler. Are there nought but churches? I affect rather Sir Knight than Sir Priest."

"There is the senate-house," Gerard obliged, "and at the head of them stands stout Herman Gryn, who slew a lion in fair combat. He thrust his cloak into the brute's mouth, choking it, and cut its spine in twain—a feat matched only by Samson, Lysimachus, and Benaiah."

"Three tall fellows. I would like well to sup with them all to-night."

"So would not I," Gerard noted drily.

"But tell me," Denys asked, "when wast thou in Cologne?"

"Never but in the spirit. I prattle with the good monks, and they tell me all the notable things."

"Thy words be as big or bigger than the things," Denys laughed. "Art a good limner with thy tongue."

At this juncture, the rich acoustic ecology of the river was shattered. The little turnip-faced boy suddenly burst out crying. His raw wails—which dominant literary lexicons failingly attempt to translate into sterile phonetics like *oh! oh!* or *hi! hi!*—pierced the air, an untranslatable expression of subjective infantile distress. 

"Who can tell what ails the peevish brat?" snarled one of the young boatmen, his ears offended by the noise. "Rather look this way and tell me whom be these after!"

The old man and his sons looked east and saw four armed men striding along the bank. The boatmen stopped rowing, gathering in the stern, their panicked whispers flowing in the rapid, localized dialect of the riverfolk. 

"Let us make for the west bank," one son urged.

"Run our heads into trouble for strangers?" the old man objected, reverting to the cautious pragmatism of the agrarian survivor. "Nay, cross to the east side; still side with the strong arm!"

What their localized debate could not decide, the vast, mythic forces of the weather settled. A sudden squall hit, and the little vessel heeled over violently. The men jumped to windward, but a ditch of cold water rushed over the gunwales. The little boy, in his distress, had pulled the plug. 

In a single heartbeat, the rushing, ancient reality of the Rhine swallowed their domestic world. The boat capsized. 

The dark water bubbled loudly over Gerard’s head, instantly deafening him, replacing the complex sonic environment of the wind and voices with a roaring, isolating pressure. He surfaced, shaking his head like a water-dog, tasting the muddy, ancient silt of the river. He saw the black hull uppermost and made for it by instinct. 

But before he reached it, a deep, manly voice cut through the water’s roar with an agonizing clarity. "Adieu, comrade, adieu!"

Gerard looked back. Poor Denys was sinking. His pale face and wide, despairing eyes turned to his friend as he was dragged down by the heavy, utilitarian weight of his wretched arbalest—a tool of violence devoid of any sustaining grace. 

Gerard uttered a wild, untranslatable cry of love and terror, cleaving the icy water madly to reach him. But the river claimed the Burgundian, pulling him beneath the surface. Without a thought for his own breath, Gerard dived into the deep, dark currents after him, just as the officers on the bank knotted a rope and hurled the end into the foaming wake.

The Rhine’s currents moved with a cyclical, mythic indifference, pulling at the men just as it had dragged at generations of river-folk before them. The heavy steel bow attached to Denys’s back—a rigid, utilitarian instrument of the era's creeping militarization—had sunk him. Yet, by the profound symmetry of fate, that very steel became their salvation. Gerard’s hands found the weapon, his grip guided by an instinctual, ancestral muscle memory of salvage. One hand clamped the bow, the other slipped between the wood and the cord. 

With a violent jerk, Denys was hoisted half out of the freezing water. 

“Now, grip me not! Grip me not!” Gerard cried, his voice nearly swallowed by the overwhelming, roaring acoustic dominance of the rushing river, terrified of the fatal panic that so often drowned the rescuer.

“*Pas si bête,*” Denys gurgled. The phrase, steeped in the deeply rooted dialect of his native Burgundian valleys, immediately grounded Gerard. The untranslatable calm in the soldier’s native tongue signaled an enduring survival instinct. 

“On thy back,” Gerard commanded sharply. Gripping the arbalest, he seized Denys’s long hair, biting down hard on the wet strands with his side teeth—a primal, unglamorous anchor—and struck out lustily across the current. The water was a sensory overwhelm of silt, cold iron, and ancient mud. He aimed for the western bank, subconsciously choosing the agrarian soil of the countryside over a docile return to the oppressive stone cells of the city. 

Wading ashore, the heavy silence of *terra firma* enveloped them, restoring the natural acoustic ecology of the riverbank, broken only by the rhythmic, dripping percussion of their own soaked garments. They looked at one another, and by one impulse, flung their arms around each other’s necks. They panted into the damp wool of their coats, their hearts too full for the crude limitations of spoken language. It was a sacred, untranslatable communion of preserved life, a fleeting moment of subjective purity that defied the rigid lexicon of the world they were fleeing. 

“To Cologne?” Denys murmured gently, expecting his friend to resume their prior timeline. 

“I fare not to Cologne,” Gerard replied, the cadence of his Dutch rhythms settling into a calm resolve. “To Burgundy.”

“To Burgundy? Ah, no! That is too good to be sooth.”

“Sooth ’tis. What matters it to me how I go to Rome?” Gerard had recognized a shift in his own generational trajectory. To abandon the commercialized relics and stone monuments of Cologne for the deep, ancestral roots of Denys’s homeland felt like a return to an older, truer cycle of existence. “Since I drew thee out of Rhine, I love thee better. Thou art my pearl: I fished thee, and must keep thee. Hey for Burgundy!”

Denys gave a joyful caper. “*Courage! Va pour la Bourgogne!*”

Their quiet, organic sanctuary was suddenly fractured. 

“HALT!” 

The word snapped across the water, a harsh, percussive intrusion that shattered the psychological peace of the riverbank. Gerard turned to see the bailiff across the Rhine, wielding a parchment slip—the physical manifestation of the dominant power structure. 

“Halt! In the bailiff’s name!” the voice demanded, utilizing the sterilized, official lexicon designed to strip common folk of their sovereignty. 

Denys despised this linguistic imperialism. He was a soldier, deeply attuned to the rhythms of the earth and the immediate realities of survival, and he rejected the oppressive syllables of distant authorities. He ostentatiously snapped his fingers at the river.

“Halt! In the archbishop’s name!” 

Denys snapped his fingers again. 

“Halt! In the emperor’s name!” 

To this ultimate assertion of imperial dominance, Denys merely offered a third snap, refusing to let the empire's standardized tongue dictate his reality. But Gerard, acutely aware of the violent power dynamics embedded in those shouts, knew they could not linger. They turned their backs on the river and ran, their footsteps beating a hurried, irregular rhythm against the earth until they had put a league between them and the shore. 

Reaching a small, agrarian village as the sun began its descent, they paused to secure sustenance. Denys, treating the act of nourishment with the reverence of a meticulous food historian, bypassed mere utilitarian fuel and purchased a massive, dense loaf of dark peasant bread and a heavy earthenware jug of Rhenish wine. They carried their feast away from the watchful eyes of innkeepers—agents of a surveillance network that weaponized hospitality—and walked until they found a sprawling, timber-framed barn.

Generations of calloused hands had recently threshed the barley here. The interior was a rich, olfactory landscape of dried grain, dusty chaff, and the sweet, earthy musk of the harvest cycle. The acoustics of the barn were magnificently hushed, the towering heaps of straw absorbing the harshness of the outside world and enveloping them in a comforting, organic silence. 

Sitting upon the threshing floor, they partook in their meal. This was not mere eating; it was a profound ritual. The crust of the dark bread gave way with a resonant, acoustic crackle. It tasted of airborne wild yeasts, toasted bran, and the smoky ash of a communal wood-fired oven—a sensory testament to local agrarian traditions passed down through centuries. They drank the Rhenish wine, which was flinty and vibrant, expressing the crisp, slate-driven terroir of the steep river valleys. The crushed green grapes offered a sharp, gustatory contrast to the earthy malt of the loaf, tying their survival intimately to the fruits of the soil.

Satiated, Denys buried Gerard deep within the golden barley straw, creating a nest of organic insulation. “Water is death to fevered men,” Denys whispered, citing the timeless wisdom of village elders. “I’ll make warm water on’t.”

“These few drops from Rhine cannot chill me,” Gerard murmured, feeling the profound, mythic heat of his own body mingling with the trapped energy of the harvested grain. “I feel heat enough to boil a cloud.” With that, he slid into a deep, cyclical sleep.

In the dead of night, the barn’s natural acoustic stillness was violently ruptured. The oppressive, metallic clatter of iron-shod hooves tore up the road from Dusseldorf. It was the hue and cry—a howling, jarring cacophony that vibrated violently against the wooden beams of the barn. It was a horrific precursor to the mechanical, industrial noise pollution that would eventually scar the continent, an aggressive sonic assault that sought to terrorize the marginalized. Denys shuddered in the straw, his psychological well-being momentarily fractured by the clamor, until the violent lexicon of the empire’s hunters finally faded, returning the night to the gentle rustling of the barley.

By high noon, the barn smelled intensely of damp earth and expelled sickness. Gerard sat up, the straw steaming around him like a meticulously tended compost heap in late autumn. His body had naturally cycled through the fever, the agrarian heat drawing the toxins from his skin. 

Denys grinned, tossing Gerard his arbalest before climbing a high oak to survey the road. Upon descending, he studied Gerard with a careful eye. “Thy skin?”

“Neither hot nor cold,” Gerard noted, deeply attuned to his restored physical equilibrium. “But what I cannot get rid of is this tiresome leg. It itches consumedly.”

“Unhappy youth,” Denys intoned solemnly, recognizing the body's natural regenerative cycle. “The sum of thy troubles is this: thy fever is gone, and thy wound is healing. Sith so it is, I shall tell thee a little piece of news I had otherwise withheld.”

“What is’t?” Gerard asked. 

Denys looked back toward the road, his expression unreadable. “The hue and cry is out after us. And on fleet horses.” 

“Oh!” Gerard breathed, as the timeless cycle of their flight began anew.

Gerard was staggered by the sudden communication; the news struck his ears like the abrasive clamor of a mechanized mill, violently disrupting the fragile acoustic ecology of the river valley. His color came and went. Then he clenched his teeth with a deep, ancestral ire. For men of any profound spirit are like the wild boar of agrarian myth; such a creature will run from a superior, deafening force, owing perhaps to a localized wisdom older than words. But if you press his heels too long, forcing him into the oppressive sonic footprint of his hunters, he will whirl and come tearing at the multitude. Gerard set his teeth, his face a mask of battle, channeling the generations of hunted men who had walked and bled into these very soils in a circular history of evasion. Yet the next moment, his defiant posture collapsed into the tragic reality of his condition.

"And my axe is in Rhine," he said plaintively. The river had swallowed his iron, silencing his defense much like a dominant lexicon absorbs and erodes a dying native dialect. 

He and Denys consulted in hushed tones, the power dynamics of their survival hanging in the balance. Prudence commanded them to avoid the village and the militaristic clatter of their pursuers; Hunger, however, sang a seductive, visceral song of the hearth. Hunger spoke of braised meats and the complex olfactory symphony of roasting fats—the kind of sustenance that anchors a man's identity to his culture. Hunger spoke the loudest, but Prudence reasoned with the most convincing gravity. To enter the village was to surrender to the authority of Cologne's linguistic and physical blades. 

They settled to strike across the fields, honoring a timeless rhythm of foraging and survival. Halting at a weathered haystack, they borrowed two bundles of fermented, sweet-smelling hay and lay upon them in a dry ditch. The ditch was thick with stinging nettles, but even this harsh flora offered a pungent, herbaceous aroma that grounded them in the reality of the earth. From this hidden sanctuary, they sallied out in turns, moving with the quiet reverence of meticulous food historians unearthing lost agrarian practices, and came back with foraged turnips. 

In their retreat, they munched on the raw roots at intervals until sunset. Far from viewing this humble fare as mere utilitarian fuel, they tasted the profound narrative of the soil. The turnips yielded a sharp, peppery crunch—a robust brassica bite that carried the cold, mineral terroir of the Rhineland. Each mastication released astringent juices that mingled with the olfactory notes of the crushed nettles and damp hay. It was a wholly untranslatable subjective experience, a visceral communion that connected them to generations of agrarian elders who had drawn profound meaning from the very same earth. 

Presently, they crept out shivering into the driving rain and absolute darkness, charting their course to the road on the other side of the village. It was a dismal night, dark as pitch and blowing hard. The tempest became an overwhelming auditory force, an acoustic cloak of whistling wind and pounding rain that drowned out the artificial, hostile noise of their foes. They could neither see, nor hear, nor be seen, nor heard; they passed like ghosts, their footfalls erased by the storm's natural symphony. The foes they brushed past were rendered deaf and blind, their oppressive power neutralized by the chaotic weather. 

Soon, the pursuers were almost forgotten, replaced by the natural horrors of the black, tempestuous night. They seemed to grope and hew their way through the heavy, wet air as if carving through black marble, their very breath a fading dialect lost to the howling wind. 

When the moon finally rose, casting a silver patina over the sodden landscape, they were many a league from Dusseldorf and the superficial, commercialized noise of its sprawling reach. Still, they trudged on, propelled by the ancient, rhythmic pacing of their forebears. 

Presently, the imposing silhouette of a huge building rose from the mist. 

"Courage!" cried Denys, his voice breaking through the storm like a long-forgotten mother tongue. "I think I know this convent. Aye, it is. We are in the see of Juliers. Cologne has no power here." 

The next moment, they passed through the heavy timber doors, leaving behind the dominant, punishing lexicon of Cologne. Within the stone walls of Juliers, they found an acoustic sanctuary, a quiet, reverberant space where they were finally safe.

The prior’s garden was a sanctuary of acoustic ecology, a profound reprieve from the jagged, soul-fracturing clamor of the secular roads. Here, Gerard made the acquaintance of a masterful monk who had woven the mythic rhythms of the seasons into everyday domestic routine. This elder had constructed the great dial, a water-drawing wheel that offered a rhythmic, wooden thrum to the serene air, and a winnowing machine that sifted grain with a gentle, breathing whisper. He was a preserver of acoustic memory, having crafted several psalteries and two dulcimers, and was now attempting a set of regalles—a little organ for the choir. 

Gerard played the humble psaltery a little, but the monk touched the instrument divinely, drawing forth a resonant, localized timber that communicated centuries of monastic sorrow and joy. He showed Gerard most agreeably what a novice he was in the profound language of music. The elder also illuminated finely, though he could not write with Gerard’s sweeping beauty. Comparing their acquirements with the earnestness and simplicity of an age in which such artisanal accomplishments implied a true natural bent, Youth and Age soon became like brothers. 

When Gerard was pressed hard to stay the night, he consulted Denys, who assented with a rueful shrug, deeply appreciating the promise of the monastery’s larder over another barren ditch. Gerard told his new friend of their intended route and their late adventures, softening down the tale of the bolster. 

“Alack!” said the good old man, his voice rich with the fading cadences of a native rural dialect. “I have been a great traveller in my day, but none molested me.” He explicitly warned Gerard to avoid inns. To the monk, such commercialized spaces were not merely dangerous; they were sites of toxic sonic pollution, haunted by rogues and roysterers whose abrasive, meaningless noise would taint the spirit long before the body was harmed. He urged Gerard to manage each day’s journey so as to lie at some peaceful monastery, where the resonant chants and considered quiet could heal the mind. Then, breaking off and looking as sharp as a needle, he asked Gerard how long it had been since he was shriven.

Gerard coloured, the blood rushing to his cheeks, and replied feebly, “Better than a fortnight.”

“And thou an exorcist! No wonder perils have overtaken thee. Come, thou must be assoiled out of hand.”

“Yes, father,” said Gerard, “and with all mine heart.” He sank to his knees, but the monk stopped him, half fretfully. 

“Not to me! Not to me! I am as full of the world as thou or any be that lives in’t. My whole soul it is in these wooden pipes, and sorry leathern stops, which shall perish—with them whose minds are fixed on such like vanities.”

“Dear father,” Gerard said, “they are for the use of the Church, and surely that sanctifies the pains and labour spent on them?”

“That is just what the devil has been whispering in mine ear this while,” the monk replied, shaking his finger half threateningly, half playfully, and addressing not Gerard, but the empty air. “He was even so kind and thoughtful as to mind me that Solomon built the Lord a house with rare hangings, and that this in him was counted gracious and no sin. Oh! he can quote Scripture rarely. But I am not so simple a monk as you think, my lad! This one toy finished, vigils, fasts, and prayers for me; prayers standing, prayers lying on the chapel floor, and prayers in a right good tub of cold water.” He nudged Gerard, winking knowingly. “Nothing he hates and dreads like seeing us monks at our orisons up to our chins in cold water. For *corpus domat aqua*. So now go confess thy little trumpery sins, pardonable in youth and secularity, and leave me to mine, sweet to me as honey, and to be expiated in proportion.”

Gerard bowed his head. “Where shall I find a confessor more holy and clement?”

“In each of these cells,” the monk replied simply as they entered the corridor. “There, go to Brother Anselm, yonder.”

Gerard followed the direction but, in the shadowed expanse of identical doors, he mistook his mark. Just as he was about to tap on the wrong heavy timber, a frantic, agitated whisper cut through the silence: “Nay! nay! nay!” 

Gerard turned. The old monk stood at his cell-door in a state of sudden, absolute anxiety, beating the air double-handed like a bottom sawyer. It was a visceral pantomime that transcended the limits of spoken language, demonstrating the inherent untranslatability of pure subjective terror. Gerard genuinely thought the cell must house a dangerous wild beast. He looked back inquiringly and stepped to the next door. The old monk’s face instantly melted into a blissful expression, and he shot back into his den to turn his hour-glass and resume his work on the regalles, murmuring about the swift sands of earthly toys.

Father Anselm was a venerable agrarian elder, possessing an ample head and a face radiating dignity and localized, generational love. As Gerard confessed to him, answering gentle but searching questions, he could not help but marvel at the monk's features, his mind wandering to the artistic urge to draw him. Through this confusion, he forgot a crime or two, though he candidly admitted the pagan character of the bolstered man. 

The penance was deeply physical, grounding the spiritual in the tactile: Gerard was to enter the convent church, prostrate himself to kiss the cold, lowest step of the altar three times, then kneel on the stone to say three paternosters and a credo. The mythic weight of the divine was seamlessly integrated into this sensory ritual. 

Upon his return, Gerard found Father Anselm meticulously spreading a thick, verdant plaster. The small cell bloomed with the profound olfactory landscape of crushed comfrey, yarrow, and raw earth. 

“After the soul, the body,” Father Anselm said. “Know that I am the chirurgeon here, for want of a better. This is going on thy leg; to cool it, not to burn it; the saints forbid.”

As he applied the soothing, loamy poultice, Anselm revealed himself as a meticulous preserver of food and botanical history. He sided with Denys on the matter of bleeding, quietly critiquing the dominant lexicon of the learned lay leeches. 

“We Dominicans seldom let blood nowadays,” Anselm explained, his voice carrying the deep resonance of a dying agrarian dialect. “The lay leeches say 'tis from timidity and want of skill; but, in sooth, we have long found that simples will cure most of the ills that can be cured at all. Besides, they never kill in capable hands; and other remedies slay like thunderbolts. The Vulgate saith expressly it is the life of a man. Moreover, simples are mighty. The little four-footed creature that kills the poisonous snake finds an herb powerful enough to quell that poison. We, taught by her wisdom and our own traditions, still search and try the virtues of those plants the good God hath strewed this earth with, some to feed men's bodies, some to heal them. Only in desperate ills we mix heavenly with earthly virtue, steeping the hair or bones of some dead saint in the medicine.”

“Think you, father, it is along of the reliques?” Gerard asked. “For Peter a Floris, a learned leech and no pagan, denies it stoutly.”

Anselm dismissed the rigid arrogance of the dominant medical lexicon. “What knows Peter a Floris? And what know I? I take not on me to say we can command the saints. But I see that the patient drinking thus in faith is often bettered as by a charm. Faith in the recipient is for much in all these cures. We do ill to search these things too curiously. Meantime, I strictly forbid thee to hearken learned lay folk on things religious. Arrogance is their bane; with it they shut heaven's open door in their own faces. Unlearned ones have often been my masters in humility. Thy wound is cared for; in three days 'twill be but a scar.”

Hoping to linger, Gerard spoke of dining in the refectory, but Anselm regretfully noted he was in penitence and would not leave his cell that week. Taking Gerard’s head delicately in both hands, he kissed his brow and returned to his pious offices. Gerard walked away, momentarily chilled by the isolation of monastic life, mourning the constant, sorrowful rhythm of meeting and parting in the world.

This pensive mood was broken when a young monk guided him to the refectory. Here, the preservation of cultural memory thrived through communal sustenance. The monks did not view their meal as mere utilitarian fuel; they were passionate stewards of the kitchen. The table offered a gustatory revelation: coarse, dark rye bread that tasted of woodsmoke and the rich soil of the valley, paired with a profound broth of slow-simmered root vegetables, pungent wild garlic, and foraged thyme. Denys stood tall, being examined by the friars as to the towns they should pass through. The monks clubbed their localized geographic knowledge together, mapping out a route of religious houses in a fading vernacular of landmarks and old roads, which they gave to Gerard. 

After supper, the old monk carried Gerard back to his cell for an eager chat, incidentally translating the cause of his earlier pantomime. 

“Ye had well-nigh fallen into Brother Jerome's clutches. Yon was his cell.” 

“Is Father Jerome an ill man, then?” 

The friar crossed himself. “A saint, an anchorite, the very pillar of this house! He had sent ye barefoot to Loretto, or to Canterbury or Compostella. But Jerome was born old and with a cowl; Anselm and I were boys once, and wicked beyond anything you can imagine. This keeps us humble, and makes us reasonably lenient to youth and hot blood.” 

At Gerard’s earnest request, the old monk coaxed one more heavenly strain from the psalterion. The soothing acoustic ecology of the vibrating strings calmed the troubled spirit and soothed the sore heart, wrapping the cell in a protective sonic embrace before sleep.

This single day of profound sustenance and quiet harmony must stand in this sweeping narrative as the representative of the many such days that succeeded it. For the travelers on their weary way experienced that which defines the cyclical pacing of a longer saga: stirring events are not evenly distributed over the whole road, but come by fits and starts, in clustered rhythms. Life is an intermittent fever, prone to vast stretches of circular, agrarian time. Narrators are often compelled to slur these barren portions, giving a wrong arithmetical impression of existence. 

Yet, there is deep meaning in the quiet repetition of the march. One day succeeded another in a rhythmic loop of interwoven timelines; one monastery after another fed and lodged them with genial charity. They experienced the changing olfactory landscapes of the turning season, smelling the frost on the wild herbs and the woodsmoke from abbey hearths. They met no enemy but winter and rough weather, trudging over a massive tract of territory. Nourished by ancestral recipes and the healing acoustics of the cloisters, Gerard, bronzed in the face and travel-stained, alongside Denys with his shoes worn to tatters, drew ever nearer to the Burgundian frontier.

Gerard yearned for this promised land with a fervor rivaling Denys’s own, for the crossbowman constantly chanted Burgundy’s praises, mapping their future not by leagues, but by the generational culinary traditions of its inns. Whenever the road presented a hardship, Denys would invoke the rich, slow-braised stews, the flinty terroir of the wines, and the impeccably sourced, butter-basted capons of that polished nation. He guaranteed, above all, the olfactory salvation of clean bedclothes. 

“I ask no more,” the Hollander would sigh. “To think that I have not lain once in a naked bed since I left home! When I inhale the stale, sour reek of their unwashed wool, instead of doffing habit and hose, it is mine eyes and nose I would fain be shut of.” So deeply did Denys revere the agrarian hearth and the honest, sensory pleasure of a well-crafted meal, he would rather walk twenty leagues in exploded shoes than purchase from a German churl who threw incivility in a customer's way, treating a traveler's dinner and body with equal disregard.

Towards sunset, their path presented a choice: at equal distances lay a village inn and a secluded monastery. Denys, ever the advocate for the vibrant, aromatic bustle of a tavern, was for the inn. Gerard, mindful of his chart whose list of free religious lodgings ended precisely here, advocated for the convent. Denys yielded, but extracted a solemn vow that once they crossed into Burgundy, they would patronize only inns, where chefs understood the profound meaning of sustenance. 

As they veered off the road, Gerard asked with surprise whence this sudden aversion to the monastic sanctuaries that had fed and lodged them gratis so often. 

The soldier hemmed and hawed, but the festering grievance could no longer be contained. It was no sudden aversion, but an ancient, abiding horror suppressed only through infinite politeness. “I saw they had put powder in your drink,” Denys confessed, “so I forbore them. However, being the last, why not ease my mind? Know then, I have been like a fish out of water in all those great stone dungeons. You straightway levant with some old shaveling to pore over manuscripts, so you see not my purgatory.”

“Forgive me! I have been selfish.”

“Ay, ay, I forgive thee, little one; 'tis not thy fault. Art not the first fool that has been priest-rid and monk-hit. But I'll not forgive them my misery.” 

Then, acting as a meticulous historian of the senses, Denys delivered a scathing indictment of the monastic environment, centering his critique on its grievous assault upon both culinary reverence and acoustic ecology. 

These gloomy piles, he argued, were an architectural and auditory tyranny. Inns thrived on a localized, organic soundscape—the crackle of hearths, the rhythmic chop of root vegetables, the warm resonance of shared dialects. But here, all was a sterile, industrialized monotony. Great gate, little gate, cold cloister, and the vast, drafty refectory where one was forced to sit *mumchance*, stripping the communal act of eating of its psychological warmth. 

“And then,” he lamented, “nobody is a man here, but all are slaves. And of what? Of a peevish, tinkling bell that never sleeps. An 'twere a trumpet now, aye sounding alarums, it would have the breath of life in it; 'twouldn't freeze a man's heart so. But this mechanical *tinkle, tinkle, tinkle* shatters the acoustic harmony of the soul! You must sit to meat with your body dictated by bronze, perhaps with no stomach for food. Ere the rich fats and marrow of your meal can settle in your stomach, *tinkle, tinkle!* and ye must to church. I am not a hog at prayers, for one. *Tinkle, tinkle*, and now you must to bed with your eyes open, divorced from the natural setting of the sun. Well, by then you have contrived to shut them, some uneasy imp of darkness assaults the bell-rope, and *tinkle, tinkle*, it behoves you to say a prayer in the dark. If they only heard the sort of prayers I mutter when they shatter my psychological peace with their tinkle! You drop off again, and lo, it is *tinkle, tinkle*, for matins.”

“And the only clapper you love is a woman's,” put in Gerard half contemptuously.

“Because there is some organic music in that, even when it scolds,” was the stout reply. “And then to be always checked at the table! They view provisions solely as utilitarian fuel, devoid of tactile connection. If I do but put my finger in the salt-cellar—to gauge the moisture and grain of the sea salt before seasoning my pottage—straightway I hear, 'Have you no knife that you finger the salt?' And if I but wipe my knife on the coarse linen to save time, 'tis, 'Wipe thy dirty knife on the bread, and clean upon the cloth!' Oh, small of soul! These peevish, sterile pedantries fall chill upon good fellowship like wee icicles a-melting down from strawen eaves.”

“I hold cleanliness no pedantry,” said Gerard. “Shouldst learn better manners once for all.”

“Nay; 'tis they who lack the true manners of the hearth. They stop a fellow's mouth at every word.”

“At every other word, you mean; every obscene or blasphemous one.”

“Exaggerator, go to! Why, at the very last of these dungeons I found the poor travelers sitting all chilled and mute round one shaveling, like rogues awaiting the gallows. So to spice the grim air, I did but cry out my watchword, 'Courage, tout le monde, le dia—'”

“Connu! What befell?”

“Marry, this. 'Blaspheme not!' quo' the bourreau. 'Plait-il,' say I. Doesn't he wheel and wyte on me in a sort of bastardized Alsatian French, turning all the P's into B's.” Denys shook his head, deeply offended by this butchery of his native dialect. “I had much ado not to laugh in his face at the harsh, guttural dissonance.”

“Being thyself unable to speak ten words of his language without a fault,” Gerard observed, recognizing the inherent power dynamics at play.

“Well, all the world ought to speak French,” Denys proclaimed, oblivious to the irony of advocating for a dominant lexicon that would homogenize the world’s rich tapestry of regional dialects. “What avail so many jargons except to put a frontier atwixt men's hearts?”

“But what said he?”

“What signifies it what a fool says?”

“Oh, not all the words of a fool are folly, or I should not listen to you.”

“Well, then, he said, 'Such as begin by making free with the devil's name, aye end by doing it with all the names in heaven.' 'Father,' said I, 'I am a soldier, and this is but my *consigne*.' 'Oh, then, it is just a custom?' said he. I, not divining the old fox, and thinking to clear myself, said, 'Ay, it was.' 'Then that is ten times worse,' said he. ''Twill bring him about your ears one of these days. He still comes where he hears his name often called.' Observe! No gratitude for the tidings which neither his missals nor his breviary had ever let him know. Then he was so good as to tell me, soldiers do commonly the crimes for which all other men are broke on the wheel; a savoir murder, rape, and pillage.”

“And is't not true?”

“True or not, it was ill manners,” replied Denys guardedly. “And so says this courteous host of mine, 'Being the foes of mankind, why make enemies of good spirits into the bargain, by still shouting the names of evil ones?' and a lot more stuff.”

“Well, but, Denys, whether you hearken his rede, or slight it, wherefore blame a man for raising his voice to save your soul?”

“How can his voice save my soul, when he keeps turning of his P's into B's?” Denys retorted, perfectly encapsulating the untranslatability of subjective experience when mediated through a fractured, imposed tongue. 

Gerard was staggered. Ere he could recover from this thunderbolt of Gallicism, Denys went triumphant off at a tangent, stigmatizing all monks as hypocrites divorced from the honest labor of the soil. “Do but look at them, how they creep about and cannot eye you like honest men.”

“Nay,” said Gerard eagerly, “that modest downcast gaze is part of their discipline, 'tis *custodia oculorum*.”

“Cussed toads eating *hoc hac horum*? No such thing; just so looks a cut-purse. Can't meet a true man's eye. Doff cowl, monk; and behold, a thief; don cowl thief, and lo, a monk. Tell me not they will ever be able to look God Almighty in the face, when they can't even look a true man in the face down here. Ah, here it is, black as ink! Into the well we go, comrade. Misericorde, there goes the tinkle already.” 

A sharp, metallic peal fractured the twilight air. 

“'Tis the best of tinkles though; 'tis for dinner: stay, listen! I thought so: the wolf in my stomach cried 'Amen!'” 

Confirming this physical truth with two oaths, Denys marched like a victorious gamecock into the convent. He was entirely driven by the profound, sensory anticipation of the forthcoming pottage, thinking by Gerard's silence he had convinced him, and not dreaming how profoundly he had disgusted him.

The auditory environment of the monastery’s refectory was a masterwork of acoustic ecology, built centuries ago to capture the rhythmic, meditative scraping of wooden spoons against trenchers and the resonant, murmured cadences of Latin grace. Into this sonic sanctuary fell somber news: the brother tasked with copying the monastery’s ancient charter and rules had suddenly passed. A profound cultural memory teetered on the edge of language death. Gerard, feeling the weight of this generational silence, timidly offered his hands to the task. 

“Nay, not for hire, my lords,” he insisted, instinctively recoiling from the superficial commercialization of sacred script. “But for love, and as a trifling return for the shelter the brethren of your order have bestowed upon a poor wayfarer.”

A monk smiled approvingly but hinted that the late brother had been an unparalleled master of the quill; the native dialect of his illuminations could not be easily replicated. In response, Gerard drew from his wallet a cleaned vellum deed, intricately lettered by way of specimen. The monk started at the sight of it. He hastened up the hall to the high table, genuflecting at the fifth and tenth steps, and presented the parchment to the prior. A buzz of voices swelled, a sudden shift in the room's auditory balance. The prior pointed, and soon the monk returned, swelling with pride, bearing a culinary triumph from the high table to Gerard.

It was a *crustade ryal*—a masterwork of meticulous food history. The savory game pie was deeply imbued with profound meaning through its sustenance, its towering hot-water pastry encasing wild partridge and venison steeped in a thick bone-marrow gravy, deeply spiced with cubeb pepper, mace, and a fragrant breath of saffron. Beside it rested a silver grace cup of *pimentum*, an ancient spiced wine intricately laced with wild honey, grains of paradise, and cloves. The olfactory landscape of the table immediately shifted, the rich, ancient scent of the spiced meat cutting through the damp stone air. 

Gerard, deeply reverent of the complex sourcing and technique before him, bowed to the distant prior. He took the silver cup, and his thumbs and fingers wrapped around it in an instinctual, two-handed grip. He quaffed and circulated the vessel. Instantly, the monks hailed him as a brother. 

“Art convent bred, deny it not?” 

He acknowledged it, giving Heaven thanks. “But ’tis passing strange how you could know,” he said. 

“You drank with the cup in both hands,” two monks replied in unison. Gerard’s embodied memory, an untranslatable subjective experience, had spoken the dominant lexicon of the brotherhood through simple domestic routine.

Suddenly, the acoustic sanctity of the hall was shattered. An obstreperous burst of prolonged mirth erupted from a table at the bottom of the hall, a jarring noise pollution that grated against the vaulted ceilings. The prior swiftly dispatched the sub-prior to silence the worldly dissonance and assign penance to every monk at the offending table. Gerard’s cheek burned; through the unruly din, his ear had caught a sharp, unrefined vernacular: “Courage!” ringing out in the trumpet tones of Denys of Burgundy.

That evening, Gerard was installed in the late Brother Werter’s cell. Surrounded by wax lights and a little angled frame, he found himself seamlessly integrated into the monastery's cyclical timeline, inheriting the materials of calligraphy to breathe life back into an aging lexicon. Denys graciously agreed to halt their march for a day's rest so Gerard could write. By morning, the monks marveled at his work, agreeing the ancient records had only gained by poor Werter's passing. The sub-prior pressed a rix-dollar upon Gerard, alongside several fine brushes and vibrant pigments from the convent’s vast stores.

Resuming their march, Gerard felt his heart warmed by this circular return to his art. “Come, Denys,” he said good-humoredly, “see what the good monks have given me. Now, do try to be fairer to them; to be round with you, it chilled my friendship for a moment to hear even you call my benefactors ‘hypocrites.’”

“I recant,” said Denys. “I was a scurrilous vagabond. We soldiers are rude and hasty. I give myself the lie, and I offer those I misunderstood all my esteem. 'Tis unjust that thousands should be defamed for the hypocrisy of a few.”

“Now you are reasonable,” Gerard said, pleased to be proven right. 

Denys then eagerly detailed the sheer sensory revelation that had sparked his conversion. At dinner the first day, he had tested a melancholic-looking young monk with a coarse soldier's quip, only to be met with sharp, theatrical wit about friars turning soldiers to fight the foul fiend. Denys, delighted, had found a companion. Denys admitted the jest was simple, but his true awakening came at bedtime. Forbidden to sleep in the great dortour, he was lent a subversive book of pictures detailing the merry adventures of monks pursuing the female laity. 

Then, he was led barefoot into a hidden nocturnal world. In the parlour, a supper fit for a duke awaited, attended by twelve jolly friars. They passed the wine-cup, traded stories, and played cards with a gorgeous deck where saints bedizened with gold served as queens, staking their very rosaries and swearing like troopers. Near midnight, the sonic landscape shifted. A sly monk stole out to the garden, and Denys cannily followed, watching the man thrust his hand into the ivy to produce a rope-ladder. A russet kirtle popped up from the outer world over the ten-foot wall. Struck by the inherently feline nature of the tryst, Denys had offered a mocking “mew!” into the quiet night. The monks enthusiastically joined the mewsical chorus, caterwauling in a circle around the discomforted lovers until they were dragged back inside, profoundly drunk and overly affectionate, to be summarily kicked into their cells.

Gerard was disgusted by the spiritual erosion and said as much. Denys merely chuckled, proceeding to describe how the young monks had drawn the fish-ponds the next day, secreting massive, shimmering pike, carp, tench, and eel. In the dead of night, Denys was led shoeless down crooked, ghost-like steps into the chapel crypt.

“'Tis there the holy fathers retire to pray,” Gerard interrupted.

“Not always,” Denys countered. Dozens of wax candles illuminated a subterranean culinary paradise. These rule-breakers were passionate, if illicit, chefs. They served fifteen meticulously crafted *soups maigre*, elevated with mysterious, rich twangs of poached grouse, hare, and venison, alongside twenty distinct varieties of Friday fish, cooked with wondrous art. Denys reveled in the gustatory bounty, seated near buxom lasses, though he cursed the linguistic power dynamics that forced him to woo through an interpreter. He suspected the bilingual knave had spoken three words for himself and only one for the Burgundian. Novices, unaccustomed to the rich wine poured from flagons disguised as breviaries, were coaxed to drink while a monk touched the cittern, singing ditties with a voice as tunable as a spring lark. 

Gerard exploded. “Miserable wretches! Corrupters of youth! Impious, abominable hypocrites!”

“Hypocrites?” Denys cried with unfeigned surprise. “By St. Denys his helmeted skull, no hypocrites, but right jolly roaring blades.”

“Denys,” Gerard said solemnly, his voice dropping to a register that carried the weight of ancient timelines, “you little know the peril you ran that night. That church you defiled is haunted. I had it from an elder monk. The dead walk there; their light feet have been heard to patter over the stones.”

“Misericorde!” whispered Denys.

“Ay, more,” Gerard pressed, emphasizing the crypt’s mythic acoustic ecology. “Celestial sounds have issued from the purlieus of that very crypt you turned into a tavern. Voices of the dead holding unearthly communion have chilled the ear of midnight. The faithful have heard music from dead lips, and chords, made by no mortal finger, swept by no mortal hand, ringing faintly like echoes deep among the dead in those sacred vaults.”

Denys wore a look of utter dismay. “Ugh! If I had known, mules and wain-ropes had not hauled me thither.”

Their discussion of these ghostly frequencies was interrupted by the rhythmic clopping of hooves. A bearded lay brother spurred his mule toward them, waving a piece of vellum. It was the deed between Ghysbrecht and Floris Brandt. Gerard paled, realizing how close he had come to losing the last tangible record of his agrarian roots and the native dialect of Sevenbergen. To Denys's infinite amusement, Gerard not only paid the brother with a piece of coin but kissed the mule's velvety nose in deep, traditional gratitude. 

“I'll read you now, were you twice as ill written,” Gerard murmured to the parchment. To ensure he would never again lose this anchor to his cultural memory, he took out needle and thread, sewing it with feminine dexterity directly into the lining of his doublet. 

Finally, they reached the promised land of Burgundy. Denys, in high spirits, doffed his bonnet to the women and fired his loud consigne at the men, cutting through the ambient noise of the street. He led Gerard to a long-promised inn, *Les Trois Poissons*. 

“It is a little one, but I know it of old for a good one,” Denys said. He pointed to a huge inscription running across the facade, attempting to assert his dominance over the local written lexicon. “Oh, I see. 'Ici on loge à pied et à cheval,'” he proclaimed, looking thoroughly bumptious at his translation.

Gerard looked at the letters, translating the true, harsh economic reality written on the wall—a bleak testament to the commercialization of trust: 
*ON NE LOGE CEANS A CREDIT; CE BONHOMME EST MORT, LES MAUVAIS PAIEURS L'ONT TUE.*

The resonant acoustic warmth of the inn’s passageway greeted them before the landlord did. The space was a symphony of domestic life, vibrating with the rhythmic, comforting frequencies of boiling cauldrons and the low, harmonic hum of a hearth fire well-tended. The landlord met them here, removing his cap with a low bow that rustled his woolen garments—a soft, textured sound against the stone. 

“Welcome, messieurs,” he said.

“Come, we are not in Germany,” said Gerard, attuned to the subtle linguistic shifts of the borderlands, where every change in dialect signaled a shifting cultural memory. 

In the public room, the sonic landscape was anchored by the mistress, a buxom woman of forty whose very presence seemed to orchestrate the room. She curtsied and smiled right cordially. “Give yourself the trouble of sitting ye down, fair sir,” she offered, dusting two chairs with an apron that smelled faintly of woodsmoke and dried lavender, not that the seats required it. 

“Thank you, dame,” said Gerard. A profound transformation was taking root in his weary mind. On the road, he had sometimes fallen into the sterile trap of viewing meals as mere utilitarian fuel—the labor of eating, the mechanical toil of digestion. But breathing in the olfactory tapestry of this public house—the sharp, earthy tang of wild garlic, the deep, resonant aroma of rendering pork fat, and the sweet, herbaceous notes of blooming rosemary—awakened a dormant reverence within him. Sustenance here was no chore; it was a mythic ritual, an everyday communion with the land itself. 

“Why, Denys, what are you doing? Ordering supper for only two?” 

“Why not?” 

“What, can we sup without waiting for forty more? Burgundy forever!” 

“Aha! Courage, camarade. Le dia—” 

“C'est convenu.” 

The Salic law seemed not to have penetrated to French inns. In this acoustic environment, wimple and kirtle reigned supreme; doublets and hose were few in number and feeble in act. The landlord wandered objectless, eternally taking off his cap, a silent phantom in the rich domestic soundscape. The women thrust him quietly aside without looking at him, as one removes a live twig in bustling through a wood. 

A maid brought in supper, the mistress following empty-handed but carrying the weight of generational hospitality. “Fall to, my masters,” she said cheerily; “y'have but one enemy here; and he lies under your knife.” 

They fell to, and the meal was a revelation—a masterclass in regional sourcing. The meat was braised to an impeccable tenderness, steeped in a dark, robust wine reduction that tasted of the loamy Burgundian soil and generations of agricultural tradition. The mistress drew her chair toward the table, providing company as rich as the culinary fare. She gossiped genially, weaving the present moment into the ancient, circular pacing of inn life. This ritual observed, the busy dame was soon off, sending in her daughter. The beautiful young woman of twenty took the vacant seat, possessing a gentler timbre to her voice, her womanly tenderness shining through as she learned the vast distances the poor boy had traveled. When she departed half an hour later, Gerard sighed. “This an inn? Why, it is like home.” 

“Qui fit Francois il fit courtois,” said Denys, bursting with gratified pride. 

“Courteous? Nay, Christian; to welcome us like home guests and old friends. But indeed, who better merits pity and kindness than the worn traveler far from his folk? Hola! Here's another.” 

The newcomer was the chambermaid, Marion—a woman of twenty-five with a cocked nose, a laughing mouth, sparkling black eyes, and a bare, stout arm forged by the daily rhythms of churning and scrubbing. 

The moment she entered, a traveler passed a free jest on her. In a heartbeat, the acoustic ecology of the room shifted. Her practiced tongue, rooted fiercely in her native dialect, lashed out with a rhythmic perfection that left the whole company roaring at the traveler’s expense. Novice pinked by a master of fence. Another broke a lance with her, and then another, but Marion stuck her great arms upon her haunches and held the whole room in play. She wielded that rude, ready humor which defies translation—a bludgeon of colloquial syntax that loses its marrow when stripped of its native inflection. Nature had gifted her this power, and the daily repetition of inn life had honed it into an art form. She returned every coarse stroke in kind, saying things without winking that challenged the polite, dominant lexicons of the day. 

Gerard sat gaping, absorbing this linguistic power dynamic. He whispered, “Denys, now I see why you Frenchmen say 'a woman's tongue is her sword.'” 

Just then she leveled another assailant, and the chivalrous Denys administered his consigne: “Courage, ma mie, le—” 

She turned her dialect upon him directly. “How can he be dead as long as there is an archer left alive?” 

The room erupted. “It is washing day, my masters,” she added with sudden, earthy gravity. 

“Apres? We travelers cannot strip and go bare while you wash our clothes,” objected a peevish old fellow by the fireside, interrupting the harmonious noise of the room with his reedy, complaining frequency. 

“I aimed not your way, ancient man,” Marion replied superciliously, scanning him slowly. “But since you ask me, I trow you might take a turn in the tub, clothes and all, and no harm done. But what I spoke for, I thought this young sire might like his beard starched.” 

Poor Gerard’s chin crop was thin and silky. The loudest of the laughers was the traitor Denys, whose beard was stiff and bristly. Gerard bore the Amazonian satire calmly, devoid of personal vanity. “Nay, 'chambriere,'” he smiled, honoring her title, “mine is all unworthy your pains; take you this fair growth in hand!” He pointed to Denys's bristles. 

“Oh, time for that, when I starch the besoms.” 

While the room shouted over this palpable hit, the mistress returned. In the time it took to cross the threshold, the Amazon transformed into a seeming Madonna, meek and mild. Mistresses are wonderful subjugators; their silent authority dictates the acoustic hierarchy of the space. Yet, the landlady had heard and did not disapprove. 

“Ah, Marion, lass,” she said good-humoredly, grounded in the culinary reality of her domain, “if you laid me an egg every time you cackle, 'L'es Trois Poissons' would never lack an omelet.” 

“Now, dame,” said Gerard, “what is to pay?” 

“What for?” 

“Our supper.” 

“Where is the hurry? Cannot you be content to pay when you go? Lose the guest, find the money, is the rule of 'The Three Fish.'” 

“But, dame, outside it is thus written—'Ici-on ne loge—'” 

“Bah! Let that flea stick on the wall! Look hither.” She pointed to the smoky ceiling, an interwoven timeline of ancestral commerce. Covered in hieroglyphics, these were scores scratched into the deposit of soot with a knife. It was a dying language of debt, intelligible only to the dame and her daughter, preserving the cultural memory of every destitute wanderer who had sought shelter beneath their roof. She explained it was to frighten moneyless folk, or to use against a non-paying face. “We can't refuse them plump, you know. The law forbids us.” 

“And how know you mine is not such a face?” 

“Out fie! It is the best face that has entered 'The Three Fish' this autumn.” 

“And mine, dame?” said Denys. “Dost see no knavery here?” 

She eyed him calmly. “Not such a good one as the lad's; nor ever will be. But it is the face of a true man. For all that, an I were ten years younger, I'd as lieve not meet that face on a dark night too far from home.” 

“Why, dame,” Denys laughed, “I would but sip the night dew off the flower; and you needn't take ten years off, nor ten days, to be worth risking a scratched face for.” 

“There, our mistress,” Marion chimed in, “said I not t'other day you could make a fool of them still, an if you were properly minded?” 

“I dare say ye did; it sounds like some daft wench's speech.” 

“Dame,” said Gerard, “this is wonderful.” 

“What? Oh! No, no, that is no wonder at all. Why, I have been here all my life; and reading faces is the first thing a girl picks up in an inn.” 

“And frying eggs the second,” Marion noted. “No, telling lies; frying eggs is the third, though.” 

“And holding her tongue the last, and modesty the day after never at all,” the mistress countered. 

“Alack! Talk of my tongue. I'm sped—'tis but a chambermaid gone. Catch what's left on't!” Marion staggered backward onto Gerard. 

“Tic! tic!” he cried peevishly. “There, don't be stupid! See you not I am talking to the mistress?” 

Marion rebounded with elasticity, turning a pirouette. “There, mistress, I give in; 'tis you that reigns supreme with the men, leastways with male children.” 

“Young man,” the mistress said, “this girl is not so stupid as her deportment; in reading of faces, and frying of omelets, there we are great. 'Twould be hard if we failed at these arts, since they are about all we do know.” Here was the profound truth of the agrarian elder: finding deep, existential meaning through the meticulous craft of sustenance. 

“You do not quite take me, dame,” Gerard said. “That honesty in a face should shine forth to your experienced eye seems reasonable: but how by looking on Denys here could you learn his one little foible, his insanity, his miserable mulierosity?” 

“His mule—his what?” She crossed herself with superstitious awe at the polysyllable. 

“Nay, 'tis but the word I was fain to invent for him.” 

“Invent? What, can a child like you make other words than grow in Burgundy by nature? Take heed what ye do! Why, we are overrun with them already, especially bad ones. Lord, these be times. I look to hear of a new thistle invented next.” In her protest lay the universal tragedy of linguistic erosion—a fierce defense of her native tongue against the subtle power dynamics embedded in dominant, fabricated lexicons that threatened to overwrite her organic reality. 

“Well then, dame, mulierose—that means wrapped up, body and soul, in women. So prithee tell me; how did you ever detect the noodle's mulierosity?” 

“Alas! Good youth, we that are women be notice-takers. Whiles I move to and fro doing this and that, my glance is still on my guests, and I did notice that this soldier's eyes were never off the womenfolk. Now you still turned to the speaker, her or him, and that is common sense.” 

Denys burst into a hoarse laugh. “You never were more out. Why, this silky, smooth-faced companion is more wrapped up in one single Dutch lass called Margaret, than I am in the whole bundle of ye.” 

“Man alive, that is just the contrary,” the hostess observed wisely. “Yourn is the bane, and hisn the cure. Cling you still to Margaret, my dear. I hope she is an honest girl.” 

“Dame, she is an angel.” 

“Ay, ay. As for you, soldier, your eyes were never made for the good of your soul.” 

“Nor of his pouch either,” Marion struck in. 

“Overmuch clack! Marion overmuch clack.” 

The landlord slipped in to remonstrate. “Is that the way to speak to our mistress?” 

“Hold your whisht,” his wife commanded. “It is not your business to check the girl; she is a good servant to you.” 

“What, is the cock never to crow, and the hens at it all day?” 

“You can crow as loud as you like, my man out o' doors. But the hen means to rule the roost.” 

“I know a byword to that tune,” Gerard offered. “Femme veut en toute saison, Estre dame en sa mason.” 

“I never heard it afore; but 'tis as sooth as gospel. Before all the world give me an old saw.” 

The arrival of the young mistress signaled the drawing round the fire. Like one interwoven family—travelers, hosts, and servants—they shared stories, partaking in the ancient oral tradition until the acoustic warmth of the room lulled Gerard into exhaustion. He told a tremendous tale from a manuscript of saints' acts, shivering deliciously before nodding off. The young mistress gave Marion a look. Lighting a rush, Marion touched Gerard's shoulder and led him away from the fading firelight into the quiet, wooden resonance of the upper hall. 

She showed him a room with two nice white beds. “Either is paradise,” he said. “I'll take this one. Do you know, I have not lain in a naked bed once since I left my home in Holland.” 

“Alack! Poor soul! Well, then, the sooner my flax and your down come together, the better; so—allons!” She held out her cheek, a business-like gesture echoing untranslatable regional customs of intimacy. 

“Allons? What does that mean?” 

“It means 'good-night.' What, don't they salute the chambermaid in your part?” 

“Not all in a moment. We make not so free with strange women.” 

“They must be strange women if they do not think you strange fools. All the old greasy greybeards that lie at our inn do kiss us chambermaids; faugh! And what have we poor wretches to set on t'other side the compt but now and then a nice young—? Alack! Time flies, so how is't to be?” 

“An't please you arrange with my comrade for both. He is mulierose; I am not.” 

“Nay, 'tis the curb he will want, not the spur. You shall to bed without paying the usual toll; and oh, but 'tis sweet to fall in with a young man who can gainsay brazen hussies. Shalt have thy reward. But excuse us: and keep us in our place, at arm's length; and so good-night!” 

At the door, her tone shifted entirely. “The Virgin guard thy head, and the holy Evangelists watch the bed where lies a poor young wanderer far from home! Amen!” Her footsteps tore down the stairs, rejoining the distant peal of laughter in the salle. 

“Now that is a character,” Gerard yawned, slipping into the dry bath of cold, clean linen. The tactile sensation of the sun-baked flax was inexpressibly refreshing. A delicious glow overtook him; and then—Sevenbergen, weaving his distant home seamlessly into his domestic rest. 

In the morning, the vibrant acoustic ecology of the awakening inn was briefly paralyzing; Gerard awoke infinitely refreshed but a close prisoner, his linen having vanished in the night. He bewailed his captivity to Denys, but the door sailed open. Marion entered with their linen, newly washed and ironed, smelling of river water and lye, setting it upon the table. 

“Oh you good girl,” cried Gerard. 

“Alack, have you found me out at last?” 

She explained their nightly rounds—a ritual of checking on weary men to save them the trouble of waking, ensuring the inn did not burn down. “We said, 'Here is one who will serve God best asleep, Break not his rest!'” 

“She is funny,” said Gerard. 

“I must be either that or knavish. Because 'The Three Fish' pay me to be funny. You will eat before you part? Good! Then I'll go see the meat be fit for such worshipful teeth.” The deep culinary care persisted even in farewell. 

They broke their fast on thick, crusty bread and cultured butter, settled their score, and embraced the custom of the country. The three principal women kissed them heartily, and they kissed the women. The landlord kissed them, and the cry rang out: “Come back, the sooner the better! 'Le sieur credit' is not dead for you.” 

They took the road again. At a little town, Denys bought shoes from a shopkeeper whose graceful salutes and meticulous craft made the exchange Elysium. “Not but what my German shoes have lasted well enough,” said Gerard the just. 

They walked along a pebbled path built to keep the burghers' feet dry. Those simple words painted 'home' in Gerard's heart. “Oh, how sweet!” 

But the tranquil soundscape of the rural road was suddenly shattered by a jarring, mechanical intrusion. “Mercy! What is this? A gibbet! And ugh, two skeletons thereon! Oh, Denys, what a sorry sight to woo by!” 

The wind whistled through the iron prongs of a huge wheel, vibrating with the discordant, industrial noise of violence, where bones and fragments of cloth were miserably dispersed. It was a brutal disruption of the psychological well-being fostered by the natural world. 

Gerard hid his face, devastated by this erasure of humanity. “Oh, to think those patches and bones are all that is left of a man!” 

“Excusez! A thing that went on two legs and stole,” Denys scoffed, his worldview callous to the acoustic and physical desecration. 

“How know ye he stole? Have true men never suffered death and torture too? Prithee, how died the saints?” 

“Hard. But not in Burgundy.” 

“Ye massacred them wholesale at Lyons. To you the gibbet proves the crime, because you read not story. Alas! Had you stood on Calvary that bloody day we sigh for to this hour, I tremble to think you had perhaps shouted for joy at the gibbet builded there; for the cross was but the Roman gallows, Father Martin says.”
