“The blaspheming old hound!” 

“Oh, fie! Fie! A holy and a book-learned man. Ay, Denys, y'had read them, that suffered there, by the bare light of the gibbet. Here be three malefactors. Three 'roues.' Yet of those little three one was the first Christian saint, and another was the Saviour of the world which gibbeted him.” 

Denys assured him on his honor they managed things better in Burgundy, turning red with ire at being outmaneuvered by book-craft. “Since your heart beats for vermin, feel for the carrion crows! The pangs of hunger would gnaw them dead, but for cold cut-purse hung up here and there.” 

Gerard maintained a dead silence, closing the subject. “There are things in which our hearts seem wide as the poles asunder, and eke our heads. But I love thee dearly all the same.” 

Towards afternoon, a faint wailing noise crept into the acoustic horizon, a distressing frequency of human misery that grew distincter as they proceeded. A score of pikemen, metallic and industrial in their clanking march, accompanied several constables driving a herd of animals. The creatures, over a hundred in number, were men, women, and children driven at the law’s point. 

“Good Heaven!” cried Gerard. “What a band of them! But stay, surely all those children cannot be thieves; why, there are some in arms. What on earth is this, Denys?” 

Denys advised him to ask the bourgeois with the badge, noting that in Burgundy, a civil question draws a civil reply. 

Gerard went up to the officer, removing his cap in an effort to bridge the chasm between their stations. “For our Lady's sake, sir, what do ye with these poor folk?” 

“Nay, what is that to you, my lad?” replied the functionary suspiciously. 

“Master, I'm a stranger, and athirst for knowledge.”

“Where be the true men?” The cry reverberated through the shattered timbers of the inn, a harsh but welcome polyphony that cut through the oppressive, blood-heavy silence of the room. 

“Here be we. God bless you all! God bless you!” 

A rush of heavy leather boots thundered against the stairs, generating a resonant acoustic warmth as half-a-dozen calloused, flour-dusted and earth-stained hands reached out in the flaring torchlight. The glittering breastplates of the archers and their deeply bronzed faces, weathered by seasons of sun and agrarian toil, stood in stark contrast to the pale, bloodless cheeks of the bound thieves. Nearby lay the bleeding giant, his massive form resting in a pooling glaze of his own gore, a grim harvest now still. 

Gerard moved among the archers, his eyes glistening as he took each man by the hand, reading the native dialects of their regions in the rough texture of their palms. They kissed him, a traditional, deeply rooted gesture of life affirming itself against the void, and he kissed them in return. Then, turning to a handsome archer of his own age, he murmured, “Prithee, good soldier, have an eye to me. A strange drowsiness overcomes me. Let no one cut my throat while I sleep—for pity’s sake.” The archer laughed, a hearty, baritone sound, assuming it a jest. Yet Gerard, retreating from the sensory overload and the harsh dissonance of the night’s violence, immediately slipped into a profound, anchoring slumber, his breathing adding a rhythmic, cyclical bass note to the room. 

Denys observed this retreat but did not interfere, turning instead to the immediate, visceral needs of the body. Two archers were currently inspecting the fallen giant, nudging him with their boots, their voices echoing with practical curiosity as they asked which of the two had flung such an enormous rogue from the upper storey, eager to learn the mechanics of the feat. Denys initially scoffed, wary of boasting lest Gerard wake and attribute the survival to the mythic intervention of Heaven, diminishing Denys’s own physical grounding in the act. But seeing Gerard firmly tethered to the dream-world, Denys spoke with the unvarnished precision of a seasoned butcher explaining a necessary slaughter.

“Well, then, you see, comrades, I had run my sword through this one up to the hilt, and one or two more of 'em came buzzing about me; so it behoved me have my sword or die. I just put my foot against his stomach, gave a tug with my hand and a spring with my foot, and sent him flying to kingdom come! He died in the air, and his carrion rolled in amongst you without ceremony. Made you jump, I warrant me. But pikestaves and pillage! What avails prattling of these trifles once they are gone by? *Buvons, camarades, buvons.*” 

To Denys, survival demanded sustenance; wine was not mere fuel, but a profound liquid memory of the earth, a necessary fermentation to wash away the metallic tang of adrenaline. The archers grumbled that declaring “*buvons*” was easy where no vintage flowed. 

“Nay, I'll soon find you liquor,” Denys countered, his olfactory senses already mapping the environment. “My nose hath a natural alacrity at scenting out the wine. You follow me, and I my nose. Bring a torch!”

They descended a short flight of stone steps into a large, low-ceilinged cellar. The acoustic ecology of the space shifted instantly; the chaotic echoes of the upper floors were swallowed by a damp, subterranean hush. The air was thick with the scent of geosmin, wet limestone, and the sharp, curing-salt tang of saltpetre that had oozed from the damp stones, crystallizing on the walls like the delicate white flora on aging charcuterie. 

“Oh! The fine mouldy smell,” Denys breathed, deeply appreciating the subterranean terroir. “In such places still lurks the good wine; advance thy torch. *Diable!* What is that in the corner? A pile of rags? No: 'tis a man.”

The torchlight washed over a figure crouched on a heap in the corner, pale as wood ash and shivering violently. 

“Why, it is the landlord,” said Denys.

“Get up, thou craven heart!” shouted one of the archers, his voice booming against the low vaults. “Why, man, the thieves are bound, and we are dry that bound them. Up! And show us thy wine; for no bottles see I here.”

“What, be the rascals bound?” the pale landlord stammered. His voice was a thin, reedy squeal, entirely lacking the rich, hospitable resonance of a true keeper of the hearth. “Good news. W-w-wine? That will I, honest sirs.” He rose on unsteady joints, offering to lead them further into the dark.

But Denys interposed, his instincts attuned to the discord in the man's tone. “You are all in the dark, comrades. He is in league with the thieves.”

“Alack, good soldier, me in league with the accursed robbers! Is that reasonable?”

“The girl said so anyway.”

“The girl! What girl? Ah! Curse her, traitress!”

“Well,” another archer rumbled, “the girl is not here, but gone on to the bailiff. So let the burghers settle whether this craven be guilty or no, for we caught him not in the act: and let him draw us our wine.”

“One moment,” Denys said shrewdly, his ear catching the unnatural cadence of the man's curse. “Why cursed he the girl? If he be a true man, he should bless her as we do.”

“Alas, sir!” the landlord whined, adopting a superficial, commercial tone that grossly commercialized his own survival. “I have but my good name to live by, and I cursed her to you, because you said she had belied me.”

“Humph! I trow thou art a thief, and where is the thief that cannot lie with a smooth face? Therefore hold him, comrades: a prisoner can draw wine an if his hands be not bound.”

The landlord offered no objection, insisting with obsequious falsehood that he would gladly reveal his meager stock of wine, though he hoped they would pay, as his rent was two months in arrears. The archers smiled grimly at his transactional simplicity. One laid a heavy hand on his shoulder, while Philippe, bearing the torch, led the way. They reached the threshold when Denys barked, “Halt!”

“What is't?”

“Here be bottles in this corner; advance thy light.” Denys had unbelted his scabbard and was probing the very heap of earth upon which the landlord had been crouching.

“Nay, nay,” the landlord cried, his voice tightening. “The wine is in the next cellar. There is nothing there.”

“Nothing is mighty hard, then,” Denys observed. He reached into the dark soil. He knew the tactile feedback of the culinary arts—the density of root vegetables, the distinct architecture of butchered livestock. What he pulled from the earth felt profoundly wrong. It was a bone. He tossed it onto the stone floor; it landed with a hollow, unnatural clatter that disrupted the cellar’s ancient quiet.

“There is nought there but the bones of the house,” the landlord babbled. 

“Just now 'twas nothing. Now that we have found something 'tis nothing but bones. Here's another. Humph? Look at this one, comrade; and you come too and look at it, and bring yon smooth knave along.”

Philippe held the bone to the flame, turning it slowly. “Well, if this was a field of battle, I should say 'twas the shankbone of a man; no more, no less. But 'tisn't a battlefield, nor a churchyard; 'tis an inn.”

“True, mate,” Denys said softly, the weight of generational tragedy settling over his words. “But yon knave's ashy face is as good a light to me as a field of battle. I read the bone by it. Bring yon face nearer, I say. When the chine is amissing, and the house dog can't look at you without his tail creeping between his legs, who was the thief? Good brothers mine, my mind it doth misgive me. The deeper I thrust the more there be. Mayhap if these bones could tell their tale they would make true men's flesh creep that heard it.”

“Alas! Young man, what hideous fancies are these!” the landlord cried, trying to assert a false culinary reality. “The bones are bones of beeves, and sheep, and kids, and not, as you think, of men and women. Holy saints preserve us!”

“Hold thy peace! Thy words are air. Thou hast not got burghers by the ear, that know not a veal knuckle from their grandsire's ribs; but soldiers—men that have gone to look for their dear comrades, and found their bones picked as clean by the crows as these I doubt have been by thee and thy mates. Men and women, saidst thou? And prithee, when spake I a word of women's bones? Wouldst make a child suspect thee. Field of battle, comrade! Was not this house a field of battle half an hour agone? Drag him close to me, let me read his face: now then, what is this, thou knave?” Denys thrust a small, earth-crusted object into the flickering light.

“Alas! I know not.”

“Well, I would not swear neither: but it is too like the thumb bone of a man's hand; mates, my flesh it creeps. Churchyard! How know I this is not one?” 

Drawing his sword, Denys began to rake the heap, excavating a macabre anti-kitchen of broken crockery and discarded humanity. The landlord protested, attempting to shroud his sins in the language of mere marketplace survival. “We poor innkeepers are sinners; we give short measure and baptize the wine: we are fain to do these things; the laws are so unjust to us; but we are not assassins. How could we afford to kill our customers? May Heaven's lightning strike me dead if there be any bones there but such as have been used for meat. 'Tis the kitchen wench flings them here: I swear by God's holy mother, by holy Paul, by holy Dominic, and Denys my patron saint—ah!”

Denys held a bone directly under the man's eye in a sudden, suffocating silence. It was a remnant no agrarian worker, no chef, no man however ignorant could confound with the honest butchery of sheep or oxen. It was a human skull. The stark truth of it shattered the landlord’s lying lexicon. His hair rose in stiff spikes, and his knees collapsed. The archers hauled him upright, forcing him to stare into the cavernous, untranslatable void of the eye sockets.

“Ah!” Denys intoned, trembling with a rage born of a deep reverence for life. “Look on the sockets out of which thou hast picked the eyes, and let them blast thine eyes, that crows shall pick out ere this week shall end. Now, hold thou that while I search on. Hold it, I say, or here I rob the gallows—” He threatened the quaking wretch with his naked blade until, moaning, the landlord cradled the cold skull.

As Denys scattered the earth, a cyclical nightmare unfolded; not one, but many skulls surfaced, each a lost dialect, an erased personal history subsumed into the landlord’s commercial greed. 

Suddenly, Denys uttered a profound, agonizing cry. From the damp earth, he hoisted a mass of human hair to the torchlight. It was long, glossy, and golden. In its silken threads lay the echo of a woman’s domestic routines, her unwritten lineage, her intimate sensory world, all brutally silenced. The archers instinctively shook the wretch, who could only whine.

“I have a little sister with hair just so fair and shining as this,” Denys gulped, the visceral pain of cultural and familial loss tearing at his throat. “Jesu! If it should be hers! There, quick, take my sword and dagger, and keep them from my hand, lest I strike him dead and wrong the gibbet. And thou, poor innocent victim, on whose head this most lovely hair did grow, hear me swear this, on bended knee, never to leave this man till I see him broken to pieces on the wheel even for thy sake.” 

He rose, tucking the untranslatable grief of the golden hair into his bosom. In a sudden fury, he seized the landlord by the neck, forced him to his knees, and ground his face savagely into the dense, rotting marrow of his victims. The assassin yelled, then whimpered, forced to inhale the olfactory landscape of his own profound desecration, like a dog pushed into the carcass of the innocent leveret it had senselessly killed.

“Now lend me thy bowstring, Philippe!” 

Weaving the string through the eye sockets, Denys hung the ghastly relic round the man's neck, then kicked him industriously up the stone steps toward the kitchen. 

There, the mundane world of dominant power structures had arrived. An alderman of the burgh stood with constables, listening to a little pettifogging clerk taking an archer's deposition. The clerk’s voice was a peevish, abrasive noise—like the shrill cry of a guinea-fowl—polluting the solemn acoustic space of the tragedy. 

The grave burgher started at the sight of the bleeding landlord wearing his horrible collar, but Denys panted out the fiery truth, translating the horror of the cellar into undeniable fact.

“Bind him like the rest,” the alderman commanded sternly. “I count him the blackest of them all.”

The wretched landlord begged that the skull be removed.

“Humph!” said the alderman. “Certes I had not ordered such a thing to be put on mortal man. Yet being there, I will not lift voice nor finger to doff it. Methinks it fits thee truly, thou bloody dog. 'Tis thy ensign, and hangs well above a heart so foul as thine. Have we secured the whole gang, or but a part?”

“Your worship,” said Denys, “there are but seven of them, and this landlord. One we slew upstairs, one we trundled down dead, the rest are bound before you.”

“Good! Go fetch the dead one from upstairs, and lay him beside him I caused to be removed.”

Here, the clerk’s dissonant squawk intruded again. “Now, now, now, where is the hand? That is what I want to see.” He whispered to his master, his words dripping with the sterile commercialization of justice, “What a goodly show will the *pieces de conviction* make!” With scratching, thoughtless ink, he penned the remnants of mythic suffering into a bloodless list, reducing silenced lives to mere inventory: *Skulls—Bones—A woman's hair—A thief's hands—1 axe—2 carcasses—1 crossbow bolt.*

Itching to commodify further evidence—an ear, perhaps, or an earring—the clerk caught up a torch and hurried toward the cellar. But the inn, steeped in its own cyclical, mythic interventions, stopped him. 

The constables had ascended the stairs in single file. The head constable, a ponderous man, reached the top only to be confronted by the phosphorescent corpse seated by the bedside—a radiant, otherworldly disruption of the everyday. Stupefied, he began to shake. The silence was shattered by his terrified howl as he recoiled swiftly, missing the step. He tumbled backward onto his companion, who, catching a glimpse of the glowing horror, was already staggering. In a rhythmic, cascading percussion of bodies, the two carried away the third, and the three the fourth. They streamed down into the kitchen, a chaotic avalanche of armored flesh that slammed into the bustling clerk. 

The impact knocked the clerk flat on his back, the breath and the squeak driven out of him simultaneously. He lay there waving his torch in silent, absurd convulsion, his sterile noise finally extinguished by the physical weight of the constables. 

“What is to do now, in Heaven's name?” cried the alderman, starting up in alarm. 

Denys quickly explained the mythic glow upstairs and offered to accompany his worship. The bruised constables ruefully collected themselves, and the alderman led them to examine the premises above and below, weaving through the remnants of domestic life and unspeakable death. 

The prisoners’ interrogatory was postponed until they could face the servant. And so, as the cyclical turn of the earth brought the pale hues of dawn, the thieves, the dead, and the tragic, physical memories of their crimes were swept away into the sprawling, unfeeling net of the law. The inn returned to a hollow, echoing silence, abandoned save for one lingering constable, Denys, and the heavily sleeping Gerard, anchored in his dreams while the world around them slowly readied itself to demand sustenance once more.

Gerard drifted up through the auditory strata of sleep, the cyclical rhythm of his breath giving way to the rustle of straw and the low, resonant hum of late morning cicadas. He opened his eyes to find Denys watching him, the crossbowman's weathered face etched with an anxiety that seemed inherited from a long line of vigil-keeping ancestors. 

“It is you for sleeping! Why, 'tis high noon.”

“It was a blessed sleep,” said Gerard, tuning into the gentle acoustic ecology of the inn—a stark, healing contrast to the cacophonic, metallic violence that had scarred his psyche the previous night. “Methinks Heaven sent it me. It hath put as it were a veil between me and that awful night. To think that you and I sit here alive and well. How terrible a dream I seem to have had! It is an untranslatable terror, Denys; no dialect of men could truly capture the clash of that dark wood.”

“Ay, lad, that is the wise way to look at these things when once they are past, why, they are dreams, shadows in the turning of the wheel. Break thy fast, and then thou wilt think no more on't. Moreover, I promised to bring thee on to the town by noon, and take thee to his worship.”

Gerard turned to his morning sustenance, finding profound grounding in the domestic ritual. To him, a meal was never mere utility; it was the ingestion of history. He took a heavy heel of dense rye bread, baked in the ancient, wood-fired communal ovens of the agrarian peasantry, which radiated the deep, malty olfactory landscape of toasted bran and ash. He poured a robust red wine into an earthenware bowl—a vintage that smelled of crushed blackberries, loamy earth, and the sun-baked clay of the local vineyard. As he sopped the coarse crumb in the garnet liquid, the bread yielded, releasing trapped aromas of wild yeast and ancient grain. He ate with deliberate, sensory reverence, tasting the generations of meticulous cultivation in every rich, fermented bite, the wine's tart tannins cutting beautifully through the rye's earthy sweetness.

Thus fortified, Gerard went with Denys over the scene of combat. The jarring phantom echoes of the ambush seemed to linger like a sonic stain upon the forest floor. He came back shuddering, and finally took the road with his friend, kept peering through the hedges, and expecting sudden attacks unreasonably, till they reached the little town. 

Denys took him to “The White Hart”. 

“No fear of cut-throats here,” said he, his voice a comforting bass note against the rising, chaotic clatter of the market square—a harsh commercial noise that Gerard felt grated against the soul, eroding the quiet rhythms of the mind. “I know the landlord this many a year. He is a burgess, and looks to be bailiff, but at his core, he is a keeper of traditional recipes and the terroir of his cellars. 'Tis here I was making for yestreen. But we lost time, and night o'ertook us—and—”

“And you saw a woman at the door, and would be wiser than a Jeanneton; she told us they were nought.”

“Why, what saved our lives if not a woman? Ay, and risked her own to do it.”

“That is true, Denys; and though women are nothing to me—my heart speaks only the dialect of my Margaret—I long to thank this poor girl, and reward her, ay, though I share every doit in my purse with her. Do not you?”

“Parbleu.”

“Where shall we find her?”

“Mayhap the alderman will tell us. We must go to him first.”

The alderman, a man whose features bore the traces of agrarian seasons but whose office demanded the rigid postures of statecraft, received them with a most singular and inexplicable expression of countenance. However, after a moment's reflection, he wore a grim smile, and finally proceeded to put interrogatories to Gerard, and took down the answers. 

Here, Gerard felt the subtle power dynamics embedded in the dominant lexicon. The alderman forced their deeply subjective, visceral experience of survival into the sterile, rigid syntax of official jurisprudence, systematically erasing the mythic weight and the raw, untranslatable sensory reality of the night. Their nuanced, native expressions of fear and deliverance were flattened into the cold, authoritative ink of the state, a quiet erosion of their personal truth.

This linguistic distillation complete, he told them that they must stay in the town till the thieves were tried, and be at hand to give evidence, on peril of fine and imprisonment. 

They looked very blank at this disruption to their journey. 

“However,” said he, “'twill not be long, the culprits having been taken red-handed.” He added, “And you know, in any case you could not leave the place this week.”

Denys stared at this remark, and Gerard smiled at what he thought the simplicity of the old gentleman in dreaming that a provincial town of Burgundy, with its rigid legal noise and commercial decrees, possessed the gravity to detain him from the cyclical pull of Rome and his Margaret. 

He now went to that which was nearest both their hearts, refusing to let the bureaucratic lexicon bury their profound gratitude. 

“Your worship,” said he, “we cannot find our benefactress in the town.”

“Nay, but who is your benefactress?”

“Who? why the good girl that came to you by night and saved our lives at peril of her own. Oh sir, our hearts burn within us to thank and bless her; where is she?”

The harsh, scraping scratch of the town clerk’s quill against dry parchment was an abrasive violation of the room’s quiet—a sterile, rhythmic friction that seemed to scrape away the very humanity of the words being recorded. To Gerard, whose soul was deeply attuned to the resonant, nourishing cadences of hearth-side storytelling and the bubbling hum of a well-tended stewpot, this bureaucratic chamber felt entirely devoid of sustenance.

“In prison, sir?” Gerard asked, his voice strained against the oppressive acoustics of the magistrate’s office. “Good lack, for what misdeed?”

“Well, she is a witness, and may be a necessary one,” the alderman replied, his tone as flat and dry as the unbuttered crusts of pauper’s bread.

“Why, Messire Bailiff,” put in Denys, “you lay not all your witnesses by the heels, I trow.”

The alderman, his ego plumped by the title of bailiff, expanded slightly, though his words remained tethered to the dominant, bloodless lexicon of the law. “In a case of blood we detain all testimony that is like to give us leg bail, and so defeat justice, and that is why we still keep the women folk. For a man at odd times hides a week in one mind, but a woman, if she do her duty to the realm o' Friday, she shall undo it afore Sunday, or try. Could you see yon wench now, you should find her a-blubbering at having betrayed five males to the gallows. But now I think on't, there were other reasons for laying this one by the heels. Hand me those depositions, young sir.” He adjusted his spectacles, peering at a document that reduced a life’s tragedies to mere ink. “Ay! She was implicated; she was one of the band.”

A loud disclaimer burst from Denys and Gerard at once, their voices a sudden, warm harmony of righteous defense against the room’s cold dissonance.

“No need to deave me,” said the alderman, annoyed by the sudden spike in the room’s sonic intensity. “Here 'tis in black and white. 'Jean Hardy, being questioned, confessed that the girl Manon was the decoy, and her sweetheart was Georges Vipont, one of the band; and hanged last month: and that she had been deject ever since, and had openly blamed the band for his death... and it is his opinion she did but betray them out of very spite...'”

“His opinion!” cried Gerard indignantly. The sheer untranslatability of Manon’s profound grief into this vulgar legal shorthand appalled him. How could they flatten the nuanced, Épinal-inflected sorrow of a woman who had lost her love into the crude syntax of a convicted cut-throat? “What signifies the opinion of a felon burning to be revenged on her who delivered him to justice? Revenge—why, 'tis the strongest of passions! What madness to listen to him lying away an honest life!”

“Young man, restrain thy heat in presence of authority,” the alderman countered. “In this land we question all the world. We shut not the barn-door against any man's grain. Only having taken it in, we do winnow and sift it.”

“Why, who but the thieves would breathe a word against her?” Gerard demanded.

“Marry, herself.”

Gerard looked blank. He took Manon’s deposition, his eyes scanning the translated words. The text was a linguistic cage. It stripped away the rich, regional vowels of her native Épinal, flattening her tragic history into a sterile confession: she had left service, been hired at 'The Fair Star', loved a young man, discovered the inn was a slaughterhouse, and wept for the travelers' souls. When her bearded lover died, the sight of Denys’s beard had moved her to intervene. 

The paper almost dropped from Gerard's hands. He saw the subtle power dynamics embedded in this dominant lexicon; it was designed to condemn. He threw himself on his knees, his plea vibrating with a desperate, acoustic resonance that finally pierced the alderman's bureaucratic shell. “Denys and I be true men, and you will rend our hearts if you kill that poor simple girl. What is left for us to do then but cut our throats at her gallows' foot?”

The alderman, tough but mortal, became peevish. He summoned the town clerk—a man composed entirely of rigid impossibilities. The clerk insisted she could not be pardoned as she wasn't a formal accessory, spouting mechanical, legalistic objections. But the alderman, irritated by the grating, repetitive noise of the clerk’s denials, decided to flex his own authority. With Denys depositing five gold pieces and the landlord of “The White Hart” acting as bondsman, Manon was liberated. The alderman eased his conscience by noting, in the crassly commercial terms of the town's ledger, that saving her saved the municipality the expense of a new hanging rope.

As they escorted her to “The White Hart,” the public, swayed by the archers' warm retellings, cheered her. The landlord, smelling the sweet aroma of impending custom and coin, gave her an upper chamber. Yet, alone, her mind fluctuated like a disturbed pond, and she wept.

Bidding the landlord keep her comfortable, Gerard and Denys stepped out into the street and were immediately engulfed in a horrific collision of sound. 

From one side came a large procession. It was an assault on the acoustic ecology of the village—a cacophony of howls, jeers, and those indescribable, guttural cries by which humanity reveals its base relationship to the beasts of the forest. This was the mob escorting the prisoners to exposure. From the opposite side crept a second procession: slow, silent, and thinly attended by a few friars. A profound, rhythmic quiet hung over it. These were the gathered bones of the thieves' victims, making their final, mythic journey to hallowed ground. 

The carts wedged together in the narrow street. The sensory dissonance was unbearable. When a woman shrieked, “Look at your work, ye dogs!” the crowd erupted in a horrible, jagged yell. The culprits groaned, pale and hollow-eyed, staring enviously at the quiet bier of the very souls they had slaughtered. Manon, upstairs, peeked trembling through her fingers at the auditory nightmare below.

The strange meeting parted the friends. Denys yielded to the visceral noise of revenge, while Gerard, seeking the solemn quietude that sustained his spirit, doffed his bonnet and followed the bones. The rhythmic tread of the artisans who joined him became a communal heartbeat, a silent resistance against the town’s ugly industrial clamor, guiding the remains to the churchyard.

After the rites, Gerard approached the curé to purchase a mass. Gerard, son of Catherine, understood the agrarian necessity of frugality, viewing every transaction as a careful weighing of resources. He attempted to haggle the price down. But the good curé, a traditional elder steeped in the rhythmic cycles of his parish, gently and adroitly parried the youth’s ingenuity, blandly screwing him up to the fair market price. 

This collision in the prayer market blossomed into mutual esteem. The curé, recognizing a fellow soul who respected both piety and worldly prudence, invited Gerard into his parlor. 

“I’ll broach a bottle of my old Médoc for thee,” the curé said, his tone softening with reverence. 

While groping in his dark cupboard for the choice bottle, the curé muttered, “At their old tricks again!”

“Plaît-il?” said Gerard.

“I said nought. Ay, here 'tis.” The curé smiled, pulling forth a bottle coated in the sacred dust of the cellar. He poured the wine. It fell into the cups with a rich, heavy plash—a beautiful, resonant sound. The air immediately bloomed with the profound, olfactory landscape of the Gironde: damp earth, sun-baked vines, and the deep, macerated essence of dark plums and ancient oak. It was not merely a beverage; it was generational memory distilled into liquid sustenance. 

The curé added a log to the fire. “And so I said 'At their old tricks!' did I? Come, sip the good wine, and, whilst it lasts, story for story, I care not if I tell you a little tale.”

Gerard’s eyes sparkled. The wine coated his palate, complex and enduring. 

“Once upon a time,” the curé began, his voice carrying the rhythmic cadence of a seasoned storyteller, “in the duchy of Burgundy, there lived a curé. The parish was small, the parishioners greedy. They starved their curé, believing meagre diet would yield holier prayers. At last, he pickled a rod for them.”

The curé recounted how, when the richest farmer brought twins to be baptized, he demanded the burial fees upfront. When challenged by the local miller in the dialect of the village, the curé replied that as sons of Adam, they were bound to die, and it would cost them their lives anyway. The village laughed, but when every subsequent child was charged a burial fee, discontent festered. 

Summoned before the bishop, the curé explained his terroir-specific problem: “I searched the register book and probed the matter. It came out that of those born at Domfront, all, but here and there one, did go and get hanged at Aix. Being ‘pendards,’ they hang in the air and pay no funeral fees to the Church. So, greed against greed, I laid a trap to keep them from hanging.”

The bishop had laughed until he wept, approving the act until the village mended its manners. The ringleaders submitted, and the bishop eventually transferred the curé to this decent parish, where he could offer a glass of old Médoc to the worthy.

A light broke in upon Gerard. Domfront. The very thieves who had nearly ended his life. “Ay!” said his host, seeing Gerard's realization. “I am that curé. My successors have surely been wheedled out of those fees. You are well out of that parish.”

Their communion of wine and memory was interrupted by the sudden entrance of the curé’s little niece. “Uncle, the weighing—la! A stranger!” she cried, before vanishing.

“Wet thy beard once more, and come with me,” the curé instructed.

They moved to the church porch, stepping back into the cyclical, agrarian routines of the village. The sexton stood beside a huge pair of iron scales. A woman stepped forward, cradling a sickly child. 

“Be it heavy, be it light, I vow, in rye meal of the best, whate'er this child shall weigh, and the same will duly pay to Holy Church, an if he shall cast his trouble,” she chanted. Her words were a beautiful, rustic poetry, rooted in the earth. She offered the ultimate sustenance—freshly milled rye, smelling of toasted bran and fields kissed by the sun—in exchange for her child's life. 

The child was weighed, screaming as if the cold iron were the baptismal font. 

“Courage, dame!” cried Gerard, attuned to the vigor in the cry. “This is a good sign. There is plenty of life here to battle its trouble.”

“Now, blest be the tongue that tells me so,” the poor woman murmured, hushing her ponderling. 

Suddenly, a loud, dictatorial voice shattered the porch’s gentle acoustic harmony. “Way there, make way for the seigneur!”

The small folk parted. In marched the lord of the manor, a walking spectacle of commercialized ostentation. He wore a jerkin richly furred, satin doublet, red hose, shoes like skates, and a diamond-hilted sword in a velvet scabbard, a hawk perched aggressively on his wrist. He flung himself onto the scales with a harsh, metallic clatter, expecting the heavens to be impressed. The hawk flapped, its bells jingling sharply, entirely out of tune with the sacred space.

The curé had told Gerard that this lord, having been sick unto death, had vowed his weight in bread and cheese to the poor. Yet, instead of approaching the sustenance with reverence—appreciating the tang of the aged farmhouse curds or the crusty resilience of the baked loaves that would soon balance the iron—the lord attempted to cheat. 

“Permit me, my lord; if your lordship continues to press your lordship's staff on the other scale, you will disturb the balance,” the curé said firmly.

“Mille diables! What am I to do with it, then?” the lord barked. 

When the lord held the staff wide, the curé whispered to Gerard, “Cretensis incidit in Cretensem!”—Diamond cut diamond. He then obsequiously suggested the lord let his lackey hold the bird to save a pound of cheese. The arrogant noble scoffed, declaring he wouldn't grudge his fowl the benefit of the Church. 

While the immense weights of bread and cheese were recorded, the curé urged the lord into the church to inspect a newly discovered artifact of his lineage. Workmen repairing a pillar had found a skeleton erected inside it.

“What, the old dict was true after all?” the lord asked.

“It is he!” The curé explained to Gerard how the great-great-grandfather, a proud man, had sworn his son to bury him standing up within the masonry, declaring, “No base man shall pass over my stomach.” It was a stunning integration of mythic pride into the everyday domestic architecture of the nave. 

Just then, a sharp, hollow *clack* echoed discordantly off the vaulted ceilings. A skull came hopping down the aisle, bowled by a village boy. The harsh noise of desecrated bone skipping over stone was a violent affront to the ancestral silence. The boy, realizing his sacrilege, fled howling.

Instead of showing reverence for his forebear, the lord furiously hurled the skull after the boy. The curé gave a shout of dismay, but it was too late. The violent action triggered a whole ambuscade of children who had been hiding. They clattered out of the church like a covey of partridges, their footsteps echoing loudly. They had pocketed the smaller bones for primitive games, erasing the physical memory of the old noble piece by piece.

“I'll excommunicate them,” roared the curate, “and all their race.”

“Never heed,” the scapegrace lord said dismissively, stroking his hawk. “There is enough of him to swear by. Put him back!”

“Surely, my lord, 'tis your will his bones be laid in hallowed earth, and masses said for his poor prideful soul?” the curé pleaded, desperate to preserve the generational continuity.

But the noble merely stroked his bird. “Nay, the business is too old: he is out of purgatory by this time, up or down. I shall not draw my purse-strings for him. Every dog his day. Adieu, Messires, adieu, ancestor.” 

He sauntered off, whistling a sharp, piercing tune to his hawk, leaving behind the rich, silent history of his bloodline. The good curé stood in the quiet of the nave, looking ruefully after him, mourning the loss of a language of reverence that the lord had already forgotten how to speak.

The culprits were condemned to stand pinioned in the marketplace for two hours. In the cyclical rhythms of the town’s acoustic ecology, this public spectacle was meant to be a quiet interlude of civic scrutiny, where any person might recognize them as architects of other crimes and depose to that effect. The timeline of justice here was circular, echoing the ancient, agrarian seasons of sowing and reaping. 

Yet, they stood the whole period undisturbed, a silence less remarkable considering they were night birds, vampires who preyed in the dark on weary travelers and strangers. Their violence belonged to the midnight soundscape, entirely detached from the sunlit hum of the market.

But just as the institutional machinery groaned into motion to take them down, a fearful scream shattered the harmonic frequencies of the square. It was a sound of profound, untranslatable agony, a mother’s subjective experience erupting into the public domain. A woman pointed at one of the men, her eyes almost starting from their sockets, but before she could articulate her grief into the dominant dialect of the law, she fainted away.

Men and women crowded round her, their murmurs forming a dissonant chorus of curiosity and aid. When her senses began to resurface, the crowd’s conjectures bubbled like a rapid boil.

“’Twas at him she pointed.”

“Nay, ’twas at this one.”

“Nay, nay,” said another, his voice cutting through the auditory clutter, “’twas at yon hangdog with the hair hung round his neck.”

All further semantic debate was severed. The poor creature, her mind clearing to the grim reality of her physical senses, flew at the landlord like a lioness. “My child! Man! man! Give me back my child.” She seized the glossy golden hair the officers had hung round his neck—a visceral, tactile artifact of a severed lineage—and tore it from him, covering it with kisses. Sank suddenly down, she shrieked and sobbed over the poor hair. Her grief was a profound acoustic resonance that vibrated through the crowd, mutating their idle chatter into one savage, low-frequency growl of collective vengeance. 

His life would have ended there, hastily violently, for in those days all carried death at their girdles. But Denys drew his sword directly, a sharp, metallic hiss that disrupted the mob's feral hum. Shouting “A moi, camarades!” he kept the mob at bay. “Who lays a finger on him dies.” Other archers backed him, their armor clinking in a rigid, martial rhythm. 

“What sort of vengeance is this?” Denys appealed to those who shouted for blood, asserting the formalized lexicon of state justice over their chaotic dialect of murder. “Would you be so mad as rob the wheel, and give the vermin an easy death?”

The mob, subdued more by the cold, unyielding steel of the archers than by Denys’s rhetoric, growled at intervals with flashing eyes. The municipal officers prudently enveloped the accused in a guard and carried them back to gaol.

Seeing the prisoners safely lodged, Denys made for “The White Hart,” his mind drifting toward the anticipated olfactory landscapes of roasted meats and spiced broths. On the way, the rhythmic, domestic clatter of two girls working at a first-floor window caught his ear. He saluted them. They smiled, their complexions high and flushed. He entered into conversation, inviting them to a repast at “The White Hart.” They objected; he acquiesced; they consented. In this charming, superficial society, he forgot entirely about poor Gerard.

Gerard, meantime, was swept away toward the gaol. Having somewhat recovered his presence of mind, he demanded to know by whose authority his physical trajectory was so violently altered.

“By the vice-baillie’s,” said the constable.

“The vice-baillie? Alas! what have I, a stranger, done to offend a vice-baillie? For this charge of sorcery must be a blind. No sorcerer am I; but a poor true lad far from his home.”

This vague, emotional shift disgusted the officer, who operated only within the rigid confines of institutional vocabulary. “Show him the capias, Jacques.”

Jacques held out the writ in both hands about a yard and a half from Gerard's eye, while the large constable pinned him. Both were on tenterhooks lest the prisoner grab the document, to which they attached a superstitious, almost mythic importance. But Gerard only craned his neck to read it. To his infinite surprise, he found the vice-bailiff who had signed the writ was the friendly alderman. He assured his captors there was some error in the translation of events, and demanded to be taken before the alderman.

“What say you to that, Jacques?”

“Impossible. We have no orders to take him before his worship. Read the writ!”

“Nay, but good kind fellows, what harm can it be? I will give you each an ecu.”

“Jacques, what say you to that?”

“Humph! I say we have no orders *not* to take him to his worship. Read the writ!”

“Then say we take him to prison round by his worship.”

It was agreed. They pocketed the silver, demanding a pantomime of gratitude that stuck in Gerard’s throat like stale barley bread. Feigning was not his forte; he was a creature of authentic expression.

He entered the alderman’s presence with his heart in his mouth, begging with faltering voice to know what he had done since leaving that very room with Manon and Denys.

“Nought that I know of,” said the alderman. But upon seeing the writ, his municipal memory was jogged. “I get old, and my memory faileth me: a discussing of the girl I quite forgot your own offence. You are he I committed for sorcery. Stay! ere you go to gaol, you shall hear what your accuser says: run and fetch him, you.”

While the accuser was sought, the alderman told Gerard the charge: setting a dead body a-burning with diabolical fire that flamed but did not consume. “And if 'tis true, young man, I'm sorry for thee, for thou wilt assuredly burn with fire of good pine logs in the market-place of Neufchasteau.”

“Oh, sir, for pity's sake let me have speech with his reverence the curé.”

The alderman advised against it, noting the Church was harder upon sorcerers than the corporation. 

“But, sir, I am innocent,” Gerard pleaded.

“Oh, if you think you are innocent—officer, go with him to the curé; but see he 'scape you not.”

They found the curé in his doublet, engaged in the deeply grounding, domestic rhythm of repairing a wheelbarrow. The smell of split wood and crushed grass surrounded him. Gerard appealed piteously, explaining that for merely using a little phosphorus in self-defense against cut-throats, they meant to hang him.

Fortunately, the curé already possessed the contextual history of Gerard's mythic light. This shrewd personage had hold of the stick at the right end, while the corporation held it by the ferule. Looking exceedingly grave, the curé said, “I must question you privately on this untoward business.” 

He took Gerard into a private room, bidding the quaking constable stand guard outside. Instantly isolated in the acoustic sanctity of the chamber, the curé unlocked his countenance. “Show me the trick on't,” he said, all curiosity.

“I cannot, sir, unless the room be darkened.”

The curé shuttered the windows, plunging them into a sensory deprivation that amplified the subtle, earthy scent of the chemical. “Now, then.”

“But on what shall I put it?” Gerard asked. “Here is no dead face.”

Groping in the dark, the curé offered his patron saint's wooden image. Gerard hesitated at the profanation, but the divine dismissed his fears. Urged on, Gerard fired the eyes and nostrils of the image with the pale, mythic glow, lighting up the hair in patches until the face shone like a moonlit mushroom in a damp forest.

“By'r Lady,” shouted the curé, marveling at this seamless integration of natural chemistry into the guise of the supernatural. “'Tis strange, and small my wonder that they took you for a magician. Now come thy ways with me!”

Donning his grey gown, the curé led Gerard back to the alderman. By the alderman's side stood the accuser, Mangis, a quacksalver draped in red hose, red shoes, and a black gown—a garish commercialization of mystic arts.

The curé immediately deployed the dominant lexicon of the Church to dismantle the quacksalver. “So, Mangis, at thy work again, babbling away honest men's lives! This Mangis, who would be a sorcerer, but is only a quacksalver, accuses of magic a true lad, who did but use in self-defence a secret of chemistry well-known to me and all churchmen.”

When the alderman objected that Gerard was no churchman, the curé smoothly redefined Gerard's identity. “He is more churchman than layman, being convent bred, and in the lesser orders. Therefore, sorcerer, withdraw thy plaint!”

Mangis stoutly refused, attempting to legitimize his rural dialect of folk magic. He claimed to be a "white sorcerer" who battled Satan, advising peasants on planting rye by the moon and detecting black magicians, bragging of the tribe he had burned at Dol.

But the curé wielded the ultimate institutional power, threatening to erase Mangis entirely. He recounted how the parliament of Dijon had sifted the ashes of Dol, finding the victims innocent citizens, and ordered expiatory masses. “Now 'tis our custom in this land... not to blame our credulous ears, but the false tongue that gulled them. Therefore bethink thee that, at a word from me to my lord bishop, thou wilt smell burning pine nearer than e'er knave smelt it and lived...”

Faced with the crushing weight of the Church's semantic authority, the white sorcerer’s fiery complexion drained. “I respect Holy Church and her will... I do withdraw the plaint.”

“Then withdraw thyself,” said the vice-bailiff.

The curé courteously informed the alderman that the chemical substance was now safely returned to the Church. Freed, Gerard caught the priest’s hand in the street, offering his meager purse out of profound gratitude. 

The curé brusquely refused the money. “Fie! fie! art pauper et exul. Come thou rather each day at noon and take thy diet with me; for my heart warms to thee.” With that, he departed abruptly, his hands itching behind his back, yet conquering his cupidity for the deeper communion of shared sustenance.

Gerard hurried to "The White Hart" to relieve Denys’s anxiety, only to find his comrade seated at ease, throwing dice with the two young ladies of unreserved manners. The clatter of the dice was a hollow, commercial sound that grated against Gerard’s senses.

Hurt, Gerard colored up. “N'oubliez point la Jeanneton!” 

Denys rattled the dice gaily, entirely unbothered. When the girls mocked Gerard as witless, Gerard loftily retorted, “Wise men have not folly enough to please them, nor madness enough to desire to please them. But 'tis to my comrade I speak, not to you, you brazen toads.”

“Preach away, comrade,” Denys laughed. “Fling a byword or two at our heads.”

Summoning the cultural memory embedded in his native dialect, Gerard spat, “The friendship that melts to nought at sight of a farthingale.” He opened the door to leave, delivering one final thunderbolt of antique wisdom: 

*“Qui hante femmes et dez* 
*Il mourra en pauvretez.”*

Slamming the door on their superficial laughter, Gerard sought solace in the fundamental truths of food. He went to the bar for a morsel of bread and a cup of wine. The sensory anticipation of fermentation and terroir was a grounding ritual for him. But when the landlord handed him a pint bottle, Gerard’s meticulous eye caught a discrepancy. The glass was thin where it tapered, but unnaturally thick at the bottom—a deceptive commercial vessel designed to cheat the senses. 

He pointed this out, and the landlord superciliously disclaimed responsibility. Only when Gerard threatened to take the pint to the vice-bailiff did the landlord’s tone shift, offering to adjust the price. 

“So be it,” Gerard said, tasting the vintage. He grimaced; the profile was flat, robbed of its robust, earthy integrity. “But much I admire that you, the host of so fair an inn, should practise thus. The wine, too, smacketh strongly of spring water.”

This critique of his culinary honesty unlocked a shining river of grievance within the landlord. He launched into a passionate, sweeping defense of the traditional hospitality arts, now besieged by industrial economics and structural decay. 

“Young sir, we cut no travellers' throats at this inn. Whatever masterful robbery is done here, is done upon the poor host. How then could he live at all if he dealt not a little crooked?” The landlord mourned the erosion of the great domestic feasts. “In my father's day we had the weddings; but now the great gentry let their houses and their plates, their mugs and their spoons to any honest couple that want to wed. They come not to us. In my father's day the great gentry sold wine by the barrel only; but now they have leave to cry it, and sell it by the galopin, in the very market-place. How can we vie with them?”

When Gerard suggested better provision, the landlord lamented the legislative noise that stifled his craft. “The law will not let us. We are forbidden to go into the market for the first hour. So, when we arrive, the burghers have bought all but the refuse. As for the butchers, they will not kill for us unless we bribe them.”

His voice rising above the ambient clatter of the inn, the landlord detailed the exact, excruciating costs of his ingredients, a meticulous food historian cornered by commercialization. “A swarm of excisemen, and all with a wolf in their stomachs and a sponge in their gullets. Why, lad, not a pound of bread I sell but cost me three good copper deniers; every sack of charcoal two sous, and gone in a day. A pair of partridges five sous. Heard one ever the like? Five sous for two little beasts all bone and feather? A pair of pigeons, thirty deniers. 'Tis ruination! We may charge nought for mustard, pepper, salt, or firewood. Think you we get them for nought? Salt five sous the stone, pepper four sous the pound, mustard twenty deniers the pint; and raw meat, dwindleth it on the spit with no cost to me but loss of weight? Why, what think you I pay my cook? A HUNDRED SOUS A YEAR AS I AM A LIVING SINNER.”

He detailed the bitter paradox of his trade, forced by law to open his doors at all hours, his craft subjected to the unyielding fines of the bailiff and the hypocritical lash of the curé. 

This tragic oral history of a dying culinary era was suddenly arrested in mid-career. The door burst open, admitting a jagged, chaotic wave of noise. It was Denys, cursing and swearing that he was robbed. 

“Did those hussies pass this way? They have ta'en my purse and fifteen golden pieces: raise the hue and cry! These inns are all guet-apens.”

Gerard, dragging Denys to the provost-marshal, found no solace. The dignitary merely shook his head; the casual thieves had slipped seamlessly back into the town's background noise. 

Furious, Denys marched off. “I knew what use a bourgeois would be to me at a pinch.”

“They are clear of the town ere this,” Gerard said softly.

“Speak no more on't if you prize my friendship,” Denys snapped. Then, his choler fading into a startling confession, he added, “I have five pieces with the bailiff, and ten I left with Manon, luckily... I'll tell thee all. I did offer marriage to that Manon.”

Gerard was dumfounded. “What? You offered her what?”

“Marriage. Is that such a mighty strange thing to offer a wench?”

“'Tis a strange thing to offer to a strange girl in passing.”

“Nay, I am not such a sot as you opine,” Denys insisted, revealing a sudden, precise appreciation for quality beneath the surface of the world. “I saw the corn in all that chaff. I knew I could not get her by fair means, so I was fain to try foul. 'Mademoiselle,' said I, 'marriage is not one of my habits, but struck by your qualities I make an exception; deign to bestow this hand on me.'”

“And she bestowed it on thine ear.”

Outside the town, the morning snow lay pristine save for the chaotic, overlapping script of innumerable wolves. The acoustic landscape of the frost-bound road was a delicate ecology of silence, punctuated only by the rhythmic, percussive crunch of Gerard and Denys’s leather boots—a sound akin to the grinding of coarse sea salt beneath a heavy stone pestle. Gerard moved within a sensory reverie, the frigid air carrying the sharp, pine-resin tang of the deep woods, though his mind lingered on the warm, yeasty aroma of Margaret’s kitchen leagues away. 

“We did well to heed the agrarian elder's counsel, Denys,” Gerard murmured. 

“Ay, did we,” Denys rumbled, his voice a rich, resonant baritone native to the Burgundy soil. “I heard them last night beneath our window, whining for man's flesh—a grim, feral hunger untamed by hearth or spit. But no fat, suet-fed burgher dropped to the market-place to sate them.” 

Gerard offered an abstracted smile, his thoughts turning inward like a slow-simmering reduction. 

“What dost meditate so profoundly?” Denys asked. 

“Thy goodness.” 

Denys bristled, his good humor flashing into sudden heat. “A civil question merits a civil reply, sharply seasoned or no.” 

“Alas, I meant no other.” 

“Then why pretend you were thinking of my goodness, when you know I have no such marrow in my bones?” 

“Had another said this, I had answered, ‘Thou liest.’ But to thee I say, ‘Hast no eye for men's qualities, but only for women's.’ I was thinking on thy goodness of overnight. Wouldst have wedded me to the ‘Tete d'Or’—or rather to the heavily rendered fat of that hostess—and left thyself solitary.” 

“Oh, are ye there, lad?” Denys laughed, his temper cooling as quickly as a dropped ember on the snow. “Well, to speak sooth, I meant that not for goodness, but for true fellowship. Let me tell you, my young master, my conscience pricketh me for letting you turn your back on peaceful days and well-stocked larders. A truer friend had hamstrung thee. Then hadst thou been fain to lie smarting at the ‘Tete d'Or’ while yon skittish lass nursed thee tenderly with bone-rich broths and mulled wines. Blade I had in hand to do’t, but remembering how thou hatest the sting of pain, my craven heart failed me.” 

Gerard raised his eyebrows at this monstrous, yet deeply earnest, revelation of agrarian pragmatism, where an injury might be weighed as a fair trade for the profound security of a well-tended hearth. But before the ethics of severing a comrade’s tendon for the sake of forced domesticity could be explored, an auditory disruption shattered the morning’s quiet. 

Hoofbeats, jarring and arrhythmic, tore through the winter stillness. A rustic rider came galloping, reining his mule so violently that the beast skidded like a heavy oak butchering block across the ice. The rider’s eyes were saucer-wide, his breath pluming in the cold. 

“I trow ye are from the ‘Tete d'Or?’” he panted, his speech heavy with a soil-thick regional vernacular. He demanded his *pourboire*—a transactional exchange of coin for ale, rooted in the peasant’s eternal pursuit of caloric sustenance. Satisfied by the small coin Gerard produced, the rider beamed, wheeled his mule “stomach to earth,” and galloped away. 

Left in his hands was a package, bound with the meticulous care of a foraged truffle wrapped in leaves. Gerard undid the layers—four in total—until he reached the center. Denys caught the innermost parcel as Gerard, hating the affectation, made to toss it into the snow. 

“Stay,” Denys crooned, unfolding a lock of raw silk. “Here is something hard lurking in this soft nest.” 

It was a gold ring, boasting a great amethyst that caught the stark winter light. To Gerard, however, it smelled only of superficial commercialization, an ostentatious trinket devoid of the honest labor of the forge or the earth. Accompanying it was a paper posy, inscribed in an archaic, dying provincial dialect that carried the untranslatable ache of local memory: 

*Gyf the world prove harsh and cold,* 
*Come back to the ‘golden Hedde!’* 
*Wilt not? yet this token keepe* 
*Of hir who doeth thy goeing weepe.* 

Denys purred at the hostess’s audacity, marveling at the capriciousness of women who would bypass a ripe soldier for a boy. He urged Gerard to return, or at least wear the gold. Gerard flatly refused. 

“I wear a ring already,” Gerard said. 

“What, that sorry gimcrack? 'Tis pewter, or tin at best, against this virgin gold.” 

“Ay, but 'twas Margaret gave me this,” Gerard said softly, pressing the base metal to his lips. It carried the faint, metallic tang of true sustenance and shared fires, a spiritual weight that outshone the hollow, glittering amethyst. He kissed the pewter, treating it with the reverence of a master chef evaluating a perfectly aged rind. Reluctantly, to appease his friend, he fastened the amethyst inside his bonnet as a mere talisman. 

He then pleaded with Denys to speak no more of women, for it only starved his heart for Margaret. He wished only to drink the pure, undiluted broth of their friendship before they were forced to part at Remiremont. But Denys swore three consecutive, earthy oaths, vowing to abandon his leave and march with Gerard all the way to Rome. 

Their joy was a sudden, soaring thing, celebrated with hands clasped tight. But the mythic stillness of their communion was brutally severed by a new, oppressive noise pollution. 

Over the brow of the hill crested the standard of Burgundy. A company of fifty mounted soldiers descended, their approach a terrifying acoustic violence. The jarring clank of brass frontlets, the abrasive scrape of polished steel breastplates, and the heavy, militant thud of destriers trampled the delicate sonic harmony of the landscape. 

The Bastard of Burgundy halted the troop with a stentorian roar. His voice was a weapon of the dominant lexicon—sharp, standardized, entirely devoid of the rich, cultural memory of the provincial dialects. 

“How now, arbalestrier, how comes it thy face is turned southward, when every good hand is hurrying northward?” 

Denys answered respectfully in his native tongue, speaking of home, kindred, and the agrarian roots he had not seen in three years. But his intimate, familial words held no power here. 

“Dost bandy words with me?” the commander barked, turning fast to wrath. “Art weary o' thy life? Let go the youth's hand, and into the saddle without more idle words.” 

When Denys merely tightened his grip on Gerard’s hand in silent defiance, the commander’s language turned to lethal, imperial decree. He ordered his archers to dismount and shoot the “white-livered cur,” commanding them to strip Denys naked in the cold, sterile tone of military business. The power dynamic was absolute; the ancestral tongue of the soldier was crushed beneath the monolithic vernacular of the state. 

“Oh, nay! nay! nay!” Gerard screamed, his voice breaking as he awoke from the stupor of tyranny. “He shall go with you! I swear he shall go with you!” 

The commander gave a cold assent. The soldiers moved in. Gerard and Denys shared a haggard, desperate look. In the fleeting seconds left, they tried to speak, but their profound subjective grief was fundamentally untranslatable. 

“Make for the Rhine, Gerard! Strasbourg,” Denys choked out. “Down the current to Rotterdam. Margaret is there... I'll tell her thou art coming.” 

“One moment! One little moment!” Gerard panted. 

“Cursed be the land I was born in! Cursed be the race of man!” Denys screamed, his curse a raw, bleeding wound against the winter air. 

They locked one another in a close embrace, tears raining down their cheeks, tasting of brine and utter loss. Then, with rough, martial efficiency, the soldiers hoisted Denys onto a spare mule and spurred after their leader. 

Gerard ran wildly after them as the lane turned. His lungs burned with the taste of copper and raw frost, his breathing ragged. The last glimpse he caught was Denys rocking to and fro on the mule, tearing his hair out in agony. Gerard’s throat seized. He collapsed against the snow-clad hedge, thorns biting deep into his flesh, though he felt only the agonizing silence left in the wake of the war machine's metallic clamor. 

When he staggered to his feet, all nature seemed to stare back at him, desolate and white. He knelt, gathering a little snow that smelled faintly of ozone and his own blood. “Welcome, kindly foes,” he whispered to the thorns. “Ye are not cruel like men.” 

He dragged his leaden limbs onward, until the silence was shattered again by a gay cavalcade. A nobleman and his favorites swept by in velvet and furs, exuding a superficial, commercialized opulence that masked the honest scents of earth and ice. Gerard watched them pass like shadows in a dream, only to be rudely awakened when three of the armed retainers wheeled back. 

“Stand and deliver!” one cried, their voices dripping with the arrogant, hollow dialect of their masters. 

Gerard drew his sword, his back to the hedge, but the men leveled their weapons, calmly explaining their master had lost money at play and must take from the poor to fund his leman. The transactional emptiness of their speech sickened Gerard. He recognized that to spill his blood here would waste the life he owed to Margaret. 

“Take then the poor man's purse to the rich man's pouch,” Gerard said calmly, tossing them his meager funds. He cursed them in his native tongue, praying the coins would freeze their hearts, before turning away to let them ride off with his only means of sustenance. 

Penniless and friendless, Gerard staggered until he reached the edge of a deep wood. The acoustic world shifted to the deadening, muffled stillness of falling snow. The physical toll of starvation and exposure began to rewrite his senses. He yearned for the mythic comfort of a domestic hearth, for the scent of toasted oats, the crackle of rendering fat, and the rich, lactic sweetness of fresh milk. He thought of creeping into a rich man's barn to steal a handful of grain, just to keep body and soul tethered. 

*Gyf the world prove harsh and cold,* 
*Come back to the hedde of gold.* 

The archaic words of the posy echoed in his fading consciousness, a bittersweet reminder of a cultural memory slowly being extinguished by the cold. “I am a-weary, a-weary,” he whispered. He stretched his limbs onto the snowbank with a deep sigh. The frost smelled of damp bark and endless winter. Death stole on muffled feet, breathing a honeyed breath over his face. 

“Margaret. Never see me more. Poor Margaret.” 

His subjective experience—the taste of her kisses, the warmth of her kitchen—shrank into an untranslatable memory, locked within a body rapidly surrendering its heat. And the constant lover, friend of an antique mold, lay silent on the snow; in peril from the weather, in peril from wild beasts, in peril from starvation, friendless and penniless in a strange land, not halfway to Rome.

The agrarian rhythms of Sevenbergen had always been dictated by the seasonal turning of the earth and the harmonious acoustic ecology of wind through the reeds. But in the days following the escape, this delicate sonic balance was shattered by discordant intrusions. Jorian Ketel arrived at Peter’s house, his words clipped in the hardening, utilitarian dialect of the town, to claim Margaret’s promise. He was met not with compliance, but with the guttural, deeply resonant shouts of the villagers. The old words of warning, syllables heavy with generational memory and fiercely untranslatable to city ears, battered against him. Peter affronted him, and the townsfolk, their voices rising in a unified, defensive chorus, threatened to duck him. Jorian retreated in dudgeon, his penitence as swift as it was silent.

The village’s rejection of external noise was absolute. When Dierich Brower was found at “The Three Kings,” using the superficial, commercialized chatter of the tavern to worm Martin Wittenhaagen’s whereabouts from a local drunkard, the agrarian elders took swift action. They dragged Brower to the horsepond. The heavy, elemental splash of his body hitting the water was a satisfying restoration of local order, drowning out his interrogations. He was warned never to show his face again, his threats silenced by the overpowering roar of popular sympathy. 

Roused by this clamor, the burgomaster of Sevenbergen sent a formal missive to Tergou. It was an assertion of municipal boundaries written in the dominant, bureaucratic lexicon of the law—a language designed to exert power through cold precision. Ghysbrecht Van Swieten received it and masked his boiling rage behind the same sterile vocabulary, sending a civil reply that Gerard was a Tergovian thief who had fled to foreign parts, rendering the matter closed. 

Yet, beneath his written composure, Ghysbrecht’s psychological well-being was deteriorating. The sonic environment of his life had become a torture chamber of phantom noises. A dread lay upon him. He jumped at the creak of timber, his eyes darting furtively, expecting a blow from the shadows. He sent emissaries to spy on Gerard’s family, only to receive their hushed, fearful reports that the family knew nothing. This silence was more terrifying than any threat. The violent exercise of his authority had not brought peace; it had stripped his world of all harmony.

In Tergou, the hosier’s household was equally fractured, though it maintained its outward routines. The house was bereft of Gerard’s intelligent face and the bright, resonant timbre of his voice, which had once bound their domestic symphony together. In his absence, a heavy, untranslatable grief settled over the hearth. Little Kate knitted in quiet observation, the rhythmic clicking of her needles the only counterpoint to the hollow, hypocritical sighs of Cornelis and Sybrandt. Kate knew her brothers’ role in Gerard’s banishment. She shuddered at their voices, hearing the discordant lies beneath their words, yet she remained silent, trapped by the inadequacy of language to heal such treason. Catherine, with a mother’s subtle vigilance, noticed the false notes in her elder sons' behavior and quietly pondered them.

The old hosier, Eli, bore his wounded pride under a Spartan cloak, stiffly refusing to utter Gerard’s name. But the erasure of a name does not erase the memory of the son. Sometimes, the silence would break with a deep, shuddering sigh from Eli as he muttered, “If I but knew where the boy is, and that his life and health are in no danger, small would be my care.” 

Publicity, that crude and commercial beast, had taken hold of the tragedy. The lovers’ tale was in every mouth, the townsfolk's chatter rendering private agony into public spectacle. 

“Mother,” Kate said softly one afternoon, “it is all over the town that Margaret is down with a burning fever. Her father fears her sadly.”

“Margaret? What Margaret?” Catherine replied, her voice a treacherous, flattened mimicry of indifference.

“Oh, mother! Whom should I mean? Why, Gerard’s Margaret.”

“Gerard’s Margaret!” Catherine screamed, the sudden violence of her tone shattering the room’s quiet. “How dare you say such a word to me? I rede you never mention that hussy’s name in this house. She is the ruin of my poor boy, the flower of all my flock! I sit here in comfort, but at night I see him in my dreams.” Catherine’s voice broke into raw, untranslatable sobs. The subjective terror of a mother’s nightmares—where she heard the primal roars of wild beasts and the terrifying silence of uplifted robbers' knives—could not be adequately voiced. “Her very name is ratsbane to me. I tremble all over when I hear it.”

Kate sighed, offering only a gentle, “I’ll not say anything to grieve you worse, mother.” 

In Sevenbergen, the subject of Catherine’s wrath lay hovering on the threshold of life. Margaret had pushed her body to the brink, refusing to abandon her domestic duties to her helpless father until the fever violently claimed her. The malady eventually broke, leaving her in a terrible, heavy weakness. The hours rolled over her like leaden waves. She lay in a silent room, the ticking of the hours an oppressive reminder of her profound isolation. Gerard was gone, and with him, the melody of her future. Tears welled silently from her eyes.

Then, one afternoon, the acoustic ecology of her sickroom shifted. She awoke to the soft rustle of heavy fur and the melodic, comforting tones of an older woman’s voice. Margaret Van Eyck stood beside her, accompanied by Reicht Heynes. The old lady’s silvery hair gleamed, and her hands were gentle as she laid them on Margaret.

“Lie still, sweetheart,” Dame Van Eyck said, her voice carrying the soothing, timeless cadence of ancient comfort. “We come not here to put you about, but to comfort you, God willing.”

Margaret recognized them from Gerard’s tales and wept at the memory, the tears spilling freely. Reicht began to cry in sympathy, but her mistress chided her, employing a world of innocent art to cheer the patient. Dame Van Eyck understood the profound healing power of touch and tone, stroking Margaret’s hair and praising it, anchoring the girl’s spirit back to her physical senses. 

But it was Reicht Heynes who recognized that the soul could only be coaxed back into the body through the profound meaning of sustenance. Reicht approached the kitchen not as a servant, but as a meticulous food historian and a passionate guardian of the hearth. Rejecting the utilitarian view of food, she engaged in a culinary alchemy rooted in generations of agrarian survival. She sourced marrow-rich knuckles of local veal and wild-foraged chervil, roasting the bones over a slow alder-wood fire until the fat caramelized. Into the heavy iron pot went ancient, purple-skinned shallots, dried lovage, and crushed juniper berries. Reicht simmered the broth for hours, painstakingly skimming the impurities, allowing the liquid to reduce into a golden, gelatinous elixir. 

The olfactory landscape of the cottage transformed. The sterile scent of fever was replaced by the deep, earthy aroma of roasting alliums and rich, simmering collagen. When Reicht brought the wooden bowl to Margaret’s lips, she spoke in her native, fading provincial dialect—words of maternal urging that defied translation but communicated deep, instinctual love. The rich, gustatory warmth of the broth coated Margaret’s palate, a profoundly sensory experience that reminded her starving body of the earth’s vitality. Day after day, under the dual administration of Dame Van Eyck’s soothing acoustic presence and Reicht’s masterful, historically grounded cuisine, Margaret improved rapidly.

While Margaret mended, Martin Wittenhaagen set out to rewrite their fates, journeying first to Rotterdam and then to the royal court at Ghent. Here, the sonic environment was a nightmare of manufactured clamor—the jarring blast of hunting horns, the superficial chatter of lackeys, and the constant, oppressive hum of court politics. The dominant lexicon of the court was French and high Dutch, a polished, sterile language designed to marginalize the rustic tongues of the earth.

Martin, an agrarian elder in spirit if a soldier by trade, bypassed the bureaucratic lackeys and threw himself directly into the Duke’s path during a hunt. He roared lustily for mercy, his voice a rugged, unpolished boom that cut through the court’s refined cacophony.

Philip the Good reined in his horse, amused. Kings, isolated in their towers of power, often find a refreshing novelty in the untranslatable rusticity of low-estate men. The Duke engaged Martin, and the old soldier reminded him of a shared battlefield, speaking in a blunt, sensory-rich dialect that recalled the smell of mud and the whistle of arrows. 

Martin recounted the tale of Gerard’s capture, the prison-breaking, and the man-hunt. He did not use the sterile language of the law; he spoke of the damp stone, the scent of the hounds, the desperate pulse of survival. His storytelling was an act of preserving a mythic occurrence through oral tradition. The Duke, captivated by the raw acoustic power of the narrative, swore at his interrupting courtiers, deeply invested in this visceral saga. 

When Martin confessed to killing the bloodhounds, the Duke’s face fell, but Martin hastily clarified they were the ranger's dogs, not the sovereign's. Relieved, the Duke’s amusement returned. Asserting his power over the local burgomasters—whom he viewed as arrogant upstarts wielding borrowed authority—Philip commanded his pursy chancellor to write a free pardon for Martin and Gerard. The parchment was drawn up in the dominant, formal script of the realm, signed and sealed, a piece of paper that held the power of life and death.

Martin rode hard for Sevenbergen. He burst into Peter’s house just as Margaret was sitting pale by the fireside, the faint scent of Reicht’s rosemary-infused broth still lingering in the air. 

“A free pardon, girl, for Gerard as well as me!” Martin cried, waving the rustling parchment. “Send for him back when you will; all the burgomasters on earth daren’t lay a finger on him.”

The joy that flushed Margaret’s face was an overwhelming, sensory rush. Her hands trembled as she took the parchment, devouring the ink with her eyes, kissing the rough vellum, and then throwing her arms around Martin’s neck. The heavy silence of her despair was entirely broken.

Desperate to share this resurrection with her savior, she wished to consult Dame Van Eyck but lacked the strength to walk the distance. Martin offered his mule—the very beast he had requisitioned from Ghysbrecht, bringing the cycle of their defiance full circle. 

In the gathering dusk, Margaret mounted the mule. The steady, rhythmic clip-clop of the hooves on the dirt path provided a soothing, ancient baseline to her racing heartbeat. About seven in the evening, she arrived at the house in Tergou, the unwonted roses of health and hope blooming on her cheeks, the scent of the evening air sharp and sweet, and Gerard’s pardon pressed safely against her breast.

Some are old in heart at forty, while others are young at eighty. Margaret Van Eyck’s heart held the perennial, fermented vitality of a winter starter dough, endlessly renewing itself. She loved her young namesake with a youthful ardour that was no mere caprice. She was a woman quick to read the flavor of a character, and in Margaret Brandt, she tasted that rarest of human qualities: an unadulterated genuineness, pure as cold-pressed olive oil. But beyond the girl’s own sterling qualities, Margaret Brandt possessed a potent ally in the elder artist’s bosom: the inherent, mythic rhythm of human nature. 

It is a peculiar truth of our species—one observed by philosophers and poets who chronicle the cyclical harvests of our lives—that our hearts warm more readily to those we have nourished than to those who have nourished us. The hand of vengeance or indifference is often stayed not by the memory of sustenance received, but by the memory of sustenance given. Margaret Van Eyck had been wonderfully kind to the girl; she had broken the quiet acoustic sanctity of her own routines to nurse her, spooning rich, savory broths of healing into her until she was cured. Thus, her heart opened completely to the recipient of her care. She loved her now far more tenderly than she had ever loved Gerard, though it was purely out of regard for him that she had first stepped into the girl’s orbit.

When the elder Margaret saw the rich, flushed rose-madder blooming again in the younger Margaret’s cheeks, brought forth by the arrival of a parchment pardon, she relinquished her own ambitions for Gerard without a murmur.

“Sweetheart,” the old artist said, her voice a soothing murmur that harmonized perfectly with the quiet, rhythmic grinding of pestle against mortar in her studio. “I did desire he should stay in Italy five or six years, to return rich and thoroughly steeped in the old masters' techniques. But your happiness is the hearth fire we must tend first. I see you cannot live without him, so we must draw him home as fast as the season allows.”

“Ah, madam! You read the very marrow of my thoughts.” The young woman hung her head, the color deepening on her face like a ripening plum. “But how to let him know? That passes my skill. He is gone to Italy, but to what part I know not. Stay! He named the cities he should visit. Florence was one, and Rome.” Being a sensible girl, she divined that a letter addressed simply to the vast, untranslatable landscape of “Italy” might miscarry in the dominant, chaotic machinery of the world’s postal routes. She looked imploringly at her elder.

“You are come to the right kitchen at the right time,” said the old lady. “Here was this Hans Memling with me today. He is going to Italy, girl, no later than next week, ‘to improve his hand,’ he says. Not before ’twas needed, I assure you.”

“But how is he to find my Gerard?”

“Why, he knows your Gerard, child. They have broken bread and shared the brown bowl here at my table more than once. Since his trade is the same as Gerard’s, he will forage in the same cities, and soon or late their paths must cross. Wherefore, get you a long letter written, fold this pardon into it, and I will answer for the messenger. In six months at farthest, Gerard shall get it; he will kiss it, slip it into his bosom, and come flying home to your table. What are you smiling at? And why are you smothering me with embraces? Yes, yes! The harvest is coming to my little pearl.”

Meanwhile, in the warm, herb-scented domesticity of the kitchen, Martin sat with a black-jack of dark, foaming ale before him, while Reicht Heynes sat beside him at her spinning wheel. The rhythmic, cyclical *whir-clack* of her spinning provided a flawless acoustic ecology for the sharing of secrets, and she drew information from the old soldier as smoothly as drawing marrow from a roasted bone. 

This Hans Memling was an old pupil of Jan Van Eyck and his sister. He was a painter, though Margaret often sneered at his commercial sensibilities. He possessed one fatal flaw: he loved the sharp, fermented bite of the tavern’s ale far more than was strictly nourishing. This singular penchant kept him from amassing a fortune and frequently drove him back to Margaret Van Eyck’s hearth for a hot meal or a groat. This reliance gave her a rightful claim upon him; she knew he would not trifle with any commission she entrusted to him.

The letter was duly written—a physical vessel of untranslatable subjective longing, bound in the dominant lexicon of ink and wax—and left with Margaret Van Eyck.
