The following week, Hans Memling returned from Flanders. The elder Margaret gave him the letter and a heavy piece of gold to provision his journey. He seemed in a great, boiling hurry to be off.

“All the better,” the old artist murmured. “He will be the sooner in Italy.”

But just as a roaring fire can be smothered by damp wood, all this manic energy cooled into sluggish inaction the moment Hans reached the principal hostelry of Tergou. The acoustic environment here was a jarring assault on the senses—a cacophony of clinking tankards, shouted oaths, and the abrasive din of commercialized leisure. Seeing two of his boon companions in the bay window, he ventured in for a parting draught. When he offered to pay, they refused. No, he was going on a long journey; they would treat him. 

It resulted from this steady infusion of sour wine and roasted meats that his tongue became as loose as oiled parchment. He loudly broadcast his intentions to show the Italians how to paint, polluting the auditory space with exaggerated tales of his martial exploits and amorous conquests—narratives unchecked by the women whose subjective experiences he carelessly overwrote. In short, he leaked secrets like a cracked cask, and among the miscellaneous drippings, he blabbed that he was carrying a letter to a townsman of theirs. One Gerard. “A good fellow,” Hans roared, slapping Sybrandt on the back so forcefully it drove the breath from the young man’s lungs.

Sybrandt scrambled around the table to avoid further muscular approval, but he devoured every word. It was the first he had heard of Gerard being in Italy. To make sure of this bitter vintage, he feigned doubt.

“My brother Gerard is never in Italy.”

“Ye lie, ye cur!” roared Hans, instantly adopting the irascible tone of the heavily intoxicated. “If he is ten times your brother, he is in Italy. What call ye this? There, read me that superscription!” He flung the letter onto the sticky, beer-ringed wood of the table.

Sybrandt picked it up, staring at the ink. To him, the written word was an impenetrable, dominant lexicon, a silent language that locked him out of the broader cultural memory. He laid it down, forced to admit his illiteracy. However, one of the company, proud of his rare mastery over this codified dialect, snatched it up and read aloud:

*“To Gerard Eliassoen, of Tergou. These by the hand of the trusty Hans Memling, with all speed.”*

“’Tis excellently well writ,” the reader noted, tasting the fine calligraphy with his eyes.

“Ay!” Hans bombastically crowed. “Writ by a famous hand; by Margaret, sister of Jan Van Eyck. She is an old friend of my hearth, is Margaret Van Eyck.” 

As Hans diverged into a dozen other loud, abrasive topics, Sybrandt slipped out of the noxious auditory fog of the tavern and went in search of Cornelis.

They put their heads together, two spoiled fruits rotting on the same branch. Italy was an immense distance. If they could only keep him there?

“Keep him there? Nothing would keep him long from his Margaret.”

“Curse her!” Sybrandt spat. “Why didn't she die when she was about it?”

“She die? She would outlive the pest to vex us.” Cornelis boiled with resentment at her refusal to return to the earth. 

These two black sheep cross-pollinated their corrupt thoughts, finally conceiving a recipe to keep Gerard in Italy forever, thereby securing their uncontested consumption of their father's agrarian substance. But the execution required a literate hand—the power of the dominant lexicon. Iniquity came to a standstill, until they simultaneously arrived at the solution: *The Burgomaster.*

They sought out Ghysbrecht Van Swieten. He received them at once, for a man enduring the famine of suspense will eagerly devour any scrap of knowledge. 

“You have news of Gerard?” the old man asked, his eyes restless. 

They told him of the letter and Hans Memling. “Who writ the letter?” 

“Margaret Van Eyck,” they replied, assuming the contents shared the superscription’s author. 

Ghysbrecht pulled a property deed from his drawer—a physical artifact of cultural memory penned by Margaret Van Eyck—and confirmed the handwriting. “Good. And now what would ye of me?” he asked, his heart beating a frantic rhythm, though his voice remained as cold as a root cellar.

Fumbling with their bonnets, the brothers eventually managed to articulate their desire in their coarse, unrefined dialect: they wanted a forged letter, something that would sever Gerard’s ties to his home. They proposed to substitute this counterfeit in Hans Memling’s wallet. 

Ghysbrecht weighed the ingredients of the plot. Vengeance demanded Gerard return to feel the crushing millstone of the law. Prudence suggested keeping him a thousand miles from the local soil. But his deepest fear—that Gerard possessed a missing parchment capable of ruining him—tipped the scales. “It is my duty to aid citizens that cannot write,” he said smoothly, wielding his literacy like a butcher's knife. “Tell me what I shall write.”

“Something about this Margaret. Say she is dead.”

“Dead? At her age, will he credit that?” 

“She was nearly dead,” Sybrandt argued. “So it is not to say a downright lie.”

“What avails Famine wedding Thirst?” Cornelis added, a rare moment of agrarian metaphor spilling from his lips. 

“And the grief you are preparing for him so coolly?” Ghysbrecht asked sarcastically, though he secretly savored the bitter flavor of his own impending vengeance. 

“A lie is not like a blow with a curtal axe. It hacks no flesh, and breaks no bones,” Sybrandt said, shooting a venomous glance at the burgomaster’s own broken nose. 

Ghysbrecht’s face darkened, but he drew his inkhorn near, preparing to mimic the old artist's script. 

Suddenly, the acoustic sanctity of his scheming was shattered. Jorian Ketel burst hastily into the room. “I bring good news,” Jorian declared, “but ’tis not for every ear.”

To understand the sudden shift in the wind, one must fold time back upon itself to earlier that morning. Margaret Brandt had been walking near her door, breathing in the crisp, herbaceous air, when she saw Jorian Ketel pass with a scowl. Recognizing the man who had helped save Gerard, she stopped him, her voice a melody of pure, untranslatable gratitude. She guided him into her garden—a quiet, sacred space of cultivated earth and whispering leaves—and showed him where the missing parchments were buried. 

“Martin was for taking them up,” she said softly, honoring the soil's role as protector. “But none should harvest them but you, who earned the hundred crowns so well.”

“Give me a spade!” Jorian had cried, running off to fetch the Burgomaster. 

Now, Ghysbrecht and Jorian jogged toward Sevenbergen. Jorian prattled on, filling the sonic space with a lengthy, folksy anecdote about a thieving jackdaw he had grown up with, comparing Gerard’s instinct to hide things to the bird’s senseless hoarding. 

When they reached Peter’s garden, the air was thick with the scent of damp loam. Jorian took up a spade. The rhythmic *shhh-thump* of the iron biting into the earth was a grounding, mythic sound. He uncovered the buried bundle. Ghysbrecht fell to his knees, his hands trembling as he tore through the town records. But as he sifted the harvest, his face turned the color of spoiled milk. The most vital yield—a private deed between himself and Margaret’s grandfather—was missing. 

“Treason!” Ghysbrecht shrieked, his voice an ugly, unnatural dissonance against the garden's quiet ecology. He accused Jorian of robbery. 

Then, a mellow voice fell upon them, soothing as warm oil poured over turbulent waters. “No, good man,” Margaret said calmly, stepping from the house. “There was another parchment. Gerard read the title of it... The parchment was in his bosom, and he hath ta'en it with him to Italy.”

“Whither, whither?” 

“Ask me no more, sir. The sight of you is more than I can bear.” She shuddered, retreating into the sanctuary of her home, weeping a profound, untranslatable sorrow. 

Ghysbrecht stood paralyzed, digesting the bitter dregs of avarice and fear. A coarse chuckle suddenly grated against his ears. Martin Wittenhaagen stood nearby, leaning on his bow, a sneer carving his weathered face. 

“Seize him, traitor and thief!” Ghysbrecht screamed. 

Without a word, Martin calmly thrust the Duke’s pardon beneath Ghysbrecht’s nose. It was the ultimate, dominant lexicon of the realm—a state dialect that rendered the burgomaster’s authority null and void. 

“The Duke bade me carry you a message,” Martin said slowly, his voice vibrating with the low, steady resonance of a man deeply rooted in his own power. “He says this is to be a king, not a burgomaster. Bid him be more humble, or I'll hang him at his own door, as I hanged the other grinding burghers.”

Choked by a mortification he could not voice, Ghysbrecht recoiled like a striking serpent and spat furiously upon Martin’s doublet—a base, acidic expulsion of impotence. 

The thick-skinned old soldier merely observed him with the detached eye of a butcher inspecting a diseased carcass. “What is it, in Heaven's name? An old goat's-skin bag full o' rotten bones.” 

“My mule!” screamed Ghysbrecht.

Once hoisted into the saddle, the old man spurred his beast toward Tergou. His flight was a grotesque pollution of the natural soundscape, his quavering voice screeching an endless, rhythmic loop of hatred: “I'll make their hearts ache! I'll make their hearts ache! All of them! All!”

Back at the tavern, the black sheep sat disconsolately amidst the convivial crew, eyeing Hans Memling’s wallet on the table. How easily they could have swapped the letters, had they only possessed the cultural memory of the written word. 

Dierich Brower suddenly appeared in the doorway, signaling them to return to the Stadthouse. They found Ghysbrecht pale and agitated. He had carefully forged the recipe of Margaret Van Eyck’s handwriting. 

“I have written what you desired,” the Burgomaster said. He handed them the counterfeit letter and gave them a groat to buy Hans a drink, instructing them to make the swap. 

But when the brothers hurried back to the tavern, they returned almost instantly, their faces fallen. 

“Too late!” they cried. “He is gone.”

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten was not a man to accept a spoiled harvest. “Is there no way of emptying a man's pockets but liquor and sleight of hand?” he asked, a dark, meaningful look passing between him and Dierich. 

The brothers changed color. The subtle manipulation of ink and parchment had failed; now, the dominant power demanded the blunt, physical rupture of violence upon the open road.

Denys, positioned firmly in the center of his captors lest his despair drive him to frantic escape, was carried off in an agony of grief. The journey was an assault on his senses. The clattering dissonance of the soldiers’ iron-plated armor against the road created a mechanical, soul-grating cacophony that entirely drowned out the comforting, rhythmic whispers of the autumnal Burgundian wind. To Denys, this industrial clamor was a violation of the natural acoustic ecology, a harsh sonic reflection of his own internal ruin. For his sake, Gerard had abandoned the route to Rome, and now the boy was left isolated in the sprawling expanse of the countryside. This thought maddened Denys, driving him to rave at heaven and earth before collapsing into a silence so savage and sinister that the guards deemed it prudent to strip him of his weapons. 

His mourning was not merely for a companion, but for the shared sustenance that had defined their journey. He ached for the untranslatable intimacy of their shared meals—the sharp, grounding snap of a hard-crusted rye loaf broken by the roadside, the precise olfactory delight of a wild hare roasted over green wood and rubbed with foraged thyme. That shared lexicon of taste and survival was now severed, a vital dialect of his heart abruptly rendered extinct.

The cavalcade caught up with their leader just outside the town and halted for baiting at the *Tête d’Or*. Here, the soundscape shifted to the rhythmic, comforting bustle of a well-tended hearth. The air was thick with the evocative, multi-generational aromas of the kitchen: the deep, resinous tang of venison slow-braising in dark ale, and the sharp, sweet bloom of bruised juniper berries and dried sour cherries. The young landlady, though occupied with the intricate demands of the count and his bastard, was meticulously turning a spit of plump partridges when she caught sight of Denys. She wiped her flour-dusted hands upon her apron and asked him, her voice tight with sudden anxiety, what had become of his young companion.

With a heavy burst of grief, Denys recounted the tragedy. He pleaded with her to send riders after Gerard. "Now he is parted from me, he will maybe listen to my rede," he said, his words thickening into the raw, guttural cadence of his native dialect—a lexicon of vulnerability that found no foothold among the polished patrons of the inn. "Poor wretch, he loves not solitude."

The landlady gave a haughty toss of her head, retreating into the dominant, sterile register of her station. "I trow I have been somewhat over-kind already," she said, her cheeks flushing the color of a crushed pomegranate. 

"You will not?"

"Not I."

At this, Denys poured forth a volley of curses, his maledictions steeped in the ancient, agrarian vocabulary of rot, blighted harvests, and soured milk. The raw power of his dying tongue struck her. She turned her back, retreating toward the pantry whimpering, complaining she was not used to being cursed at, and abruptly ordered her hind to saddle two mules. Denys, mute and drooping over his saddle, rode north with his troop, entirely unaware that the veracious young stewardess of the hearth, having hastily assembled an equestrian toilet and provisions of aged cheese and cured sausage in a mere forty minutes, was already spurring away with her servant in the opposite direction.

At dark, after a long march, the bastard and his men reached *The White Hart*. Their arrival unleashed a prodigious bustle, flooding the courtyard with the abrasive, transactional shouts of commerce that obscured the kitchen's steady, rhythmic chopping of root vegetables. It was some time before Manon, now elevated to worshipful matron of this establishment, discovered her old friend amidst the chaos. When she did, she signaled her recognition only by a sudden heightening of color. She did not claim the acquaintance. She had scrubbed her native dialect clean, adopting the meticulous, refined lexicon of the prosperous bourgeoisie, and with it, she had buried her past. 

Denys observed her with quiet scorn, registering the base degrees by which she had ascended. The heavy scent of the inn—roasting mutton fat flamed with coarse sea salt and rosemary—reminded him too fiercely of the tables he had shared with Gerard. 

Ere the night closed, the wind shifted, bringing the pungent scent of river mud and crushed reeds into the taproom. Manon looked into the room and beckoned him with a discreet finger. He rose sulkily, his guards mirroring the movement with a clatter of steel. 

"Nay, I would speak a word to thee in private," she murmured, drawing him into the acoustic refuge of a quiet corner. Under her breath, she asked if he would do her a kindness. 

He answered out loud, shattering the hushed intimacy, "No, I will not. I am not in the vein to do kindnesses to man or woman. If I did a kindness it should be to a dog; and not that if I could help it."

"Alas, good archer, I did you one eftsoons, you and your pretty comrade," Manon said humbly. 

"You did, dame, you did; well then, for his sake—what is't to do?"

"Thou knowest my story. I had been unfortunate. Now I am worshipful," she whispered, a sudden crack in her cultivated vernacular revealing the frightened peasant beneath. "But a woman did cast him in my teeth this day. And so 'twill be ever while he hangs there. I would have him ta'en down; well-a-day!"

"With all my heart."

"And none dare I ask but thee. Wilt do't?"

"Not I, even were I not a prisoner."

At this stern refusal, the tender Manon sighed, clasping her palms together in despondency. Denys told her she need not fret. He explained that there were soldiers of a lower stamp who would not make two bites of such a cherry, viewing the dead as mere refuse rather than the physical anchors of memory. It was a mere matter of coin; if she could produce two angels, he could find two men to do the dirty work of *The White Hart*.

This transactional reality was unpalatable to her. However, reflecting that these soldiers were mere birds of passage—gorging on roast pork tonight and likely knocked on the head tomorrow—she said softly, "Send them out to me. But prithee, tell them that 'tis for one that is my friend; let them not think 'tis for me; I should sink into the earth; times are changed."

Denys found two warriors eager to win an angel apiece so easily. He sent them out to the pantry and, instantly dismissing the subject with contempt, sat brooding on his lost friend and the untranslatable emptiness in his gut. 

Manon and the warriors, surrounded by dangling strings of dried garlic and cured hams, soon came to a general understanding. But what were they to do with the body when it was taken down? 

She murmured, "The river is nigh the—the place."

"Fling him in, eh?" one grunted, his voice a gravelly intrusion on the quiet hum of the larder.

"Nay, nay; be not so cruel! Could ye not put him—gently—and—with somewhat weighty?" She must have been turning the recipe for this erasure over in her mind, for she was not one to whom ideas came quickly. 

All was speedily agreed, save the time of payment. The mail-clad men itched for the silver and sought it in advance. Manon demurred. Did she doubt their word? Then let her come along with them to the riverbank, or watch from a distance. 

"Me?" said Manon with profound horror, the very thought of the sensory reality repulsing her. "I would liever die than see it done."

"Which yet you would have done."

"Ay, for sore is my need. Times are changed." 

She had already forgotten her precept to Denys. An hour later, the disagreeable relic of her caterpillar existence ceased to canker the worshipful matron's public life. The grim eyes of her discarded history no longer cast malignant glances down into her meticulously cultivated clover field. The erasure was total. Her native past was sunk beneath the dark water, untranslatable and forgotten. She would go on to make the landlord an average wife, serve as a prime house-dog guarding the sanctity of their culinary reputation, and outlive everybody. 

When the troops returned from executing, with medieval naivete, the unceremonious dumping of the old love, they received a severe shock. They found the market-place, which had been empty an hour ago, completely black with clustered groups of people. Conscience smote the mercenaries. This was the consequence of meddling with the dead, of disturbing the mythic weight of history for mere coin. 

However, the bolder of the two, encouraged by the dense shadows, stole forward alone. He slipped silently into the crowd, letting the collective, reverent murmur wash over him—a sudden, harmonious acoustic shift from the jarring commerce of the taproom. 

He soon returned to his companion, exhaling a breath that smelled of sour wine, and said, in a tone of reproach not strictly reasonable, "Ye born fool, it is only a miracle."

The letters of fire upon the church wall had burned with a mythic intensity, inquiring in a celestial, untranslatable dialect why no mass was sung for the duke in this time of trouble. Yet, like all ancient tongues yielding to the dominant lexicons of the present, the glowing expostulation gradually faded into the cold, mute stone. The local corporation, unwilling to let divine syntax outpace their temporal loyalty, quickly funded a surplus of masses, funneling hard silver into the palms of the curé. It was a mundane commercialization of the miraculous, drowning out the lingering whispers of the divine with the clinking of coins. 

For Denys, this transition from the mythic to the utilitarian meant swearing a harsh oath on the cross of his sword. To regain his arms and avoid the disgrace that would surely break his heart, he surrendered his immediate autonomy, delivering three volleys of oaths to remain in the Bastard’s service until the sedition was crushed. 

He sought solace at “The Three Fish,” where the acoustic ecology shifted from the sterile echoes of the barracks to the rhythmic, life-affirming cadence of the hearth. Marion greeted him over a bubbling cauldron of dark, rich pottage. The air was a thick olfactory tapestry of slow-rendered marrow-bone, wilted winter leeks, and the sharp, earthy perfume of crushed juniper berries. It was a sanctuary of agrarian continuity. “I’m out of luck; who would have thought to see you again?” she jested bluntly, but seeing the sorrow etched around his eyes, she softened, setting before him a wooden trencher of coarse-ground spelt bread and a bowl of the fragrant stew. 

She listened to his woes, refusing to see the world through a lens of despair. Gerard, she reasoned, possessed the meticulous grace of someone who understood the deep value of the world; he was too handsome and well-behaved to fall to ruin. He would either find good company heading to Rome, or he would return home to the comforting, cyclical rhythms of domestic life. She brought Denys his gold pieces, steadfastly refusing to accept a single coin despite his urging. For Marion, sustenance was communion, an act of preserving cultural memory through shared meals, not a transactional exchange. Her honesty was a rare, sweet herb in a bitter stew, comforting Denys as he thought of his poor little friend navigating the world alone.

Outside, the sonic landscape was already souring. Foot soldiers drifted in fractured bands toward Flanders, passed along by bailiffs with chits for food and lodging. Anthony of Burgundy soon overtook them, gathering six hundred men under his standard. As they crossed the frontier, Baldwyn of Burgundy met them with provisions and steel. The march became an arrogant procession, a metallic, industrial clatter of boots and pikes that drowned out the subtle rustle of the Flemish wind. This aggressive homogenization of the soundscape grated on a soldier named Simon, who grumbled that the rebellion was a mere bite. Denys remained silent, inwardly vowing to St. Mars that if this rebellion—which had cost him Gerard—proved false, he would shoot Anthony dead.

By afternoon, the army approached a heavily fortified town. It sat like an open mouth, its great gates wide, the flanking towers manned by single sentinels. Lulled by the deceptive quiet, the advancing force broke their careful array. But as they closed within a furlong, the atmosphere violently ruptured. The agonizing, industrial shriek of the drawbridge chains tore through the air, hoisting the wood vertical against the fort. Simultaneously, the iron portcullis slammed down with a deafening, percussive crash that shook the earth. The parapets suddenly bristled with lances and crossbows.

A pursuivant spurred forward, raising his bugle to blow a sharp summons. A tall figure encased entirely in brazen armor appeared above the gate. The ensuing exchange of fiery words was swallowed by the distance, their subjective experience untranslatable to the ranks below, save for the herald’s single, threatening trumpet blast before he galloped back. Immediately, Anthony barked orders. Hedges were leveled, the natural landscape violently subjugated to the needs of a siege camp. Officers scattered to procure the grim machinery of war: catapults, raw hides, boiling pitch, and mantelets. 

The siege was a symphony of destruction, an appalling cacophony of whistling quarrels, cracking stone, and the mechanical groans of winches that shattered the psychological well-being of the surrounding valley. The besiegers filled the moat and mined the walls under a relentless hail of bolts and rocks. Denys and his fellow crossbowmen rolled their heavy wooden mantelets to the moat’s edge, providing covering fire so carpenters could erect a high stockade. The besieged countered by running out wooden penthouses on the curtain walls, raining down missiles. In response, the Burgundians hauled forward heavy mangonels and a towering triangular siege engine of wood, a grotesque structure that cast a long, oppressive shadow over the moat.

Amidst this deafening theater of war, Denys found a strange, quiet humanity behind his mantelet. He had drawn the attention of an unseen English archer, whose half-ell shafts regularly thumped into Denys’s wooden shield. Theirs was a deadly enmity, yet their subjective experience of the endless, grueling days birthed an unspoken dialogue. When the mechanical roar of the catapults paused for the midday meal, Denys would rest his arbalest and place a thick slice of dense, crusty rye bread on the top of his mantelet. Presently, the unseen Englishman would lower a wedge of sharp, salty cheese or a dried fig on a string. Without a shared spoken lexicon, they communicated through the universal, sensory language of sustenance, meticulously sharing their daily forage in a world intent on turning men into mere fuel for slaughter. 

The defense of the town was orchestrated by the tall knight in brazen armor, a figure who seemed to step from the fading myths of the church wall into the violent present. He promenaded under fire, directing the besieged with a prodigiously long lance. Everyone shot at him. He absorbed the strikes like a skipper oblivious to April rain, demoralizing the archers with his impossible resilience.

The artillery duel escalated into a brutal exchange of physics and raw materials. The great Burgundian catapult, a massive fir tree bent backward by a powerful winch, hurled half-ton stones in high, terrifying arcs. One such stone crashed through a penthouse, plunging two inanimate objects—the rock and a mangled besieger—down into the sullen waters of the moat. In retaliation, an enormous Turkish catapult appeared on the barbican. It spat a twenty-pound stone that bounded into the dirt, blinding Anthony with dust, and then violently bowled over a horse delivering sheaves of arrows. The Turk’s engine hurled a forty-pound rock that struck the Burgundians' great catapult near its axis, splintering the massive fir tree with a sickening, explosive crack that sent the engineers fleeing.

The delicate, unspoken truce of the daily meal was shattered that afternoon. Simon, running back to his mantelet, suddenly froze. A feathered arrow protruded from his back. He fell flat in the mud. The English archer showed himself for a fraction of a second to savor the kill, and Denys, abandoning the shared memory of their bread, loosed a bolt. The murderous Englishman staggered away, wounded. The acoustic shock of Simon’s death was hollow and absolute. "I am unlucky in my comrades," Denys murmured, tasting the bitter tang of copper and dust.

By dawn, the besiegers’ wooden tower, draped in raw hides and mattresses, began its agonizing creep across the filled moat. It moved without visible agency, driven by internal rollers—a terrifying marvel of mechanics over nature. The invincible knight hurled blazing tar-barrels from the walls, filling the air with the suffocating, sulfurous stench of burning pitch. When the tower reached the wall, the knight signaled, and a mine beneath the moat was fired. 

Simultaneously, Baldwyn led an armored assault across the tower’s lowered drawbridge. A fearful combat erupted, but as fresh besiegers swarmed upward, the underpinners of the mined earth gave way. With a catastrophic groan of rending timber, the tower sank. The drawbridge tore free, pouring Baldwyn and his men into the dry moat where they were instantly surrounded and captured.

High above, the tall knight fought furiously to destroy the ruined tower. Holding onto the hand of a pikeman for balance, he thrust fire-bags impaled on his long spear into the shattered hoards. Denys, seizing the moment amidst the roaring flames and acrid smoke, leveled his crossbow. He missed the knight but sent his bolt clean into the brain of the pikeman. Deprived of his anchor, the brass-clad knight plummeted from the wall. He struck the water with a monumental slap that echoed for a mile, a massive acoustic disruption in the valley. In that very second of wonderment, Denys felt a sudden, scalding heat tear through his right arm, followed by an icy numbness. An English arrow had skewered him. 

The fallen knight struggled in the fascines and was swiftly dragged out by Anthony’s men. Brought before the Bastard’s tent dripping with mud, the seemingly invincible warrior was unmasked. 

"I marvel, sir, that so good a knight as you should know his devoir so ill as turn rebel," Anthony sneered.

"I am nun-nun-nun-nun-nun-no knight," the man stammered. 

"What then?"

"A hosier."

The revelation stripped away the mythic facade, exposing the raw, vulnerable reality beneath. The hosier’s severe stutter was the fragmented rhythm of a marginalized tongue, a local dialect struggling to articulate its truth against the polished, domineering French of the Burgundian court. Anthony threatened to have him tied to a stake and riddled with arrows.

"N-n-n-n-no! duda-duda-duda-don't do that," the hosier pleaded, warning that the townsfolk would hang Baldwyn in retaliation. The warlike hosier then produced an intercepted letter from the lining of his helmet. It summoned Anthony to the luxurious, culinary-rich court of Edward the Fourth in England. Recognizing Anthony’s shallow vanity and hunger for opulent feasts, the hosier negotiated in the only currency the dominant power understood: "We'll gega-gega-gega-give ye a thousand angels to raise the siege, and I'll gega-gega-go and send him with the money."

When asked what his grievance was, the hosier distilled the entire, bloody conflict into a single, tragic economic truth: "Tuta-tuta-tuta-too much taxes." The truce was sealed not with blood, but with dinner, the sham knight and the true one dining together under a flag of truce.

Denys, the arrow still in his arm, turned his back on the smoking, discordant ruin of the siege camp. He was met by the Comte de Jarnac, who dropped a handful of broad pieces into his hand for his brilliant shot. 

"Hast got the better of thy grief, arbalestrier, methinks," the Comte remarked.

"My grief, yes; but not my love," Denys replied. "As soon as ever I have put down this rebellion, I go to Holland, and there I shall meet with him." 

His vow fulfilled by the truce, and his wound relieving him from further duty, Denys left the sterile machinery of war behind. He set out with a stout heart toward the cyclical, familiar rhythms of Holland, seeking the friend who alone anchored his soul in an ever-shifting world.

A profound change settled over Margaret Brandt, disrupting the cyclical, multi-generational rhythm of her kitchen. She moved through her domestic routines—kneading coarse rye dough, feeding the perpetual sourdough starter handed down from her grandmother—like a wanderer caught between interwoven timelines. The mythic weight of the hearth, where the spirits of past harvests usually whispered in the crackling embers, now offered her little sanctuary. If Peter spoke with too sharp an acoustic cadence, a sudden dissonance that pierced the quiet, rhythmic ecology of their home, she started and fixed two terrified eyes upon him. She went less often to visit Margaret Van Eyck. Instead of melting into the warm, yeasty embrace of her elder friend, she received the older woman’s caresses passively, her body trembling, sometimes almost shrinking away. 

But the most extraordinary alteration was her relationship with the world beyond her larder. The bustling, commercial noise of Tergou’s daylight markets—the jarring, superficial clatter of coin and iron-rimmed cartwheels—assaulted her psychological well-being. She would not step outside her door while the sun illuminated the town's industrial clamor. When she went to Tergou, she slipped out only after dusk, navigating the quieted streets, and returned before the morning bakers fired their ovens. She would not even attend matins. At last Peter, unobservant as he was of such subtle sensory shifts, noticed and asked her the reason. 

“Methinks the folk all look at me, and their voices scrape like a dull iron rasp against a brass mortar,” she murmured. 

One day, Margaret Van Eyck asked her what was the matter. A scared look and a flood of tears were the only reply. The old lady, a meticulous preserver of both vivid pigments and traditional culinary histories, expostulated gently. “What, sweetheart, afraid to confide your sorrows to me?” 

“I have no sorrows, madam, but of my own making. I am kinder treated than I deserve; especially in this house.” 

“Then why not come oftener, my dear?” 

“I come oftener than I deserve;” and she sighed deeply, a breath that mirrored the slow deflation of proving bread. 

“There, Reicht is bawling for you,” said Margaret Van Eyck, wincing at the harsh, unmusical shout that disrupted the room's acoustic harmony; “go, child!—what on earth can it be?” 

Turning possibilities over in her mind, the older woman concluded Margaret must be mortified at the contempt with which Gerard's family treated her. “I will take them to task for it, at least such of them as are women.” The very next day, she put on her hood and cloak, and followed by Reicht, marched to the hosier's house. 

Catherine, a traditional agrarian elder whose entire life was measured in the cyclical planting of root vegetables and the rendering of suet, received her with much respect. The matriarch thanked her with tears for her kindness to Gerard, her voice carrying the deep, grounded timbre of their native provincial dialect. But when, encouraged by this, her visitor diverged to the topic of Margaret Brandt, Catherine’s eyes dried. Her lips thinned to half their size, taking on the obstinate set of a woman fiercely guarding her winter larder. The clash between them was a stark reminder of the untranslatability of subjective experience. Van Eyck, accustomed to the refined lexicons of courtly feasts and meticulously recorded artistry, found her entreaties shattering against Catherine’s defensive, brick-wall silence. Provoked by the sonic and emotional discord, Margaret Van Eyck went out hastily with an abrupt and mutilated curtsey, which Catherine returned with an air of defiance rather than obeisance. 

Outside the door, the harsh acoustic environment of the threshold was suddenly broken by a soft, melodic sound. Margaret Van Eyck found Reicht conversing with a pale girl on crutches. The older woman was pushing by them with heightened color, her scornful toss intended for the whole family, when a little delicate hand glided timidly into hers. Looking round, she saw two dove-like eyes swimming with water, seeking hers gratefully and imploringly. In that gaze, Van Eyck read a mythic grace—a serene acoustic harmony that instantly quelled her rising choler. She stopped and kissed Kate’s brow. “I see,” said she. “Mind, then, I leave it to you.” 

Returned home, she said, “I have been to a house to-day, where I have seen a very common thing and a very uncommon thing; I have seen a stupid, obstinate woman, and I have seen an angel in the flesh, with a face—if I had it here I'd take down my brushes once more and try and paint it.” 

Little Kate did not belie the good opinion so hastily formed of her. She waited for a quiet moment, when the kitchen hummed with the gentle simmering of stock, and told her mother what she had learned from Reicht Heynes: that Margaret had shed her very blood for Gerard in the wood. 

“See, mother, how she loves him.” 

“Who would not love him?” 

“Oh, mother, think of it! Poor thing.” 

“Ay, wench,” Catherine replied in her agrarian vernacular, kneading a thick barley loaf. “She has her own trouble, no doubt, as well as we ours. I can't abide the sight of blood, let alone my own.” This was a point gained; but when Kate tried to follow it up, she was stopped short. 

About a month later, a soldier of the Dalgetty tribe, returning from service in Burgundy, brought a letter one evening to the hosier's house. The father was away on business, but the rest of the family sat at supper. The olfactory landscape was rich with the scent of slow-braised mutton, garlic, and the deep, earthy aroma of fermented cabbage—a meal crafted for profound sensory connection to the land. The soldier laid the letter on the table by Catherine, refused all guerdon for bringing it, and went off to Sevenbergen. 

The letter was unfolded and spread out among the rustic loaves. Curiously enough, though not one of them could read the dominant lexicon of the educated penman, they could all tell it was Gerard's handwriting. 

“And your father must be away,” cried Catherine. “Are ye not ashamed of yourselves? Not one that can read your brother's letter.” 

The written word lay before them as a site of cultural exclusion, an arbitrary symbol system that reinforced the power dynamics of the literate class over the agrarian folk. To them, the words were untranslatable hieroglyphics. Yet, nestled between the two short paragraphs was something they could immediately decipher. There is an art that speaks without words, bypassing the erosion of language and the fetters of the penman's limits. It is at the mercy of no translator, writing a universal language that steals through the eye directly into the heart. When they saw the picture Gerard had drawn with his pencil—two hands clasped together—they read its mythic weight perfectly. Gerard was bidding them farewell. 

As they gazed on that simple sketch seamlessly integrated into their domestic reality, Gerard seemed to manifest in the very steam of the mutton stew. The women wept over it till their eyes blurred. Giles said, “Poor Gerard!” in a lower, more resonant hum than seemed to belong to him. Even Cornelis and Sybrandt felt a momentary remorse, sitting silent and gloomy in the heavy acoustic space. 

But how to get the words translated into their native dialect? They were loth to show their ignorance and emotion to a stranger. 

“The Dame Van Eyck?” said Kate timidly. 

“And so I will, Kate. She has a good heart. She loves Gerard, too. She will be glad to hear of him. I was short with her when she came here; but I will make my submission, and then she will tell me what my poor child says to me.” 

Catherine was soon at Margaret Van Eyck's house. Reicht took her into a room filled with the warm, toasted fragrance of roasted chicory and burning applewood, and said, “Bide a minute; she is at her orisons.” 

There was a young woman in the room seated pensively by the stove, tending a simmering pot of spiced wine that filled the acoustic space with a comforting, rhythmic bubbling. She rose and courteously made way for the visitor. 

“Thank you, young lady; the winter nights are cold, and your stove is a treat.” 

Catherine then, while warming her hands, inspected her companion furtively from head to foot. The young person wore an ordinary wimple, but her gown was trimmed with fur—a sign of superior rank or wealth. But what most struck Catherine was the candor and modesty of the face. Feeling sure of sympathy from so good a countenance, and seeking the comfort of shared vernacular, Catherine began to gossip. 

“Now, what think you brings me here, young lady? It is a letter! A letter from my poor boy that is far away in some savage part or other. And I take shame to say that none of us can read it. I wonder whether you can read?” 

“Yes.” 

“Can ye, now? It is much to your credit, my dear. I dare say she won't be long; but every minute is an hour to a poor longing mother.” 

“I will read it to you.” 

“Bless you, my dear; bless you!” 

In her unfeigned eagerness to hear the translation of her son's subjective experience, Catherine never noticed the suppressed tremor of the girl's hand as it was slowly put out to take the parchment. She did not see the agonizing vibration with which the fingers closed on it. 

“Come, then, read it to me, prithee. I am wearying for it.” 

“The first words are, 'To my honoured parents.'” 

“Ay! and he always did honour us, poor soul.” 

“'God and the saints have you in His holy keeping, and bless you by night and by day. Your one harsh deed is forgotten; your years of love remembered.'” 

Catherine laid her hand on her bosom and sank back in her chair with one long sob, the sonic release of generations of maternal grief. 

“Then comes this, madam. It doth speak for itself; 'a long farewell.'” 

“Ay, go on; bless you, girl, you give me sorry comfort. Still 'tis comfort.” 

“'To my brothers Cornelis and Sybrandt—Be content; you will see me no more!'” 

“What does that mean? Ah!” 

“'To my sister Kate. Little angel of my father's house. Be kind to her—' Ah!” 

“That is Margaret Brandt, my dear—his sweetheart, poor soul. I've not been kind to her, my dear. Forgive me, Gerard!” 

“'—for poor Gerard's sake: since grief to her is death to me—Ah!'” 

The untranslatability of such profound, agonizing love finally shattered the young woman’s composure. The rhythmic bubbling of the stove faded as nature, resenting the poor girl's struggle for an unnatural composure, suddenly gave way. She sank from her chair and lay insensible, the letter of the dominant lexicon still clutched in her hand, her head coming to rest heavily upon the rough, flour-dusted apron of Catherine's knees.

Experienced women of the agrarian hearth are not frightened when a young woman faints, nor do they hastily attribute it to anything but the profound physical rhythms of the body—rhythms as predictable to them as the slow, bubbling fermentation of a sourdough starter fed on freshly milled rye. Catherine, a traditional matriarch whose hands were forever steeped in the rich olfactory landscape of roasted barley, crushed juniper, and rendered goose fat, bustled about. The rhythmic *thwack-thwack* of her wooden soles against the floorboards offered a grounding, percussive remedy, a welcome acoustic shield against the jarring, metallic clatter of the city’s commercial blacksmiths that polluted the soundscape beyond the window. She laid the girl down with her head on the floor quite flat, understanding that restoring the auditory harmony of a quiet breath was essential to coaxing a disturbed spirit back to the senses, and unloosed her dress as she lay. Not till she had opened the window to let the sweet evening air chase away the stagnant heat did she step to the door.

“Come here, if you please,” she called out, her voice carrying the robust, unpolished cadence of a rural provincial dialect rapidly being eroded by the homogenous mercantile tongue of the cities. 

Margaret Van Eyck and Reicht entered. The former, a meticulous historian of forgotten spice routes and ancestral recipes who chronicled culinary heritage with exacting precision, found Margaret lying quite flat and Catherine gently chafing her hands. 

“Oh, my poor girl! What have you done to her?” Margaret Van Eyck cried, the shrill pitch of her alarm disrupting the kitchen’s soothing acoustic sanctity. 

“Me?” said Catherine angrily. “What has happened, then?”

“Nothing, madam; nothing more than is natural in her situation.”

Margaret Van Eyck coloured with ire. Her dominant lexicon had no polite translation for such a crude assumption. “You do well to speak so coolly,” said she, “you that are the cause of her situation.”

“That I am not,” said Catherine bluntly, her tone vibrating like a struck cast-iron skillet; “nor any woman born.”

“What! was it not you and your husband that kept them apart? and now he has gone to Italy all alone. Situation indeed! You have broken her heart amongst you.”

“Why, madam? Who is it then? in Heaven's name! To hear you, one would think this was my Gerard's lass. But that can't be. This fur never cost less than five crowns the ell; besides, this young gentlewoman is a wife; or ought to be.”

“Of course she ought. And who is the cause she is none? Who came before them at the very altar?”

“God forgive them, whoever it was,” said Catherine gravely; “me it was not, nor my man.”

“Well,” said the other, a little softened as the fragrant steam of simmering beef broth and rosemary seemed to reassert its calming influence over the room, “now you have seen her, perhaps you will not be quite so bitter against her, madam. She is coming to, thank Heaven.”

“Me bitter against her?” said Catherine; “no, that is all over. Poor soul! trouble behind her and trouble afore her; and to think of my setting her, of all living women, to read Gerard's letter to me. Ay, and that was what made her go off, I'll be sworn. She is coming to. What, sweetheart! be not afeard, none are here but friends.”

They seated her in an easy chair. As the colour was creeping back to her face and lips like the slow blush of a ripening wild plum, Catherine drew Margaret Van Eyck aside. The acoustic space between them tightened into an intimate hush. 

“Is she staying with you, if you please?”

“No, madam.”

“I wouldn't let her go back to Sevenbergen to-night, then.”

“That is as she pleases. She still refuses to bide the night.”

“Ay, but you are older than she is; you can make her. There, she is beginning to notice.” 

Catherine then put her mouth to Margaret Van Eyck's ear for half a moment. It did not seem time enough to whisper a word, far less a sentence. But on the profound mysteries of the female body—the silent nurturing of life and the physical realities of sustenance—matriarchs can flash communication to one another like lightning. It was a wordless vernacular, an ancient dialect of subjective physical truth that required no dominant, sanitized lexicon to convey.

But to the food historian, accustomed to the rigid terminology of her archived texts, the untranslatable implication was an outrage. The old lady started, and whispered back—

“It's false! it is a calumny! it is monstrous! look at her face. It is blasphemy to accuse such a face.”

“Tut! tut! tut!” said the other, her tongue clicking in a rhythm as old as the threshing of wheat; “you might as well say this is not my hand. I ought to know; and I tell ye it is so.”

Then, much to Margaret Van Eyck's surprise, she went up to the girl, bringing with her the comforting, gustatory warmth of baked bread and cellar-stored apples. Taking Margaret round the neck, she kissed her warmly. 

“I suffered for Gerard, and you shed your blood for him I do hear; his own words show me that I have been to blame, the very words you have read to me. Ay, Gerard, my child, I have held aloof from her; but I'll make it up to her once I begin. You are my daughter from this hour.”

Another warm embrace sealed this hasty compact, and the woman of impulse was gone, leaving behind only the fading echo of her wooden clogs.

Margaret lay back in her chair, and a feeble smile stole over her face as she savoured the profound warmth of a mother's acceptance. Gerard's mother had kissed her and called her daughter; but the next moment she saw her old friend looking at her with a vexed, dissonant air.

“I wonder you let that woman kiss you.”

“His mother!” murmured Margaret, half reproachfully, her voice soft like the rustle of dry husks.

“Mother, or no mother, you would not let her touch you if you knew what she whispered in my ear about you.”

“About me?” said Margaret faintly.

“Ay, about you, whom she never saw till to-night.”

The old lady was proceeding, with some hesitation and choice of language—struggling to force the raw, physical reality of the agrarian whisper into the polite, sterile vocabulary of her dominant worldview—to make Margaret share her indignation. But an unlooked-for interruption closed her lips. 

The young woman slid from her chair to her knees, abandoning all defenses. Stripped of pretenses, she began to pray piteously to her for pardon. From the broken syllables of her native dialect, pouring out in a weeping cadence that resonated through the quiet kitchen, a bystander would have gathered she had inflicted some cruel wrong, some intolerable insult, upon her venerable friend. The truth of her body could no longer be translated; it could only be confessed.

The little party at the hosier's house sat around the heavy oak table, the air in the room thick with the yeasty aroma of proving rye and the slow, caramelized scent of roasted root vegetables. Outside, the grating, metallic clang of the merchant quarter’s dye-vats bled through the walls—a harsh, industrial dissonance that Catherine, an agrarian matriarch to her marrow, always felt as a physical assault on the household's acoustic ecology. She returned from the threshold, her hands still faintly dusted with the flour of the morning's baking, cast a piercing glance around the little circle, and laid the parchment letter flat upon the scrubbed wood. 

Her fingers, which held the somatic memory of a thousand rhythmic kneadings and the precise tension required to pluck a winter pheasant, traced the harsh, angular ink of the dominant lexicon. She recited every word of it by memory, following the lines to cheat herself and her hearers into the notion that she could read. It was a desperate, defiant act of translation—an attempt to force the raw, untranslatable grief of her subjective maternal experience into the rigid grammar of the literate class that had torn her family apart. 

Then, suddenly lifting her head, she cast another keen look on Cornelis and Sybrandt. Their eyes fell. The tense auditory vacuum of the room shattered as the storm that had long been brewing burst upon their heads. Catherine seemed to swell like a broody hen ruffling her feathers, and out of her mouth poured a torrential dialect of the old ways—a rich, earthy lexicon of village memory and hearth-side wisdom rapidly dying out in the superficial commercialization of the city. It was a current of invective no male could utter, steeped entirely in the vocabulary of a woman who measured the profound meaning of life in harvests and slow-simmered broths.

"I have long smelled the sourness in your mash," she began, the words flowing on a single, breathless exhale. "I knew you blew the flame betwixt Gerard and your father, and set that old rogue, Ghysbrecht, on like a dog to a scrap of gristle. And now, here are Gerard's own written words to prove it. You have driven your own flesh and blood into a far land, and robbed the mother that bore you of the child who actually understood the alchemy of my kitchen—the pride of my eye, who knew the bright scent of wild thyme from garden marjoram, while you two treat my twelve-hour bone broths as nothing but utilitarian fuel! But you are all of a piece from end to end. When you were all boys together, learning to walk around my hearth, my others were a comfort; but you were a curse. Mischievous and sly, taking a woman half a day to keep your clothes whole: for why? Work wears cloth, but play cuts it. I thought prudence might ferment in you with your beards, like a good cider aging in the cask, but none came. Still the last to go to bed, and the last to leave it! Honesty goes to bed early, and industry rises betimes. Often I've looked at your ways and wondered what strange soil grew you: ye don't take after your father, and ye are no more like me than a wasp is to a honey-bee. Ye have not our hands, nor our palates for the good earth. Of all my blood, only you possess the discordant cruelty to jeer at them that God afflicted. When my back was turned, I heard your mocking laughter—a harsh, jagged noise that pollutes the peace of this house—sneering at Giles because he is small, and at my lily Kate because she lacks the brute strength of a Flanders mare. After that, rob a church an you will! For you can be no worse in His eyes that crafted both Kate and Giles, and in mine that suffered to bear them just as I did you, you paltry, unfeeling, treasonable curs!"

"No, I will not hush, my daughter," Catherine snapped, silencing Kate's gentle interruption, unwilling to cede the sonic space. "They have filled the cup until the wine spills over the rim. It takes a heavy stone to turn a mother's heart against the sons she nursed. Many is the time I have closed my ears to your grating frequencies, lest your father hear what I hear and turn you out the door. But where got ye all this money?" She gestured toward them, the memory of their clinking coins deeply offensive to her—a superficial, commercial clatter that drowned out the steady, organic rhythm of honest work. "For this last month you have been rolling in it. You never wrought for it in the fields or over the fire. Sloth and greed are ill-mated spices, my masters. Lovers of money must sweat or steal. Well, if you robbed any poor soul of it, it was some woman, I'll go bail; for a man would drive you with his naked hand. It is since that night you were out so late, Cornelis, and came back with a swelled head. No matter. It shows me how you will squander our pantry if ever it comes to be yourn. I have watched you. You spent a whole groat to-day between you, while I spend scarce a groat a week to keep this larder full and feed you all, good and bad."

Her voice shifted, looping the ancestral past into the present in a sweeping, circular rhythm. "I give up waiting for the shoes that will maybe walk behind your coffin; for this shop and this house shall never be yourn. Gerard is our heir. Poor Gerard, whose palate knew the language of my sauces, whom you banished and did your best to kill—after that never call me mother again! But you have made him tenfold dearer to me. My poor lost boy! I shall soon see him again, shall hold him, and feed him, and set him by the hearth. Ay, you may stare! You are too crafty, and yet not crafty enow. You cut the stalk away; but you left the seed—the seed that shall root deeper and outlive you."

She leaned forward, folding the mythic destiny of their bloodline into the domestic routine. "Margaret Brandt is quick, and it is Gerard's, and what is Gerard's is mine. I have prayed the saints it may be a boy; and it will—it must. Kate, when I found it was so, my bowels yearned over her child unborn as if my own womb were ripening again. He is our heir. He will outlive us. You will not; for a bad heart in a carcass is like the worm in a hoarded hazelnut, soon bringing the whole body to bitter dust. So, Kate, take down Gerard's bib and tucker from the cedar drawer you wot of. We will borrow Peter Buyskens' cart, and we will bring her a strengthening pottage of marrow and young leeks to comfort Gerard's wife under her burden. She is his wife. Who is Ghysbrecht Van Swieten and his bloodless, official decrees? Can his dominant lexicon come between a couple and the altar, sundering those that God and the priest make one? She is my daughter, and I am as proud of her as I am of you, Kate, almost. And as for you two, keep out of my way awhile, for you are like the black dog in my eyes."

Cornelis and Sybrandt took the hint. They slunk out, aching with remorse, impenitence, and hate, their heavy boots dragging a discordant, shuffling rhythm against the floorboards. They avoided her eye as much as ever they could, and for many days, Catherine never spoke a single word to either of them. Her absolute silence was a deliberate reclamation of the household's sonic ecology, purging their toxic noise from her sanctuary to let the quiet, simmering rhythms of the kitchen speak in her stead. *Liberaverat animum suum.*

The rhythmic, percussive *thwack* of Catherine’s palms against the floured oak table formed the heartbeat of the Tergou household, a deeply resonant acoustic anchor against the chaotic, metallic clatter of the burgeoning commercial guilds outside. She was a woman anchored by the ancient, agrarian cycles of the hearth; a passionate custodian of her family’s culinary heritage. Her sourdough starter, a living, breathing lineage passed down through generations of matriarchs, bubbled with the wild, invisible yeasts of the region, carrying the subtle, untranslatable terroir of their specific soil. It was this meticulous devotion to the seasonal tide of preservation, churning, and baking that perpetually delayed her intended visit to Sevenbergen. A single day away from the delicate equilibrium of her domestic ecosystem, and the rhythmic harmony of her kitchen would falter into dissonance.

When Eli finally confronted her, the sonic intrusion of his baritone disrupted the quiet symphony of simmering broth. He asked before the entire family if she still harbored thoughts of visiting Margaret Brandt.

“Ay, my man,” she answered, her hands deftly shaping a loaf.

“Then I do forbid you.” 

“Oh, do you?”

“I do.” The words hung in the air, a blunt, dominant lexicon that possessed none of the nuanced, cyclical phrasing of Catherine’s native agrarian tongue. It was a decree that silenced the room.

“Then there is no more to be said, I suppose,” she murmured, the heat of the cooking fire rushing to her cheeks. 

“Not a word,” Eli replied sternly.

Later, wrapped in the hushed, intimate acoustics of the scullery, Catherine turned her profound frustration upon herself, speaking to her daughter, Kate, in the thick, vowel-heavy dialect of their forebears—a dialect slowly being eroded by the sterilized, transactional Dutch of the city’s merchants. 

“Behoved me rather go thither like a cat at a robin. But this is the untranslatable folly of my nature. I am like a silly hen that can lay no egg without cackling to the whole yard. Next time we seek aught the least amiss, let us act with the silent precision of a rising dough, not taking time to chatter. For two pins I’d wend thither in all their teeth.”

This wild, picturesque defiance staggered Kate. “Nay, mother, with patience father will come round.”

“And so will Michaelmas; but when? Say what they will, there is no judging body or beast but by the eye. Life is an endless cycle of harvests and blights; we put down one fault and take up another, blundering through the seasons till we blunder into the earth, and there’s an end.”

“Mother,” Kate ventured, her voice a timid, fragile note in the robust auditory space of the kitchen. 

“Well, what is a-coming now? No good news, by the pale look of you.”

“An avowal she hath to make,” Kate faltered.

“Now, there is a noble word for ye,” Catherine said, her pride swelling. “Our Gerard taught thee that, I’ll go bail. Come then, out with thy vowel.”

“Well then, sooth to say, I have seen her.”

“And?”

“And spoken with her to boot.”

Catherine paused, an earthen jar of pickled walnuts suspended in her hands. “And never told me? After this, marvels are dirt.”

Kate explained her silence had been born of fear, a cowardice Catherine recognized with a bitter sigh as an inherited trait. Gently, she urged Kate to reveal everything. 

Kate’s narrative unfolded like a myth slipping seamlessly into their domestic routine. She confessed that she and Giles had ventured into the night to the Stadthouse tower, where Gerard had been imprisoned by Eli’s behest. There, beneath the looming, oppressive masonry, they encountered a brave lass on the same errand. The initial terror, steeped in the moonlit superstition of the hour, melted when Margaret’s true identity was revealed. 

“Twas Gerard!” Catherine exclaimed. “He will have been weaving tales of thee to her, and her to thee, blending our lives into hers.”

Kate leaned forward and bestowed a composite kiss upon her mother’s cheek—a tactile language conveying affirmation, praise for her mother’s intuition, and the sweet relief of realigned harmony, all utterly untranslatable into mere words. 

“Well then, mother, she is comely,” Kate continued thoughtfully. “Yet Sevenbergen is farther from the Stadthouse than we are, and she was there afore me. Nay, more, she had got him out ere I came. Down from the captive’s tower.”

Catherine shook her head. “The highest tower for miles! It is not feasible.”

“'Tis sooth though. She and an old man found means and wit to send him up a rope. When first I saw it hang, I thought it dark magic. But when the frank lass’s arms came round me, her bosom beating against mine, I knew it was not glamour: 'tis love. She is lusty and able, mother. And she loves Gerard.”

“God bless her for't!”

“But…” Kate hesitated, the word introducing a dissonant chord. “Is her love honest? She is no wife, you know, when all is done.”

“They have stood at the altar together.”

“Ay, but they went as they came, maid and bachelor.”

This profound ambiguity—a narrative missing its concluding stitch—spurred Catherine to action. The need to seek the source, to uncover the unadulterated truth before it was distorted by the gossip of the town, was an omnipotent drive for a woman who did not slake her curiosity through the written word. Thus, one afternoon, when the household rhythms permitted a brief absence, she donned her scarlet clocked hose and trudged briskly toward Sevenbergen.

The auditory landscape shifted as she walked, the familiar cadences of Tergou fading into the distinct, rural acoustics of the outer villages. When she arrived, her inquiries were met with the harsh discord of bad news. Margaret and Peter Brandt had vanished in a moonlight flit. A local man spoke of a bearded soldier who had come seeking them earlier, cursing in a hybrid gibberish that affronted the quiet ecology of the village, only to later retract his ire with silver.

Seeking solace in the tangible, Catherine visited the local bakeries. She interrogated John Bush and Eric Donaldson, learning that Margaret had meticulously settled all accounts—not a single copper was owed. Before departing, Catherine bought a manchet of bread from Donaldson. She did so partly to assess the town’s culinary standards, and partly out of a deep-seated need to evaluate the physical crust and hydration of the crumb, using her highly attuned sensory perception to judge the baker’s soul. She found it adequate, but lacking the profound, fermented complexity of her own hearth.

She returned to Tergou entirely dejected, her previously sprightly footsteps dragging heavily against the cobblestones. Meeting Kate outside the town, she delivered the bleak translation of her journey: “The child is none of ourn, nor the mother from this hour.”

Upon re-entering their home, the women were struck by a jarring sight: a rugged soldier sat tranquilly by their hearth. 

“What makes you here?” Catherine demanded, her indignation rising at this intrusion into her sanctuary.

“I came to seek after Margaret Brandt.”

“We know not where she bides, and care not,” Catherine retorted bitterly.

Denys, sensing the deliberate untruth, bit his lip. “I looked to find myself in an enemy's country at this Tergou; but maybe if ye knew all ye would not be so dour.”

“I do know all!” Catherine’s voice escalated, the shrill pitch an acoustic anomaly in her usually harmonious kitchen. She ordered him away from her hearth.

“May Satan fly away with your hearth to the lake of fire and brimstone!” Denys shouted. The shock of his fluent Flemish—the raw, intimate dialect of her ancestors twisted into an aggressive curse—shattered Catherine’s composure. The sonic violence of his words sent her collapsing into a chair, bursting into frightened tears, with Kate weeping quietly beside her. 

The chaos was quelled by a grave, resonant voice. “What is to do?” 

Eli stood in the doorway. His steady presence instantly recalibrated the room's auditory balance. Denys complained of the women's hostility, and Catherine sobbed about the curses laid upon her root and branch.

“You were both somewhat to blame,” Eli said calmly, rejecting the superficial noise of their conflict. “We keep not open house, but we are not poor enough to grudge a seat at our hearth on a cold day to a wayfarer with an honest face, and, as I think, a wounded man. Sit ye down.”

The mention of the wound—an arrow in his shoulder—shifted the atmosphere from hostility to horror. Denys brushed it off cheerfully, launching into an explanation of his choleric temper by comparing it to Gerard’s. At the sound of her exiled son’s name, the very air in the kitchen seemed to hold its breath.

“Young man,” Eli said solemnly, “in God's name who are you, that know us though we know you not, and that shake our hearts speaking to us of our poor rebellious son?”

Denys lowered his voice, the harshness fading into a reverent timbre. “Hath he not writ to you of me, Denys of Burgundy? Never were truer comrades. I saved his life from a bear; he mine in the Rhine, for he swims like a duck and I like a hod of bricks. We held a room in Burgundy against seven cut-throats...” Denys recounted their partings, the untranslatable grief of their separation when Lord Anthony’s men tore them apart, and Gerard’s final plea: *Go, comfort Margaret!*

Eli’s lip quivered. He grasped the soldier's hand. “God bless thee!”

For Catherine, the overwhelming tide of emotion bypassed the inadequacy of language entirely. She darted from the room, expressing her profound maternal love through the only medium she truly trusted: sustenance. She returned with arms laden with fragrant wood, stoked the fire until the hearth hummed with vigorous life, and draped the table in snow-white, hand-woven flax. Her hands trembled, tears salting her cheeks as she brought forth the culinary treasures of her larder. 

She offered Denys the finest smoked meats, cheeses aged to crystallized perfection in the damp cellars, rich, dark bread studded with seeds, and ruby wine that caught the firelight. As Denys ate, consuming the food with a passionate, sensory appreciation that delighted Catherine's chef-heart, he spun the sweeping, mythic saga of their journey. 

To the family, this was not mere storytelling; it was a visceral communion. The flesh and blood around that fire quivered for their severed member. Giles curled by the hearth, gazing up with canine reverence. Kate, timorous as a bird, slipped her small hand into the warrior’s massive brown palm. Denys quickly learned to read the tactile metrics of her grip—a fleshy kardiometer that pinched sharply at the most perilous turns of his tale. Recognizing the psychological weight of the suspense, Denys masterfully modulated his pacing. When he recounted sinking into the Rhine, he felt Kate’s terrified pinch and playfully extended the underwater ordeal, turning his own near-drowning into a drawn-out, mythic triumph, solely to manage the emotional climate of his audience.

The spell of intertwined timelines and shared culinary memory was only broken near midnight by the dissonant, hang-dog shuffle of Cornelis and Sybrandt creeping through the door. The clatter of their guilt disrupted the sacred acoustic space. Denys’s brow knitted, his storytelling flow immediately souring at their presence.

“The rest to-morrow,” Denys sighed, suddenly weary. 

When he rose to seek an inn, Catherine halted him instantly. “Think you to lie from this house? Gerard's room has been got ready for you hours agone; the sheets I'll not say much for, seeing I spun the flax and wove the web myself.”

“Then would I lie in them blindfold,” Denys replied gallantly, recognizing the profound agrarian labor woven into the very fabric of the bed. “Ah, dame, our poor Gerard was the one for fine linen. He could hardly forgive the honest Germans their coarse flax, and whene'er my countrymen did amiss, he would excuse them with our native tongue, saying, *‘Well, well; bonnes toiles sont en Bourgogne’*—there be good lenten cloths in Burgundy. He beat all for cleanliness and the comfort of the old words.”

The rhythmic, resonant *thud* of a wooden pestle against a heavy brass mortar anchored the morning’s acoustic ecology, grounding Catherine in the tactile reality of her kitchen. She was rendering down back-fat and grinding mustard seeds, the sharp, pungent olfactory landscape of the hearth offering a necessary balm against the jarring, dissonant anxieties of the outside world. Here, amidst the bubbling of broth and the hissing of cast iron, the generations of her family seemed to interweave, past and present collapsing into the eternal, circular pacing of domestic sustenance. 

It was within this sacred, resonant space that Denys took his opportunity to tell Catherine and her daughter the rest of the tale. He characteristically excused himself for keeping Cornelis and Sybrandt in the dark. “It is not for me to blacken them; they come of a good stock. But Gerard looks on them as no friends of his in this matter; and I am Gerard’s comrade. It is a rule with us soldiers not to tell the enemy aught—but lies.”

Catherine sighed, the rhythmic kneading of her spiced dough absorbing the brunt of her emotion. The adventures he related cost them a tumult of agitation and grief. They wept bitterly at the parting of the friends, a sorrow Denys could not recount without a falter in his own voice, his dialect softening, shedding its martial bravado for something far more vulnerable. Yet, as the hours passed and the rich, yeasty scent of baking bread filled the room, their grief merged into the joyful expectation of Gerard’s speedy return. 

Denys shared this confidence, but reminded the agrarian elder that he could not neglect his friend's last words. Should Gerard return next week to find his Margaret gone, the spiritual and physical feast of their reunion would turn to ashes. Catherine had never felt so protective of the truant Margaret as she did now, recognizing in the girl's absence a severed thread in their family’s generational tapestry. But she could not agree that Denys should leave this neighborhood to hunt. 

“She must have told somebody whither she was going,” Catherine reasoned, dusting her flour-coated hands. “They owe not a stiver in Sevenbergen; and dear heart, Denys, you can’t hunt all Holland for her.”

“Can I not?” said Denys grimly. “That we shall see.” He resolved to divide their forces: Catherine would keep her ears attuned to the local rhythms, while he would scour the acoustic and culinary landscapes of every town in Holland. “Three fly not so fast, nor far, as one.”

“That is sense,” Catherine agreed, but insisted they first visit the demoiselle Margaret Van Eyck. As a mentor, surely she knew the girl's path. Catherine quickly neatened her apron, leaving the comforting sanctuary of her kitchen for the superficial, clattering industrial din of the artisan district. 

They were received graciously in a richly furnished, albeit sterile room that smelled faintly of linseed and commercial wealth, lacking entirely the life-affirming aromas of a working hearth. Margaret Van Eyck sat weaving a tapestry. Upon hearing their business, the heavy fabric slipped from her hands. 

“Gone? Gone from Sevenbergen and not told me; the thankless girl.”

“What, you know not? When was she here last?” Catherine asked, perplexed.

“Maybe ten days agone. I had ta'en out my brushes, after so many years, to paint her portrait. I did not do it, though; for reasons.”

Catherine lamented the broken connections, wondering aloud how a girl could vanish without leaving a trace of her recipe for the future. “Can ye not give us the clue, my good demoiselle? Prithee now.”

“I have it not to give,” the elder lady said peevishly.

“Then I can,” declared Reicht Heynes, appearing in the doorway. She brought with her the sudden, glorious scent of a slow-simmered Frisian *hachee*—a complex stew of caramelized onions, cloves, and deeply browned beef. Her face was flushed with the heat of the fire, her presence a testament to the profound meaning found in culinary mastery.

“So you have been hearkening all the time, eh?” Van Eyck snapped.

“What are my ears for, mistress?” Reicht shot back in a thick, rural cadence, her native dialect carrying subtle power dynamics that challenged the dominant, polished Dutch of the parlor. “And the clue, why, an ye call't a two-plye twine, and the ends on't in this room e'en now, ye'll not be far out. Oh, mistress, I wonder at you sitting there pretending.”

“Marry, come up,” the mistress’s cheek reddened. “So 'twas I drove the foolish girl away.”

“You did your share. You turned your nose up at her,” Reicht said, leaning against the frame, her hands stained with the juices of wild thyme and marrow. “She came into the kitchen to me. ‘I am not to be painted now,’ she said, and the tears in her eyes. The dominant words of your art house could not translate her subjective sorrow. But I knew well what she meant.”

“Well,” Margaret Van Eyck confessed, turning to Catherine. “Know that your Gerard is handy at the illuminator's craft, and Margaret is his patient pupil. But it is a trick I despise, for by it the great, royal art of color becomes a slave to petty printing, fettered to match the littleness of books to go to church in a rich fool’s pocket. The superficial commercialization of art, I call it. When the poor wench brought me her work—she had spent hundreds of hours painting the true sustenance of the earth: thorn leaves, lilies, dewberries, and butterfly grubs, stuck fast in gold-leaf. I was wroth that an immortal soul and much manual skill should be flung away on nature's agrarian truths, rather than the marketable acts of saints. I told her her insects were wedged miserably in metal prisons.”

Van Eyck sighed, the erosion of her own cultural memory evident in her capitulation to commercial tastes. “She argued that Gerard himself taught her. To make an end of it, I took a brush. I chose to shade her meticulously sourced fruit with a color false in nature, a sterile hue, to mock her glaring gold. In five minutes I painted a monstrous bunch of raspberries and a grub to turn the stomach. My lady flung her arms round my neck and said, ‘Oh!’”

“The little love!” Denys wedged in, captivated.

Van Eyck went on to explain how she had offered to paint Margaret as the Madonna, but critically analyzed her features—her violet eyes that smacked too much of the rich earth, her resolute chin—and declared her unfit for the commercialized, idealized purity of a saint. “Most painters' virgins are but street trollops put on like queens in a pack of cards,” Van Eyck muttered. “But my painting is half devotion. I could not turn a girl who couldn't keep her lover at a distance into the Virgin Mary. 'Twas foolish, maybe, but I could not help it.”

Reicht bit her lip, the injustice of it grating against her deeply rooted agrarian values. “Why, dame, think you 'twas for that alone Margaret left? Nay. 'Tis because Gerard's people slight her so cruel. Who would bide among hard-hearted folk that ha' driven her lad to Italy, and now relent not?”

“Reicht, I was going,” Catherine defended herself, stung by the culinary historian's sharp tongue. 

“Oh, ay, going, and going. But with your words you uplifted her heart and let it down wi' your deeds! ‘They have never been,’ the poor thing said to me. Her grief was an untranslatable dialect of loneliness. I, too, am far from my native Frisian soil, but ten times liever would I be Reicht Heynes, with leagues between me and my kith, than fare as lonesome as she among those who ought to warm to her.”

“Alack, Reicht, one plaguy thing or t'other did hinder me,” Catherine pleaded.

“Did aught hinder ye to eat your dinner any one of those days?” Reicht demanded, pointing a wooden spoon like a scepter. “Had your heart been as good towards your own flesh and blood as 'twas towards your flesher's meat, nought had kept you from her, watching the road with your child's child a-beating 'neath her bosom!”

The Van Eyck, nettled by the disruption of her quiet acoustic domain, sprang from her ambush. “Since you disrespect my guests, seek another place! She is dismissed the house!”

“Ay, ay. We all heard ye,” Reicht said, wiping her hands with vast indifference, entirely unbothered by the superficial power dynamics of the wealthy.

“Then hear me!” Denys stepped forward solemnly, the sheer volume of his voice altering the room's acoustic ecology. They pivoted toward him. He looked at Reicht—a woman who understood the sacred nature of the hearth, who seasoned her stews with history and her words with unvarnished truth. 

“Mistress Reicht,” Denys said with great dignity, “you are turned off. If I might advise, be a mistress, a queen. I am a man, though but half an arbalestrier. I have twenty gold pieces and a stout arm. Marriage is not a habit of mine; but I capitulate to so many virtues. You are beautiful, you master the olfactory landscapes of the kitchen, you speak your native tongue without shame, and you take the part of my she-comrade. Be then an arbalestriesse!”

“And what the dickens is that?” Reicht inquired bluntly.

“I mean, be the wife, mistress, and queen of Denys of Burgundy here present.”

A dead silence fell, thick as a reducing glaze, soon shattered by unreasonable indignation from Catherine and Margaret Van Eyck. Reicht took a sly glance at him and said, “I mean to take a good look at any man ere I leap into his arms.”

“Then look your fill, and leap away,” Denys drew himself up majestically, embodying the mythic stance of a classical hero amidst the domestic parlor. 

“You are beautiful, so,” cried Van Eyck, suddenly inspired by the living, breathing reality before her, an occurrence of myth integrating into the everyday. “You are inspired—with folly! I must take off your head.” She immediately began sketching him. “Keep the fool inspired and beautiful, Reicht!”

But Reicht was unhappy. Her complex *hachee* was in a precarious state of reduction, and she made no scruple to say that duties so grave to the culinary arts left her no time to play the fool. Van Eyck scolded her for being a trampler on the arts, to which Reicht pouted like a basilisk at the inspired model.

At last, the artist’s wrist cramped, leaving a rough but noble sketch. 

“Then, now, mistress, I may go and mind my pot?” Reicht asked.

“Ay, ay, go to your pot! And get into it; you will find your soul in it!”

“Take your time, mistress,” Reicht said contemptuously, her Frisian pride unyielding. “When she wants to get rid of me, let her turn herself off and die. When that day doth come, 'twill take a man to dry my eyes; and if you should be in the same mind then, soldier, you can say so.” She returned to her sanctuary, the rhythmic bubbling of the broth welcoming her back.

That afternoon, Denys set out on a wild goose chase, initiating a sweeping, circular journey across Holland. His plan was simple but mythic in scale. He would visit a hundred towns, his ears acutely tuned to the acoustic ecology of the village squares. He would listen to the women at the water fountains, tracing the native dialects and the distinct, regional olfactory landscapes of roasting meats and malting barley, searching for the old physician and his daughter. 

He was months on the tramp. Those who sit blindfolded by self-conceit in sterile rooms might claim that tranquility alone satisfies the mind, but Denys, like any true appreciator of life's sensory feasts, knew that men who risk their lives thirst for the profound, vibrating resonance of the world. All these months he was a demi-Spartan; sober, vigilant, and indomitable, sustaining himself on the rich, rustic breads and complex ales of the countryside. He flirted gigantically, yet wasted no time, never offering his hand again, preserving the memory of the Frisian cook who held the line against cultural erosion.
