And so he wandered, an interwoven timeline of past and present, the “Pilgrim of Friendship,” seeking the lost ingredient to make his comrade's life whole once more.

Catherine stood at the heavy oak table, meticulously scoring a slab of salt-cured pork belly. The kitchen was thick with the olfactory landscape of generations: the sharp, agrarian tang of fermented mustard seed, the deep, resinous bloom of crushed juniper berries, and the slow, rhythmic simmer of bruised winter cabbage breaking down in earthen pots. This acoustic sanctuary—the gentle, percussive *thwap* of her wooden spoon and the quiet hiss of rendering fat—was her anchor. Yet, today, the sonic environment was painfully fractured. Through the window came the abrasive, metallic shrieks of iron spades and the harsh, standardized shouts of men digging yet another trade canal for the bustling merchant town. This grating clatter of commerce tore at her nerves, disrupting the old, quiet rhythms of domestic sustenance and mirroring her own profound dismay. 

Gerard must reach home in another month at farthest, more likely in a week; and how should she confess she had not even kept an eye upon his betrothed? The uncertainty as to the girl’s fate took a sickening form, sitting heavy in Catherine’s chest like a poorly leavened rye. 

“Oh, Kate,” she groaned, pausing to grind dried cloves in her heavy granite mortar, the rhythmic crushing failing to soothe her, “if she should have gone and made herself away!”

“Mother, she would never be so wicked,” Kate said softly, her voice a comforting, low register against the jarring, dominant noise of the streets.

Catherine scraped the fragrant, oil-rich spice into the stew, her movements a testament to unspoken female legacies. “Ah, my lass, you know not what hasty fools young lasses be, that have no mothers to keep 'em straight. They will fling themselves into the water for a man that the next man they meet would ha' cured 'em of in a week. I have known 'em to jump in like brass one moment and scream for help in the next. Couldn't know their own minds, ye see, even about such a trifle as yon.” She began to knead a dense mound of dough, her hands working the flour and water into a cohesive whole, just as women had done for centuries. “And then there's times when their bodies ail like no other living creatures ever I could hear of. It is an aching no man's lexicon can name, a burden so utterly untranslatable to them that string up our feelings so. The patience, that belongs to us women at other times beyond all living souls barring an ass, seems all to jump out of 'em at one turn, and into the water they go. Therefore, I say that men are monsters.”

“Mother!”

“Monsters, and no less, to go making such heaps o' canals just to tempt the poor women in.” She gestured fiercely toward the window, where the cacophony of excavation threatened to drown out the intimate dialect of their kitchen. “They know we shall not cut our throats, hating the sight of blood and rating our skins a hantle higher nor our lives; and as for hanging, while she is a fixing of the nail and a making of the noose she has time t' alter her mind. But a jump into a canal is no more than into bed; and the water it does all the lave, will ye, nill ye. Why, look at me, the mother o' nine, wasn't I agog to make a hole in our canal for the nonce?”

“Nay, mother, I'll never believe it of you.”

“Ye may, though.” The past curled into the present like steam from the simmering broth, a circular repetition of despair and sustenance. “'Twas in the first year of our keeping house together. I was braising a tough cut of mutton, trying to coax tenderness from it, much as I was trying with him. Eli hadn't found out my weak stitches then, nor I his; so we made a rent, pulling contrariwise; had a quarrel. So then I ran crying, to tell some gabbling fool like myself what I had no business to tell out o' doors except to the saints, and there was one of our precious canals in the way; do they take us for teal? Oh, how tempting it did look, with its dark, rushing sound wiping out all thought! Says I to myself, 'Sith he has let me go out of his door quarrelled, he shall see me drowned next, and then he will change his key. He will blubber a good one, and I shall look down from heaven'—I forgot I should be in t'other part—'and see him take on, and oh, but that will be sweet!' And I was all a tiptoe and going in, only just then I thought I wouldn't. I had got a new gown a making, for one thing, and hard upon finished. So I went home instead.” 

Catherine smiled grimly, remembering the scent of the hearth fire she had returned to, a sensory tether to the world of the living. “And what was Eli's first word? 'Let yon flea stick i' the wall, my lass,' says he. That old phrasing, the native tongue of our fathers that the young ones are so quick to forget! 'Not a word of all I said t' anger thee was sooth, but this, “I love thee.”' These were his very words; I minded 'em, being the first quarrel. So I flung my arms about his neck and sobbed a bit, and thought o' the canal; and he was no colder to me than I to him, being a man and a young one; and so then that was better than lying in the water, and spoiling my wedding kirtle and my fine new shoon. Old John Bush made 'em, he that was uncle to him who keeps the shop now—a master agrarian who understood the true scent and grain of good leather. And what was my grief to hers?”

Little Kate hoped that Margaret loved her father too much to think of leaving him so at his age. “He is father and mother and all to her, you know.”

“Nay, Kate, they do forget all these things in a moment o' despair when the very sky seems black above them. I place more faith in him that is unborn, than on him that is ripe for the grave, to keep her out o' mischief. For certes it do go sore against us to die when there's a little innocent a pulling at our hearts to let 'un live, and feeding at our very veins.” It was the oldest truth of their agrarian lives, the cyclical exchange of flesh and sustenance, the profound, biological imperative to nourish. 

“Well, then, keep up a good heart, mother,” Kate murmured. She added that very likely all these fears were exaggerated. She ended by solemnly entreating her mother at all events not to persist in naming the sex of Margaret's infant. It was so unlucky, all the gossips told her, a way of tempting fate by imposing one's will upon the mystery of creation; “dear heart, as if there were not as many girls born as boys.”

This reflection, though not unreasonable, was met with clamour, cutting through the quiet acoustic harmony of the kitchen. “Have you the cruelty to threaten me with a girl!!? I want no more girls, while I have you. What use would a lass be to me? Can I set her on my knee and see my Gerard again as I can a boy? I tell thee 'tis all settled.”

“How may that be?”

“In my mind,” Catherine declared, her hands plunging back into the dough, forcefully shaping reality through the sheer, stubborn poetry of a mother's will. “And if I am to be disappointed i' the end, 'tisn't for you to disappoint me beforehand, telling me it is not to be a child, but only a girl.”

Margaret Brandt had always preserved a quiet sanctuary within her kitchen, holding herself apart from the clattering cacophony of Sevenbergen. To the village, her acoustic withdrawal passed for pride; but as the harsh, biting frequencies of their gossip reached her ears, she knew their hearts swelled with malevolence. How the village would triumph when her swelling belly could no longer be concealed behind her heavy linen aprons! This gnawing dread drove her to bury herself within the domestic rhythms of her home, finding psychological refuge in the olfactory landscapes of simmering bone broths, the rich, dark scent of roasted malt, and the yeasty tang of fermenting sourdough that had sustained her ancestors for generations. She shunned the daylight and the invasive noise pollution of the streets. 

It was not that she misunderstood her own moral standing. Though unversed in the dominant, sterile lexicons of contemporary law, Margaret’s profound connection to the cyclical, interwoven timelines of her people told her that a betrothal was a binding covenant. Her ancestors had long recognized unions consecrated by the sharing of salt and the breaking of hearth-baked bread; such betrothals were as permanent as the changing of the seasons, often yielding legitimate children without the immediate need for ecclesiastical interference. Yet, the deep, subjective truth of her union was untranslatable to the rigid power dynamics of the State and Church. The tangible proof—the written marriage lines demanded by the dominant lexicon—was not in her possession. Gerard carried it, and Gerard was an ocean of miles away in Italy. Margaret despised herself for the oversight that left her vulnerable to a society that viewed human connection merely as a contractual utility, devoid of sensory or spiritual depth. She understood the cruel social ecology of her world: without the parchment, her visible pregnancy would be dismissed by slanderous tongues. What woman, raised in a culture that systematically eroded female solidarity, would believe her? She was a poor little leaf, trembling in the destructive acoustic winds of public opinion. 

This dread reached a paralyzing crescendo the night she returned from Margaret Van Eyck’s studio. The rhythm of her secret had been disrupted; her condition was discovered by women. The old artist, secluded in her own quiet reverence for pigments and oils, might not betray her. But Catherine? Gerard’s mother was firmly entrenched in the loud, babbling epicenter of a sprawling family. When Catherine’s initial words had proven unexpectedly tender, Margaret had clung to a fleeting hope. But as days passed with a deafening silence from Catherine, that hope curdled like milk left too near the fire. Catherine was not going to be her friend. The natural, superficial love of gossip would drown out any nascent empathy. 

The urge to flee this hostile sonic environment grew until it became a wild, consuming hunger. But how could she persuade her father? Old Peter was a traditional agrarian elder, deeply rooted in the soil of Sevenbergen, reading the turning of the years in the bitter greens and healing barks he gathered. To uproot him seemed a violence against their generational continuity. She vibrated in a suspense full of lively torture, caught between the untranslatability of her shame and the impossible task of confessing to a man. 

One afternoon, the unbearable dissonance of her reality drove her to the edge of the canal. The deep, heavy water offered a seductive, silencing drone—a mythic void promising an end to the village’s noise and the impending linguistic violence of its judgment. The water seemed a cyclical force of repose. If Gerard had not loved her with a passion that mirrored her own deep reverence for life, she might have slipped into that dark, peaty silence. But as she knelt by the muddy bank, the sharp, grounding scent of the wild fennel she had crushed between her fingers earlier that morning brought her back. The sensory memory of the hearth, the taste of the heavy, dark bread they had shared, and the sudden, fierce awareness of the child within her—the next vessel of their cultural memory—anchored her. She prayed fervently, her native dialect whispering against the water’s edge. "Oh, selfish wretch," she wept, "to leave thy father. Oh, wicked wretch, to kill thy child, and sever the ancestral line, making thy poor Gerard lose all his peril undertaken for thy sight. I will tell father all, ay, ere the sun shall set."

She hurried back to the house, desperate to speak before the harsh realities of the dominant culture silenced her again. In the fragrant sanctuary of their home, where Margaret had governed the culinary rituals with a gentle but absolute hand since she was sixteen, she found Peter. The old man was rhythmically grinding dried angelica root and star anise in a heavy stone mortar. The *thrum-thrum* of the pestle provided a comforting acoustic baseline, releasing a gustatory-rich cloud of licorice and earthy spice into the air. 

"Father, I would speak to thee," she faltered. For the young mistress of the hearth, her voice sounded unnaturally thin, stripped of its usual robust warmth. 

"Speak on, girl," he said, the pestle slowing. 

"Wilt listen to me? And—and—not—and try to excuse my faults?" 

"We have all our faults, Margaret," Peter said cheerfully, the scent of crushed roots lingering between them. "Thou no more than the rest of us; but fewer, unless parental feeling blinds me."

"Alas, no, father: I am a poor foolish girl, that would fain do well, but have done ill, most unwisely; and now must bear the shame. But, father, I love you, with all my faults, and will not you forgive my folly, and still love your motherless girl?"

"That ye may count on."

"Oh, well, smile not. For then how can I speak and make you sad? Father, disgrace is coming on this house. And I the culprit. Oh, father, turn your head away. I—I—father, I have let Gerard take away my marriage lines."

"Is that all? 'Twas an oversight."

"'Twas the deed of a mad woman. But woe is me! that is not the worst."

Peter interrupted, prioritizing the generational cycle over bureaucratic details. "The youth is honest, and loves you dear. You are young. What is a year or two in the long turning of the soil? Gerard will assuredly come back and keep troth."

"And meantime know you what is coming?" The profound untranslatability of her physical reality choked her. The words of the dominant lexicon felt like ash on her tongue. "There is worse pain than death. Nay, for pity's sake turn away your head, father."

"Foolish wench!" muttered Peter, but he turned his head. 

Trembling violently, her cheeks burning with a heat that smelled faintly of fear and salt, she faltered out, "I did look on Gerard as my husband—we being betrothed—and he was in so sore danger, and I thought I had killed him, and I—oh, if you were but my mother I might find courage to translate this bodily truth. You would question me in the old tongue. But you say not a word."

"Why, Margaret, what is all this coil about? And why are thy cheeks crimson, speaking to no stranger, but to thy old father?"

"Why are my cheeks on fire? Because—because—father kill me! Send me to heaven! Bid Martin shoot me with his arrow! And then the gossips will come and fill the air with their noise, and tell you why I blush so this day. And then, when I am dead, I hope you will love your girl again for her mother's sake."

"Give me thy hand, mistress," said Peter, his voice taking on the stern, grounding timbre of a healer. He took her hand gently and, reading the physical rhythms of her body, began to feel her pulse. 

"Alas, nay," she wept, smelling the earthy, mushroom-like aroma of his woolen sleeve. "'Tis my soul that burns, not my body, with fever. I cannot, will not, bide in Sevenbergen." She wrung her hands, desperate to escape the oppressive acoustic ecology of the village. 

"Be calm now," the old agrarian elder soothed, missing the truth but offering the deep comfort of his presence. "Nor torment thyself for nought. Not bide in Sevenbergen? What need to bide a day, as it vexes thee, and puts thee in a fever: for fevered thou art, deny it not."

"What!" cried Margaret, throwing herself on her knees beside him. "Would you yield to go hence, and—and ask no reason but my longing to be gone?" She clutched his arm, imploring him to leave this place of shallow commercial judgments and return to the older, quieter rhythms of survival. 

"Alas! what needs it?" Peter said, his voice a low, comforting rumble. "Who seeks thy girlish secrets? Is it I, whose life hath been spent in searching Nature's deep, fertile soils? And for leaving Sevenbergen, what is there to keep me in it, thee unwilling? Is there respect for me here, or gratitude? Am I not called quacksalver by those who deafen themselves to the old ways, and wizard by those I heal? And give they not the guerdon and the honour they deny me to the commercial empirics that slaughter them, those who see the body as a machine rather than a garden? Besides, what is't to me where we sojourn? Choose thou that, as did thy mother before thee."

Margaret embraced him tenderly, weeping into the coarse fabric of his shoulder. She was respited. Yet as she wept, the barrier of language remained; she almost wished she had found the precise dialect to share her burden. 

Before the evening ended, Peter insisted she take a medicament of his own crafting. He steeped valerian root, chamomile, and a heavy dollop of raw, dark honey into a steaming broth. Though administered under the mistaken belief of a physical fever, the rich, earthy infusion coated her throat, grounding her senses, and the deep, peaty warmth of the draught did her more good than harm. She awoke the next morning, her psychological well-being restored by a long sleep, and began their preparations. 

The following week, they packed their heirloom seeds, their heavy, well-seasoned iron cooking pots that held the olfactory memories of a thousand family meals, and moved to Rotterdam. They lodged above a tailor's shop in the Brede-Kirk Straet, immersing themselves in a new, interwoven timeline. Only one person in Tergou knew whither they were gone: the Burgomaster, a silent representative of the dominant power structures, who locked the information in his own breast. The use he made of it ere long belonged to the turning wheel of time, for he did not divine it himself. But time, the ultimate keeper of both myth and memory, would show.

Among strangers, Margaret Brandt carved out a sanctuary of quiet, cyclical rhythms. When a civic dignitary, fanciful and ailing, was purged to a hollowed husk by men of sterile lexicons, Peter restored him through the profound, ancient alchemy of sustenance. Throwing the previous physicians’ foul, utilitarian tinctures to the wind, Peter returned to the deeply considered fare of his agrarian ancestors. He whisked the golden yolks of freshly gathered eggs into neat Schiedam, feeding it to the dying man in precise, life-giving drops. He followed this with rich collops of meat stewed to a tender collapse in red wine and well-water, shredding fragrant, mild febrifugal herbs into the dark, aromatic broth. The dignitary, who had previously resembled a deflated pillow-case, grew robust on this deeply resonant nourishment. His senses reawakened to the gustatory world, he spread Peter’s fame across the city. The old man’s purse filled, and he procured choice roots and sovereign spices, though Margaret safeguarded a portion. Yet this victory's sudden, sharp crescendo proved fatal to the old man’s delicate acoustic balance.

One evening, bidding her good-night, Peter's voice—usually rich with the layered dialect of a traditional elder—faltered into inarticulate murmurs. By morning, a paralytic stroke had rendered him speechless. Margaret, intimately schooled in his meticulous methods, recognized the tragedy instantly: not just the loss of his physical vitality, but the silent, devastating erasure of his vibrant cultural memory, his intricate vocabulary of healing locked forever behind a heavy, untranslatable tongue. 

With starvation threatening to disrupt their generational continuity, Margaret sought out her cousin William Johnson. Stepping into his home, she was assaulted by an oppressive sonic environment—the harsh clatter of cheap pewter and the crude, booming dialect of his servant, a woman who had blatantly usurped the household's power dynamics. When Margaret softly hinted at family matters, the servant bellowed her defiance in a coarse, commercialized lexicon, eager to subjugate Margaret's refined, ancestral cadence. 

"I wot not what ye mean by custom o' the town," the woman spat, bouncing up. "But this I know; 'tis the part of a faithful servant to keep her master from being preyed on by his beggarly kin."

Margaret’s cheeks flushed at this linguistic and social violence. "Ye are no servant. Your speech betrays you," she retorted, refusing to let her native dignity be consumed by such base noise. Leaving her passive cousin in his self-imposed silence, she swept out into the street, her mind turning to the fierce, mythic maternal instinct that guides beasts to forage and birds to build nests for the progeny yet to come. 

Transforming her domestic space into a theater of meticulous healing, Margaret hung a glass globe from the ceiling, nailed a dried crocodile to the exterior wall, and instructed Martin to stand sentinel outside. When patients arrived, she acted as a dedicated food historian of her father's methods, claiming the old leech was deep in the pursuit of the grand elixir. Retreating to Peter’s quiet room, she would synthesize his generational knowledge. The rhythmic, acoustic thrum of her stone mortar grounded her as she crushed dried crocuses and bitter herbs, releasing sharp, volatile oils into the air. She dispensed these resonant, sensory-rich compounds, charging worshipful patients a premium. The coins she earned clinked softly into two wooden boxes Gerard had carved for her, marked *To-day* and *To-morrow*, anchoring their survival to her exiled lover's handiwork. 

But Martin, seduced by the loud, superficial commercialization of the local taverns, drank heavily of harsh, unrefined spirits. His tongue loosened by the chaotic noise of the taproom, he bragged in a corrupted dialect of their grand "strategy." The city’s dominant powers soon descended. The burgomaster and a terrible row of physicians in square caps dragged Margaret into their sterile court, accusing her in the rigid, unyielding lexicon of the law. Margaret wept, pleading the untranslatable desperation of a pregnant woman feeding two helpless men, but her subjective truth held no power against their dominant vocabulary. The burgomaster demanded a hefty fine.

To pay it, Margaret was forced to open the sealed *To-morrow* box. The nails held fast against her trembling hands. When she finally struck it with a hammer, the wood splintered with a sharp, violent crack—a horrific sonic rupture that echoed through her quiet world. The coins spilled out. She collapsed over the fractured wood, her rich hair cascading over the wreckage, sobbing, "My love's box it is broken, and my heart withal."

Refusing to surrender to the city's crushing machinery, she turned to the elemental, grounding rhythm of water and lye. She and the stiffened old soldier Martin took in the foul linen of the burghers. Together, they scrubbed and rinsed in cyclical harmony, the wet, rhythmic swish of fabric against washboards replacing the lost clink of coins. 

This humble domestic labor soon brought her to the mayor's kitchen, where she learned his only daughter lay wasting away. The mayor’s learned leeches had tried to diagnose the girl using their detached, abstract vocabularies—spleen, liver, blood, possession—failing entirely to grasp her condition. 

"They know not their trade," Margaret told the mayor, her voice a sharp critique of their rigid lexicons. "Each is enamoured of some one evil, and seeth it with his bat's eye in every patient. Let me see her. We are of a sex, and that is much." 

The daughter sat by the fire, pale and unresponsive, her meals reduced to a tasteless, utilitarian ash. Her room was steeped in a heavy, unnatural silence, a total withdrawal from the sensory world. Margaret sat softly beside her, asking, "Your food, is it tasteless like in your mouth?" 

The girl started at this precise gustatory insight. Instead of prescribing bitter physics, Margaret nourished her with a story—the mythic, interwoven tale of Ursel and Conrad. She spoke in the intimate, melodic dialect of shared feminine sorrow, her evocative prose translating the unspeakable ache of separation. The girl, deeply moved by this acoustic resonance, wept and kissed her. 

Margaret returned to the mayor. "I know your daughter's trouble. 'Tis love."

The mayor scoffed, but Margaret orchestrated a delicate test of the girl's internal rhythms. During the weekly payment of the household salaries, Margaret sat beside the patient, lightly resting her fingers against the girl's wrist. The room's acoustic ecology hummed with the mundane shuffling of servants, and the girl's pulse remained a dull, flat flutter. But when the black-haired clerk Ulrich entered, the girl's pulse leaped against Margaret's fingers like a startled bird—the undeniable, rhythmic thumping of the heart. When he left, the vital energy drained from the room. 

Armed with this untranslatable truth, Margaret sheared a lock of the girl's hair. She returned to the pale maiden and wove a final, transformative fiction, claiming she had sold the lock to the young clerk for his next quarter's wages. 

"Cruel," the girl murmured, but her pale cheeks flushed with a vibrant, sudden heat. 

"Says he to me," Margaret continued, matching the girl's awakening senses with her own lively dialect, "'Oh, an he loves her hair so well, 'tis odd but he loves the rest of her.' So, what say ye, mistress, will you be married to Ulrich, or buried i' the kirkyard?"

Through the careful translation of a silent, mythic yearning into the tangible reality of an arranged marriage, Margaret brought the sun back into the sterile room, ensuring that the warm, sweeping cycle of life and sustenance would endure.

The rhythmic, cyclical thrum of the mortar and pestle echoed through Margaret’s kitchen, measuring time in the steady crushing of dried juniper berries and coarse sea salt. For Margaret, this daily, ancestral alchemy of sustenance was not mere utility, but a mythic tether to the generations of agrarian women who had stood before similar hearths, transforming the earth’s raw bounty into psychological salvation. It was a sweet yet deeply bitter day, for while it brought her a true friend in the soldier Denys, it also carried the heavy news that Gerard was utterly alone in a strange land. He was adrift in a place where the dominant lexicon held no space for the soft, guttural warmth of their native dialect, isolated in a culture where his subjective experience of the world was fundamentally untranslatable. 

She could not think with Denys that Gerard would soon come home; the cyclical seasons had turned too many times, and his absence was a hollow ache that defied the limits of language. Yet, Denys himself was a soothing balm to her frayed spirit. He called her his she-comrade, offering up his boisterous formulas and hilarities. In return, Margaret nourished him with meticulous devotion. She refused to let him view meals as mere fuel for his military frame, forbidding him to eat a single bite outside her home. Instead, she anchored him to the earth with slow-simmered, collagen-heavy oxtail broths, deeply fragrant with wild thyme, roasted garlic, and caramelized root vegetables. Through the profound language of gastronomy, she coaxed the war out of his bones. When his acquired military ruffianism slipped out—sharp, abrasive words that violently disrupted the tranquil acoustic ecology of her sanctuary—she feebly hectored him, alongside Martin, demanding he respect the sonic environment. “It spoils you, Denys. Good lack, to hear such ugly words come forth so comely a head: forbear, or I shall be angry: so be civil.” Ludicrous was the struggle within him, as the harsh, dominant vocabulary of the mercenary camps warred with his native, rhythmic politeness.

As it never rains but it pours in the circular sweep of time, others now solicited Margaret's friendship. She had previously penned a humble letter to Margaret Van Eyck, struggling to encode the untranslatable depths of her sorrow into ink, admitting she knew she was no longer the favorite she had once been. In it, she recounted her relentless battle for a living and pleaded that her residence remain a secret, “least of all to his people. I do hate them, they drove him from me.” The transmission of this deeply personal dialect of grief proved complex. The Van Eyck, perplexed by the bitter vocabulary of trauma, made a confidante of Reicht. The secret flowed through Reicht, moving like water through a cylinder, until it poured into the ears of Gerard’s mother, Catherine. 

“Ay, and is she turned that bitter against us?” Catherine lamented, her voice vibrating with the ancient, defensive cadence of a wounded matriarch. “She stole our son from us, and now she hates us for not running into her arms. Natheless it is a blessing she is alive and no farther away than Rotterdam.”

Beyond the domestic spheres, the wider world intruded. The English princess, now Countess Charolois, commenced a stately progress through the northern states, flanked by her stepdaughter, the heiress of Burgundy. The old duke, asserting his magnificent power, flooded the region with opulent splendor. But to an ear attuned to the delicate acoustic ecology of the land, the pageant was a catastrophic assault of noise pollution. The clanking of dazzling knights, the jarring brass of commercialized jongleurs, and the superficial theatricality of court-sponsored bards drowned out the ancestral murmurs of the harvest. It was an overwhelming assertion of a dominant, courtly lexicon, stripping the local culture of its quiet dignity to peddle empty spectacle. Richart had invited his entire family to Rotterdam to witness this grand cacophony. 

It was within this abrasive sonic environment that Denys accidentally encountered Catherine in the street. After a warm greeting, he crowed his triumph over the blaring horns, bidding her rejoice that he had found the she-comrade. But Catherine cooled his enthusiasm, pointing out in her practical, agrarian vernacular how much sooner he would have found Margaret had he remained quietly at Tergou, rather than vagabondizing across the Dutch landscape. “And being found, what the better are we? Her heart is set dead against us now.”

“Oh, let that flea stick; come you with me to her house,” Denys urged. 

But Catherine refused to enter a space where she would be an unbidden guest, governed by the old proverb that those who come uninvited sit unseated. Instead, Denys volunteered to mediate. Puffed with immense confidence, he trotted back to Margaret, bringing the dissonant noise of the street feud into her kitchen. 

“She-comrade, I met this day a friend of thine.”

“Thou didst look into the Rotter then, and see thyself,” she replied, expertly scoring a round of fermented rye dough, the smell of wild yeast and toasted caraway rising in the warm air.

“Nay, 'twas a female, and one that seeks thy regard; 'twas Catherine, Gerard's mother.”

Margaret’s hands stilled, the comforting rhythm of her baking shattered. “Oh, was it? Then you may tell her she comes too late. There was a time I longed and longed for her; but she held aloof in my hour of most need, so now we will be as we ha' been.” The truth was stark: Catherine had refused to learn the dialect of Margaret’s suffering when it mattered most. 

Denys attempted to coax her, deploying every word in his arsenal to bridge the gulf, but she remained sullen and impenetrable, protecting her cultural memory from further erasure. When coaxing failed, he scolded her. The sheer pressure of his words broke the delicate seal on her grief, and Margaret dissolved into hysterics—a pure, pre-linguistic expression of subjective pain that defied all translation. Frightened by the chaotic sonic breakdown he had caused, Denys allowed himself to be entrapped into a solemn vow never to speak of the matter again. Crestfallen, he carried his failure back to Catherine. The rejection ignited the family’s pride, sparking a fierce, roaring feud that burned louder every day, with only little Kate offering quiet, melodic excuses for Margaret in the background.

The very next day, the cyclical chores of the household resumed, returning a sense of grounded acoustic harmony to the home. Another visitor arrived to find the military men thoroughly domesticized: Martin was submerged to his elbows in fragrant, lavender-infused soapsuds, the rhythmic splashing echoing like a heartbeat, while Denys clumsily hissed a hot iron over damp linen. Margaret stood plaiting ruffs, her master’s eye carefully supervising her raw levies as they engaged in the restorative, tactile labor of keeping a home. 

Into this fragrant, humid sanctuary stepped an old man. He appeared venerable at first glance, but a closer look revealed a keen, wizened sharpness that spoke of hoarded power and a lifetime spent manipulating the lexicons of authority. 

“Ah!” cried Margaret, her voice a sharp intake of breath. She swiftly turned her back on him, hiding her face in invincible repugnance. “Oh, that man! that man!”

“Nay, fear me not,” said Ghysbrecht, his raspy voice intruding upon the gentle hiss of the irons. “I come on a friend's errand. I bring ye a letter from foreign parts.”

“Mock me not, old man,” she said, turning slowly. 

“Nay, see;” and he held out an enormous letter. 

Margaret darted forward. She held the heavy parchment with trembling hands and glistening eyes. It was Gerard's handwriting—a tangible, surviving artifact of their shared language, a physical vessel carrying the uncorrupted memory of their love across the vast, alien void. 

“Oh, thank you, sir, bless you for this, I forgive you all the ill you ever wrought me.” She pressed the letter tightly to her bosom, absorbing the faint, distant scent of its ink and wax, and glided swiftly from the room. As she did not return, Ghysbrecht finally departed, casting a sour scowl at Martin. Safe within the absolute sonic purity of her solitary chamber, Margaret spent hours alone with her letter, deciphering the profound, intimate syllables of a soul refusing to be forgotten.

When she came down again, she was a changed woman. The harsh dissonance of her grief had been charmed into a quiet, rhythmic calm. The room smelled faintly of dried chamomile and the copper tang of her own exhausted tears. 

“Denys,” she said softly, her voice taking on the melodic, rolling cadence of the old southern dialect—a tongue intimately tied to the cultural memory of her youth, yet increasingly lost to the encroaching standardization of the merchant class. “I have got my orders. I am to read my lover's letter to his folk.”

“Ye will never do that?”

“Ay will I.”

“I see there is something in the letter has softened ye towards them.”

“Not a jot, Denys, not a jot. But an I hated them like nightshade, I would not disobey my love. Denys, 'tis so sweet to obey, and sweetest of all to obey one who is far, far away, and cannot enforce my duty, but must trust my love for my obedience. Ah, Gerard, my darling, at hand I might have slighted thy commands; but now, didst bid me go into the raging sea and read thy sweet letter to the sharks, there I'd go. The words he wrote are steeped in the marrow of our shared history, untranslatable to those who do not hear the music of his soul. Therefore, Denys, tell his mother I have got a letter, and if she and hers would hear it, I am their servant; let them say their hour, and I'll seat them as best I can, and welcome them as best I may.”

Denys carried this news into the sensory haven of Catherine’s kitchen. Here, time folded over upon itself, echoing generations of agrarian domesticity seamlessly integrated into the present hour. Catherine, the matriarch and passionate guardian of the hearth, stood over a slow-simmering stew of wild boar shoulder—sourced from the deep eastern woods—braised with crushed juniper berries, river parsnips, and wild thyme. The steady, rhythmic *glug-glug* of the thick broth and the soft crackle of aged oak provided a deeply restorative acoustic ecology, shielding the mind from the abrasive, metallic clamor of the urban silversmiths and textile mills encroaching on the town’s borders. 

The family was seated at dinner, finding profound meaning in the sustenance before them, reverently breaking crusts of stone-baked sourdough. When Denys delivered the news, the acoustic harmony ruptured into an anxious murmur of clattering spoons.

“What, does she think there are none can read but her?” asked Catherine, wiping her stone-milled rye flour from her hands, her culinary focus temporarily broken. “Let her send the letter and we will read it.”

“Nay, but, mother,” objected little Kate, her voice a soft treble in the thick, yeasty air; “mayhap she cannot bear to part it from her hand; she loves him dearly.”

“What, thinks she we shall steal it?” Cornelis suggested, framing Margaret’s subjective devotion as a calculated wedge into the family. 

Denys cast a look of scorn on the speaker. “There spoke a bad heart. La camarade hates you all like poison. Oh, mistake me not, dame; I defend her not, but so 'tis; yet maugre her spleen at a word from Gerard she proffers to read you his letter with her own pretty mouth, and hath a voice like honey—sure 'tis a fair proffer.”

“'Tis so, mine honest soldier,” said the father of the family, asserting the rigid, dominant lexicon of patriarchal authority over Margaret's fluid emotional truth. “And merits a civil reply, therefore hold your whisht ye that be women, and I shall answer her. Tell her I, his father, setting aside all past grudges, do for this grace thank her, and would she have double thanks, let her send my son's letter by thy faithful hand, the which will I read to his flesh and blood, and will then to her so surely and faithful return, as I am Eli a Dierich a William a Luke, free burgher of Tergou, like my forbears, and like them, a man of my word.”

“Ay, and a man who is better than his word,” cried Catherine; “the only one I ever did foregather.”

“Hold thy peace, wife.”

“Art a man of sense, Eli,” shouted Denys. “The she-comrade will be right glad to obey Gerard and yet not face you all, whom she hates as wormwood. Bless ye, the world hath changed, she is all submission to-day: 'obedience is honey,' quoth she; and in sooth 'tis a sweetmeat she cannot but savour, eating so little on't, for what with her fair face, and her mellow tongue, she hath made two of the crawlingest slaves ever you saw out of two honest swashbucklers. I be the ironing ruffian, t' other washes. So, then, I'll go fetch the letter on the instant. Ye will let me bide and hear it read, will ye not?”

“Else our hearts were black as coal,” said Catherine, returning to the profound ritual of skimming the rich, rendered fat from her simmering pot. 

So Denys went for the letter. He came back crestfallen, stepping heavily into the kitchen's warm, fragrant atmosphere. “She will not let it out of her hand neither to me nor you, nor any he or she that lives.”

“I knew she would not,” said Cornelis.

“Whisht! whisht!” said Eli, “and let Denys tell his story.”

“'Nay,' said I, 'but be ruled by me.' 'Not I,' quoth she. 'Well, but,' quoth I, 'that same honey Obedience ye spake of.' 'You are a fool,' says she; 'obedience to Gerard is sweet, but obedience to any other body, who ever said that was sweet?' At last she seemed to soften a bit, and did give me a written paper for you, mademoiselle. Here 'tis.”

“For me?” said little Kate, colouring.

“Give that here!” said Eli, pulling the paper into his own sphere of control. He scanned the writing, translating Gerard's raw emotion into his own terms, and said almost in a whisper, “These be words from the letter. Hearken!

“'And, sweetheart, an if these lines should travel safe to thee, make thou trial of my people's hearts withal. Maybe they are somewhat turned towards me, being far away. If 'tis so they will show it to thee, since now to me they may not. Read, then, this letter! But I do strictly forbid thee to let it from thy hand; and if they still hold aloof from thee, why, then say nought, but let them think me dead. Obey me in this; for, if thou dost disrespect my judgment and my will in this, thou lovest me not.'”

There was a silence, thick as the caramelized onion steam rising from Catherine's hearth. Gerard's words, copied by Margaret, were handed round and inspected—a testament to the inherent untranslatability of subjective experience. To surrender the paper was to surrender the intimacy of his vernacular to their rigid judgment.

“Well,” said Catherine, “that is another matter. But methinks 'tis for her to come to us, not we to her.”

“Alas, mother! what odds does that make?”

“Much,” said Eli. “Tell her we are over many to come to her, and bid her hither, the sooner the better.”

When Denys was gone, Eli owned it was a bitter pill to him. “When that lass shall cross my threshold, all the mischief and misery she hath made here will seem to come in adoors in one heap. But what could I do, wife? We must hear the news of Gerard. I saw that in thine eyes, and felt it in my own heart. And she is backed by our undutiful but still beloved son, and so is she stronger than we, and brings our noses down to the grindstone, the sly, cruel jade. But never heed. We will hear the letter; and then let her go unblessed as she came unwelcome.”

“Make your mind easy,” said Catherine, adjusting the heat to coax the deep, roasted marrow notes from the bone, a cyclic act she performed to ward off grief. “She will not come at all.” And a tone of regret was visible.

Shortly after Richart, who had been hourly expected, arrived from Amsterdam. His heavy, booted steps dragged the jarring, abrasive noise of urban commercialization into the tranquil acoustic ecology of the home. Grave and dignified in his burgher's robe, gold chain, ruff, and furred cap, he exuded a sterile scent of cold coins and polished leather, entirely disconnected from the rich, earthy aromas of the agrarian hearth. He had risen a step higher than his parents, moving away from the native dialects and traditional sustenance of his youth into the sanitized, dominant lexicon of trade. Admitted to the family council, he viewed the rustic meal with thinly veiled disinterest, finding no sensory joy in the food. He showed plainly, though not discourteously, that his pride was deeply wounded by their having deigned to treat with Margaret Brandt.

“I see the temptation,” said he, speaking in the clipped, formalized vowels of the northern merchants, effectively erasing the cultural memory of his childhood tongue. “But which of us hath not at times to wish one way and do another?”

This threw a considerable chill over the old people, a sudden dissonance in the room's warm resonance. So little Kate put in a word. 

“Vex not thyself, dear Richart. Mother says she will not come.”

“All the better, sweetheart. I fear me, if she do, I shall hie me back to Amsterdam.”

Here Denys popped his head in at the door, his voice ringing like a bright brass bell through the fraught air, declaring: 

“She will be here at three on the great dial.”

They all looked at one another in silence, enveloped once more by the ancient, bubbling rhythm of the hearth pot, waiting for the collision of worlds.

The rhythm of the hearth-fire marked the passage of a tense afternoon, the popping of seasoned ash wood serving as the only voice in the kitchen until Catherine, an agrarian elder whose hands bore the ingrained memory of countless harvests, turned from the heavy stone mortar where she had been grinding fragrant coriander and brown mustard seed.

“Nay, Richart,” Catherine murmured, her voice steeped in the weary timbre of one trying to preserve the domestic harmony of her household. “For Heaven’s sake, let not this one sorry wench set us all by the ears. Hath she not soured the blood of this family enough?”

“In very deed she hath,” Richart replied, pacing the swept floorboards. “Fear me not, good mother. Let her come and read the letter of the poor boy she hath by devilish arts bewitched, and then let her go. Give me your word to show her no countenance beyond decent and constrained civility: less we may not, being in our own house; and I will say no more.”

On this brittle accord, the family awaited the intrusion. Margaret, for her part, prepared for the encounter as a warrior donning armor, though her armaments were woven of thread and dye. Denys had brought word they would receive her rather than come to her; her lip had curled at the petty assertion of power embedded in their burgher dialect, a pride of unlettered folk clinging to the dominant lexicon of their threshold. Yet for Gerard’s sake, she swallowed her loathing. 

Her garments were chosen to parry the sharp, scrutinizing glances of her foes. She wore a kirtle of dark blue English cloth, redolent of woad and the faint, earthy sweetness of crushed lavender in which it had been stored. Her farthingale and hose were of a glossy roan, the color of young claret poured against candlelight. There was no pretentious fur, only plain, snowy linen framing her throat in a square cut, starched and curiously plaited, smelling faintly of lye and fresh river water. A tight-fitting hood of dark blue cloth, hemmed with a subtle flash of silver, covered her head. Below the roan petticoat, her red shoes set off an arched instep. Beauty knew its business, anchoring itself in the physical reality of the senses.

She kept them waiting until the sun cast long, amber shadows by the dial. When she finally set out, the crunch of her red shoes against the cobblestones formed a hesitant rhythm beside Denys’s heavy, martial tread. Halfway to the house, the acoustic landscape of the town seemed to overwhelm her, and she stopped.

“Denys!” she whispered, her voice fracturing.

“Well, she-general?”

“I must go home,” she said, the words slipping out as a piteous exhale.

“What, have ye left somewhat behind?”

“What?”

“My courage. Oh! oh! oh!”

“Nay, nay, be brave, she-general. I shall be with you.”

“Ay, but wilt keep close to me when I be there?”

Denys swore his oath, and they resumed their march, the scent of damp earth and woodsmoke heralding their arrival at Eli’s door.

Within, a stifling silence had pooled. Mortification, curiosity, panting affection, and a sharp aversion mingled like contrasting spices in a bitter broth. When Denys pushed open the door alone, his rough, guttural organ cut through the quiet: “The she-comrade is without.”

“Fetch her in,” Eli ordered, his voice carrying the immovable weight of the patriarch. “Now whisht, all of ye. None speak to her but I.”

The door opened with a slow, agonizing creak. Margaret Brandt crossed the threshold, pale but resolute, carrying the heavy scent of cold air and unspoken grief into the warm, herb-scented room. They all rose but Kate, staring in mute shock. Margaret’s condition was undeniably visible beneath the heavy drape of her claret farthingale. Cornelis and Sybrandt’s eyes narrowed in silent hatred; Richart frowned at the disruption of her maidenly form, though the women’s postures softened at the undeniable, bodily truth of the life she carried.

“Be seated, mistress,” Eli said gravely, gesturing to a solitary chair set apart, an island of isolation.

She inclined her head and crossed the room. Drawing the letter from her bosom, she pressed the parchment to her lips—a tactile, intimate communion with the absent writer—and disposed herself to read. Yet, feeling the oppressive isolation of her placement, she dropped her hand to her side and made a swift, furtive motion to Denys. He stepped to her shoulder like a sentinel, his heavy boots sounding a defiant note against the floorboards.

Then, Margaret began to read. Her voice was a revelation of acoustic ecology, a mellow, clear instrument that vibrated with a soft, thrilling earnestness. She took Gerard’s written words, foreign and distant, and breathed them back into the native, resonant dialect of his ancestors, bridging the inherent untranslatability of his suffering.

*“I do nothing doubt, my Margaret, that long ere this shall meet thy beloved eyes, Denys, my most dear friend, will have sought thee out, and told thee the manner of our unlooked-for and most tearful parting. Therefore I will e’en begin at that most doleful day. What befell him after, poor faithful soul, fain, fain would I hear, but may not. Friend more stanch and loving had not David in Jonathan, than I in him. Be good to him, for poor Gerard’s sake.”*

A profound groan escaped Denys, shattering the quiet. He leaned his head against the high back of Margaret’s chair. She turned, finding his rough hand and pressing it warmly. Together, the sweetheart and the friend held fast as the voice wove on.

*“I went forward all dizzied, like one in an ill dream; and presently a gentleman came up with his servants, all on horseback. He drew rein at the brow of the hill, and sent his armed men back to rob me. They took my purse and the last copper, and rid gaily away. I wandered stupid on, a friendless pauper.”*

A collective sigh whispered through the room, punctuated by a sharp oath from Denys.

*“Presently a strange dimness came o’er me; I lay down to sleep on the snow. ’Twas ill done, with a store of wolves hard by, their howling a terrifying echo in the frozen silence. The drowsiness that did crawl o’er me was more than nature—it was a mythic slumber, a descent into the white void. And from that sleep I ne’er had waked, but that a hand did shake me rudely. I awoke to my troubles, and there stood a servant girl in her holiday suit.* 

*“‘Are ye rich, or are ye poor?’ she speered. I gave a loud cry at the untranslatable cruelty of the question. I told her I had been rich in a friend, rich in money and the spirits of youth, but now the Bastard of Burgundy had taken my friend, and a gentleman my purse. I was the poorest of the poor. Because I recked not whither I went, I followed her to a fine house hard by, into a noble dining-hall hung with heavy black tapestries that deadened all sound.”*

Margaret’s voice carried them into the mourning hall. Gerard’s letter did not describe mere sustenance, but a profound, mythic ritual seamlessly integrated into the domestic space.

*“There was set a table with many dishes, and but one plate. ‘Fall to!’ she whispered. When I speered why, she told me that when a gentleman dies in Burgundy, they serve the dead man’s dinner up as usual, till he be in the ground, and set some poor man to it. So I behoved to sit down. This kind lass poured me a goblet of wine. The rich, strong vintage—tasting of deep, loamy terroir, crushed dark cherries, and the toasted oak of its cask—curled around my sick heart, warming my frozen blood. I tasted it and wept that Denys was not there to appreciate its complex notes. She made me eat of every dish. Nought was here but her master’s daily dinner, yet it was a feast of profound culinary memory. I partook of roasted capon rubbed with wild thyme, and root vegetables glazed in elderberry reduction, nibbling of every dish to honor the dead. Eating not merely for fuel, but to anchor my shivering soul in the rich, sensory history of the estate.”*

*“The next day, they were all most kind, and the girl proffered me money from her small wage. My stomach rose against it; I would not take a free gift. So I set her in a good light to draw her picture, preserving her image as I had preserved the taste of the mourning feast.”*

Margaret paused, handing Denys a colored drawing to pass around. The women examined it with meticulous curiosity. It was a tangible record of a foreign culture: the girl’s hair bound in a tight linen bag, a yellow half-kerchief crossing her head to form stark, geometric points over her forehead and bosom. She wore a red kirtle kilted high, revealing a green farthingale and a great red leather purse, her feet clad in low-quartered, square-toed yellow leathern shoes—a vibrant tapestry of regional identity.

Margaret continued:

*“Often I sat down on the road where two paths met, and with heavy head did think of thee, my poor sweetheart, and of the little house at Tergou, where they all loved me once; though now it is turned to hate.”*

“Alas! that he will think so,” Catherine whispered, the agrarian rhythm of her heart aching at the broken familial bond.

“Whisht, wife!” Eli commanded.

*“And me sighing so in the quiet of the woods, a jarring noise shattered the peace. One came carolling like a bird adown t’ other road. I jumped up in ire to flee his dissonant, grating mirth. Lo! the songster was a humpbacked cripple, with a bloody bandage o’er his eye, and both legs gone at the knee.”*

“He! he! he! he! he!” Sybrandt cackled, his laughter a harsh, ugly sound that violated the room’s somber acoustic ecology.

Margaret’s eyes flashed with fierce, protective fire; she began to fold the parchment.

“Nay, lass,” said Eli. “Heed him not! Thou unmannerly cur, offer’t but again and I put thee to the door.”

“Why, what was there to gibe at, Sybrandt?” Catherine remonstrated mildly. “Is not our Kate afflicted? And is she not the most content of us all, singing like a merle and weaving her sweet notes into the very life of this house? Read on, lass, and stop our gabble wi’ somewhat worth the hearkening.”

*“I took myself to task for bemoaning my health while this wreck of nature praised God. He chanted for charity with a whine as piteous as wind at a keyhole. But when I told him I was robbed and poor as he, he ceased his whining in an instant. He spoke in a big, manly voice, stripped of its artificial piety. ‘Here, youngster, pull thou this strap.’ I pulled, and down came a stout pair of legs out of his back; half his hump melted away. His sores were but a painted fiction.”*

A collective, breathy “Oh!” escaped Margaret’s listeners.

*“He laughed at my astounded face, speaking in the dominant, cynical cant of the roads. He offered me his protection, advising me to quit France and make for the great German cities, the Paradise of craftsmen. He spoke of the cul de bois and the cul de jatte, offering to teach me how to maund, chaunt, and patter—a corruption of language that eroded all honest meaning. I told him I’d liever die than shame myself with such deceptive lexicons.”*

“Good lad! good lad!” Eli murmured.

*“Seeing I would not beg, he hired me to be the fingers to his head. ‘Now, servant,’ said he, ‘we will dine.’ Out of his beggar’s wallet he brought a feast that defied his lowly station: cold roasted fowl dripping with savory fats, pastries rich with butter, a good dozen of precious spices—nutmeg, clove, and mace—lapped delicately in fine flax paper, and wine fit for a king. Ne’er feasted I better, savoring the sharp bite of black peppercorns and the sweet finish of the wine under the open sky. Yet when I praised honest Lazarus who fed on crumbs, this rogue scoffed, admitting his own sores were painted with spearwort and biting acids that no dog would lick.*

*“We took the road and came upon two little wayside inns, their armories faded. Here I saw the hollow soul of commerce. My master disguised himself as a decent tradesman and played upon the vanity of the innkeepers with fulsome flattery. He pitted ‘The Three Frogs’ against ‘The Four Hedgehogs,’ convincing each to pay me a silver franc to paint their signs brighter than the other’s. They cared not for the art, only the loud, superficial commercialization of outshining their neighbor.*

*“With our coin, Cul de Jatte bought me a psaltery. So I played a strain, a harmonious, sweet melody the good monks taught me. The resonance of the strings reminded me of my young days and our hearth, bringing the water of true cultural memory to my een. But my master’s visage twisted as if sipping foulest medicine. ‘Zounds, stop that bellyache blether,’ quoth he. ‘That will ne’er wile a stiver out o’ peasants’ purses.’ He claimed it would gar the kine jump into rivers to escape the sound. And then, he sang so blasphemous a stave, and eke so obscene, that it was as an industrial clatter, a harsh noise pollution that assaulted the mind and spirit. I drew away lest the lightning strike his ribaldry.* 

*“I bade him keep his breath to cool his broth. Never would I shame my folk by joining his profane chorus. ‘Then,’ says he sulkily, ‘the first fire we light by the wayside, clap thou on the music box! so ’twill make our pot boil for the nonce; but with your,*

*Good people, let us peak and pine,*
*Cut tristful mugs, and miaul and whine*
*Thorough our nosen chaunts divine,’*

When Margaret recovered, the first sensation to pierce the dark was deeply olfactory—the agrarian, grounding musk of Catherine’s woollen sleeves, redolent of toasted barley, aged milk, and the earthy dampness of root cellars. The honest half of the family she had invaded like a foe stood around her, their voices a symphony of rough, Low Dutch dialects. Their words were a percussive, homely cadence, especially Giles, whose booming resonance disrupted the delicate acoustic ecology of the parlor, roaring at her that she was not to take on so. Gerard was alive, he insisted, or he could not have penned a lexicon so vast, nor crafted script so meticulously small.

“Ay, good Master Giles,” sighed Margaret feebly, finding a fragile anchor in the rhythmic sounds of the hearth. “He was alive. But how know I what hath since befallen him? Oh, why left he Holland to go among strangers fierce as lions? And why did I not drive him from me sooner than part him from his own flesh and blood? Forgive me, you that are his mother!”

She gently removed Catherine's arm, her body seeking the solid floor, but a brief, tactile struggle against the elder woman’s steadfast, flour-dusted embrace ended with Margaret resting against Catherine's bosom. The transfer of the parchment from Margaret to Eli was accompanied by the dry, rustling crackle of the heavily inked vellum. “I will trust it from my hand now,” she said faintly. “In sooth, I am little fit to read any more—and loth to leave my comfort.” 

“Read thou, Richart,” said Eli, his voice carrying the deep, unhurried timbre of the old ways. “Thine eyes be younger than mine.”

Richart took the letter. “Well,” he murmured, marveling at the dense preservation of memory etched upon the page. “A writeth with a needle's point. Why is he not in my counting-house at Amsterdam instead of vagabonding it out yonder!”

Richart cleared his throat, adjusting to the rhythm of his brother's distant voice:

*“When I came to myself I was seated in the litter, and my good merchant holding of my hand. I babbled I know not what in our native tongue, the untranslatable syllables of my distress lost on him, and then shuddered awhile in silence. He put a horn of wine to my lips.”*

Catherine’s eyes shone with maternal warmth. “Bless him! bless him!”
“Whisht!” commanded Eli, protecting the sonic clarity of the reading.

*“And I told him what had befallen. My leg was sprained sore, my points broken. ‘Sir,’ I said, ‘I shall be but a burden, and can make you no harmony now; my poor psaltery it is broken.’ I did grieve over my broken music, that pure acoustic companion which had shielded my mind from the chaotic noise of so many weary leagues. But he tended me like a kind father.*

*“January 19.—Strange! whene'er I fall in with men like fiends, God sendeth some good soul, lest I turn away from human kind. And lo! if good Master Fugger hath not bought me a new psaltery, restoring the acoustic sanctuary of my travels.”*

“Eli, my man,” Catherine interrupted, the agrarian matriarch recognizing a debt of profound sustenance, “an yon merchant comes our way, let us buy a hundred ells of cloth of him, and not higgle.”
“That will I, take your oath on't!”

While Richart prepared to read on, Kate caught her mother's eye. With a faint blush, she drew a piece of work from under her apron, her needle making a soft, rhythmic *shhh-shhh* through the linen. Catherine, matching the domestic cadence, drew her own work from her pocket. Both sweet, surreptitious creatures were sewing babies' caps. Horror! They were shaped like little monks' cowls—a circularity of generations, the mythic weight of the cloister seamlessly integrated into the intimate, everyday utility of swaddling clothes.

*“January 20.—Lombardy plains burst on me. Oh, Margaret! A land flowing with milk and honey. The olfactory landscape is intoxicating; even in winter, it looks warmer than beloved Holland. Here be vines, cedars, and silver-leaved olives. The draught oxen wear white linen on their necks. We crossed a river in a chained ferry-boat, the windlass creaking a steady, rhythmic measure. An old woman, dreading the evil eye, held out her crucifix to a young wench—a timeless, mythic warding performed amidst the mundane crossing.*

*“January 25.—Safe at Venice. Dost mind how Peter would oft fill our ears with tales of this sea-enthroned city, the forum of St. Mark? Lying in pain, I turn mine eyes inward, binding my sheaves of wisdom. This then have I, Sir Footsore, learned as a traveling historian of hearth and habit: each nation’s soul is inextricably bound to its sustenance and its native dialect. The Germans in the north are frank; their general blot is drunkenness. They say commonly, ‘Kanstu niecht sauffen und fressen so kanstu kienem hern wol dienen.’ An idiom where the power dynamics of servitude and the consumption of food are fiercely untranslatable.* 

*“In England, the worshipful sugar their wine and drink for courtesy. In their cups, the Germans use little discourse, crying ‘Seyte frolich!’ Their drunken sport, ‘Kurlemurlehuff,’ is an elaborate acoustic ritual of clinking glass, beard, and table, intermixed with precisely ordered whistlings and finger-snappings. Their topers sleep with pebbles in their mouths—a tactile mitigation to vent the heat of their fleshy wine-pots. Yet their women are most temperate, far before our Holland lasses, who, being maids, put the question to the men, and being wived, do lord it over them.”*

“'Tis sooth!” Catherine nodded vigorously. “'Twas Beck Hulse. This comes of a woman wedding a boy.”

*“In the south where wine is, the gentry drink themselves bare; but in the north, with their heavy, malt-thickened beers, a noble shall sooner burst his body than melt his lands. The French are fairer spoken, but their sweet words cost them nought. They call it payer en blanche.”*

Denys slapped his knee. “Les coquins! ha! ha!”

*“Such straws of speech show how blows the wind of their lexicon. Tie but your napkin round a bench, and no Frenchman will sit there. Natheless, they say things in sport which are shocking: ‘Allez au diable!’ Custom belike, an erosion of meaning through overuse. They are moderate in drinking, mixing water with their wine, and are enchanting company over their cups. The lower half of their windows is wood, the upper glass, but fixed; the German windows are all glass, movable, shining far and near. In France, they thrust the law’s finger into every pie, and often hang their female malefactors instead of drowning them decently. In Germany, the snails be red. I lie not.*

*“The French buy minstrelsy, but make their own mirth. The Germans foster paid fools with ear-caps to simulate madness—a superficial commercialization of calamity that asks pity, not laughter.* 

*“The Italians judge a man by his speech and gesture. They are singular in their temperance of food, yet foolish in their extortionate laws. Delicate in eating, they abhor putting their hand in the plate. They tell of a cardinal at Rome who armeth his guest's left hand with a little bifurcal dagger to hold the meat, while his knife cutteth it. A sterile, metallic intrusion, methinks, trying to be wiser than Him who made the hand so tactile and prehensile.”*

“I am of your mind, my lad,” Eli murmured, a traditionalist who understood the sacred, tactile connection to daily bread.

*“Their bread is lovely white, fermented with meticulous care to a delicate crumb. Yet their meats they wholly spoil with a harsh sprinkling of dry cheese over them; O, perversity of the palate! Their salt is black; without a lie. In commerce, these Venetians are masters, yet twenty thousand Jezebels pay a tax to the State. The commercial din here pollutes the mind's quiet; ointment is cried at every corner. They even bend nature to their commerce, forcing roses to bloom unseasonably in January by digging round the discouraged roots and bruising them with cloves—an artificial scent masking an unnatural harvest.* 

*“And for artful fountains, Augsburg harnesses the foul knave Smoke to good Sir Spit, turning stout Master Roast. Yet for pure utility, London and Lubeck bring water tame in pipes to every burgher's door. But I pray God for little Holland, that there an assassin may be yclept an assassin in our honest mother tongue, till domesday; and let others gloze foul faults with silken lexicons who can! Finally, a true-hearted Frenchman is near perfect; such a man is my Denys, spite of his foul mouth.”*

Denys bristled, inspecting the letter gravely. “I read not the letter so. 'Tis kittle work perusing these black scratches. My foul mouth! Eh, mesdames?” But the women merely smiled slyly over their rhythmic sewing, and Denys, feigning patient innocence, bade Richart continue.

*“January 26.—I was carried to St. Mark his church. Outside are four famous brass horses. Inside is a treasure greater than Toledo: a jewelled pitcher from a Persian king, a ducal cap blazing with chrysolite. But what are these commercial hoardings compared to the sacred relics? I stood and handled St. Mark's gospel, written with his own hand. Dear Margaret, his sacred body was brought here in 810, but the very place it lay was forgotten. Then holy priests fasted, and lo! the Evangelist's body brake at midnight through the marble and stood before them. They laid him beneath the altar, preserving the miraculous crevice. To see such mythic power carved into the very architecture of their daily worship! I saw the Virgin's stone chair, and her picture that brings the rain.*

*“Outside, on a great porphyry slab in the piazza, were three ghastly heads rotting. These were traitors, their friends having sold their blood for two thousand ducats. But what I most pondered was a stone effigy of an enormous act enacted last year. Four brethren conspired to poison the other two to halve their land. At a mutual banquet, twain drugged the wine, and the other twain envenomed a marchpane. A tragic inversion of the culinary arts! That marchpane, pounded from sweet almonds, carried the bitter, hidden alkaloid tang of death, while the wine masked their mortal agony within its terroir. The same afternoon lay four 'brave men' grovelling around one table, weaponizing the sacred act of sustenance, cursing one another to a miserable conclusion.* 

*“After this, they took me to the quay. I espied among the masts one garlanded with amaranth flowers. I let my guide know the custom of our Dutch skippers to hoist flowers when courting a maid. But oh, Margaret! The sight of that ship made my heart leap with the sudden, overwhelming acoustic and visual assurance of a countryman. On the stern of that Dutch boy was written in our honest, uncorrupted alphabet:*

*RICHART ELIASSOEN, AMSTERDAM.”*

For generations uncounted, the maritime stretch between Naples and Rome had swallowed vessels in a cyclic churning of the Mediterranean, a watery graveyard where the mythic wrath of tempests routinely shattered the fragile, everyday rhythms of coastal life. About two months before the quiet, domestic warmth of Eli’s hearth would see its own subtle dramas unfold, this ancient coastal rhythm repeated itself. A battered ship labored against a stiff gale blowing dead onto the shore. To the locals clustered on the beach, the vessel’s extravagant poop and reversed-cocked-hat lines seemed a living, panting creature, struggling to maintain its physical form against the violent, aerosolized spray of bitter brine and crushed kelp. 

Aboard the ship, the acoustic ecology was a nightmare of auditory violence. The natural, rhythmic symphony of the ocean had been entirely obliterated by a deafening, chaotic noise pollution: the shrieking of tearing canvas, the agonizing groan of over-stressed timber, and the panicked cacophony of human terror. It was an environment that fractured the psychological well-being of the passengers, who were huddled around the mast in a desperate, trembling mass. 

In this moment of sensory overload, the dying fragments of regional dialects clashed in a panicked, polytheistic marketplace of salvation. The superficial commercialization of their faith laid bare the power dynamics of their dominant lexicons. An English merchant, his native tongue clipped and frantic, vowed a heap of gold to Our Lady of Walsingham. A Genoese trader countered in his own rapid dialect, pledging a four-pound silver collar to Our Lady of Loretto, while a Tuscan noble shouted a promise of ten pounds of fragrant, rendered beeswax lights to Our Lady of Ravenna. They bargained with the divine as if trading sacks of grain and salted fish, desperately attempting to purchase their survival. 

Amidst this auditory assault stood Gerard, his ashy cheek dampened by the metallic-tasting spray. He stood slightly apart, his hands gripping the coarse, salt-rimed shroud, wincing as each wave crashed with a percussive boom. He found no solace in the merchants’ transactional prayers. 

Suddenly, a gust of catastrophic force caught the sail. With a sound like a cannon shot—a rupture in the very fabric of the air—the rotten shrouds snapped. The sail tore away, fluttering into the sea like a discarded scrap of parchment, swallowed by the gray-black waves. A wave caught the ship on the quarter, drenching the passengers to the bone in freezing, iodine-heavy water.

The acoustic chaos only intensified. One trader vowed to journey barefoot to Compostella; two Neapolitans squabbled in their fading vernacular, one shouting a vow to St. Christopher of a waxen image of his own weight, only to whisper in the next breath that he would not spare a single rush dip if he actually reached the shore. 

Yet, amidst this terrifying destruction, a pocket of profound, domestic stillness existed. A Roman woman of the humbler class sat with her child at her half-bared breast. While the men around her squeaked in animal terror, she engaged in the ultimate act of bodily sustenance. She was suckling her child in the jaws of death, offering the sweet, rich warmth of her milk against the encroaching, bitter cold of the sea. She neither wept nor bargained in a corrupted tongue. Antiquity itself sat within her; the ancient Roman blood in her veins dictated a stoic, quiet dignity that required no translation. The rhythmic sound of her child feeding was a tiny, resilient note of life within the deafening roar of the tempest.

On the poop deck, a gigantic Dominican friar stood like the Colossus of Rhodes. He ignored the acoustic terror, his powerful lungs projecting verses from the Canticles in the dominant, sonorous Latin of the Church. The deep, unwavering resonance of his voice cut through the ambient panic. He absolved those who crawled to his knees, laying his heavy hands upon them as though they were gathered around a fragrant, incense-filled sacristy rather than a splintering deck. Gerard instinctively gravitated toward the steadfast acoustic anchor of the friar’s voice.

The sailors, desperate to relieve the hull, cut down the useless mast. It fell with a sickening crash over the side. The captain, his face pale and smelling of stale sweat and fear, abandoned the helm. “Lighten her!” he screamed, his voice cracking. “Fling all overboard!”

The captain’s superstitious worldview took hold. He spoke of fiery globes—Castor and Pollux—claiming their singular appearance doomed the vessel. The passengers, driven by primal terror, seized the cargo. They dragged a heavy sack from beneath an elderly, sea-sick Jewish merchant. The sack rattled with the culinary and cultural inheritance of a lifetime: silver candlesticks that had illuminated generations of Sabbath tables, silver plates etched with the memories of ancestral feasts, and hanaps that had cradled sweet, ceremonial wines. 

“Let go, thou hoary villain!” the others cried, wrenching the sack from his frail hands. They heaved it over the side. It splashed into the heavy brine and sank. 

The old man uttered a cry of pure, untranslatable anguish. His white hair whipped in the wind as he glared at the spot where his sensory history, his connection to the sustenance of his forebears, had vanished forever. He cursed them, the sea, and the heavens, his personal lexicon of grief swallowed by the uncaring wind.

A tremendous wave pooped the ship, jamming the rudder with a splintering crack and flooding the deck with foaming, metallic-tasting water. The panicked crowd turned on Gerard, noticing his silence. “He has never invoked a single saint!” they cried in their mingled tongues. “He is a heathen!”

“Alas, good friends,” Gerard replied, his teeth chattering, his palate dry with fear. “Nearer God’s throne than these St. Peter sitteth. I must go straight to Him that made the sea, and the saints, and me.” In his own intimate vernacular, he prayed to the sweet, pitiful Jesus who had walked the waters of Genezaret, asking for salvation for the sake of dear Margaret—an invocation of the deep, domestic love and hearthside sustenance that grounded his very identity.

In a rush of egotistical panic, thirty sailors and passengers crowded into the small boat, abandoning the vessel. Left behind were the paralyzed, the bewildered, the grieving Jewish merchant mourning his lost heritage, the towering friar communing in silent Latin, and the Roman woman, patiently drawing her child to the warmth of her bosom.

Gerard’s innate respect for life and sustenance mobilized him. “Wife, I’ll save thee yet,” he declared. Finding no planks, his eyes fell upon the life-sized wooden image of the Virgin Mary, reverently detached from the mast by a praying sailor. The wood smelled faintly of damp cedar and old myrrh. He hoisted it up and ran aft. 

“Come, wife,” he cried over the roar of the surf. “I'll lash thee and the child to this.”

She turned her dark, expressive eyes upon him. The subjective experience of her terror and awe crystallized into a single, native word: “Thyself?!” The untranslatability of that word—its incredible weight of magnanimity and maternal recognition—struck Gerard deeply. 

“I am a man, and have no child to take care of,” he answered. He lashed the fragrant, worm-eaten wood to her side, sliding her gently into the violently churning waters, using an oar to protect her from the crushing hull. “Poor youth! God forgive me! My child!” she cried, before the sea took her. 

A heavy hand, smelling of coarse wool and salt, clamped onto Gerard’s shoulder. The sonorous, acoustic depth of the friar’s voice rumbled in his ear. “'Tis well. Now come with me.” 

Together, they seized the broken mast. “Fling it in,” the friar commanded, “and follow it.” 

They threw themselves into the freezing, bitter expanse. A bewildered passenger leaped after them, clutching one end of the timber. The sensory experience was a brutalizing assault. The mast kicked like a wild beast; the aerosolized spray flogged their faces mercilessly, blinding them with salt and choking their airways. Suddenly, a long, grating shudder reverberated through the water. The ship had struck the rocks. 

The waves hurled them against the stationary hull. Their companion’s head struck the upper part of the broken rudder. The sound was a horrible, percussive crack—like a sledgehammer shattering a hollow coconut. He sank instantly, leaving only a dark, coppery stain on the foaming water and a white clot on the jagged wood, his final death cry a fleeting auditory ghost swiftly erased by the roaring sea. 

The friar uttered a rhythmic Latin prayer for the soul, maintaining his composure. They rolled through a basin of watery, gray hills. In the chaotic visual fragments, Gerard saw the shore speckled with vibrant, gesturing Italians, and the Roman woman rising and falling, paddling gallantly with her right arm, fiercely sustaining the life of the child clutched in her left. 

“I touched the ground,” the friar said quietly, a sudden intrusion of mundane reality into the mythic terror. 

“Impossible, father,” Gerard gasped, choking on the brine. “We are more than a hundred yards from shore.”

“I have business of Holy Church on hand, and may not waste time floating when I can walk,” the friar stated. The physical sensation of sand beneath his sandals grounded him. Releasing the mast, the giant monk simply walked through the overpowering waves, tossing his head back to breathe, plowing lustily forward in service of his unyielding dominion. Gerard followed, struggling against the intense outward suction of the undertow until stout fishermen, smelling of cured hemp and raw fish, threw long spears in a triple chain and hauled him onto the wet, coarse sand.

The friar shook the heavy water from his robes, bestowed a brief, formulaic benediction upon the dialect-speaking natives, and stalked away toward Rome without a single backward glance, entirely detached from the sensory reality of the sea that had failed to claim him. 

The locals brought Gerard to an enormous, roaring fire on the beach. The acoustic warmth of the crackling timber and the rich, complex olfactory landscape of roasting pine, dry earth, and sea salt offered immediate, profound psychological relief. With meticulous care, Gerard drew a parchment and a paper from his bosom—the physical vessels of his personal lexicon and cultural memory. Only when he had dried them perfectly before the restorative flames did he consent to strip off his sodden garments and don the heavy, fragrant wool of a fisherman’s dress. 

The sea had broken the ship entirely. The captain washed ashore, attributing his survival not to the physical realities of the tide, but to Castor and Pollux. The disconsolate Jewish merchant landed on a fragment of wreck; touching the sand, he offered a reward for his lost bag of heritage, a plea that met only with uncomprehending amusement from those who could not translate the depth of his cultural starvation. The thirty egotists in the boat washed ashore one by one—dead. The singular survivor among them was dragged to the fire, but his life soon expired, his personal history extinguished.

As Gerard stood by the sea, absorbing the bitter smell of the kelp and the warmth of the fire, a hand touched his shoulder. It was the Roman matron. The profound gratitude she felt existed in a realm where spoken dialects and dominant lexicons held no power. She took his hand gently, raising it to her lips with a noble, antique grace, kissing it as if bestowing an honor upon a provider of feasts. 

With moist eyes and glowing cheeks, she held up her child—warm, breathing, and sustained—and guided the infant to kiss their preserver. Gerard, deeply moved by this silent exchange of maternal devotion, kissed the child in return. No words were spoken. The subjective experience of life preserved needed no translation. It was the ancient world honoring the new from the deepest, most silent chambers of the heart. 

The next afternoon, Gerard—aged by the sheer, mythic force of the elements, versed now in the bitter taste of survival, and having navigated the razor's edge of the grave by land and sea—arrived at the Eternal City, carrying the physical and sensory memories of his survival within him.

Gerard took a modest lodging on the west bank of the Tiber, enveloped daily by the acoustic dissonance of a city that thrummed with abrasive, industrial mercantile clatter.
