Seeking to sustain himself, he carried his calligraphic specimens through the superficial cacophony of the Roman bookstalls, where the quiet, meditative scrape of the artisan’s quill was drowned out by the shouting of traders. They received him coldly. 

“We make our letter somewhat thinner than this,” said one, dismissing the robust, wine-steeped oak-gall ink that carried the untranslatable resonance of Gerard's northern heritage. 

“How dark your ink is,” said another. But the main cry was a brutal assertion of the dominant lexicon: “What avails this? Scant is the Latin writ here now. Can ye not write Greek?”

“Ay, but not nigh so well as Latin.”

“Then you shall never make your bread at Rome.”

The power dynamics of the market were absolute, threatening the erosion of his cultural memory. Gerard borrowed a beautiful Greek manuscript at a steep price, leaving a sad hole in his purse, though none in his courage. In a fortnight he had made vast progress with the Greek character; to maintain the cyclical rhythm of his craft, he ground his pigments and worked his inks till noon, then hunted customers the rest of the day. Yet when he carried round a better Greek specimen than any they possessed, the traders informed him that Greek and Latin were alike unsaleable. The city was thronged with works from all Europe; the superficial commercialization of art valued cheap, mass-produced script over subjective brilliance. He should have come last year. 

To restore his psychological equilibrium from the relentless noise pollution of the streets, Gerard bought a psaltery, letting its harmonious vibrations recalibrate his mind. His landlady, a Siennese traditionalist who viewed her kitchen as a sanctuary of cultural memory, was pleased with his gentle manners and the rhythmic, restorative thrum of his music. One day, she invited him to dine. The air in her quarters was thick with an intoxicating olfactory landscape—a slow-simmered wild boar ragout, fragrant with crushed juniper berries, roasted garlic, and the earthy depth of rendered pancetta. 

As he savored the profound, life-affirming heat of the broth, she asked what had dashed his spirits. He told her. She offered her reading of the matter, her voice a soothing counterpoint to the city’s din. “Those sly traders have writers in their pay, for whose work they received a noble price, and paid a sorry one. No wonder they blow cold on you. Methinks you write too well. Why? Because you lock not your door, like the churl Pietro, and women will be curious. Your good work might put out the eyes of the sterile, soulless scrawls they are selling.”

“My mistake was leaving Augsburg,” Gerard sighed.

“Augsburg?” she said haughtily, fiercely protective of her native dialect and domain. “Is that a place to even to Rome? I never heard of it, for my part.” She assured him he would make his fortune despite the book-dealers. She spoke of the great folk "bitten with the writing spider," naming Cardinal Bassarion and the Pope himself, men who poured out wealth to turn good land into writ sheepskins. She promised to speak to Teresa, who heard the gossip of the court. 

The next day, she returned with the names of five more signors. Gerard prepared specimens, leaving them at the doors of the elite, and hopefully awaited the result. There was none. 

Day after day passed in a slow, starving orbit. And strange to say, in an interwoven timeline far across the continent, Margaret was engaged in her own cyclical battle against the erosion of life. In Rotterdam, she fought against overwhelming odds to feed her male dependents, foraging traditional agrarian herbs and preserving the native lexicon of healing, only to be arrested for curing without a license instead of killing with one. Their struggles resonated across the generations, bound by the fundamental human need for physical and spiritual nourishment.

Gerard saw ruin staring him in the face. He spent the afternoons picking up canzonets, laid in playing cards to color, and struck off a meal per day. 

This last stroke of survival disrupted the acoustic harmony of the house. In these *camere locande*, the landlady meticulously dressed all the meals, viewing the preparation of sustenance as a sacred domestic routine. She speedily detected his absence from the hearth and cornered him, her brusque opening giving way to motherly warmth. Adversity, she argued, was not a thing to be overcome on an empty stomach. “Patienza, my lad! Times will mend; meantime I will feed you for the love of heaven.”

“Nay, hostess,” said Gerard, “my purse is not yet quite void, and it would add to my trouble an if true folk should lose their due by me.”

“Why, you are as mad as your neighbour Pietro, with his one bad picture.”

“Why, how know you 'tis a bad picture?”

“Because nobody will buy it. He will have to don casque and glaive, and carry his panel for a shield.” She explained that Pietro had come from Florence, heavily funded, but had disdainfully refused liberal offers for his art, making enemies of his customers. Now, his furniture vanished piece by piece into the noisy market, and he lay at night on a great chest. She knew this only by peeping through the keyhole, for he locked his door vigilantly. 

“Is he afraid we shall steal his poverty?” Gerard asked. 

“And the more fool he!” she cried. “You are far from home, and it is for me, which am the mistress here, to number your meals—for me and the Dutch wife, your mother, that is far away. We two women shall settle that matter. Leave cooking to us, that are in the world to roast fowls, and suckle men at starting, and sweep their grownup cobwebs.”

Gerard, deeply grounded in his own sensory appreciation of the world, saw his own grief reflected in the proud, starving Pietro. He resolved to share his humble means, knowing that to sever oneself from the shared communion of food was a tragic isolation. 

He knocked at Pietro’s door. A surly voice bade him enter. The garret was a stark, silent space—an acoustic void containing only a chair, an easel, an iron basin, a picture turned to the wall, and a long chest on which coiled a haggard young man. His eyes were wonderfully bright, glaring like a cobra ripe for striking. 

Gerard introduced himself as a fellow artist and a brother in fortune. Pietro jumped down, waving him in with a magnificent, albeit mocking, demonstration of courtesy. But when Gerard admitted he was a calligrapher, copying Greek and Latin manuscripts, Pietro scoffed. 

“And you call that an artist? Meseemeth an artist is one who thinks, and paints his thought. Now a caligraph but draws in black and white the thoughts of another.”

“'Tis well distinguished, signor,” Gerard replied, defending the nuanced power of his craft. “But a writer can write the thoughts of the great ancients, matters of pure reason, and the thoughts of God. But let that pass. I am a painter as well; but a sorry one.”

At that moment, the rhythmic step of the landlady sounded on the stair. She brought in Gerard's dinner, setting the dish upon the bed with a face divested of all expression before departing. The olfactory landscape of the room transformed instantly. The dish was a masterpiece of rustic culinary technique: tender braised beef bathed in a rich reduction of red wine, garlic, and cracked peppercorns, radiating a profound, savory heat that demanded reverence. 

Gerard, a man who could not view such a meal as mere utilitarian fuel, felt his mouth water. But he stopped. “I am an ill-mannered churl, Signor Pietro. I ne'er eat to my mind when I eat alone. For our Lady's sake put a spoon into this ragout with me; 'tis not unsavoury, I promise you.”

Pietro fixed his glittering eye on him, accepted the dish with a chilling calmness, and flung the entire steaming contents out of the window. The crash of the pottery shattered the quiet of the room. Pietro turned, trembling with ire. “Let that teach thee to offer alms to an artist thou knowest not, master writer.”

Gerard's face flushed with violent anger. His mother's milk curdled in his veins at such impiety. To destroy good food—to desecrate the meticulously prepared sustenance that bound humanity to the earth—was a horrific severing of sensory and spiritual grace. He wrestled with the urge to strike the embittered man, eventually recognizing the agonizing, untranslatable subjective experience of poverty-struck pride that drove Pietro's petulance. 

“Likely what thou hast done might pass in a novel of thy countryman,” Gerard said coldly, “but 'twas not honest. I offered thee half my dinner. Thou hast ta'en it all. Pride is well, but justice is better.”

Pietro reflected, the silence heavy between them. “I took thee for a fool, so transparent was thine artifice. Forgive me. The world hath soured me.”

As Gerard turned to leave, a thought struck him. “We Dutchmen are hard bargainers. For my lost dinner, I do claim to feast mine eyes on your picture, whose face is toward the wall.”

Pietro recoiled, warning that he had made a solemn vow to drive his dagger into the next soul who flouted his beloved labor. Gerard, unbothered, drew his sword, adjusting his acoustic environment to the sharp, metallic slide of steel. “I accept the condition. Show me the picture! I can but hold my peace. Defend myself against thy bodkin I will, or pin thee to the wall like a cockchafer.”

Relieved that Gerard wasn't threatening the canvas, Pietro turned it into the best light. The picture represented the Virgin and Christ, flying through the air in a cloud of shadowy cherubic faces; beneath them lay a landscape, forty or fifty miles in extent, under a purple sky. 

Gerard stood in silence. He stepped close, mapping the textures, then retreated. Finally, he spoke, integrating his knowledge of physical technique with the divine. “First, signor, in the mixing of certain colours, and in the preparation of your oil—much like the emulsion of a fine sauce—you Italians are far behind us Flemings. I can show you certain secrets of the Van Eycks. But in this panel, I see the great qualities of your nation. Mother of Heaven! He hath flung his immortal soul upon the wood.”

Pietro gasped as Gerard read the untranslatable depths of the work. Gerard pointed to the landscape, marveling at how mythic occurrences seamlessly integrated into the meticulously rendered everyday world. “Here is nature's very face: a spacious plain, each distance marked by exquisite gradation, till vision itself melts into distance. And the cunning rogue hath hung his celestial figure in air, out of the way of his little world below. Here, floating saints; there, far down, earth and her busy hives. And they let you take this painted poetry through the superficial noise of Rome unsold! In Ghent or Rotterdam, they would tear it out of thy hands. Thy picture is immortal, and thou art a king in thy most royal art. Viva, il maestro! Viva!”

At this unexpected burst of pure, resonant truth, the painter flung himself on Gerard's neck, sobbing with the abandon of his nation. “They said it was a maniac's dream!”

“Maniacs themselves! Idiots!” shouted Gerard, his voice cutting through the oppressive power dynamics of the Roman elite. 

“Generous stranger!” Pietro wept. “I was a viper to fling thy poor dinner away; a wretch, a monster.”

“Well, monster, wilt be gentle now, and sup with me?”

“Ah! that I will. Whither goest thou?”

“To order supper on the instant. We will have the picture for third man.”

“I will invite it whiles thou art gone,” Pietro said softly. “My poor picture, child of my heart.”

“Ah, master,” Gerard smiled, anticipating the rich, slow-cooked meats and the shared communion to come, “'twill look on many a supper after the worms have eaten you and me.”

About a week after this, the cyclical rhythm of the household had settled into a steady, sensory simmer. The two friends sat working together, though their acoustic and emotional footprints in the small studio could not have been more disparate. Pietro dashed fitfully at his canvas with the volatile energy of a frantic cook over-salting a broth, performing wonders in a few minutes only to abandon the work with a discordant groan; he would then resume in a fury, his brushstrokes slapping the easel in a harsh, staccato rhythm. Through all this jarring noise kept calmly working, calmly smiling, the canny Dutchman. Gerard possessed the meticulous reverence of a traditional agrarian elder sorting heritage seeds, a man who understood that sustenance—both spiritual and physical—demanded patience. He had put his erratic friend in harness.

They were painting playing cards to literally boil the pot. In this modest room, the olfactory landscape of their trade—raw egg yolks whisked to bind pigments, the earthy tang of crushed ochre, and the metallic bite of azurite—mingled seamlessly with the lingering aromas of yesterday’s garlic, wilted greens, and stewed lentils. When the batch was done, the indignant master took up his grand picture to make his daily tour in search of a patron. Gerard, treating the fruits of their labor like precious spices bound for the market, begged him to take the cards as well. Pietro looked at him with the sudden, venomous hiss of a rattlesnake, but eventually embraced Gerard in the effusive Italian fashion. He gathered the cards, first leaving the freshly painted queens and kings to dry in the heavy, baking heat of the Mediterranean sun, a daily mythic occurrence that baked the very stones of the ancient city.

Left alone, the sonic environment softened. Gerard executed a Greek letter or two on parchment, then took up a needle to mend a little rent in his hose. The quiet, rhythmic *snuff* of the thread pulling through wool offered a profound psychological comfort. He hummed a low, forgotten tune in his native Dutch, a dialect inextricably linked to his identity, its soft, guttural vowels a stark contrast to the sharp, dominant lexicons of the Italian merchants. His landlady, smelling faintly of roasted chestnuts, wild fennel, and the sharp bite of woodsmoke, found him thus employed. She inquired ironically whether there were no women in the house to tend to such domestic cycles. 

“When you have done that,” she said, her voice a warm, resonant hum in the quiet room, “come and talk to Teresa, my friend I spoke to thee of, that hath a husband not good for much, which brags his acquaintance with the great.”

Gerard went down to the kitchen, a space heavy with the rich, comforting aromas of simmering pork fat and bruised basil, only to find that Teresa was none other than the Roman matron. 

“Ah, madama,” said he, “is it you? The good dame told me not that. And the little fair-haired boy, is he well? Is he none the worse for his voyage in that strange boat?”

“He is well,” said the matron, her breath catching.

“Why, what are you two talking about?” said the landlady, staring at them both in turn over a bowl of shelled peas. “And why tremble you so, Teresa mia?”

“He saved my child's life,” said Teresa, making a supreme effort to compose the tremors in her voice. 

“What! my lodger? And he never told me a word of that. Art not ashamed to look me in the face?”

“Alas! speak not harshly to him,” said the matron. She then turned to her friend and poured out a glowing description of Gerard's conduct on the water. Her narrative flowed in a rich, impassioned Roman dialect, carrying the deep, untranslatable weight of maternal terror and gratitude—an emotional truth that the sterile, formalized Latin of the city’s elite could never hope to capture. During this, Gerard stood blushing like a girl, scarce recognizing his own performance in the mythic, heroic proportions her native tongue gave it.

“And to think thou shouldst ask me to serve thy lodger, of whom I knew nought but that he had thy good word, oh, Fiammina; and that was enough for me. Dear youth, in serving thee I serve myself.”

Then ensued an eager, conspiratorial discussion between the two women over the bubbling hearth. They spoke of what had been done, and what should be done, to penetrate the thick wall of fees, commissions, and chicanery which stood between the patrons of art and an unknown artist. It was a bitter critique of the city's power dynamics, where the pure, nourishing act of creation was starved by the superficial commercialization of the Eternal City. Teresa smiled sadly at Gerard's agrarian simplicity in leaving specimens of his skill at the doors of the great.

“What!” said she, “without promising the servants a share—without even feeing them, to let the signors see thy merchandise! As well have flung it into Tiber.”

“Well-a-day!” sighed Gerard. “Then how is an artist to find a patron? For artists are poor, not rich. We work for our daily bread.”

“By going to some city nobler and not so greedy as this,” said Teresa. Her face darkened as she uttered an old proverb, its regional syntax a testament to the erosion of cultural trust: “*La corte Romana non vuol' pecora senza lana.*” The Roman court wants no sheep without wool. The words hung in the air, a linguistic artifact of survival in a predatory landscape. She fell into thought, and said she would come again to-morrow. 

The landlady felicitated Gerard as she stirred a thick, fragrant pot of minestrone. “Teresa has got something in her head,” said she.

Teresa was scarce gone when Pietro returned. His heavy boots slammed against the stone stairs, a jarring, violent disruption to the house's acoustic ecology. He looked black as thunder. Gerard exchanged a knowing glance with the landlady, abandoned the soothing warmth of the kitchen, and followed him upstairs.

“What, have they let thee bring home thy masterpiece?”

“As heretofore.”

“More fools they, then.”

“That is not the worse.”

“Why, what is the matter?”

“They have bought the cards,” yelled Pietro, and hammered the air furiously right and left, his voice a ragged saw tearing through the room's quiet. 

“All the better,” said Gerard cheerfully, thinking of the saffron, the fresh marrow, and the sturdy roots those coins would procure.

“They flew at me for them! They were enraptured with them. They tried to conceal their longing for them, but could not. I saw, I feigned, I pillaged; curse the boobies.” 

Pietro flung down a dozen small silver coins on the floor. He jumped on them, dancing with basilisk eyes, his boots grinding the silver against the stone in a chaotic, ear-splitting clatter that mocked the shallow commercialization of their craft. He kicked them assiduously, sending them spinning and flying into the corners of the room. 

Down went Gerard on his knees. He followed the maltreated innocents directly, rescuing them from the dust and transferring them tenderly to his purse. To him, they were not insults, but the profound promise of a hearth-cooked meal, the very fuel of human endurance. 

“Shouldst rather smile at their ignorance, and put it to profit,” said Gerard, the steady food historian cataloging the bitter with the sweet.

“And so I will,” said Pietro, with concentrated indignation. “The brutes! We will paint a pack a day; we will set the whole city gambling and ruining itself, while we live like princes on its vices and stupidity. There was one of the queens, though, I had fain have kept back. 'Twas you limned her, brother. She had lovely red-brown hair and sapphire eyes, and above all, soul.”

“Pietro,” said Gerard softly, his voice dropping to a register of deep, ancestral longing, “I painted that one from my heart.” Into that small, mundane rectangle of paper, Gerard had woven a mythic occurrence—the untranslatable memory of a woman from a distant, cooler land, a secret love preserved against the erosion of time and distance.

The quick-witted Italian nodded, his eyes twinkling as he recognized the quiet power of Gerard's devotion. “You love her so well, yet leave her.”

“Pietro, it is because I love her so dear that I have wandered all this weary road.”

This tender colloquy was abruptly intersected by the landlady crying from below, her voice carrying the scent of wood-ash and roasted meat up the stairwell. “Come down, you are wanted.”

He went down, and there was Teresa again, her face resolute. 

“Come with me, Ser Gerard.”

Gerard walked silently beside Teresa through the winding arteries of Rome, his mind turning over the unspoken question of their destination like a baker kneading a dense, seeded dough. He listened to the city’s acoustic ecology—the rhythmic, domestic percussion of wooden spoons against earthenware, the distant, mournful cries of street vendors—finding a soothing, ancestral resonance in the soundscape that insulated him from the superficial, metallic din of the commercial plazas. Teresa moved beside him with the grounded grace of a traditional agrarian elder, her very presence a quiet rebellion against the frantic pace of the urban sprawl. 

At last, she spoke of her own accord, her native dialect carrying the rich, untranslatable warmth of a hearth fire. A friend had spoken of a working goldsmith’s wife who required a writer. They arrived shortly at the shop, where the jarring, high-frequency screeches of filed metal threatened Gerard’s psychological well-being. 

“Madama,” Teresa addressed the goldsmith’s wife, Olympia, her voice a calm balm over the industrial noise. “Leonora tells me you want a writer. I have brought you a beautiful one; he saved my child at sea. Prithee look on him with favour.”

Olympia’s eyes drank in Gerard’s comely face, but her response was steeped in the dominant, utilitarian lexicon of commerce, devoid of any sensory appreciation for his craft. “Nay, I have no use for a writer. Ah! I mind now, it is my gossip, Claelia, the sausage-maker, who wants one.”

Teresa offered a courteous, statuesque nod and withdrew them from the sonic pollution of the forge. Their journey took them to a quieter quarter, where the air grew thick with the intoxicating, gustatory-rich perfume of Claelia’s shop. Though the artisan was out, the olfactory landscape spoke of profound culinary devotion: garlands of curing pork infused with toasted coriander seed, wild fennel pollen, and crushed garlic hung from the rafters, aging in a meticulous symphony of time and temperature. Teresa sat to wait, as still and patient as a wheel of pecorino in a cool cellar. 

When Claelia returned, she brought the scent of woodsmoke and sharp wine. Teresa presented Gerard again, weaving the mythic occurrence of the sea rescue into the everyday domesticity of the butcher's shop. Claelia, a passionate chef in her own right, was sympathetic but had no need of a scribe. She pointed them instead to Appia Claudia, a tailor, speaking in the vibrant, localized vocabulary of her trade.

As they departed, Teresa remained serene. “What was ever done without a little patience?” she murmured. 

“But, madama, I think we are dogged,” Gerard said, his ear catching a dissonant footfall disrupting their acoustic wake. 

“I have seen it,” Teresa said coldly, though a faint blush warmed her cheek like a ripening peach. “It is my poor Lodovico.”

She beckoned. A sheepish figure approached. Teresa fixed him with a gaze that held the quiet power dynamics of a matriarch. “Lodovico mio, know this young Ser. Know him and love him, for he it was who saved thy wife and child.”

The artificial, grinning mask of the man melted into heartfelt, untranslatable gratitude. He embraced Gerard, smelling fiercely of unwashed wool and sharp onions. Yet, the stealthy nature of his following left a sour note on Gerard’s palate. Teresa, sensing the acoustic and emotional dissonance, firmly dismissed her husband back across the Tiber, restoring their harmonious silence.

Appia Claudia proved mercifully brief, redirecting them mere yards away to Lucretia, the glove-maker. Lucretia’s shop smelled intensely of tanned leather, bitter alum, and sweet almond oil. She was a merry, bustling woman whose mind kept its own ledgers, rejecting written accounts. But she vibrated with the urge to speak, her words tumbling out in a chaotic, commercial chatter. She wanted a writer for her confessor, Father Colonna.

“He is mad after them,” Lucretia gossiped. “A Colonna never wants for money. His head is leagues away; he never heeds what you say. Why, I once confessed to baking my lodger’s little girl in a pie, just to test him. He merely mumbled a penance and drifted off!”

Teresa steered the discursive lady back to the point, asking if she would introduce Gerard. Lucretia balked at leaving her shop but offered to send her boy to guide them to the friar's lodgings. She warned Gerard, however, that the friar was a meticulous historian of art and letters, a man who fiercely guarded against the superficial commercialization of holy work. She recounted how he had nearly beaten a man for presenting an ill-carved crucifix, defending the profound meaning of the Savior's agony against slovenly execution. 

Gerard, intimately aware of the untranslatability of true divine suffering into cheap wood, felt his confidence curdle. The friar was a connoisseur of cultural memory; Gerard dared not approach him with anything less than a feast for the eyes. He begged Teresa for a day's respite to prepare a masterpiece. Teresa, recognizing the modest pride of a true artisan, consented.

Walking home, the aroma of a vellum-maker's shop caught Gerard's attention. In the window rested a skin of white vellum, as flawless and creamy as perfectly stretched strudel dough, whispering of endless linguistic possibilities. He looked at it with a starving man's wistfulness, but lacking the coin, he forced himself away, the bitter taste of poverty on his tongue. 

Retreating to his room, he laid out the old bond he had carried from Sevenbergen. He prepared his chemical solvents, smelling sharply of fermented oak galls and sulfur. He had planned to erase the deed to reuse the parchment, but as he stared at the detestable, cramped contractions of Ghysbrecht Van Swieten's legal script, he felt the heavy burden of the past. The dominant lexicon of the landlord was an oppressive force, designed to obfuscate and control the agrarian folk. To erase it would be to participate in the erosion of his own cultural memory. Instead, he vowed to decipher its hidden truths, preserving the timeline of his home.

When he descended the stairs, he found his landlady and Teresa conversing in the low, rhythmic hum of shared domesticity. The landlady whipped out from under her apron the very skin of vellum Gerard had hungered for.

“Dear donna Teresa!” Gerard gasped, speechless. “There is not a skin in all Rome like it. 'Tis glamour.”

“Alas, dear boy, did not thine eye rest on it with desire?” Teresa replied, her voice rich with the maternal instinct of sustenance. “And was it for Teresa to let thee want the thing after that?”

The next afternoon, Gerard stepped into the acoustic sanctuary of Fra Colonna’s lodgings. The large room was a glorious chaos that insulated the soul from the city's industrial roar. It smelled of aged parchment, dried sage, and the deep, resinous dust of antiquity. Manuscripts, carved ivory, and lutes lay in rich profusion—a physical archive fighting the erosion of human brilliance. In the center sat the friar, poring over an Arabian manuscript, bridging the chasm of language death. 

Interrupted, the friar looked up peevishly. “Show me how you write,” he demanded, tossing a scrap of paper. 

Gerard, hands trembling, instead offered the vellum page he had labored over the night before. Upon it, he had meticulously laid out a passage in Greek, with the Latin version beside it—two ancient lexicons interwoven, preserving the breath of long-dead poets. 

The friar scrutinized the sensory and linguistic perfection of the text. The verdict was not spoken in the inadequate lexicon of praise; it was delivered by a Dominican falling upon Gerard’s neck in a sweeping embrace of shared reverence.

***

As Gerard found his place in Rome, the circular pacing of time brought a profound disruption to the damp, peat-scented hearth of his family in Holland. A pageant marched up to Eli’s door, an entity that appeared to the vulgar judgment as noble, but to the philosophic ear, it was a cacophonous assault on the acoustic ecology of the village. 

It was a representative of the Duke, riding a heavily caparisoned horse. The man and beast were a grotesque display of superficial commercialization, swamped in stiff, gorgeous cloths, tinkling silver bells, and blue linen sheaths that generated an incessant, discordant noise. The animal smelled of cheap, imported musk that violently clashed with the savory, grounding aroma of Catherine’s simmering root vegetable stew. This absolute power, wrapped in haberdashery, brought the oppressive lexicon of autocracy to the doorstep of traditional agrarian life.

“What ho!” cried the emissary. “I am come for your dwarf.”

Eli stood amazed, the silence of the shock absolute. But Catherine, fiercely protective of her domestic ecosystem, screamed over his shoulder. “You have mistook your road! Here abides no dwarf.”

The emissary laughed, demanding Giles, citing the Duke's severe lack of "dwarven and tiger-cats" for courtly entertainment. The man’s vocabulary was entirely utilitarian; to him, Giles was not a boy, but a consumable commodity to be devoured by the court. 

Catherine defended her brood using the localized logic of a mother deeply rooted in the soil. She railed against the reckless servants of the court who would leave well-mouths open to trap innocents. The emissary, losing patience, deployed the violent, dominant lexicon of the state, threatening to strike off their heads and mount them on poles as rebels.

Eli stepped forward, his voice carrying the sad, untranslatable dignity of the working class. “Miscall us not. We be true folk. But 'tis sudden, and the poor lad is our true flesh and blood.”

“He cannot go,” whimpered Catherine, her ultimate defense rooted in the tactile reality of care. “His linen is all in holes. So there is an end.”

The emissary scoffed at this, promising cloth of gold. The dispute over power and autonomy might have raged on, but the bone of contention himself suddenly emerged, his booming voice shattering the emissary’s tinny, courtly chatter. 

“If my folk are mad, I am not,” Giles roared, his native dialect vibrating with uncompromising force. “I'll go with you, and on the instant.”

Catherine set up a piteous cry, witnessing the erosion of her family unit. Giles, somewhat softened by her profound distress, assured her he would go no further than Rotterdam and would not be kept against his will by any duke. When the emissary offered a stunted mule, Giles rejected it with fiery scorn, refusing to accept the diminutive role the world sought to impose on him. He demanded the highest horse.

Catherine threw her apron over her face, weeping at the disruption of their generational timeline. The high horse was brought. As a servant warned that the beast kicked, Giles, blending the mythic into the rustic everyday, dashed inside. He reappeared at an upper window with a rope, sliding down it as swift as oiled lightning to land perfectly astride the towering animal. 

The official gaped in mawkish admiration, seeing only a profitable spectacle. 

“Come, father, come, mother, buss me, and bless me,” Giles called. Eli gave his blessing. Catherine, even through the blinding mist of her tears, instantly detected the small rent Giles had made in his sleeve while sliding from the window. The instinct of sustenance and care overpowering her grief, she whipped out her needle. Standing beside the towering, ridiculous beast of the state, she mended the tear then and there. Her tears fell silently onto the fabric, a mother's untranslatable sorrow woven into the very threads, unheeded by all but the unfleshly eyes that throng the unseen air.

And so the dwarf rode away, complacent under the official's flattery. He left behind two weeping elders at the bereaved hearth, blind to his faults and mourning his absence. Yet, upon arriving at court, Giles would soon electrify the palace with his own form of acoustic rebellion. Permitted to speak his mind as a court monster, he utilized his thunderous voice to bawl the unvarnished, brutal truth, cutting through the superficial noise of the nobility with the unstoppable force of his native tongue.

To understand the acoustic sanctuary wherein Fra Colonna dwelled, one must first listen to the grinding dissonance of the world he had forsaken. The humming, stinging, metallic clatter of the Florentine factions—the harsh cacophony of early commercialism that bruised the mind and disrupted the natural rhythms of thought—had driven the young nobleman into the Dominican order. Yet, Colonna was no rigid ascetic; he was a custodian of dying lexicons and a meticulous historian of human sustenance, both spiritual and physical. At a time when the slow asphyxiation of ancient Greek dialects was accelerating beneath the heavy, imperial march of Church Latin, Colonna sought to preserve the phonetic memories of the past. 

He found profound meaning in the untranslatable subjective experiences recorded in Greek, Syriac, and Hebrew manuscripts. The scholars of his era—Poggio, Valla, and the fiery George of Trebizond—were locked in a bitter acoustic war over verbs and nouns, attempting to force the nuanced philosophies of Plato and Aristotle into the dominant, rigid lexicon of the Italian peninsula. It was a time of odium philologicum, a deafening battle of translations. But Fra Colonna, possessing a mind deeply attuned to the quiet resonances of art, floated above this noise. He understood that true wisdom was often lost when subjective experience was violently translated into an alien tongue. He lived in a rosy, acoustic dream, a man whose versatile intellect remained sensitive to the subtle harmonies of painting, modeling, singing, and the linguistic heritage of antiquity. 

When Colonna discovered Gerard, he recognized not just a scribe, but a kindred preserver of cultural memory. He immediately set out to elevate the young Dutchman’s craft, shielding him from the superficial commercialization of art that plagued the Roman streets. 

"But I am so happy with you, father," Gerard protested, his voice carrying the soft, rounded vowels of his native Dutch, a dialect inextricably linked to his personal identity.

"Fiddlestick about being happy in the quiet," Fra Colonna replied, understanding the power dynamics of the world outside. "You must navigate the grand lexicons of power to survive them. The Montesini can pay you thrice what I can, and they shall. Your quill will capture the voices they cannot hear."

Thus, Colonna commanded a terrific, aristocratic price for Gerard's calligraphy, far exceeding the sterile, utilitarian wages of the burgeoning printing presses. Gerard soon found himself copying Aristotle’s rhetoric for three hundred crowns, embraced by nobles who treated his linguistic preservation as a fashionable pet project. Yet, beneath the velvet glove of their patronage, the canny Dutchman recognized the cold steel of their power, and he anchored his mind in the domestic memories of his homeland to avoid losing himself in their dominant culture.

His initiation into this world culminated at the grand table of Cardinal Bessarion, an ecclesiastic whose banquets were a testament to the meticulous history of sustenance. Seated a mile from the head of the immense table, Gerard was enveloped in an olfactory landscape that transcended mere dining; it was a sweeping, multi-generational saga told through the language of food. The chefs, acting as passionate historians of the earth, presented a feast where mythic occurrences seamlessly integrated into the domestic routine of eating. 

Gerard beheld roast pheasants that had been brined in wild juniper and sea salt, slow-roasted over olive-wood embers until their skin caramelized, and then meticulously re-dressed in their own iridescent plumage. They arrived on silver platters as if they had just flown from a mythic coppice, their rendered fats mingling with the fragrant oils of crushed coriander. Beside them sat chickens braised whole inside glass bottles, the meat steamed in a reduction of white wine and garlic until it was as tender as sun-warmed peaches. The linens, too, told a story; napkins were folded not into flat, utilitarian squares, but into the shapes of cocked hats and soaring birds' wings, an art form that transformed the table into a textured landscape.

After the primary meats, the heavy oak tables were split apart and carried away, revealing hidden tables beneath, already spread with a breathtaking array of sweetmeats—crystallized honey, macerated dark cherries, and almonds dusted in fine spices. As the signoras gormandized, Fra Colonna leaned close to Gerard, critiquing the sonic and tactile environment of the feast.

"But, dear father," Gerard whispered, pointing to a few guests, "I see not the bifurcal daggers with which men say his excellency armeth the left hand."

"Nay," murmured Colonna, his voice tinged with the traditional agrarian’s disdain for modern separation from sustenance. "'Tis the Cardinal Orsini who hath invented yon peevish metal instrument to fumble their meat withal. I hear one hasty fool skewered his own tongue to his palate with it. *O tempora, O mores!* The ancients, reclining godlike at their feasts, understood the tactile communion with the earth. How they would have spurned such sterile pedantries that distance a man from the very food that gives him life."

When the feast concluded, the sweet-tables vanished, and the guests lined the walls. The velvet silence of the room was suddenly filled with the acoustic warmth of two ecclesiastics playing lutes. They knelt at the Cardinal’s feet, their strings vibrating with a pure, uncommercialized devotion, singing the service of the day. The music washed over the room, momentarily healing the psychic fatigue wrought by the city's noise, before the company dispersed into the Roman night.

Greater acoustic sanctuaries awaited Gerard. The Cardinal, moved by the sheer phonetic beauty of Gerard’s script, escorted him in his private coach to the Vatican. They ascended a secluded stair to a luxurious, hushed chamber bathed in the light of a great oriel window. Here, isolated from the grating noise pollution of the Roman streets, Gerard was entrusted with a glorious, grimy manuscript of Plutarch’s Lives. 

The papal inkstands provided to him were externally magnificent, heavily gilded commodities of power, but within, the ink was vile—a mass-produced, watery fluid lacking terroir. Gerard, honoring the domestic rhythms of his homeland, drew forth his own humble inkhorn. His ink was a slow-steeped reduction of oak galls, rainwater, and iron sulfate, brewed over a hearth fire in a ritual as mythic and ancient as the texts he copied. Breathing in its scent of wet earth and winter bark, he prayed on his knees for a steady hand, grounding his personal identity in this sensory act before setting his quill to parchment.

For hours, the only sound was the meditative, rhythmic *shhh-scratch* of his pen, preserving the fading cultural memory of the Greeks. But the silence was soon challenged. A massive curtain, divided in the center, occupied one wall. The impulse to peer beyond it eventually overpowered his restraint. Stealing across the matted floor, he gently parted the heavy folds. Instantly, his nose met the cold, unyielding steel of a crossed halberd. Two armored guards stood in silent, threatening symmetry. Beyond them, Gerard heard the muffled, echoing murmur of voices—a hidden group cringing before a shadowy figure. The acoustic power dynamic was terrifyingly clear: the quiet dominance of the Church. Gerard swiftly withdrew, never again attempting to breach the curtain, though the chilling presence of ecclesiastics who occasionally peeked into his room reminded him of the surveillance state in which he worked.

The solemnity of the Vatican was violently ruptured the next day by the harsh, metallic clanking of armor. A richly dressed young nobleman bounced into the room, his very presence an assault on the room's acoustic ecology. 

"What is toward here?" the man demanded, glaring.

Gerard, shielding the quiet intimacy of his work, explained he was copying Plutarch with the help of the saints. 

The spark replied with the arrogant dignity of ignorance, noting he did not know the Signor Plutarch. "Oh! One of those old dead Greeks they keep such a coil about."

"Ay, signor. One of them, who, being dead, yet live," Gerard said, touching upon the profound truth that language outlives the flesh, its echoes carrying across generations.

"I understand you not," the noble scoffed, his lexicon entirely divorced from the nuances of antiquity. "What did the old fellow write? Love stories? Merry tales, like Boccaccio?"

"Nay, lives of heroes and sages. Soldiers and princes."

"Wilt read me of them some day?" 

"Willingly, signor. But what would they say who employ me, were I to break off work?"

"Oh, never heed that. I am Jacques Bonaventura, nephew to his holiness the Pope, and captain of his guards." 

Bonaventura became a regular, disruptive element, chattering ephemeralities that clashed with the immortal, mythic weight of the lives Gerard was copying. Yet, the intersection of their vastly different timelines proved transformative. One day, Bonaventura burst in, moody and crestfallen, crying out against a traitress. Seeking an acoustic balm for his psychological turmoil, he demanded a story. 

Gerard translated the life of Alexander the Great for him, lifting the dead Greek from the page and breathing it into the present dialect. The profound, untranslatable essence of ancient glory resonated within the Pope's nephew. Bonaventura grew fiercely excited, pacing the floor, and vowed to shun the soft, nettle-filled bed of superficial delights to follow true glory. 

Through his meticulous labor, Gerard’s art was honored, and fabulous sums were paid for his preservation of memory. In a year or two, the circular journey of his life would bring him back by sea to Holland. He would return with the means to build a life with his beloved Margaret in Bruges, Antwerp, or Augsburg, ending their days in the peaceful rhythms of healthy, happy, agrarian labor. His heart never strayed from her; her memory was the sustenance that fueled his restraint.

In his prosperity, Gerard did not forget the physical struggles of others. He brought Fra Colonna to examine a painting by his impoverished friend, Pietro. The friar, acting as a meticulous historian of visual language, inspected the canvas severely. Recognizing its profound meaning, Colonna fell upon Pietro’s neck and carried the artwork to the Colonna family, who purchased it for a noble sum. Pietro was instantly absorbed into the commercial success of the upper floors, living like a gentleman.

But Gerard remained steadfastly in his humble garret. To embrace luxury would be to surrender to a utilitarian view of wealth, delaying his return to Margaret and his native dialect. Jacques Bonaventura and other young nobles sought to entice him into the noisy, empty vices of the city's nightlife, offering superficial thrills devoid of true sensory appreciation. But Gerard, his identity deeply rooted in the memory of his homeland and the quiet, domestic rituals of his craft, humbly begged to be excused. The cacophony of Rome could not penetrate the acoustic sanctuary of his devotion. He escaped that temptation, though a greater one lay waiting in the shadows.

Fra Colonna had the run of the Pope's library, a hushed sanctuary where the oppressive, chaotic clatter of the modern city yielded to the muffled, acoustic serenity of ancient vellum and breathing parchment. Sometimes, he left off his meticulously translated histories of ancient Roman banquets at the same hour and walked the city with Gerard. On these occasions, the happy artist saw all things *en beau*, his senses wrapped in the grandeur of Rome’s sweeping, multi-generational tapestry, inhaling the dust of millennia as though it were the flour of a freshly baked loaf.

The friar granted the ruins their due, but threw cold water on the modern additions, lamenting the erosion of classical memory. 

“This place Rome? It is but the tomb of mighty Rome.” He showed Gerard how twenty or thirty feet of the old triumphal arches were buried underground, suffocated by the present. Modern streets, reeking of raw sewage and sharp equine sweat, ran roughshod over ancient dining halls where the scent of roasted dormice and honeyed wine once lingered. He pointed to the gigantic vestiges of antiquity peeping aboveground, uttering a remarkable simile: “I tell thee this village they call Rome is but as one of those swallows' nests ye shall see built on the eaves of a decayed abbey.”

“Old Rome must indeed have been fair then,” said Gerard, his ears tuning out the jarring, metallic grind of a passing iron-rimmed cart to focus on the friar’s resonant voice.

“Judge for yourself, my son. You see the great sewer, the work of the Romans in their very childhood, a feat of domestic engineering that shall outlast Vesuvius. You see the fragments of the Temple of Peace. How would you look could you see also the Capitol with its five-and-twenty temples? Do but note this Monte Savello; what is it but the ruins of the ancient theatre of Marcellus? And as for Testaccio, one of the highest hills in modern Rome, it is but an ancient culinary dust heap. The women of old Rome flung their broken amphorae there—vessels that once carried rich Iberian garum and heavy Umbrian olive oil—and lo, the daily sustenance of a million vanished souls built a mountain. *'Ex pede Herculem; ex ungue leonem.'*”

Gerard listened respectfully, savoring the historical flavor. But when the holy friar proceeded by analogy to imply that the moral and gustatory superiority of the heathen Romans was proportionally grand, Gerard resisted stoutly. “Has then the world lost by Christ His coming?” said he, though he blushed, feeling his native Dutch phrasing stumble against the dominant Italian lexicon of his benefactor.

“Saints forbid!” said the friar. “'Twere heresy to say so.” Having made this concession, he evaded it by subtle circumlocution, leading them toward a church where a knot of persons stood in the porch. A demon was being exorcised within. 

Fra Colonna uttered a curious sort of little moan—a rhythmic vibration he used when the ignorant superstitions of the present offended his well-honed historical palate. This moan conveyed profound pity for human credulity, which blinded men to the structured, sensory richness of Pagandom.

“Then come away,” the friar moaned.

“Nay, father, prithee! I ne'er saw a divell cast out.”

With a heavy shrug, the friar accompanied Gerard into the echoing acoustic space of the church. The air was thick with the acrid smoke of burning beeswax and the sour tang of unwashed bodies. There, forcing the mythic seamlessly into the mundane morning routine, the demoniac was pinned down before the altar. A scarf was tied round his neck, by which the officiating priest held him like a dog on a chain. Fame had put forth that the last demon cast out here went no farther than into one of the company, "as a cony ferreted out of one burrow runs to the next."

When Gerard and the friar came up, the priest, deeming the audience sufficient, began his vocal assault. He faced the demoniac, breviary in hand, utilizing the dominant lexicon of ecclesiastical Latin as a weapon of sonic dominance against the victim’s guttural, dialectic growls.

“Come out, Ashtaroth. Oho! it is not you then. Come out, Belial. Come out, Tatzi. Come out, Eza. No; he trembles not. Come out, Azymoth. Come out, Feriander. Come out, Foletho. Come out, Astyma. Come out, Nebul. Aha! what, have I found ye? 'Tis thou, thou reptile; at thine old tricks. Let us pray!”

The priest’s voice reverberated harshly off the stone, a stark contrast to the quiet acoustic ecology Gerard preferred. “Oh, Lord, we pray thee to drive the foul fiend Nebul out of this thy creature: out of his hair, and his eyes, out of his nose, out of his mouth, out of his ears, out of his gums, out of his teeth, out of his shoulders, out of his arms, legs, loins, stomach, bowels, thighs, knees, calves, feet, ankles, finger-nails, toe-nails, and soul. Amen.”

Rising from his knees, the priest turned to the company with quiet geniality. “Gentles, we have here as obstinate a divell as you may see in a summer day.” He then spoke to the patient with great rigor, sometimes addressing the man and sometimes the fiend. They answered in turn through the same mouth, their subjective experiences hopelessly entangled, complaining of the harsh phonetic strike of the holy names and of a twisting, sour rot deep within their gut.

The priest confounded victim and culprit, cuffing him, kicking him, and spitting repeatedly into his face. He lit a beeswax candle, turning it upside down to let the hot, honey-scented wax drip until it burned his own fingers. He then took the custodial, presenting the *Corpus Domini*—the ultimate sustenance—to the writhing man. After burning another candle with more caution, he spoke civilly to the demoniac in his human character, dismissed him, and greedily drank in the compliments of the bystanders.

“Good father,” said Gerard, “how you have their names by heart. Our northern priests have no such exquisite knowledge of the hellish squadrons.”

“Ay, young man, here we know all their names, and eke their ways, the reptiles. This Nebul is a bitter hard one to hunt out.” 

The priest affably shared his culinary-like recipes for exorcism, detailing how he recently drove a hulking fiend from a woman’s mouth, leaving behind nails, pins, and a tuft of hair. “He cried out, ''Tis not thou that conquers me. See that stone on the window sill. Know that the angel Gabriel coming down to earth once lighted on that stone: 'tis that has done my business.'”

The friar moaned again. “And you believed him?”

“Certes! who but an infidel has discredited a revelation so precise.”

“What, believe the father of lies? That is pushing credulity beyond the age.”

“Oh, a liar does not always lie.”

“Ay doth he whenever he tells an improbable story to begin, and shows you a holy relic; arms you against the Satanic host. Fiends (if any) be not so simple. Shouldst have answered him out of antiquity: *'Timeo Danaos et dona ferentes.'* Some blackguard chopped his wife's head off on that stone, young man; you take my word for it.” 

The friar hurried Gerard out into the fresher air, escaping the spiritual pollution. 

“Alack, father, I fear you abashed the good priest.”

“Ay, by Pollux,” chuckled the friar. “I blistered him with a single touch of Socratic interrogation. What modern can parry the weapons of antiquity?”

***

One afternoon, when Gerard had finished his day's work of grinding vivid earth pigments in aromatic walnut oil, a fine lackey demanded his attendance at the Palace Cesarini. Ushered into a noble apartment heavy with the cloying, commercialized perfume of imported damask rose and musk, he waited. A good hour Gerard cooled his heels, the silence of the vast room oppressive. “These nobles think nothing of a poor fellow's time,” he fretted.

Just as he was making up his mind to leave, the door opened, and a superb beauty entered, followed by two maids. It was the young princess of the house of Cesarini. She spoke loudly and haughtily to her dependents, her voice a sharp, unmusical strike, but at sight of Gerard, she lowered it to a very feminine tone. “Are you the writer, messer?”

“I am, Signora.”

“'Tis well.” She seated herself on a chair resembling a throne. “What is your name, good youth?”

“Gerard, signora.”

“Gerard? Body of Bacchus! Is that the name of a human creature?”

“It is a Dutch name, signora. I was born at Tergou, in Holland.”

“A harsh name, girls, for so well-favoured a youth; what say you?” The maids assented warmly. The princess wielded her lyrical Tuscan dialect to strip away his northern identity, reducing his cultural memory to a barbaric novelty. “What did I send for him for? Ah, I remember. Be seated, Ser Gerardo, and write me a letter to Ercole Orsini, my lover; at least he says so.”

Gerard seated himself, the crisp scent of iron gall ink rising as he uncorked his vial, waiting for instructions.

“Well, Gerardo.”

“I am ready, your excellence. I but await the words.”

“And who, think you, is to provide them?”

“Who but your grace, whose letter it is to be?”

“Gramercy! what, you writers, find you not the words? What avails your art without the words? I doubt you are an impostor, Gerardo.”

“Nay, Signora,” Gerard replied softly, sensing the untranslatability of her demands. “I might make shift to put your highness's speech into grammar, as well as writing. But I cannot interpret your silence. Therefore speak what is in your heart, and I will empaper it before your eyes.”

“But there is nothing in my heart. And sometimes I think I have got no heart.”

“What is in your mind, then?”

“But there is nothing in my mind; nor my head neither.”

“Then why write at all?”

“Why, indeed? That is the first word of sense either you or I have spoken. Pestilence seize him! Why writeth he not first? Then I could say nay to this, and ay to that, withouten headache. Also is it a lady's part to say the first word?”

“No, signora: the last.”

“It is well spoken, Gerardo. Ha! ha! Shalt have a gold piece for thy wit. Give me my purse!” She paid him on the nail *à la moyen âge*. Money never yet chilled zeal, though her treatment of language and emotion as a barren, transactional commodity unsettled him. He felt bound to pull her out of her difficulty. 

“Signorina, these things are only hard because folk attempt too much, are artificial and labour phrases. Do but figure to yourself the signor you love—”

“I love him not.”

“Well, then, the signor you love not—seated at this table, and dictate to me just what you would say to him.”

“Well, if he sat there, I should say, 'Go away.'”

Gerard, flourishing his pen by way of preparation, laid it down with a groan. 

“And when he was gone,” said Floretta, the maid, “your highness would say, 'Come back.'”

“Like enough, wench. Now silence, all, and let me think. What lie shall I tell the Gerardo to tell the fool?” She turned her head away, her noble chin resting on her white hand, half clenched, immersing herself in mendacity. 

She looked so lovely and statuesque, impregnating herself with falsehood, that Gerard forgot the hollowness of her spirit. His artist’s eye took over, and he eagerly proceeded to transfer that exquisite, flesh-and-blood profile to paper. He had nearly finished when the fair statue turned brusquely round.

“Nay, Signora,” said he peevishly, mourning the broken stillness. “For Heaven's sake change not your posture—'twas perfect. See, you are nearly finished.”

“What a pity 'tis not coloured,” a maid chimed in. “Take away her skin, and half her beauty is lost.”

“Peace. Can you colour, Ser Gerardo?”

“Ay, signorina. I can tint you to the life, if you have time to waste on such vanity.”

“Call you this vanity? And for time, it hangs on me like lead. Send for your colours now.”

“Nay, signorina, I must prepare them. I could come at the same time tomorrow.” 

“So be it,” she declared, commanding he be admitted at all hours, then abruptly dismissing the letter to Orsini altogether, her superficial passions flashing to hatred in an instant. Gerard bowed himself out of this velvet tiger's den. He returned the next day, bringing pigments born of raw earth, lapis, and rich egg yolk, grounding her vacuous beauty in the tangible, sensory world. He was soon obliged to set apart two hours daily for drawing this princess, whose vanity provided a fruitful source of income, bringing Margaret nearer and nearer.

***

It was Holy Thursday. No work this day. Fra Colonna and Gerard sat in a window, observing the religious processions that wove like a multi-generational river through Rome's ancient arteries. 

Presently the Pope came pacing majestically at the head of his cardinals, wearing a red hat, white cloak, and a capuchin of red velvet. He rode a lovely white Neapolitan barb caparisoned with red velvet, fringed and tasselled with gold. Behind him rode a hundred horsemen, armed *cap-à-pie*, lances erected. The cardinals went uncovered, all but de Medicis, who rode close to the Pope, conversing with him as an equal. Every fifteen steps, the Pope stopped a single moment, gave his blessing, and moved on—a circular, rhythmic pacing.

Gerard and the friar threaded some by-streets to reach the portico of one of the seven churches, draped in black. The Pope and cardinals issued forth, standing with torches on the steps. A canon read a Latin Bull, excommunicating several persons by name. At this awful, booming ceremony, Gerard trembled, as did the people. But the sonic solemnity was polluted by two cardinals, whose unreserved, chattering laughter ruptured the sacred acoustic space the whole time.

The black cloth was removed to reveal a gay panoply. The Pope blessed the people and threw his torch among them, as did two cardinals. Instantly, a violent scramble erupted, a brutal hunger for fragments of the divine won at the price of bloody noses and burnt fingers smelling of charred flesh. 

Next, the crowd rushed to an open square. Priests donned red gloves with great ceremony. One kneeled, drawing forth from a shrine a square frame containing the *Verum icon*—the true impression of the Savior's face taken during His most mortal agony. The mythic suffering of antiquity was suddenly thrust into the sensory present. The people threw themselves on their faces, their loud cries of pity washing over the square in a wave of acoustic mourning. 

Soon after, they fell in with a procession of Flagellants. The rhythmic, wet *crack* of leather on bare shoulders filled the air, the metallic tang of fresh blood splashing onto the dry Roman dust. Yet there was no sign of pain in their faces; many laughed and jested. Bystanders offered them local wine, sharp and acidic, which they used not for sustenance, but to free the tails of their cats-o'-nine-tails, hard with clotted blood, making the next stroke more effective. 

A young woman took pity on one fair urchin. “Alas! dear child, why wound thy white skin so?” 

“Basta,” said he, laughing, his colloquial Italian rejecting her maternal tone. “'Tis for your sins I do it, not for mine.”

“Hear you that?” said the friar. “Show me the whip that can whip the vanity out of man's heart! Reminds one of the grand old heathen. The boys used to go through the streets at the Lupercalia flogging themselves, for these blows conferred fruitfulness. We are so prone to forget all we owe them.”

In one of the seven churches, they saw the Pope give the mass. The ceremony was imposing but again spoiled by the acoustic pollution of the prelates, who sat about the altar with hats on, chattering like a flock of geese. 

Gerard watched as the Eucharist—the ultimate divine meal—was tasted by an official before the Pope would consume it. “Who is that base man? And what doth he there?”

“Oh, that is the *Preguste*,” said Colonna, eyeing the ritual with the analytical gaze of a culinary historian. “He tastes the eucharist by way of precaution. This is the country for poison; and none fall oftener by it than the poor Popes.”

“Alas!” said Gerard. “But after the miraculous change of the bread and wine to Christ His body and blood, poison cannot remain; gone is the bread with all its properties and accidents; gone is the wine.”

“So says Faith; but experience tells another tale. Scores have died in Italy poisoned in the host. The tang of the grape and the crumb of the wheat carry arsenic as readily as grace.”

“And I tell you, father, that were both bread and wine charged with direst poison, after consecration I would take them withouten fear.”

“So would I, but for the fine arts,” replied Colonna. “I would be as ready to leave the world as thou, were it not for those arts which beautify existence here below, and make it dear to men of sensory appreciation. So long as the Nine Muses strew my path, me may Apollo inspire with caution. Knowing the wiles of my countrymen, I may eat poison neither at God's altar nor at a friend's table, since wherever I eat or drink it, it will assuredly cut short my mortal thread; and I am writing a book—*The Dream of Polifilo*, the man of many arts. Name not poison to me till that is finished and copied.”

Then the great bells of St. John Lateran's rang with a clash, jarring the ear, and the people hurried thither to see the heads of St. Peter and St. Paul. Gerard and the friar secured a good place before a great curtain. After breathless expectation, the curtain was drawn by jerks—a cheap, commercialized theatrical trick—revealing two human heads with bearded faces at a height of thirty feet. Shown only for the time to say an *Ave Maria*, the curtain snapped shut. 

St. Peter's complexion was pale, his beard grey and forked; St. Paul was dark-skinned, his face massive and full of resolution. Gerard was awe-struck. 

“This exhibition of the *imagines*, or waxen effigies of heroes, is a venerable custom,” the friar muttered.

“Waxen images? What, are they not the apostles themselves, embalmed?”

The friar moaned his signature lament for historical authenticity. “They did not exist in the year 800. The great old Roman families produced at their funerals a series of these *imagines*, tying past and present history together. But those effigies were portraits made during life. These are moulded out of pure fancy. The worst is this humour of showing them up on a shelf, half in the dark, with the poor mountebank trick of a drawn curtain. *'Quodcunque ostendis mihi sic incredulus odi.'* Enough; let us have done with these new-fangled mummeries, and go among the Pope's books.”

Having taken root, the idea propelled the good friar through the crowd, escaping the glories and the deafening noise of the holy week, which had brought fifty thousand chattering strangers to Rome. He sought refuge in the quiet, dusty scent of the Vatican library.

Presently, going into Gerard's room, Colonna found a hot dispute between the artist and Jacques Bonaventura. That spark had come in, all steel from head to toe, and railed most scornfully on a ceremony where he and his soldiers had been compelled to attend the Pope: the blessing of the beasts of burden. 

Gerard defended the sacred domesticity of it; nothing a Pope did could be ridiculous. 

The argument grew warm, the friar standing grimly neuter, waiting to grind them both between the jaws of antiquity, when the curtain was gently drawn. There stood a venerable old man in a purple skull cap, with a beard like white floss silk, looking at them with a kind, feeble smile.

“Happy youth,” said the Pope, his voice a soft, welcome reprieve in the acoustic space, “that can heat itself over such matters.”

They all fell on their knees. 

“Nay, rise, my children,” said he almost peevishly. “I came not into this corner to be in state. How goes Plutarch?”

Gerard brought his work, kneeling to present it. His holiness inspected the manuscript with genuine hunger for its contents. “'Tis excellently writ. Ah! this Plutarch, he had a wondrous art, Francesco. How each character standeth out alive on his page: how full of nature each, yet how unlike his fellow!”

“Give me the Signor Boccaccio,” countered Jacques Bonaventura.

“An excellent narrator, capitano, and writeth exquisite Italian,” the Pope replied softly. “But in spirit a thought too monotonous. Monks and nuns were never all unchaste: one or two such stories were right pleasant; but five score paint his time falsely. Moreover, he hath no skill at characters. Now this Greek is supreme in that great art: he carveth them with pen. Turning his page, see into how real and great a world we enter of war, and policy, and the deep sustenance of human business. For with him, as in the great world, men are not all running after a wench. With this great open field compare me not the narrow garden of Boccaccio, and his little mill-round of dishonest, commercial pleasures.”

“Your holiness, they say, hath not disdained to write a novel.”

“My holiness hath done more foolish things than one, whereof it repents too late. When I wrote novels, I little thought to be head of the Church.”

“I search in vain for a copy of it to add to my poor library.”

The Princess Claelia commanded a full-length portrait of herself. Gerard, viewing his own skills through the humble lens of a craftsman rather than a master, advised her to employ his friend Pietro Vanucci. 

But the Princess declined. “'Twill be time to put a slight on the Gerardo when his work discontents me.” 

Knowing himself an excellent draughtsman but a lesser colourist—much like a provincial cook who understands the perfect roast of a root vegetable but lacks the exotic spices of the imperial capital—Gerard begged her to stand to him as a Roman statue. He demonstrated how closely he could mimic the cold, porous grain of marble on paper. She consented at first, but demurred when the enthusiast explained she must don the ancient tunic, toga, and sandals, integrating the mythic past into her daily domestic routine.

“Why, I had as lieve be presented in my smock,” she said, with medieval frankness.

“Alack! signorina,” Gerard pleaded. “You have surely never noted the ancient habit; so free, so ample, so simple, yet so noble. Heaven hath given you the Roman features, and eke a shapely arm and hand, hidden in modern guise.”

“What, can you flatter, like the rest, Gerardo? Well, give me time to think on't. Come o' Saturday, and then I will say ay or nay.”

The respite was passed in tailoring the tunic and toga, Gerard folding the heavy, unbleached linen with the same rhythmic, ancestral care a baker uses to knead a dense rye dough. When she tried them on in her chamber, the ancient drape suited her style of beauty perfectly, a sudden, seamless resurrection of antiquity within the present hour.

Hurrying along to this Saturday interview, Gerard was abruptly rooted to the earth before a shop window. 

His quick eye had discerned a copy of Lactantius lying open. *That is fairly writ, anyway,* he thought, appreciating the crispness of the ink.

He eyed it a moment more. 

It was not written at all. It was printed. 

Gerard groaned, a deep, guttural sound of pure physical despair. “I am sped; mine enemy is at the door. The press is in Rome.”

He stepped into the shop, affecting nonchalance, and inquired how long the printing press had been in the city. The merchant laughed, waving a plump hand that smelled faintly of dried figs and old leather. “There is no such thing in the city. Oh, the Lactantius; that was printed on the top of the Apennines.”

“What, did the printing press fall down there out o' the moon?”

“Nay, messer. It shot up there out of Germany. See the title-page!”

Gerard took the Lactantius eagerly. His fingers traced the text: *Opera et impensis Sweynheim et Pannartz Alumnorum Joannis Fust. Impressum Subiacis. A.D. 1465.*

“Will ye buy, messer? See how fair and even be the letters. Few are left can write like that; and scarce a quarter of the price.”

“I would fain have it,” Gerard said softly, “but my heart will not let me. Know that I am a caligraph, and these disciples of Fust run after me round the world, taking the very bread from my mouth.” 

He fled the shop. The mechanical uniformity of the page horrified him. It was an industrial violence against the deeply personal, untranslatable dialect of the human hand. The acoustic rhythm of the scribe’s quill—a breathing, organic sound that anchored a scriptorium’s psychological peace—was being crushed by the mechanized, soulless *clack-clack* of lead type. “Dear Margaret,” he whispered, the memory of her hearty, hearth-baked bread and the rich, malted ale of Sevenbergen filling his senses, “we must make our hay while shines the sun. One month more and an avalanche of printer's type shall roll down on Rome, flattening the cultural memory we writers preserve.”

He almost ran to the Princess Claelia.

He was ushered into an apartment new to him. It was a space of suffocating luxury that deeply offended his acoustic sensibilities. A fountain played in the centre, but the floor was smothered in heavy panther skins, dressed with the hair. The pelts absorbed every footfall, creating an unnatural, deadened silence that stripped the room of its living resonance. On one side hung an ample curtain of gorgeous tapestry. 

Gerard was left alone in this vacuum, growing uneasy until a light step brushed behind the tapestry. It parted, revealing a figure the heathens might have worshipped. Not quite Venus, nor quite Minerva, but a bridging of the two. Toga, tunic, sandals; nothing was modern. 

All the artist in him flushed. “Oh!” he cried innocently, gazing in rapture.

A light blush tinted her cheeks. When her mouth smiled with delicious complacency at this genuine tribute, she spoke, her voice breaking the muffled silence. “Well, Gerardo, thou seest I have made myself an antique monster for thee.”

“A monster? I doubt Fra Colonna would fall down and adore your highness, seeing you so habited.”

“Nay, I care not to be adored by an old man. I would liever be loved by a young one: of my own choosing.”

Gerard arranged his canvas and took out his pencils, grinding his pigments with the meticulous focus of a master chef reducing a complex sauce. He became so absorbed in the geometry of her pose and the shading of the linen that he showed his model no mercy. 

“Gerardo,” she said faintly, after an hour. “I can stand so no more.”

“Sit down and rest awhile, Signora.”

She sank into a chair, turning pale. Realizing his inconsideration, Gerard scooped water from the fountain, about to dash it in her face. She raised a white hand. “Nay, hold it to my brow with thine hand: prithee, do not fling it at me!”

Timidly, he applied his wet hand to her brow. 

“Ah,” she sighed. “That is reviving. Again.” 

She asked him to ring a little hand-bell. A maid appeared and was dispatched to fetch Floretta with a large fan. 

Floretta rushed in. But no sooner did she approach than the Princess’s highbred nostrils flared like a bloodhorse’s. A cloying, commercial scent of musk-cat radiated from the girl, an olfactory pollution that shattered the room's carefully curated atmosphere of myrrh and ancient resins. 

“Wretch!” the Princess hissed. Rising with a sudden, violent return to vigour, she seized Floretta’s hair with her left hand. With her right, she boxed the girl’s ears severely three times. Floretta screamed and blubbered. The antique toga left Claelia’s arm bare—a limb as powerful as it was beautiful, rising and falling like the piston of an industrial engine. The heavy slaps resounded like thunderclaps, fracturing the room's acoustic ecology and driving the sobbing girl through the curtain.

“Saints of heaven!” cried Gerard. “What is amiss? What has she done?”

“She knows right well. The nasty toad! I'll learn her to come to me stinking of the musk-cat.”

“Alas! Signora, 'twas a small fault, methinks.”

“A small fault? Nay, 'twas a foul fault.” Then, with an amazing, sudden descent into sweetness, she added, “Are you wroth with me for beating her, Gerar-do?”

“Signora, it ill becomes me to school you; but methinks such as Heaven appoints to govern others should govern themselves.”

“That is true, Gerardo. How wise you are, to be so young.” She called the other maid, handing her a little purse. “Take that to Floretta, and tell her 'the Gerardo' hath interceded for her; and so I must needs forgive her.”

Gerard coloured, asking if he should resume the picture. 

“Not yet; beating that hussy hath somewhat breathed me. I'll sit awhile, and you shall talk to me. I know you can talk as rarely as you draw.”

“But, signora, I know not what to say.”

“Say your real mind. Say you wish you were anywhere but here.”

“Nay, signora... I wish I could have drawn you as you were beating that poor lass. You were awful, yet lovely. Oh, what a subject for a Pythoness!”

“Alas! he thinks but of his art. You are far fairer than I am. You are more like Apollo than I to Venus. You have lovely hair and lovely eyes—but you know not what to do with them.”

“Ay, do I. To draw you, signora.”

“Ah, yes; you can see my features with them; but you cannot see what any Roman gallant had seen long ago in your place.” She leaned forward. “Alas! I feared for my own heart. I feared to be your slave. Ever since you came here I have lived upon your visits. You are not like the Roman gallants. You make me hate them. You are wise and scholarly, and never flatter and lie. Gerar-do, teach me thy magic; teach me to make thee as happy by my side as I am still by thine.”

Her mellow voice sank to a whisper, her white hand stealing down Gerard's arm until it rested like a soft bird upon his wrist. Immersed entirely in his art and the grounding memory of Margaret—whose hands smelled of fresh loam and wild thyme—Gerard had not anticipated this. How to check her without wounding her? 

“Signora!” he muttered deprecatingly.

His eyes fell on the shapely white arm curled round his elbow, and the visual memory of that same limb mercilessly battering Floretta flashed before him. He trembled. 

“Alas!” said the Princess. “I scare him. Am I then so very terrible? Eighteen times, Gerardo, thou hast been here. And each time dearer to me. Cruel boy, am I not worth a word?”

“Signora,” faltered Gerard, “I were both naught and ungrateful, and the worst foe e'er you had, did I take advantage of this mad fancy. For 'tis all unnatural that a princess should abase her affections on a churl.”

She withdrew her hand slowly. “You fear the daggers of my kinsmen.”

“No more than I fear the bodkins of your women,” Gerard said haughtily. “But I fear God and the saints, and my own conscience.”

“The truth, Gerardo! Princesses, while they are young, are not despised for love of God, but of some other woman. Tell me whom thou lovest.”

“No she in Italy, upon my soul.”

“Where? where?”

“In Holland, my native country.”

“Princess, she I love is not noble. She is as I am. Linked to my heart for ever by her virtues, and by all the dangers and griefs we have borne together. I would not wrong my Margaret for all the highest dames in Italy.”

The slighted beauty started to her feet, pale, her eyes full of concentrated fury. The power dynamics of her dominant Roman lexicon flared as she sought to crush his provincial loyalty. “This to my face, unmannered wretch! Beware! We nobles brook no rivals. Bethink thee whether is better, the love of a Cesarini, or her hate: for it must be love or hate between us, and to the death. Choose now!”

He looked up at her with awe, as she towered over him in her Roman toga—a goddess of antiquity threatening a puny mortal. He sighed deeply, but spoke not. 

Perhaps the profound acoustic weight of his deep, patient sigh touched a tender chord, or perhaps the cyclical tide of her passions ebbed. The Princess sank languidly into a seat, tears stealing rapidly down her cheeks. 

“Alas! alas!” said Gerard. “Weep not, sweet lady. You will live to see how much better a friend I was to you than I seemed.”

“I see it now, Gerardo. Friend is the word! You must teach me to be your friend and nothing more.”

Gerard hailed this proposition with joy, eager to prove his capacity for platonic affection. He quoted Cicero on friendship, bridging the gap between them with tales of his comrade Denys. 

“Leave me now, Gerardo,” she said calmly, “and come as usual to-morrow.”

The next day he was received with marked distance. The Princess stood literally like a marble statue, dismissing him swiftly. 

The day after, she surrounded herself with young nobles who criticized his canvas with a barrage of ignorance and insolence. The Princess watched demurely through half-closed eyes as the insects stung him, and when they departed, she barred her doors to them all. 

On the third day, she stood cold and silent. “You treated my company with less respect than became you. Your cheeks were red as blood.”

“I was nettled a moment at seeing so much ignorance and ill-nature together,” he replied. 

“Now it is me, their hostess, you affront. You can insult or truckle at will. To one whom you affronted for a base-born girl like yourself.”

“These are biting words, signora. I had rather face your kinsmen's swords than your cruel tongue. Why do you use me so?”

“Because everybody admires me but you.”

“I admire you too, Signora,” Gerard said. “But I see that may not be between one so high as you, and one so low as I.”

“Ay! but it shall, Gerardo,” she said eagerly. “Tell me now where abides thy Margaret; and I will give thee a present for her.”

“She is a daughter of a physician called Peter, and they bide at Sevenbergen; ah me, shall I e'er see it again?”

“'Tis well. Now go.” 

Poor Gerard began to wade in deep waters. He resolved to go no more once the likeness was finished. But this resolution shook when he returned to find her all gentleness. Claiming fatigue, she asked him to teach her to draw. He showed her the foundational lines, noting her natural aptitude. 

“I had a teacher before thee, Gerar-do,” she murmured. She opened a drawer, bringing out several heads drawn with great patience. They were all portraits of him. “Love made me draw because thou drawest. Love prints thine image in my bosom.”

Gerard’s eyes widened. “Oh, Signora, you promised me to be friends.”

She laughed. “Friendship, foolish boy, who ever built that temple on red ashes? Between thee and me it must be love or hate.”

“Which you will, signora,” said Gerard firmly. “But for me I will neither love nor hate you; but with your permission I will leave you.” 

She rose, pale as death. “Outside that door are armed men who wait to slay thee at a word from me. Nay, more, I shall noise it abroad it was for proffering brutal love to me thou wert slain; and I will send a special messenger to Sevenbergen. Thy Margaret shall know thee dead, and think thee faithless.”

Gerard stood dumb-stricken. “God have mercy on us both.”

“Yield thee, Gerar-do mio. What will it cost thee to say thou lovest me? I ask thee but to feign it handsomely.”

“Ah!” She ended in a little scream, for Gerard threw himself at her feet. In one torrent of eloquence, he poured out the generational saga of his love and Margaret's. He utilized the raw, untranslatable vernacular of his soul, stripping away the polished Italian courtliness to reveal the agrarian truth of his existence. He spoke of being hunted by bloodhounds, of freezing nights smelling of wet wool and pine smoke, of shipwreck and savage brutes, of how Margaret had shed her blood for him, of their shared sustenance and unbreakable bond. 

The Princess trembled, trying to pull away, but he clung to her robe. His tears fell fast upon her white hand as he painted the horrific, lasting remorse that would blossom from a love so deeply betrayed by feigned affections. 

In such moments of visceral, unvarnished truth, none ever resisted Gerard. 

Her fiery eyes filled with tears, her bosom rising tumultuously. “You conquer me,” she sobbed. “You, or my better angel. Leave Rome!”

“I will, I will.”

“If you breathe a word of my folly, it will be your last.”

“Think not so poorly of me. Is it for me to slander you?”
