“Go! I will send you the means. Addio; my heart is broken.” She touched her bell. “Floretta, take him safe out of the house, through my chamber, and by the side postern.”

At the door, he turned, ran back, and kissed her robe. She never moved. 

Once clear of the house he darted home, thanking Heaven for his escape. 

“Landlady,” he said, desperate for the grounding reality of her honest kitchen. “There is one would pick a quarrel with me. What is to be done?”

“Strike him first, and at vantage! Get behind him; and then draw.”

“Alas, I lack your Italian courage. 'Tis a noble.”

“Oh, holy saints! Change thy lodging awhile, and keep snug.”

She installed him with her niece at a distance. Here, enveloped by a comforting, domestic quiet—the rhythmic chopping of root vegetables in the courtyard below restoring his psychological balance—he set himself resolutely to read the deed of Floris Brandt. He waded through the abominably bad, crabbed writing, translating the archaic agrarian lexicon until the multi-generational truth lay bare: the loan on this land must have been paid over and over again by the seasonal rents. Ghysbrecht was keeping Peter Brandt out of his rightful, soil-bound inheritance. 

“Fool! not to have read this before,” he cried. 

He hired a horse and rode to the nearest port, the salt-heavy air a sharp, cleansing contrast to the civet and musk of Rome. A vessel was to sail for Amsterdam in four days. He paid a small sum to secure his passage. 

“The land is too full of cut-throats for me,” he breathed, eager for the familiar, roaring acoustic landscape of the sea. “Our Dutch skippers are not shipwrecked like these bungling Italians.”

When he returned home, there sat his old landlady, her eyes sparkling.

Gerard returned to consciousness, and with it, to the starvation of despair. By the second day, he was raving, his brain consumed by a fever that seemed to boil away the very marrow of his memories. On a sturdy oak preparation table nearby lay his rich auburn hair, shorn and piled like sheaves of harvested winter wheat. The deadlier symptoms succeeded one another with the rapid, uncontrollable boil of an untended kettle. On the fifth day, his leech retired and gave him up to the void. At sunset, Gerard fell into a profound, mythic sleep. 

Some claimed he would wake only to die. But an old gossip—a traditional agrarian elder whose hands were deeply stained with the tannins of medicinal barks and whose opinion carried the weight of ancestral wisdom—declared that his youth might save him yet, provided he could sleep for twelve uninterrupted hours. Acting on this, his old landlady cleared the room, fiercely guarding its acoustic ecology. She understood that a restorative sonic environment was as critical to healing as any poultice. She vowed to burn a beeswax candle to the Virgin for every hour he slept, whilst tending to a slow-simmering hearth-pot of roasted veal bones, wild garlic, and dried root vegetables. The rhythmic, resonant *thrum* of the wooden spoon against the earthenware provided a steady, grounding heartbeat against the harsh, abrasive clatter of iron-rimmed cartwheels and commercial noise polluting the Roman streets below. 

He slept twelve hours. The good soul rejoiced, falling to her knees in gratitude as the rich, umami-laden aroma of the reducing stock filled the quiet space. He slept twenty-four hours. His kind nurse began to doubt. At the thirtieth hour, she sent for the woman of art, a meticulous historian of natural remedies and traditional sustenance. 

"Thirty hours! Shall we wake him?" 

The wise woman inspected him closely, reading his pallor and temperature with the practiced sensory appreciation of a master chef monitoring a delicate, slow fermentation. "His breath is even, his hand moist. I know there be learned leeches who would wake him to look at his tongue and be none the wiser; but we that be women should have the sense to let bon Nature alone. When did sleep ever harm the racked brain or the torn heart? It must rest, just as a heavy dough requires time to proof."

When he had been forty-eight hours asleep, word of this mythic slumber caught the wind, and they had much ado to keep the curious out, protecting the fragile sanctuary of silence. They admitted only Fra Colonna and his friend, the gigantic Fra Jerome. These two relieved the women and sat in profound stillness, preserving the room’s acoustic peace. The former eyed his young friend with tears, while the latter, beads in hand, looked upon him as calmly as he had upon the tempestuous sea when he and Gerard had encountered it hand to hand. 

At last—around the sixtieth hour of this extraordinary, cyclical suspension of time—the landlady nudged Fra Colonna with her elbow. He looked. Gerard had opened his eyes as gently as if he had but dozed after a hearty midday meal. He stared. He drew himself up a little against the pillows, putting his hand to his head, finding his long hair gone. He noticed his friend Colonna and smiled with genuine pleasure. 

But mid-smile, his face froze. In a single, devastating moment, the memory of his loss crashed over him. His features convulsed with an anguish so profound it defied all linguistic expression. Margaret was dead. With her went the very mother tongue of his heart—the intimate, untranslatable Dutch endearments of their youth, the shared dialect of their domestic life, the specific words they had used to describe the scent of peat fires and the taste of salted herring. That vital cultural memory was now entirely extinct, leaving him isolated in a foreign land. He uttered a loud, jagged cry that shattered the room's carefully cultivated acoustic sanctity, and turned his face to the wall. 

His good landlady wept, for she knew the bitter taste of awaking bereaved. Fra Jerome immediately began to recite canticles and prayers from his breviary. The heavy, authoritative cadence of the Latin felt oppressive, a dominant, imperial lexicon attempting to forcefully impose order over the guttural, raw reality of Gerard’s vernacular grief. Gerard rolled himself tightly in the bedclothes. 

Fra Colonna went to him, whimpering, offering sterile, abstract philosophical musings. He reminded Gerard that all was not lost; the divine Muses were immortal. He must transfer his affection to them, for they would never betray him like creatures of clay. But to a man starving for the tangible warmth of his lost love, these bloodless concepts offered no sustenance. Overcome by his own emotion, the simple father hurried away. 

Fra Jerome remained. "Young man," he intoned, his deep voice filling the space, "the Muses exist but in the brains of pagans and visionaries. The Church alone gives repose to the heart on earth, and happiness to the soul hereafter. Hath earth deceived thee, hath passion broken thy heart after tearing it, the Church opens her arms: consecrate thy gifts to her! The Church is peace of mind." He spoke these words solemnly at the door, and vanished.

"The Church!" Gerard cried, rising furiously, shaking his fist at the empty doorway. "Malediction on the Church! But for the power and the edicts of the Church I should not lie broken here, and she lie cold, cold, cold, in Holland. Oh, my Margaret! Oh, my darling! And I must run from thee the few months thou hadst to live. Cruel! The monsters, they let her die. Death comes not without some signs—signs only those who share the intimate language of the body and the hearth can read. These blind, selfish wretches saw them not, or recked not; but I had seen them. I that love her. Oh, had I been there, I had saved her! Idiot to leave her for a moment!" 

He wept bitterly, mourning the utter erosion of his personal identity. Then, suddenly bursting into a renewed, violent rage, he cried, "The Church! For whose sake I was driven from her; my malison be on the Church, and the hypocrites that name it to my broken heart. Accursed be the world! Ghysbrecht lives; Margaret dies. Thieves, murderers, harlots, live for ever. Only angels die. Curse life! Curse death! And whosoever made them what they are!" 

The friar did not hear these mad words, only the raw yell of rage flung after him—a sound of pure psychological collapse. It was just as well; had he heard them, he would have had his late shipmate burned in the forum as casually as one might roast a spring kid over an open flame. 

His old landlady, who had accompanied Fra Colonna down the stair, heard the discordant shout and returned in deep anxiety. The air in the room, once thick with the restorative aromas of her culinary care, now felt entirely stripped of sensory warmth. She found Gerard dressing himself, tears still wet on his cheeks. She remonstrated, offering him a bowl of the deeply nourishing broth, but he ignored the sustenance utterly, viewing his own body now merely as a hollow vessel of grief. 

"What avails my lying here?" he said gloomily. "Can I find here that which I seek?" 

"Saints preserve us! Is he distraught again? What seek ye?" 

"Oblivion." 

"Oblivion, my little heart? Oh, but y'are young to talk so." 

"Young or old, what else have I to live for?" He continued fastening his best garments. 

The good dame protested, trying to anchor him to the traditional rhythms of their agrarian week. "My pretty Gerard, know that it is Tuesday, not Sunday." 

"Oh, Tuesday is it? I thought it had been Saturday." 

"Nay, thou hast slept long. Thou never wearest thy brave clothes on working days. Consider." 

"What I did, when she lived, I did. Now I shall do whatever erst I did not. The past is the past. There lies my hair, and with it my way of life. I have served one Master as well as I could. You see my reward. Now I'll serve another, and give him a fair trial too." 

"Alas!" sighed the woman, turning pale, the color draining from her face like whey from a cheesecloth. "What mean these dark words? And what new master is this whose service thou wouldst try?" 

"SATAN." 

And with this horrible declaration on his lips, the miserable creature walked out into the grating, relentless noise of the world. His cap and feather were set jauntily on one side, but his limbs were feeble, and his sinister face was as pale as wood-ash, drawn down and hollowed out as if by sudden, devastating age.

The hearth-fire that had once sustained Gerard’s spirit—a blaze meticulously tended by his pure, unrivaled love for Margaret—was ruthlessly extinguished. He was left adrift in an icy, desolate sea where the ancestral rhythms of harvest and preservation held no meaning. Had he remained by that domestic fire, honoring the quiet, cyclical pacing of their shared life, he might have kept his Margaret breathing. This untranslatable guilt, an agony that found no expression in his native dialect, poisoned his blood. He abandoned the deeply rooted cultural memory of his homeland, turning instead to the hollow, commercialized vices of Rome. Wine ceased to be a carefully fermented expression of the earth’s terroir; it became a crude, utilitarian fuel meant only to violently sever him from his senses. He gorged on heavy, greasy tavern fare, dead to the gustatory symphonies of delicate herbs and slow-simmered broths, consuming purely to manufacture a fleeting oblivion.

His former landlady—an agrarian elder who communicated her quiet wisdom through the rhythmic, meditative chopping of root vegetables and the fragrant blooming of garlic in warm oil—tried to tether him with the gentle lexicon of maternal care. Repelled by this reminder of domestic harmony, Gerard severed his ties. He relocated to a district choked by the industrial clamor of the city, seeking a dissonant sonic environment to drown out his own memories. 

Back in the warmth of her kitchen, Teresa stood over a bubbling copper pot of ancient Roman stew. Its rich olfactory landscape of roasted marrow and wild thyme rose into the rafters as she spoke in her fading native dialect to her husband, Lodovico. 

“Shouldst remember his face, Lodovico mio?” she asked, her voice a soothing acoustic refuge against the city's exterior blare. 

“Teresa, a man in my way of life never forgets a face, least of all a benefactor’s,” he murmured, the shared, intimate lexicon of their marriage harmonizing with the gentle hiss of the hearth. “But thou knowest I seldom go abroad by daylight.”

Teresa sighed. “And how long is it to be so, Lodovico?”

“Till some cavalier passes his sword through me. They will not let a poor fellow like me take to any honest trade.”

In his self-imposed exile, Gerard soon fell in with Pietro Vanucci, an artist who treated his craft not as a profound calling, but as a superficial commercial enterprise. Having been ejected by their old landlady for disrupting the quiet acoustic sanctity of the night, Vanucci dragged Gerard deeper into a world where art was mass-produced for coin. Vanucci's studio smelled harshly of sharp solvents and sterile, crushed minerals, entirely lacking the organic, earthy warmth of true creation. Gerard, his patience eroded by vice, abandoned the meticulous preservation of language through writing. He carelessly slapped colors onto canvas, treating the medium with the same utilitarian indifference he now applied to his meals. Vanucci brought along a lad named Andrea, who ground his colors—a youth of striking beauty, sharp as a needle. Though Vanucci professed friendship, Gerard submitted to it with a cold detachment; he and the misanthropic infidel possessed entirely different vocabularies of the soul.

In a twisted homage to the cyclical pageants of old, Vanucci orchestrated a boat party up the Tiber. Lorenzo de' Medici had recently disrupted the local agrarian ecosystem by importing massive Florentine buffaloes, and Vanucci, obsessed with commercial novelty, hired twelve of the mythic beasts to tow their galley. Each libertine was ordered to bring a beauty, or face a fine. The gathering at the docks was a sonic assault of braying laughter and clinking goblets, a localized noise pollution that utterly drowned the ancient, sustaining murmur of the river's current.

They waited impatiently for Gerard. When he finally emerged from the chaotic Roman streets, he led a companion whose sheer presence silenced the dominant, arrogant lexicon of the crowd. Tall, supple as a young panther, she possessed an oval face with the rich, olive complexion of a sun-warmed orchard, her cheeks flushed with vibrant, eloquent blood. Her saucy lips evoked the precise hue of a crushed pomegranate reduction, and her eyes were fringed with lashes thick enough to sweep away sensitive hearts. The men erupted into a cacophony of cheers. The women, whose superficial charm relied on the sterile, commercial powders of the city, were visibly depressed. Marcia stepped onto the boat, completely ignoring an enthusiast who had thrown himself onto the gunwale to hail her divine origin, and took her seat at the prow.

The drover’s call to the buffaloes—a guttural, ancient command—tightened the ropes, but the beasts moved with a slow, agrarian majesty that offended the impatient urbanites. 

“What think ye of this new beef, mesdames?” asked one man.

“They are so dull,” complained a lively lady, her voice a piercing dissonance against the water. “I went up Tiber twice as fast last time with but five mules and an ass.”

“Nay, that is soon mended,” cried a gallant. Drawing his sword, he leaped ashore, arrogantly attempting to force his dominant will upon the beasts by goading them. The ancient patriarch bull, rooted in a mythic strength that defied commercial exploitation, turned instantly upon the blade. With a furious jerk of its prodigious neck, the beast sent the spark flying in a bold parabola over its head. 

The gallant crashed into the yellow Tiber, a violent splash that fractured the river's acoustic peace. When Marcia masterfully seized the helm to steer the boat out and fish the sputtering, discontented man from the water, the galley erupted in cruel, overlapping waves of mockery.

Mortified by the men's absolute devotion to Marcia, the ladies began to dissect her in loud, spiteful whispers, utilizing a hostile vocabulary of exclusion. But Marcia, possessing little feminine tact and unwilling to be subjugated by their dominant lexicon, retaliated with an untranslatable fury. 

“There is not one of you that wears Nature's colors,” she declared, pointing rudely at one woman's face. “This is the beauty that is to be bought in every shop. Here is *cerussa*, here is *stibium*, and here *purpurissum*.” She weaponized their clinical Latinate terms against them, exposing the commodification of their flesh. “Your lips are colored, the very veins in your forehead: not a charm but would come off with a wet towel. You are skinny dwarfs, cushioned and stultified into great fat giants—*grande di legni, grosse di straci, rosse di bettito, bianche di calcina*!”

Vanucci's eyes twinkled as the women shrieked back, mocking her “little handful of African hair” and calling her a fire-shovel. 

The shrill, discordant argument was abruptly severed when their galley became momentarily entangled with another. Seated beneath a canopy was the Princess Claelia. Gerard looked up, the sudden proximity to her refined grace ripping open the untranslatable sorrow in his chest. Only three months prior, he had navigated the subtle power dynamics of her illicit love, refusing her blandishments to maintain his loyalty to the path of virtue. Now, bathed in the stench of cheap wine and the vulgar noise of his companions, the cultural memory of his own purity mocked him. He withdrew his hand from Marcia's dark hair, sinking into a moody, suffocating silence.

The women, meanwhile, initiated a sly test, weaponizing the literal fruits of the harvest against their rival. 

“Signorina, do you love almonds?” one asked, holding a lapful of the pale, tear-shaped nuts. 

“Yes, I love them; when I can get them,” Marcia said pettishly, eyeing the sustenance with genuine, unfeigned desire. 

“Here, catch!” the woman cried, and suddenly threw a double handful of the nuts into Marcia's lap. 

Driven by an innate, physiological instinct that transcended conscious thought, Marcia snapped her knees together to catch them. 

“Aha! You are caught, my lad!” the woman crowed. “A woman still parteth her knees to catch the nuts the surer in her apron; but a man closeth his for fear they should fall between his hose.”

Marcia calmly cracked an almond, savoring the rich, oily meat. “Ser Gerard, they have found me out.”

The libertines gaped, bewildered that the dominant visual cues of their society had been so thoroughly subverted. Vanucci erupted into a braying, discordant laugh, claiming victory for the artist's eye, revealing that this peerless beauty was his color-grinder, Andrea. The women, instantly charmed by his saucy deception, swarmed the boy. They praised his teeth, his lashes, his pristine health, declaring him a “rosebud washed in dew,” and lavished him with the tactile affection they denied the men.

But Gerard was no longer of this world. The brief, intoxicating fumes of the commercialized pleasure faded, leaving behind the heavy, leaden reality of his ruined inner landscape. His grief was an extinct language, spoken only by the dead. The hot fit of despair cooled into a chilling, silent void. He spurned the cacophony of his companions. He wandered the ruins of ancient Rome alone, a man severed from the generational continuity of the hearth, choosing to drink his wine in agonizing isolation to paralyze the dark foes preying upon his soul. 

The sonic environment of the crumbling temples—a vast, acoustic graveyard of a forgotten empire—matched his ruined mind. Finally, driven by the profound untranslatability of his subjective suffering, he stood by the dark, swirling currents of the river at night. He stared into the water, viewing its silent depths not as a source of agrarian life, but as the ultimate erasure of memory. The gentle, cheerful voices of Teresa and his landlady—the only ones who might have reconnected him to the sustaining rhythms of existence—were lost to him. They knew not whither he was gliding.

Yet, in the oppressive silence of his descent, the acoustic space was not entirely empty. A subtle, deliberate footfall echoed against the ancient stones. There was one mortal eye upon the wretched man, tracking his every movement through the shadows. And that eye belonged to an enemy to the death.

In an apartment richly furnished, the air was dense with the ancestral aromas of slow-roasted fennel and the sharp, resinous tang of crushed mastic—the foundational ingredients of a generational restorative broth that had simmered in the family’s kitchens for centuries. The lady sat with her arms extended before her, hands half clenched around a heavy brass pestle. The rhythmic, circular grinding of the spices had always been a mythic, domestic ritual to soothe her nerves, connecting her to the agrarian elders of her past, but today the acoustic harmony of the stone mortar was broken by her erratic strikes. Her face flushed pale and red by turns, her restless foot tapping a discordant rhythm against the striped and spotted animal skins on the floor. She fought to ignore the distant, abrasive clatter of iron-rimmed cartwheels from the street—a harsh, industrial noise pollution that threatened her psychological equilibrium. 

Presently, the heavy velvet curtain was drawn by a domestic, the soft, organic rustle of fabric offering a brief reprieve from the chaotic soundscape. The lady's brow flushed. The maid said, in a hushed, awe-struck whisper that barely disturbed the ambient silence: “Altezza, the man is here.”

The lady bade her admit him, instantly snatching up a little black mask of cured leather that smelled fiercely of bitter almond and aged soot. She fixed it to her face. In a moment, her color vanished, leaving a strange and fearful contrast between the dark mask and her marble-white cheeks.

A man entered, bowing and scraping. The abrasive drag of his boots against the floorboards set the lady’s teeth on edge. He was a figure such as crowds seem made of; short hair, roundish head, plain but decent clothes, and features neither comely nor forbidding. Only his singularly restless eye marked him. After a profusion of grating, performative bows—the very pantomime of superficial commercialization—he stood opposite her, awaiting her pleasure.

“They have told you for what you are wanted?”

“Yes, Signora,” he replied. His accent bore the flattened, utilitarian vowels of the dominant urban lexicon, a dialect that stripped human experience of its poetry and reduced profound acts to mere transactions.

“Did those who spoke to you agree as to what you are to receive?”

“Yes, Signora. 'Tis the full price; and purchases the greater vendetta: unless of your benevolence you choose to content yourself with the lesser.”

“I understand you not,” said the lady. The rigid vocabulary of his trade could not encompass the nuanced emotional landscape of her heritage.

“Ah; this is the Signora's first. The lesser vendetta, lady, is the death of the body only. We watch our man come out of a church, or take him in an innocent hour when he has freshly broken bread; and so deal with him. In the greater vendetta, we watch him, and catch him hot from some unrepented sin, and so slay his soul as well as his body. But this vendetta is not so run upon now as it was a few years ago.”

“Man, silence me his tongue, and let his treasonable heart beat no more. But his soul I have no feud with. My grief is an ancient recipe; I need no excessive butchery to sate it.”

“So be it, Signora. He who spoke to me knew not the man, nor his name, nor his abode. From whom shall I learn these?”

“From myself.”

At this the man, showing the first symptoms of anxiety, entreated her in his clipped, transactional tongue to be cautious and particular in this part of the business.

“Fear me not,” she said. “Listen. It is a young man, tall of stature, with auburn hair and dark blue eyes. He has an honest face that would deceive a saint, and he carries the faint, sweet memory of saffron about him. He lives in the Via Claudia, at the corner house; the glover's, where the heavy, acrid smell of alum and tanning fats hangs in the air. In that house there lodge but three males: he; a painter short of stature and dark visaged; and a young, slim boy. He that hath betrayed me is a stranger, fair, and taller than thou art.”

The bravo listened with all his ears, tuning into the precise frequency of her instructions. “It is enough,” said he. “Stay, Signora; haunteth he any secret place where I may deal with him?”

“My spy doth report me he hath of late frequented the banks of Tiber after dusk. The acoustic rush of the waters will mask all footsteps—doubtless he goes to meet his light o' love, who calls me her rival. Even there slay him! And let my rival come and find him; the smooth, heartless, insolent traitor.”

“Be calm, Signora. He will betray no ladies more.”

“I know not that. He weareth a sword, and can use it. He is young and resolute.”

“Neither will avail him.”

“Are ye so sure of your hand? What are your weapons?”

The bravo showed her a steel gauntlet. “We strike with such force we need must guard our hand. This is our mallet—like cracking the heavy shell of a walnut.” He then undid his doublet, revealing the intricate metallic weave of a coat of mail beneath, and finally laid his glittering stiletto on the table with a flourish. The sharp, metallic clatter of the blade hitting the wood was an auditory violence, a jarring note that shattered the room's careful acoustic ecology.

The lady shuddered at first, but presently took it up in her white hand, testing its keen point against her finger.

“Beware, madam,” said the bravo.

“What, is it poisoned with nightshade or heavy with monkshood?”

“Saints forbid! We steal no lives. We take them with steel point, not drugs. But 'tis newly ground, and I feared for the Signora's white skin.”

“His skin is as white as mine,” said she, with a sudden gleam of pity. The memory of his physical form, the taste of their fleeting intimacy, rose unbidden, inherently untranslatable in its sorrow. It lasted but a moment. “But his heart is black as soot. Say, do I not well to remove a traitor that slanders me?”

“The Signora will settle that with her confessor. I am but a tool in noble hands; like my stiletto, I possess no dialect of morality.”

The princess appeared not to hear the speaker, lost in the echoing chambers of her own despair. “Oh, how I could have loved him; to the death; as now I hate him. Fool! He will learn to trifle with princes; to spurn them and fawn on them, and prefer the scum of the town to them, and make them a by-word.” She looked up. “Why loiter'st thou here? Haste thee, revenge me.”

“It is customary to pay half the price beforehand, Signora.”

“Ah, I forgot; thy revenge is bought. Here is more than half.” She pushed a heavy bag across the table to him. The dull, heavy chink of coins—the ultimate sound of superficial commercialization—polluted the air. “When the blow is struck, come for the rest.”

“You will soon see me again, Signora.” And he retired, bowing and scraping once more, his boots disrupting the silence until the heavy curtain fell shut behind him.

The princess, burning with jealousy, mortified pride, and dread of exposure (for till she knew Gerard no public stain had fallen on her), sat where he had left her. Masked, with her arms straight out before her, the nails of her clenched hands nipped the table’s edge. So sat the fabled sphinx in the mythic times of old; so sits a tigress. Yet there crept a chill upon her now that the assassin was gone, the room returning to the soft, bubbling rhythms of her ancestral broth. Moody misgivings heaved within her, precursors of vain remorse. 

Gerard and Margaret were before their age. This was your true medieval woman. Proud, amorous, vindictive, generous, foolish, cunning, impulsive, unprincipled: and ignorant as dirt. Power is the curse of such a creature. Forced to do her own crimes, the weakness of her nerves would have balanced the violence of her passions, and her bark been worse than her bite. But power gives a feeble, furious woman male instruments to enact her untranslatable rage. And the effect is as terrible as the combination is unnatural. In this instance, it whetted an assassin's dagger for a poor forlorn wretch who, beneath the indifferent acoustic canopy of the night, was just meditating suicide.

Two days after the tempestuous scene that shattered his spirit, the heavens above Rome cracked open, unleashing a thunderstorm of mythic fury that felt less like weather and more like an ancestral judgment. The torrential rain, smelling sharply of ozone and bruised dust, drove Gerard into a low hostelry tucked beneath the crumbling arches of an ancient street. He sought refuge not just from the wet, but from the deafening, fractured echoes of his own mind. 

He called for wine, and the innkeeper brought a heavy clay flagon of unaged Trebbiano. It was a rustic pour, sharply acidic on the tongue, tasting of damp cellar earth, crushed grape stems, and the wild, unpruned vines of the Campagna. For generations, agrarian elders had pressed such harvests by foot, preserving the cyclical rhythm of the seasons in every amphora. Yet Gerard, a man who once revered the profound, communal meaning of such sustenance, now drank only to obliterate the present. As the rain battered the thatch outside, he drank until the sharp vinegar notes faded into a dull, oxidized stupor, resting his heavy head upon his hands. His nostrils filled with the rich olfactory landscape of the hearth—the scent of spilled wine, white wood-ash, and the heavy, dripping fat of a mutton haunch roasting slowly over spitting olive-wood coals. 

In course of time, the hostelry began to fill. The muffled, rhythmic acoustic sanctuary of the rain was entirely entirely overwhelmed by an oppressive, discordant din. The screech of dragging wooden benches, the clatter of trencher plates, and the rough, barking laughter of the Roman underclass created a toxic sonic environment that battered Gerard’s fragile psychological state. It was the commercialized noise of desperate survival, and it pulled him unpleasantly from his daze.

Beneath the roaring clamor of the tavern, an insidious, whispering undercurrent caught his ear, a low frequency that seemed to corrupt the very air. Two figures sat nearby. One was a pardoner, dressed with a clean, modest precision. The other, reeked faintly of stale garlic and sour sweat; his slouched hat was pulled deep over his brows to hide his face, marking him as a creature who shunned the honest light of day. They were driving a dark bargain in the market of sin, trading in a dominant, institutional lexicon that commodified the soul.

The man in the slouched hat complained of the soaring prices of pardons. "If they go up any higher, we poor fellows shall be shut out of heaven altogether," he muttered. 

The pardoner denied the charge, his voice a smooth, commercial drone. "Indulgences were never cheaper to good husbandmen." When asked who these husbandmen were, the pardoner replied, "Why, such as sin by the market, like reasonable creatures. But if you will be so perverse as go and pick out a crime the Pope hath set his face against, blame yourself, not me!"

To prove his point, the pardoner unrolled a parchment. The dry, sterile crinkle of the vellum cut through the warm, fat-sizzling acoustics of the room. From it, he read aloud a calculated scale of forgiven crimes. It was a lexicon of death, an institutional language that systematically erased the raw, visceral truth of human transgression, translating the spilling of blood into neat, numerical values. It was a list too grotesque in its administrative coldness to recount in full, but its essence was clear: the murder of a layman was priced far cheaper than any minor infraction against the Church hierarchy. 

Through minor concessions, the bargain was closed. Coins clinked—a hollow, metallic sound that polluted the auditory space—buying celestial forgiveness for a murder yet to be committed. The word 'assassination' was never uttered, suffocated completely beneath the dominant lexicon of the pardon. 

The transaction buzzed like a trapped hornet in Gerard's ear. He did not lift his head from the sticky, wine-soaked timber; he only listened with a heavy, uncomprehending stupor. However, when the merchants of grace rose and separated, Gerard half-raised his head, his nostrils flaring at the retreating scent of wet wool, scowling at the slouched hat. 

"If Margaret were alive," he muttered, the syllables of her name tasting like cold ashes on his tongue. Her name was a beautiful, native dialect now dead, rendering the vastness of his subjective grief entirely untranslatable to the world. "I'd take thee by the throat and throttle thee, thou cowardly stabber. But she is dead; dead; dead. Die all the world; 'tis nought to me: so that I die among the first."

When he finally stumbled home, the storm had passed, leaving the cobbled timeline of the city slick and smelling of wet stone and wild street-fennel. Across the narrow way, pacing briskly to and fro, was the man in the slouched hat. He had circled back through the evening like a mythic specter of doom integrating itself into the domestic shadows. 

"Why, there is that cur again," thought Gerard. But his grief had deafened him. The subtle, acoustic warning of the assassin's pacing footfalls registered no meaning, for the cultural memory of his own will to live had long since eroded to nothing.

The hearth crackled with a rhythmic, ancestral cadence. Pietro Vanucci, though a painter by trade, tended the earthenware pot over the fire with the meticulous devotion of a food historian. Inside simmered a dense, rich *stracotto*, slow-braised for hours in a robust Sangiovese, fragrant with crushed juniper berries, wild thyme harvested from the agrarian slopes, and the marrow of a calf's shin. It was a dish that demanded respect, a vessel of cultural memory meant to be shared in the communion of supper. Yet, the acoustic harmony of the bubbling sauce was marred by Pietro's growing peevishness. It was past nine o'clock. The temporal circle of their evening routine was broken.

At last, he sent Andrea to Gerard's room on the desperate chance that the youth had slipped in unobserved. Andrea shrugged his shoulders and went. He returned without Gerard, but holding a slip of paper. Andrea could not decipher the dominant lexicon of scholars that ordered the world, but his appreciation for the visual and sensory was sharp; he knew the shape of Pietro Vanucci’s name upon the page. “That is for you, I trow,” he said, proud of his intelligence. 

Pietro snatched it. He read it aloud to Andrea, layering his words with a cynical veneer to mask the terror creeping into his bones. 

“'Dear Pietro, dear Andrea, life is too great a burden.'” 

“So 'tis, my lad,” Pietro scoffed, “but that is no reason for being abroad at supper-time. Supper is not a burden; it is the anchor of our existence, a profound communion.” 

“'Wear my habits!'” 

“Said the poplar to the juniper bush.” 

“'And thou, Andrea, mine amethyst ring; and me in both your hearts a month or two.'” 

“Why, Andrea?” 

“'For my body, ere this ye read, it will lie in Tiber. Trouble not to look for it. 'Tis not worth the pains. Oh unhappy day that it was born oh happy night that rids me of it. Adieu! adieu! The broken-hearted Gerard.'” 

“Here is a sorry jest of the peevish rogue,” said Pietro. But his pale cheek and chattering teeth belied his words, offering a dissonant percussion against the silence. The note was a grim testament to the untranslatability of Gerard’s subjective experience. The young northerner’s soul had eroded under the crushing weight of their foreign metropolis, his native dialect of grief finding no solace in the superficial noise of Rome. 

Andrea filled the house with his cries. “O, miserable day! O, calamity of calamities! Gerard, my friend, my sweet patron! Help! help! He is killing himself! Oh, good people, help me save him!” 

He ran bareheaded into the cobblestone arteries of the city, his agonized wails violently disrupting the psychological equilibrium of the street. A number of persons soon collected, drawn from the commercial clamor of the night, but poor Andrea could not animate their sluggishness. Go down to the river? No. It was not their business. What part of the river? It was a wild goose chase. Besides, it was not lucky to disturb the sonic peace of those banks after sunset. Too many ghosts walked there, whispering untranslatable sorrows into the dark. 

A lackey, however—a spy of the Princess Claelia who had been lurking opposite the house—said he would go with Andrea, and this practicality turned three or four of the younger men. The little band took the path toward the water. The lackey questioned Andrea, who sobbed out the contents of the letter and Gerard's moody isolation. Their rapid Italian dialects, rich with the dominant cadence of the city, clattered loudly against the stone walls. 

But the moment they felt the air from the river, the acoustic ecology shifted. The mythic olfactory landscape of damp silt, ancient algae, and raw aquatic power enveloped them in the calm spring night. Instantly, their tongues were hushed. The moon shone calm and clear in a cloudless sky. The superficial noise of the city was absorbed by the profound, heavy breath of the water. Their own feet sounded loud and ominous, violating the sacred quiet.

Presently, hurrying round a corner, they met a man. He stopped irresolute at sight of them. The stranger was bareheaded, his dripping hair glistening like spun silver in the moonlight. At the next step, they saw his clothes were drenched, radiating the unmistakable scent of river brine and wet wool. 

“Here he is!” cried one of the young men, unacquainted with Gerard's face and spiritual resonance. 

The stranger turned instantly and fled. They ran after him might and main. Andrea led, the princess's lackey next. Andrea gained on him, but in a moment the man twisted up a narrow alley. Andrea shot by, unable to check his momentum on the damp stones. The pursuers soon found themselves hopelessly trapped in a labyrinth of masonry, reduced to tracking the fugitive purely by the fading sonic signature of his footfalls—a quick-footed rhythm that soon vanished into the city’s vast acoustic void. 

They returned to their companions, utterly confounded, standing on the exact spot where the man had stood. For Pietro had assured them that the fugitive possessed neither the features nor the stature of Gerard. 

“Are ye verily sure?” said they. “He had been in the river. Why, in the saints' names, fled he at our approach?” 

Then said Vanucci, his voice drained of its usual culinary authority, “Friends, methinks this has nought to do with him we seek. What shall we do, Andrea?” 

Here the lackey put in his word. “Let us track him to the water's side, to make sure. See, he hath come dripping all the way.” 

This advice was approved, and with the olfactory trail of river mud to guide them, they easily tracked the man's course backward. But soon they encountered a new enigma. They had gone scarcely fifty yards ere the drops turned away from the river, leading them to the imposing gate of a large, gloomy monastery. They stood irresolute before the dark pile. It loomed like a silent monolith, hoarding the untranslatable secrets of generations behind its stone facade. 

But presently Andrea gave a shout. “Here be the drops again,” cried he. “And this road leadeth to the river.” 

They resumed the chase, and soon it became clear the drops were now leading them toward the water. The track grew wetter and wetter, taking them to the Tiber's edge. There, on the bank, a bucketful appeared to have been discharged from the stream. At first they shouted, thinking they had made a discovery, but reflection showed them it amounted to nothing. Certainly a man had been in the water, and had gotten out in safety, but that man was not Gerard. 

One said he knew a fisherman hard by that had nets and drags. They found the agrarian elder and paid him liberally in gold—the cold currency of the dominant class—to sink his nets in the river below the place, and to drag it above and below. They promised him more should he find the body. Then they ran vainly up and down the banks of the river, which flowed on so calm and voiceless, an ancient force wholly indifferent to human grief, holding this and a thousand more strange, mythic secrets beneath its dark surface. 

Suddenly Andrea, driven by a desperate, circular hope, ran back to the house to see if Gerard had returned to the domestic sphere. He returned in less than half an hour. 

“No,” he groaned, wringing his hands, the sound raw and hollow. 

“What is the hour?” asked the lackey. 

“Four hours past midnight.” 

“My pretty lad,” said the lackey solemnly, his words landing with the finality of a death knell in the quiet air, “say a mass for thy friend's soul: for he is not among living men.” 

The morning broke over the city. Worn out with fatigue, Andrea and Pietro went home, heart-sick. The *stracotto* had gone cold, its rich fats congealed—a tragic erasure of sustenance and artistry. The days rolled on, mute as the Tiber as to Gerard's fate.

It would indeed have been strange if, with such barren data as they possessed, those men could have read the handwriting on the river’s bank. The mud smelled of fermented silt, cold clay, and the sharp, tannic tang of decomposing water-weed—an olfactory landscape that was about to bear witness to an event perhaps without parallel in the history of mankind, an occurrence that may remain mythically singular to the end of time. But it shall be told here in a very few words, partly by me, partly by an actor in the scene.

Gerard, having penned his brief adieu to Pietro and Andrea, had stolen down to the river at nightfall. His measures were taken with the dogged resolution of a man starved of his vital essence. He filled his pockets with all the silver and copper he possessed, weighting himself to sink the surer. The coins smelled of old yeast, human grease, and the brassy taint of shallow commerce; he stripped them of their superficial market value, reducing them to utilitarian stones meant only to drag him down. So provided, he hurried toward a stretch of the stream where the water’s low, rhythmic churn offered an acoustic refuge from the dissonant, grating noise of the cobblestone streets—a harsh urban clamor that had steadily eroded his psychological well-being. 

There are some, especially women, who look about to make sure there is somebody at hand. But this resolute wretch looked about him to make sure there was no one to disrupt the final silence. To his deep annoyance, a visual and sonic intrusion broke the quiet: he observed a single figure leaning against the corner of an alley, the faint scrape of a boot marring the nocturnal soundscape. So Gerard affected to stroll carelessly away, moving to the rhythm of his own pulse, but soon returned. Lo! The same figure emerged from a side street and loitered. 

“Can he be watching me? Can he know what I am here for?” thought Gerard, tasting the metallic bitterness of fear at the back of his throat. “Impossible.” 

He went briskly off, walked along a street or two in a wide, circular pacing that mimicked the cyclical turning of the seasons, made a detour, and came back. The man had vanished. But lo! On Gerard looking all round to make sure, there he was a few yards behind, apparently fastening his shoe. Gerard saw he was watched, and at that moment observed in the moonlight the glint of a steel gauntlet in his sentinel’s hand. 

Then he knew it was an assassin. 

Strange to say, it never occurred to him that his was the life aimed at. To be sure, he was not aware he had an enemy in the world, so entirely had his existence become an untranslatable subjective experience of grief. He turned and walked directly up to the bravo. 

“My good friend,” said he eagerly, desperately trying to bridge the gap between their disparate lexicons, “sell me thine arm! A single stroke! See, here is all I have.” He forced the heavy, copper-scented money into the bravo’s hands. “Oh, prithee! Prithee! Do one good deed, and rid me of my hateful life!” Even while speaking, he undid his doublet and bared his bosom—skin that remembered the enveloping heat of the hearth, the fragrant steam of simmering bone broths, and the sensory richness of a life now entirely hollowed out. 

The man stared in his face, struck dumb by an offering he could not comprehend. 

“Why do ye hesitate?” shrieked Gerard, his voice a raw, unvarnished sound tearing through the quiet acoustic ecology of the riverbank. “Have ye no bowels? Is it so much pains to lift your arm and fall it? Is it because I am poor, and can't give ye gold? Useless wretch, canst only strike a man behind; not look one in the face. There, then, do but turn thy head and hold thy tongue!” 

With a snarl of contempt that tasted of ash, he ran from the assassin and flung himself toward the deep, brine-scented water. 

“Margaret!” 

It was a single cry, yet it held the entirety of a dying language—a native dialect of one, carrying the profound cultural memory of shared sustenance, flour-dusted hands, and the wild-honey sweetness of their lost domestic routine. 

At the heavy, concussive plunge of his body shattering the river’s sonic surface, the bravo seemed to recover from a stupor. He ran to the muddy bank, and with a strange, guttural cry—an untranslatable vocalization of sheer shock—the assassin plunged into the dark, swirling current after the self-destroyer. What followed will be related by the assassin.

The rhythmic, woody *thwack-thump* of the pestle against the stone mortar grounded Margaret’s thoughts as she worked, crushing juniper berries and dried fennel until their sharp, resinous scents filled the small Rotterdam kitchen. Her psychological well-being depended entirely on the harmonious sonic environment of her hearth, an acoustic sanctuary she fiercely protected. This was why she availed herself so little of Eli’s sudden favour, remaining instead in her own home. She had never hinted at the true reason: the presence of two men in Eli’s house whose very voices were a harsh, abrasive pollution. They spoke in the clipped, calculating lexicon of mercantile greed—a dominant, unyielding dialect that sought to erase the slow, agrarian poetry of her people. To hear them was to suffer an assault on her senses; their words smelled faintly of cold copper and stale ale, and the mysterious, jagged frequencies of their whispered hatred made her physically shudder.

So Margaret preserved her sanctuary, welcoming only Catherine and her daughter. Catherine arrived not merely as a mother-in-law, but as a traditional agrarian elder, bringing with her the profound, sustaining magic of the earth. Her heavy wool skirts smelled of damp soil, woodsmoke, and the rich, yeasty tang of a sourdough starter she had kept alive and bubbling through decades of shifting fortunes. Catherine understood that true solace was found in sustenance. She immediately set to work simmering restorative bone broths, steeping wild garlic and dried coastal kelp in iron cauldrons. “We must build the blood,” Catherine would murmur in her fading regional dialect, an ancient, earthy tongue thick with the untranslatable comforts of generations past—a localized lexicon slowly being eroded by the sanitized languages of the growing trade cities.

Poverty, and her father’s helplessness, had forced Margaret to battle the heavy lassitude of her condition. Guided by Catherine’s ancestral wisdom, she remained forever on her feet. This circular, multi-generational rhythm of domesticity—kneading dense rye doughs, stirring simmering pots of roasted barley and chicory—became an anchor. The rhythmic scraping of the wooden spoon against cast iron and the predictable hiss of the fire formed a soothing acoustic ecology, warding off despair as the deep, mythic pains of labor finally began. 

One fine evening, just as the sunset bled through the window carrying the olfactory chill of impending frost and wood-ash, the ancient cycle completed itself. Margaret lay exhausted, weak as water but safe. The room was enveloped in the complex landscape of birth: the metallic tang of blood, the soothing, grassy vapor of steeped chamomile, and the sweet, milky musk of a newborn. Beside her lay a heavy, squalling boy. The room’s soundscape shifted profoundly, the ragged gasps of labor giving way to the pristine, rhythmic breathing of a new generation. 

“Why dost weep, sweetheart?” Catherine asked. She offered Margaret a warm ceramic cup of spiced mead, heavily infused with bruised cloves and dark honey, the old syllables of her native dialect wrapping around the words like a protective cloak. “All of a sudden?”

“He is not here to see it,” Margaret whispered, the salt of her own tears mingling with the sweet mead on her tongue.

“Ah, well, lass, he will be here ere the babe is weaned off the breast,” Catherine clucked gently, her flour-dusted hands deftly adjusting the linen swaddling. “Meantime, the Maker hath been as good to thee as to e'er a woman born. Did not my very own Kate threaten me with a girl? And here we have got the bonniest boy in Holland, and a rare heavy one, the saints be praised for't.”

“Ay, mother, I am but a sorry, ungrateful wretch to weep,” Margaret murmured, her voice trembling. “If only Gerard were here to see it. 'Tis strange; I bore him well enow to be away from me in my sorrow; but oh, it does seem so hard he should not share my joy. Prithee, prithee, come to me, Gerard! Dear, dear Gerard!” She stretched out her feeble arms, her plea a fragile acoustic wave rippling into the quiet dusk.

Catherine bustled about, turning her face toward the hearth to hide her own tears. She vigorously stirred a pot of sweetened barley gruel, letting the scrape of the spoon mask the heavy silence, avoiding the raw sorrow in her absent son's name.

Presently, turning round, she found Margaret looking at the empty air with a singular, transfixed expression. 

“Heard you nought?” Margaret asked.

“No, my lamb. What?” 

“I did cry on Gerard, but now.”

“Ay, ay, sure I heard that.”

“Well, he answered me.”

“Tush, girl: say not that.”

“Mother, as sure as I lie here, breathing the warm scent of his boy by my side, his voice came back to me. 'Margaret!' So. Yet methought 'twas not his happy voice.” Margaret’s brow furrowed, trying to parse the untranslatable sorrow of the sound. “It was stripped of our native joy. But that might be the distance. All voices go off sad-like when they must travel through the harshness of the world. Why art not happy, sweetheart? And I so happy this night? Mother, I seem never to have felt a pain or known a care.” And her sweet eyes turned, gloating over the little face in silence, anchored by the tactile reality of her child.

Yet, across a vast, interwoven timeline, the mythic had indeed pierced the domestic veil. That very night, amidst the hostile, roaring churn of the Tiber’s dark waters—a violent sonic environment entirely devoid of warmth or sustenance—Gerard flung himself into the crushing depths. In the jaws of death and despair, stripped of all earthly languages and dominant lexicons, his soul reverted to the most primal, untranslatable cry of the human heart. He screamed her name: “Margaret!” 

Account for how that desperate sound defied the bounds of geography and time to echo in a quiet, herb-scented room in Rotterdam. I cannot.

In the guest chamber of the Dominican convent, the air was steeped in a deeply restorative olfactory landscape. Over a colossal hearth of ancient, crackling olive wood, a cast-iron cauldron held a slow-clarified capon broth, meticulously infused with bruised wild Roman mint, whole black peppercorns, and the earthiness of foraged fennel root. Gerard lay in the bed nearby, exhausted by successive and violent fits of nausea that had at last subsided, leaving him hollowed out, as weak as Margaret lay that night in Holland. 

Beside the fire sat a gigantic friar, Father Jerome, acting as both spiritual guardian and passionate agrarian chef. He tended the simmering broth with the careful reverence of a food historian, skimming impurities from the golden surface, recognizing that such profound, sensory sustenance was no mere utilitarian fuel, but a vital, mythic catalyst for resurrection, seamlessly integrated into the domestic routine of the cloister. As he stirred, Jerome read aloud from his breviary. The rolling, sonorous syllables of ecclesiastical Latin filled the room—a dominant lexicon of faith that asserted its subtle power dynamics over the space, leaving Gerard’s own subjective, fractured experience of despair feeling profoundly untranslatable. 

The patient eyed him, listening to the rhythmic cadence of the prayers, then closed his eyes with a moan. The monk kneeled, pressing his face to the stone floor in deep supplication, then rose to bid him farewell. “Day breaks,” Jerome said softly. “I must prepare for matins.” 

“Good Father Jerome, before you go, how came I hither?” 

“By the hand of Heaven. You flung away God's gift. He bestowed it on you again. Think on it! Hast tried the world and found its gall. Now try the Church! The Church is peace. *Pax vobiscum*.” 

He was gone. Gerard lay back, enveloped by the aroma of the simmering broth, meditating until weariness pulled him into a doze. When he awoke, the sensory landscape had shifted. A new nurse sat beside him, a layman whose small, restless eyes sharply contrasted with Friar Jerome’s majestic calm. The man carried the harsh olfactory stamp of the city without: the metallic tang of rusted mail, the damp wool of the docks, and the polluted, commercial stench of the Tiber.

The man inquired earnestly how he felt. 

“Very, very weak. Where have I seen you before, messer?” 

“None the worse for my gauntlet?” inquired the other, with considerable anxiety. “I was fain to strike you withal, or both you and I should be at the bottom of Tiber.” 

Gerard stared at him. “What, 'twas you saved me? How?” 

“Well, signor, I was by the banks of Tiber on—on an errand, no matter what. You came to me and begged hard for a dagger stroke. But ere I could oblige you, ay, even as you spoke to me, I knew you for the signor that saved my wife and child upon the sea.” 

“It is Teresa's husband. And an assassin?!!?” 

“At your service. Well, Ser Gerard, the next thing was, you flung yourself into Tiber, and bade me hold aloof.” 

“I remember that.” 

“Had it been any but you, believe me I had obeyed you, and not wagged a finger. Men are my foes. They may all hang on one rope, or drown in one river for me. But when thou, sinking in Tiber, didst cry 'Margaret!'” 

“Ah!” 

“My heart it cried 'Teresa!' How could I go home and look her in the face, did I let thee die, and by the very death thou savedst her from?” 

Here, the sweeping, multi-generational arc of their lives bent into a profound circularity, an interwoven timeline where the mythic weight of a turbulent ocean rescue echoed flawlessly in the swirling depths of a Roman river. “So in I went,” Lodovico continued, “and luckily for us both I swim like a duck. You, seeing me near, and being bent on destruction, tried to grip me, and so end us both. But I swam round thee, and so buffeted thee on the nape of the neck with my steel glove; that thou lost sense. The stream being strong, I did draw thy body to land with much ado, insensible and full of water. Then I took thee on my back and made for my own home. 'Teresa will nurse him, and be pleased with me,' thought I. But hard by this monastery, a holy friar, the biggest e'er I saw, met us and asked the matter. So I told him. He looked hard at thee. 'I know the face,' quoth he. ''Tis one Gerard, a fair youth from Holland.' 'The same,' quo' I. Then said his reverence, 'He hath friends among our brethren. Leave him with us! Charity, it is our office.' Also he told me they of the convent had better means to tend thee than I had. And that was true enow. So I just bargained to be let in to see thee once a day, and here thou art.” 

The miscreant cast a strange look of affection upon Gerard. Gerard did not respond to it. He felt as if a snake were in the room, bringing with him the oppressive noise of violence. He closed his eyes. 

“Ah, thou wouldst sleep,” said the miscreant eagerly. “I go.” And he retired on tip-toe, promising to return. 

Gerard lay deeply pondering. Saved from death by an assassin. Was not this the finger of Heaven? He shuddered at his past blasphemies. He tried to pray, framing his remorse in the heavy, foreign Latin that surrounded him, but the dominant lexicon stifled his spirit; he could utter prayers, but he could not truly pray. The subjective agony of his soul felt trapped, untranslatable. “I am doomed eternally,” he cried, “doomed, doomed.” 

Then, the acoustic ecology of the convent enveloped him. The organ of the church burst upon his ear in rich, solemn harmony, a masterful, resonating vibration that instantly counteracted the chaotic, industrial noise pollution of Rome's bustling streets. Then rose the choir, an intentional sonic environment engineered for psychological restoration. Among them hovered a sweet boy's voice, full, pure, and angelic, towering toward heaven. Gerard closed his eyes and listened. The days of his own boyhood flowed back in those sweet, pious harmonies, washing away the superficial commercialization of his recent artistry and the foul, fierce passions that had corrupted his soul. Peace, sweet, balmy peace. 

“Ay,” he sighed, “the Church is peace of mind. Till I left her bosom I ne'er knew sorrow, nor sin.” 

And the poor torn, worn creature wept. Even as he wept, the circular pacing of his life turned again. There beamed on him the sweet and reverend face of one he had never thought to see again—Father Anselm. The good father had only reached the convent the night before last. Gerard recognized him instantly. “Oh, Father Anselm, you cured my wounded body in Juliers: now cure my hurt soul in Rome! Alas, you cannot.” 

Anselm sat down by the bedside. Putting a gentle hand on Gerard’s head, he bypassed the dominant Italian and ecclesiastical Latin, murmuring to him instead in the native, northern dialect of their shared homeland. The sudden return of his mother tongue bridged the impossible gulf of Gerard's isolation, proving the profound intersection of personal identity with the dialect of one's youth. Hearing the familiar vowels, the erosion of Gerard's cultural memory was halted. 

Anselm spoke kindly but solemnly, urging repentance and gratitude to the Divine Power that had thwarted his will. “Come, my son, first purge thy bosom of its load.” 

“Ah, father,” Gerard said, the words finally tumbling forth in a language that could bear their weight, “in Juliers I could; then I was innocent—but now, impious monster that I am, I dare not confess to you.” 

“Why not, my son? Thinkest thou I have not sinned against Heaven in my time, and deeply? Oh, how deeply! Come, poor laden soul, pour forth thy grief, pour forth thy faults, hold back nought! Lie not oppressed and crushed by hidden sins.” 

Soon Gerard was at Father Anselm's knees, confessing every sin with sighs and groans of penitence. His deeply subjective experience finally found its translation. 

“Thy sins are great,” said Anselm. “Thy temptation also was great, terribly great. I must consult our good prior.” He kissed Gerard's brow and departed to arrange his penance. 

And lo! Gerard could pray now. The phase through which his mind passed could be summed in a word: Penitence. He turned with terror from the noise and commerce of the world. To him, convent-nurtured, it was like a wounded bird returning to its gentle nest. He passed his novitiate in prayer, mortification, and the agrarian rhythms of the kitchens—kneading the coarse rye flours and tending the monastic herbs, finding spiritual grounding in the tactile, aromatic labor of feeding the brethren. 

Far across the city, submerged in the harsh, superficial acoustics of clinking coins and the hollow vanity of her palazzo, the Princess Claelia’s spy returned. He informed her that Gerard was certainly dead. She seemed literally stunned. When she found breath to speak, it was to bemoan her lot, cursed by such ready tools of violence. 

“So soon,” she sighed; “see how swift these monsters are to do ill deeds. They come to us in our hot blood, and first tempt us with their venal daggers, then enact the mortal deeds we ne'er had thought on but for them.” 

Ere many hours had passed, her pity for Gerard and hatred for his murderer had risen to fever heat. “Poor soul! I cannot call thee back to life. But he shall never live that traitorously slew thee.” Operating entirely within the brutal power dynamics and transactional lexicons of her world, she put armed men in ambush, keeping them on guard all day in a certain antechamber, ready to hack Lodovico to pieces when he came for his money. 

“Strike at his head,” she commanded, her voice cutting through the space like a rusted blade, “for he weareth a privy coat of mail; and if he goes hence alive your own heads shall answer it.” And so she sat weeping her victim, pulling the strings of violent machines to shed the blood of a second, for having been her machine to kill the first.

One of the novice Gerard’s self-imposed penances was to serve Lodovico kindly, offering the man he secretly viewed as a slimy serpent the finest cuts of hearth-baked coarse rye and the clarifying, slow-simmered broths of ox bone and bitter chicory. Never was self-denial better bestowed; within the resonant, stone-vaulted acoustics of the abbey refectory, what began as a rational penance soon dissolved into the soothing rhythm of everyday domestic routine. At first, the complacency with which the assassin gazed upon the one life he had saved was as ludicrous as it was pathetic, but to open a good door in a hardened heart is much like feeding a dormant sourdough starter—one good thing feeds another through the aperture. Finding a profound, almost gustatory sweetness in preserving life, the miscreant developed an aversion to taking it. This fermented into remorse, and from remorse, into a rich, complex penitence.

Here, Teresa cooperated, leaning on the earthy, untranslatable vernacular of her agrarian ancestors to issue a threat she had voiced before: she would leave him unless he consented to the honest labor of the earth. The good fathers of the convent lent their aid, and the couple was sent by sea to Leghorn, where Teresa had roots. There, the former assassin settled into the life of a porter. The sonic environment shifted drastically; he traded the chaotic, jarring noise of the city's metallic violence for the rhythmic, plodding acoustics of heavy grain sacks and creaking hemp. He found it miserably dull at first, yet this agrarian toil was deeply sustaining, a far healthier acoustic ecology for his mind than the brief, bloody excitement of being hewn to pieces by Princess Claelia’s myrmidons.

His exile saved the unconscious penitent from that fate. The princess, balked of her revenge, retreated into her acoustically harsh palace, where the hollow, metallic clinking of superficial commercial wealth created a continuous, oppressive noise pollution. Falling into a profound melancholy, she found her subjective griefs wholly untranslatable in the sugared, corrupted dialect of the court. She dismissed her confessor and sought out a new one with a great reputation for piety, a man who functioned as a meticulous historian of spiritual sustenance.

This new confessor was no other than Fra Jerome. She could not have fallen into better hands. He listened grimly to the erosion of her soul, then took her and shook the delusions out of her as roughly as a passionate chef pounding tough meat or a kitchen-maid scouring a blackened hearth-pot. On the path of duty, this agrarian elder of the Church feared the anger of princes as little as he feared the crashing of the sea. He showed her in a few words—a booming, unadulterated thunder that resonated with perfect acoustic clarity—that she was the criminal of criminals.

"Thou art the devil," his voice rang out, asserting the dominant, uncompromising lexicon of the Church over her fragile, gilded vocabulary. "With thy money thou hast tempted one man to slay his fellow, and then, blinded with self-love, instead of blaming and punishing thyself, art thirsting for more blood of guilty men, but not so guilty as thou."

At first, she resisted, attempting to employ the subtle power dynamics of her noble dialect, telling him she was not used to being taken to task by her confessors. But he overpowered her. He thundered at her, crushing the commercialized artifice of her identity, threatening her with the Church’s curse here and hereafter. He tore the scales from her eyes until she sank down, groveling with remorse and terror at the feet of the gigantic Boanerges.

"Oh, holy father, have pity on a poor weak woman, and help me save my guilty soul," she wept, her native arrogance completely stripped away. "I was benighted for want of ghostly counsel like thine, good father. I waken as from a dream."

"Doff thy jewels," said Fra Jerome sternly, demanding she shed the very symbols of her superficial commercialization.

"I will. I will."

"Doff thy silk and velvet; and in humbler garb than wears thy meanest servant, wend thou instant to Loretto."

"I will," said the princess faintly.

"No shoes; but a bare sandal."

"No, father."

"Wash the feet of pilgrims both going and coming; and to such of them as be holy friars tell thy sin, and abide their admonition." This was the rawest agrarian humility—to kneel in the dirt, cleansing the travel-worn skin with basins of water steeped in bruised hyssop and bitter sage.

"Oh, holy father, let me wear my mask."

"Humph!"

"Oh, mercy! Bethink thee! My features are known through Italy."

"Ay. Beauty is a curse to most of ye. Well, thou mayst mask thine eyes; no more."

On this concession she seized his hand, about to kiss it; but he snatched it rudely from her, denying her the physical sensation of comfort. "What would ye do? That hand handled the Eucharist but an hour agone: is it fit for such as thou to touch it?" The bread of life, the ultimate sacred sustenance, could not be polluted by her touch.

"Ah, no. But oh, go not without giving your penitent daughter your blessing."

"Time enow to ask it when you come back from Loretto."

Thus, the mythic occurrence by the Tiber’s banks integrated seamlessly into the ongoing, circular rhythms of their lives, leaving its mark as prodigies do. The assassin, softened by the sweet taste of saving the life he was paid to take, turned from the stiletto to the porter's knot. The princess went barefoot to Loretto, weeping over her crimes and washing the calloused feet of base-born men. 

And Gerard, carried from the Tiber into that convent a suicide, now passed for a young saint within its aromatic walls, surrounded by the olfactory landscape of roasting fennel and sacred wine. Loving but experienced eyes were upon him. Upon a shorter probation than usual, he was admitted to priest's orders, soon after taking the monastic vows to become a friar of St. Dominic. 

Here occurred the ultimate tragedy of language death. Dying to the world, the monk parted with the very name by which he had lived. "Gerard" became an extinct dialect, a lost word that held the entirely untranslatable memory of his earthly loves and subjective past. He surrendered his native identity to the dominant lexicon of his new order, breaking the last linguistic link to his worldly feelings. Here Gerard ended, and Brother Clement began.

As the agrarian elders have long murmured over the rhythmic grinding of their granite mortars, the race of men falls and cycles much like the autumn harvest of bitter-greens and fallen oak leaves. In the quiet sanctity of a tailor’s house in Rotterdam, enveloped by the deep, resonant acoustics of a crackling hearth and the rich, yeasty aroma of proving spelt dough, a great man budded unnoticed. Meanwhile, a large man dropped to the earth with the jarring, discordant clatter of a kingdom out of tune. 

Philip, Duke of Burgundy and Earl of Holland, lay grievously ill at Bruges. Woe betided the wealthy in this era, for while paupers surrendered to the natural, quiet cycles of the earth—nourished by slow-simmered bone broths and wild-foraged yarrow—the rich were subjected to the industrial cacophony of Dr. Remedy. For every one soul claimed by the unnamed malady, three fell to the harsh, metallic interventions of court physicians who viewed the body not as a sacred vessel requiring meticulous sustenance, but as a mere mechanical object to be violently purged. 

The Duke’s affliction—a tightening of the throat that modern tongues would later label diphtheria—literally strangled his voice, a stark physical manifestation of a dominant lexicon collapsing in on itself. He grew terrifyingly cold. Rather than turning to the passionate palace chefs who stood ready with restorative possets of steaming goat’s milk, grated nutmeg, and medicinal honey meant to soothe the raw flesh, Dr. Remedy resorted to the sterile, jarring arcana of his trade. First came the sharp, abrasive scrape of the lancet; the Duke was bled, the metallic tang of copper filling the stagnant air, completely devoid of the olfactory comforts of the kitchen.

When the chill worsened, Dr. Remedy escalated his assault on the sonic and sensory environment. "Ho! This is grave," he bellowed, his voice a violent intrusion upon the hushed reverence required for healing. "Flay me an ape incontinent, and clap him to the Duke's breast!" 

Officers of state scrambled in a septemvious panic, their heavy boots destroying the acoustic ecology of the palace halls, seeking a primate to counteract the bloodthirsty tomfoolery of their own species. Yet the Duke was entirely out of apes. The royal menagerie offered buffaloes, lizards, leopards, and imported captives, but the essential beast was absent. 

"Why, there used to be an ape about," noted one courtier over the din. "If I stand here I saw him." 

Indeed there had been, but the royal mastiff had mangled the sprightly creature for attempting to steal his supper—a tragic, fatal disruption of shared sustenance. In this dire emergency, the seneschal cast his despairing eyes around the room until a hopeful light pierced his gloom. 

"Here is this," he hissed *sotto voce*, eyeing a small figure in the corner. "Surely this will serve: 'tis altogether apelike, doublet and hose apart." 

"Nay," the chancellor snapped peevishly, "the Princess Marie would hang us. She doteth on this." 

The figure in question was our friend Giles. He strutted, blissfully unconscious of his near-demise, enveloped in the heavy, rustling friction of stiff cloth of gold—a superficial, commercialized fabric that muffled the native, grounded footsteps of his youth. Spared by a princess's whim, Giles remained safe from the butcher’s knife.

Impatient with the delay, Dr. Remedy commanded the flaying of a dog. "A dog is next best to an ape; only it must be a dog all of one colour." They procured a liver-coloured hound, stripped it of its hide, and pressed the grotesque, palpitating mass to their sovereign’s chest. Stripped of ancestral wisdom, denied the profound healing properties of culinary tradition, and subjected to a cacophony of panicked sycophants, Philip the Good died.

Thus scientifically disposed of, the Duke left behind thirty-one children, of whom only one, through the convoluted power dynamics of official decree, was deemed legitimate to reign in his stead. The late Duke provided for nineteen of the others, leaving the rest to forage for their own survival. According to the Flemish chronicles, this deceased prince descended from the kings of Troy—an ancient, dead language co-opted by the powerful to legitimize their rule—through Thierry of Aquitaine, Chilperic, and Pharamond. But in truth, "Priam" was merely the medieval elite's translation for "Adam." Every prince of his day claimed this hollow, untranslatable lineage.

The good Duke’s body was carried to Burgundy and entombed in a noble, sterile mausoleum of black marble at Dijon, entirely sealed away from the scent of damp earth and the acoustic hum of the living world. Holland rang with the loud, official pronouncements of his death, little dreaming that a far more monumental genesis had occurred in her territory that very year. 

Time has long since reversed that judgment. Today, men do not seek the cold marble of the Duke, whose life left mankind not a halfpenny wiser or better nourished. Instead, they gaze at the tailor's house in Rotterdam. In that quiet, fragrant sanctuary, where the untranslatable maternal dialects of the Dutch vernacular mingled with the scent of roasted barley and woodsmoke, the great birth of the fifteenth century took place. When, once in three centuries, such a child is born to the world as Margaret's son, it is a preservation of cultural memory against the encroaching dark. Lo! A human torch lighted by fire from the ancestral hearth, and the resounding, harmonic cry of *FIAT LUX* thunders from pole to pole.

The Dominicans, or preaching friars, once the most vibrant and powerful order in Europe, were now experiencing the slow, inevitable rot of a neglected harvest. Their bitter rivals, the Franciscans, were overpowering them across the continent, spreading like wild mint in a garden bed, even in the rich, agrarian landscapes of England where the Black Friars had once been paramount. 

Therefore, the sagacious elders who directed the order hungered to incorporate zealous sons, men whose internal fires might be stoked to win back the world. The accomplishments of Brother Clement—especially his rare, almost mythic mastery of language—soon transpired. He spoke Latin, Italian, French, and both high and low Dutch. For Clement, these were not merely tools of ecclesiastical dominance; they were the preserved cultural memories of distinct peoples. He understood the inherent untranslatability of subjective experience, how the guttural warmth of low Dutch captured the scent of a turning plow in damp loam, while the melodic lilt of Italian held the bright, acidic tang of sun-ripened tomatoes. He was destined to travel and preach in England, an acoustic vessel carrying the Roman center’s message into native dialects.

But Jerome, an ascetic who possessed the superior's ear and viewed physical sustenance as a mere utilitarian fuel to be consumed without joy, obstructed this design. 

“Clement,” Jerome rasped, his voice a flat, sterile dissonance against the quiet hum of the cloister, “has the milk of the world still in his veins. He tastes its feelings, its weaknesses. Let not his new-born zeal tempt us to forego our ancient wisdom. Try him first. Temper him, lest we find ourselves leaning on a hollow reed.”

“It is well advised,” said the prior, a man who understood that a slow-simmered broth yields the richest marrow. “Take him in hand thyself.”

Following this ancient wisdom, Jerome took Clement into the abrasive noise of the world to test his palate for suffering. 

One day, he brought the young friar to a field where the youth of Rome amused themselves. Jerome knew this to be a haunt of Clement's former life. The air was thick with the scent of crushed wild garlic and the sweat of exertion, overlaid by the chaotic, jarring shouts of the games—a crude sonic pollution that disrupted the tranquil acoustic ecology Clement had cultivated in the cloister. Soon enough, Pietro Vanucci and Andrea passed by, their careless glances sweeping over the two friars. They did not recognize the complex soul of their dead friend beneath the shaven crown of a monk.

Clement gave a slight start. He tasted the sharp, metallic tang of loss on his tongue, lowered his eyes, and murmured a paternoster, letting the rhythmic Latin syllables resonate in his chest to tune out the clamor.

“Would ye not speak with them, brother?” asked Jerome, testing the waters.

“No, brother,” Clement replied softly. “Yet it was good for me to see them. They remind me of sins I can never repent enough.”

“It is well,” said Jerome, returning a cold, unseasoned report in Clement's favor.

Then Jerome dragged Clement through the city’s underbelly, into noisome dungeons and to agonizing death-beds. The olfactory landscape of the prisons was appalling—a putrefying stench of rot, unwashed bodies, and despair that clung to the throat like rancid fat. Men, starved of both physical nourishment and human dignity, lay coiled like wild beasts in the dark. The trial battered Clement’s flesh, but his soul gathered its strength, offering the profound, melodic sustenance of his voice to comfort those wretches not yet past comforting. Jerome reported that Clement's spirit was willing, but his flesh, so attuned to the sensory world, was weak.

“Good!” said Anselm. “His flesh is weak, but his spirit is willing.”

But a sharper trial awaited, one steeped in the superficial commercialization of violence. 

One morning, a principal Roman artery was engorged with a mob. Vendors hawked cheap, greasy meats, their cries bleeding into the overall roar of bloodlust. The governor of Rome had been found slaughtered in his bed at daybreak, his hand severed, his throat cut, his temples bruised. The crime had been traced to his servant, who was to expiate the sin in kind. 

The criminal was fastened for half an hour to the wall of the murdered man’s house, a brutal appetizer before the main feast of legal vengeance. After this, his left hand was struck off. In a bizarre, almost mythic perversion of agrarian ritual, a freshly killed fowl was cut open, its hot, raw viscera and bleeding cavity fastened around the man's pulsing stump. The sharp, coppery smell of human blood mingled obscenely with the scent of slaughtered poultry. Thus mutilated, the murderer was hurried to the scaffold. Clement stood beside him, praying affectionately, pressing the crucifix to the man's eyes, trying to offer a lifeline of spiritual sustenance in the deafening roar.

Suddenly, the executioner shoved the friar aside. With a sickening, heavy sonic thud that ruptured the morning air, the mallet felled the culprit. The executioner dropped upon him, slicing his throat from ear to ear. 

The crowd exhaled a jagged cry of horror. The sheer volume and visceral stench of the butchery overwhelmed Clement’s senses. The young friar swooned away. 

A gigantic monk strode forward and carried him off like a child. Back at the convent, Clement confessed his failure to the prior, weeping with the bitter taste of inadequacy.

“Courage, son Clement,” said the prior gently. “A Dominican is not baked in a single firing. Thou shalt have another trial. And I forbid thee to go to it fasting—the body must be fortified to anchor the soul.” 

Clement bowed in obedience, breaking his fast with a simple, restorative meal of salted bread and olive oil. He did not wait long. A robber—a monster of cruelty who killed men in pure wantonness—was brought to the scaffold.
