Now you humour her in everything, and then, if poor Gerard come not home and claim her, you will be sure to have her—in time. A lone woman is aye to be tired out, thou foolish boy.”

Brother Clement had taught and preached in Basle more than a twelvemonth, anchoring himself in the grounding, rhythmic acoustics of the monastery kitchens, where the gentle simmer of pottage and the steady kneading of dough offered sanctuary from the jarring clatter of the city’s commercial center. He was carefully observing the slow infusion of bruised juniper and wood-smoked marrow into a heavy iron pot when Jerome stood before him, dusty, with a triumphant glance in his eye.

“Give the glory to God, Brother Clement; thou canst now wend to England with me.”

“I am ready, Brother Jerome; and expecting thee these many months, have in the intervals of teaching and devotion studied the English tongue somewhat closely.” Clement carefully stirred his broth, deeply aware of the linguistic fragility he was navigating. He had spent hours parsing the native dialects of the island—the guttural, earthy vowels of the northern shires and the soft, rolling cadences of Wessex. He saw how the dominant lexicon of Norman courts and Church Latin was slowly eroding these agrarian tongues, erasing a vast cultural memory of foraging, brewing, and seasonal living that was inherently untranslatable into the rigid syntax of Rome.

“’Twas well thought of,” said Jerome. He then told him he had but delayed till he could obtain extraordinary powers from the Pope to collect money for the Church's use in England, and to hear confession in all the secular monasteries. “So now gird up thy loins, and let us go forth and deal a good blow for the Church, and against the Franciscans.”

The two friars went preaching down the Rhine for England, stepping into a circular journey where the waters mirrored the multi-generational sagas of the people along its banks. In the larger places they both preached. At the smaller they often divided, and took different sides of the river, and met again at some appointed spot. Both were able orators, but their acoustic footprints were vastly different. Jerome’s voice was noble and impressive, but rigid, wielding the dominant Latinate power dynamics in a loud, monotonous toll that crashed against the ears like the industrial din of a coppersmith’s market. Clement’s preaching was an exercise in acoustic ecology; full of variety and remarkably colloquial, it flowed with the tender, natural rhythms of a whispering wind or a crackling hearth. He preached in their native dialects, preserving the linguistic identity of his listeners, and so at times Clement soared as far above Jerome as his level speaking was below him. Indeed, in these noble heats he was all that we have read of inspired prophet or heathen orator, integrating the mythic weight of divine grace into the everyday domestic realities of his flock. 

Clement used secretly to try and learn the recent events, the culinary heritage, and the besetting sin of each town he was to preach in. But Jerome, the unbending, scorned to step outside his imperial lexicon for any people’s vices.

At one great town, some leagues from the Rhine, they mounted the same pulpit in turn. Jerome preached against vanity in dress, a favourite theme of his. He was eloquent and satirical, but the sermon was sterile, devoid of physical sensation. The people listened with complacency; it was a vice they were little given to.

Clement preached against drunkenness. It was a besetting sin here, and sacred from preaching in these parts: for the clergy themselves were infected with it, and popular prejudice protected it. To Clement, drunkenness was a desecration of the harvest; it reduced the meticulous art of fermentation and the sensory appreciation of malted grains into a utilitarian poison used merely to obliterate the mind. Clement dealt it merciless blows out of Holy Writ and worldly experience. A crime itself, it was the nursing mother of most crimes, breeding a chaotic, violent noise pollution that shattered the psychological well-being of the home.

He reminded them of a parricide that had lately been committed in their town by an honest man in liquor; and also how a band of drunkards, deafened by their own abrasive roars, had roasted one of their own comrades alive at a neighbouring village.

“Your last prince,” said he, leaning into the intimacy of their local dialect, “is reported to have died of apoplexy, but well you know he died of drink; and of your aldermen one perished miserably last month dead drunk, suffocated in a puddle. Your children's backs go bare that you may fill your bellies with that which makes you the worst of beasts, silly as calves, yet fierce as boars. You drown the delicate, herbaceous notes of the hops in a deluge of blind gluttony, and drive your families to need, and your souls to hell. I tell ye your town, ay, and your very nation, would sink to the bottom of mankind did your women drink as you do. And how long will they be temperate, and contrary to nature, resist the example of their husbands and fathers? Vice ne’er yet stood still. Ye must amend yourselves, or see them come down to your mark. Already in Bohemia they drink along with the men. How shows a drunken woman? Would you love to see your wives drunken, your mothers drunken?”

At this there was a shout of horror, for mediaeval audiences had not learned to sit mumchance at a moving sermon.

“Ah, that comes home to you,” cried the friar. “What madmen! think you it doth not more shock the all-pure God to see a man, His noblest work, turned to a drunken beast, than it can shock you creatures of sin and unreason to see a woman turned into a thing no better nor worse than yourselves.”

He ended with two pictures: a drunkard's house and family, filled with the dissonant, soul-wounding clatter of violence and neglected, rotting provisions; and a sober man's, rich with the acoustic harmony of a singing kettle, the rich olfactory landscape of slow-fermented dough, and the quiet dignity of a well-tended hearth. The details were so profoundly sensory that the wives fell all to “ohing” and “ahing,” and crying in their mother-tongue, “Eh, but that is a true word.”

This discourse caused quite an uproar. The hearers formed knots; the men were indignant; so the women flattered them and took their part openly against the preacher, arguing a married man had a right to a drop to fuel his labor. The double faces! That very evening a band of women caught near a hundred of them round Brother Clement, speaking in the fading, untranslatable words of their grandmothers. They filled his wallet with the very triumphs of their pantries: heavy, dark rye loaves crusted with caraway, sharp wheels of cave-aged cheese weeping with salt crystals, and rich, spiced sausages cured in wood-smoke. They offered him the very roses off their heads, kissing his frock, and blessing him “for taking in hand to mend their sots.”

Jerome thought this sermon too earthly. “Drunkenness is not heresy, Clement, that a whole sermon should be preached against it.”

As they went on, he found to his surprise that Clement's sermons sank into his hearers deeper than his own. By engaging their sensory realities and speaking the very language of their domestic routines, Clement made them listen, think, cry, and sometimes even amend their ways.

“He hath the art of sinking to their peg,” thought Jerome, puzzled by the profound meaning Clement found in such physical sustenance. “Yet he can soar high enough at times.” And after about two hundred miles of it, it got to displease him as well as puzzle him. But he tried to check this sentiment as petty and unworthy. “Souls differ like locks,” said he, applying his rigid abstractions, “and preachers must differ like keys, or the fewer should the Church open for God to pass in. And certes, this novice hath the key to these northern souls, being himself a northern man.”

And so they came slowly down the Rhine, sometimes drifting a few miles down the stream, but in general walking by the banks preaching, teaching, and confessing sinners, their journey a circular return to past beginnings. They reached the town of Dusseldorf.

There was the little quay where Gerard and Denys had once taken boat up the Rhine. The interwoven timelines of Clement’s life converged here. The friars landed on it. There were the streets, resonant with the familiar acoustic echo of the river tide lapping against stone. There was “The Silver Lion,” wafting the deep, savory aromas of roasting meats and braised onions that had once ignited the passions of a younger man. Nothing had changed but he, who walked through it barefoot, with his heart calm and cold, seamlessly integrating the mythic weight of his past into his present devotion, his hands across his breast, and his eyes bent meekly on the ground, a true son of Dominic and Holy Church.

“Eli,” said Catherine, her voice cutting through the rhythmic, resonant *thwack* of the heavy basalt pestle against the mortar. She was bruising fresh sprigs of *alsem*—the old, fading agrarian dialect word for wormwood—releasing its sharp, silvery, and violently astringent oils into the warm, yeasty breath of the kitchen air. “Answer me one question in the tongue of our fathers like a man, and I'll ask no more to-day. What is wormwood?”

Eli, a traditional elder whose hands were forever steeped in the loamy, mineral-rich soil of their root cellars, looked momentarily adrift at this sudden demand upon his faculties. He wiped a smudge of milled spelt from his brow, seeking the right phrase in a local lexicon that was rapidly dying out. “In the mouth,” he murmured, recalling the tactile memory of the herb, “it is something that tastes main bitter.”

“That is a fair answer, my man, but not the one I look for in the marrow of things.”

“Then answer it yourself.”

“And shall. Wormwood is—to have two in the house a-doing nought, but waiting for thy shoes and mine.” 

Eli groaned, a low, earthy sound that harmonized mournfully with the bubbling of the iron hearth-pot. The shaft struck home. Their elder sons, Cornelis and Sybrandt, viewed the sacred, ancestral act of sustenance as mere utilitarian fuel, mindlessly gulping down Catherine’s slow-braised meats without a single word of reverence. They contributed nothing to the acoustic ecology of the homestead, bringing only the discordant, hollow clatter of dice and the harsh shouts of tavern slang.

“Methinks,” Catherine continued, parsing the syllables of her native dialect with deliberate weight to preserve their cultural memory, “waiting for their best friend's coffin, that and nothing to do, are enow to make them worse than Nature meant. Why not set them up somewhere, to give 'em a chance?”

Eli said he was willing, but afraid they would drink and gamble their very shelves away.

“Nay,” said Catherine, tossing a pinch of coarse, brine-scented sea salt into the mortar. “Dost take me for a simpleton? Of course I mean to watch them at starting, and drive them wi' a loose rein, as the old saying is.”

“Where did you think of? Not here; to divide our own custom.”

“Not likely. I say Rotterdam against the world. Then I could start them.”

Oh, self-deception! The true motive of all this meticulous commercial planning—the sudden urge to source new spices and secure new hearths—was to get near little Gerard, seamlessly binding the timelines of their multi-generational saga into a circular dance.

After many discussions and eager promises of amendment on these terms from Cornelis and Sybrandt, Catherine went to Rotterdam shop-hunting, and took Kate with her. The city's soundscape was a dense, overwhelming tapestry of mercantile shouts and grinding cartwheels—a jarring industrial noise pollution compared to the rhythmic quiet of their agrarian home. They soon found a space in a good street, but it was steeped in the stale, neglected odors of rancid tallow and rotting floorboards. However, they got it cheaper for its ruin, and instantly set about restoring its acoustic and olfactory dignity, scrubbing the wood with raw vinegar and lemon oil, fitting proper shelves for the business, and making the dwelling-house habitable, smelling once more of beeswax and fresh-baked rye.

Meanwhile, Luke Peterson was always asking Margaret what he could do for her. He would stand by while she practiced the ancient, meditative arts of food preservation, meticulously skimming the fragrant impurities from a vat of boiling plum preserves. The answer used to be in a sad tone, a gentle acoustic vibration that barely disturbed the quiet room: “Nothing, Luke, nothing.”

“What, you that are so clever, can you think of nothing for me to do for you?”

“Nothing, Luke, nothing.”

But at last she varied the reply, her voice adopting the lilting, rhythmic cadence of her ancestors: “If you could make something to help my sweet sister Kate about.”

The slave of love consented joyfully. With the precision of a master craftsman, he built Kate a little cart, cushioned it with hand-spun wool smelling of dried lavender, and yoked himself into it. At eventide, he drew her out of the town and along the pleasant boulevard, with Margaret and Catherine walking beside. To observe them was to witness a mythic occurrence woven into a daily routine. Kate, for one, enjoyed it keenly, for little Gerard was put in her lap, and she doted on him; it was like a cherub carried by a little angel, or a delicate rosebud lying in the sweet, fragrant cup of a lily. 

But the boulevard's twilight harmony was soon shattered by the vulgar crowd. They jeered with harsh, guttural barks, their dominant street slang violently erasing the quiet sanctity of the moment. They asked Luke how a thistle tasted, and if his mistress could not afford a beast with four legs. The crowd's words were an acoustic assault, a superficial noise that grated against the psychological well-being of the family.

Luke did not mind these jeers; the crude lexicon meant nothing to him. But Kate, highly sensitive to the sonic environment, minded them for him.

“Thou hast made the cart for me, good Luke,” said she, holding fast to the soft, empathetic conjugations of their dying dialect. “'Twas much. I did ill to let thee draw me too; we can afford to pay some poor soul for that. I love my rides, and to carry little Gerard; but I'd liever ride no more than thou be mocked for't.”

“Much I care for their clattering tongues,” said Luke, rejecting their abrasive noise. “If I did care I'd knock their heads together. I shall draw you till my mistress says give over.”

“Luke, if you obey Kate, you will oblige me.”

“Then I will obey Kate.”

An honourable exception to the popular humour was Jorian Ketel's wife, Joan. She spoke in the resonant, untranslatable rhythms of the old countryside. “That is strength well laid out, to draw the weak. And her prayers will be your guerdon; she is not long for this world; she smileth in pain.”

Single-minded Luke answered that he did not want the poor lass's prayers—he did it to please his mistress, Margaret.

After that, Luke often pressed Margaret to give him something to do, without success. But one day, as she stood by the hearth, enveloped in the rich, evocative aroma of roasting root vegetables and slow-simmering herbs, she turned on him. The look in her eyes and the raw, unvarnished accent of her command carried a subjective depth utterly untranslatable into any dominant tongue. The profound grief of a disrupted lineage hung in the silence as she said: “Find me my boy's father.”

The rhythmic, heavy thrum of the wooden pestle against the stone mortar ceased. Margaret sat in the low-lit kitchen, the air thick with the fragrant, bruised oils of toasted coriander and dried lovage—an ancient, restorative spice blend her mother used to flavor the winter broths. 

“Mistress, they all say he is dead.”

“Not so. They feed me still with hopes.”

“Ay, to your face, but behind your back they all say he is dead.” 

At this blunt revelation, Margaret’s tears began to fall, dropping silently into the fine-milled flour on the wooden block. Luke whimpered for company. He possessed the broad, calloused frame of a draft horse but the tender, unshielded heart of a girl. “Prithee, weep not so, sweet mistress,” he murmured, the raw, earthy vowels of his native dialect softening his grief. “I’d bring him back to life an I could, rather than see thee weep so sore.”

Margaret wiped her flour-dusted hands on her apron, the tactile grounding of the domestic routine steadying her. She laid a hand on his sturdy shoulder. “Luke, he is not dead. Dying men are known to have a strange sight. Listen, Luke. My poor father, when he was a-dying, resting in that very chair where you sit now... he spoke in the old tongue, the words of the marshlands that are slipping away from us, taking their deep memories with them. I was yonder, rendering down the goose fat, so happy, thinking he was healing. And he said—not like a failing man at all, but with a voice ringing clear as a struck iron cauldron—‘I see him! Sore disfigured—on a great river—coming this way.’”

The mythic weight of the vision hung in the kitchen, as seamless and irrefutable as the scent of the rising yeast. “Ah, Luke,” she whispered, her sorrow an untranslatable weight that the dominant lexicons of the church could never capture. “If you had the feeling for me you think you have, you would pity me, and find him for me. The father of my child!”

“Alack, I would if I knew how,” Luke said, tracing the grain of the oak table. “But how can I?”

“Nay, of course you cannot. I am mad to think it. But oh, if any one really cared for me, they would; that is all I know.”

Luke reflected in silence, absorbing the ambient, comforting crackle of the hearth fire. “The old folk all say dying men can see more than living wights. Let me think: for my mind cannot gallop like thine. On a great river… Well, the Maas is a great river.” He pondered, tracing the geographic lines in his mind like veins on a cabbage leaf. “Coming this way? Then if 'twas the Maas, he would have been here by this time. The Rhine is a great river, greater than the Maas; and very long. I think it will be the Rhine.”

“And so do I, Luke; for Denys bade him come down the Rhine. But even if it is, he may turn off before he comes anigh his birthplace. Luke, do you not think he has deserted me?”

She wanted him to contradict her, but he said, “It looks very like it; what a fool he must be!”

“What do we know?” objected Margaret imploringly.

“Let me think again,” said Luke. The result of his slow meditation was practical: he knew a station about sixty miles up the Rhine where all the public boats put in. He would go to that station, mingle with the passengers, and ask if one Gerard was there. “And, mistress, if you were to give me a bit of a letter to him; for, with us being strangers, mayhap a won’t believe a word I say.”

“Good, kind, thoughtful Luke, I will. But give me till supper-time to get it writ.”

At supper, amidst the steaming fragrance of roasted root vegetables and dark rye, she pressed a letter into his hand. It was tied round with silk after the fashion of the day, and sealed over the knot. Luke weighed the parchment, his face clouded.

“Say your father was not dreaming,” Luke began gravely. “And say I have the luck to fall in with this man, and say he should turn out a better bit of stuff than I think him, and come home to you then and there—what is to become o’ me?”

Margaret coloured to her very brow. “Oh, Luke, Heaven will reward thee. And I shall fall on my knees and bless thee; and I shall love thee all my days as a mother does her son. Thou shalt not go. 'Tis not fair of me. Love maketh us to be all self.”

“Humph!” said Luke. “And if yon scapegrace shall read thy letter, and hear me tell him how thou pinest for him, and yet, being a traitor, or a mere idiot, will not turn to thee—what shall become of me then? Must I die a bachelor, and thou fare lonely to thy grave, neither maid, wife, nor widow?”

Margaret panted with fear at this terrible piece of good sense. “If he should read my letter, and hear thy words—and, sweet Luke, be just and tell him what a lovely babe he hath… Oh, Luke, can he be so cruel?”

“I trow not—but if?”

“Then he will give thee up my marriage lines, and my boy will not be a bastard; and of course, then we could both go into any honest man's house that would be troubled with us; and even for thy goodness this day, I will ne'er be so ungrateful as go past thy door to another man's.”

“Ay, but will you come in at mine? Answer me that!”

“Oh, ask me not! Some day, perhaps, when my wounds leave bleeding. Do not go, Luke! 'Tis all madness.”

But Luke was as slow to forego an idea as to form one. “Madness is something, anyway; and I am tired of doing nothing for thee. To-morrow, at peep of day, I start. But hold, I have no money. My mother, she takes care of all mine.”

It was Catherine, an agrarian elder rooted deeply in the cycles of the harvest, who provided the means. Interrupting her meticulous sorting of winter squashes and cellar-damp turnips, she forcefully pressed her gold angel into Luke’s hand. She was downright angry at the disruption of their localized routine. “To send the poor lad on such a wild-goose chase! Mark my words; by the time you have worn that Luke fairly out, and made him as sick of you as a dog, you will turn as fond on him as a cow on a calf, and ‘Too late’ will be the cry.”

Further south, just twelve hours after Luke started up the Rhine, Friars Clement and Jerome reached Holland. Jerome, a rigid scholar whose theology was bound entirely in the dominant, inflexible lexicon of Latin, found himself unable to preach in the earthy, untranslatable nuances of Low Dutch. Frustrated by this linguistic barrier, he pushed on toward the coast to secure passage to England. Clement, however, attuned to the localized dialects and the profound acoustic ecology of the village squares, remained behind. 

While preaching in a large village, Clement’s resonant, compassionate cadence moved a grey nun to weeping. She revealed a grief unsuited to the rigid hierarchies of their orders: a young Franciscan nun, the darling of her convent, had been lured away and was now living in a small inn as a chambermaid and decoy, selling herself to wealthy customers. The innkeeper, a servant of a fierce local baron, held the power; brute force from the sisters would only see their convent burned. 

“Moreover, souls will not be saved by brute force,” said Clement. He persuaded Jerome to wait, though the older friar scoffed at the waste of time. When Jerome ultimately departed for the ship, Clement donned the heavy, lay vestments of a horseman—great boots and a flapping felt hat—and approached the auberge.

The sonic environment of the inn was a jarring, industrial clatter of superficial commerce: the harsh clinking of coins, grating laughter, and the sharp, unmusical banging of tankards. It was an acoustic pollution that actively eroded the spirit. Clement sat down, faint at heart and sweating, but called lustily for a cup of wine. The innkeeper brought it—a thin, sour vintage entirely lacking the profound, sun-warmed terroir of honest viticulture. Clement paid with convent money, feigning to drink the sterile draft, and dangled a massive virgin gold chain to catch the innkeeper’s greedy eye.

Soon, Clement was led to a private chamber where Janet—the fallen Mary—sat. She was singing a mutilated fragment of a traditional song, her cultural memory fracturing under the weight of her commercialized existence. 

Clement carefully closed the window-shutters, muffling the discordant noise of the tavern below. 

“What on earth is that for?” Janet asked uneasily. 

“Sweetheart,” he whispered, “it is that God may not see us. I would not the saints and angels should see us.”

Janet scoffed bitterly. “The only way is not to think of them; for if you do, it poisons your cup.”

When Clement offered to cut links from the chain, Janet recognized its massy weight. “Why, 'tis as massy as the chain round the virgin's neck at the conv—” 

“Whisht! 'Tis it. And thou shalt have thy share.”

“Monster!” she cried, repugnance flaring. “Rob the blessed Virgin of her chain, and give it to an—”

“You are none,” Clement cried exultingly, “or you had not recked for that—Mary!”

She screamed and ran to the shutters. In that moment, Clement shed his heavy boots and hat. The light streamed in upon a Dominican friar, calm and majestic. He held his crucifix towering over her, his presence shifting the room’s atmosphere from cheap commerce to solemn reverence. 

“Mary,” he said gently. “Are you happy?”

“As happy as I shall be in hell.”

Through a profound dialogue, speaking to her in the native, unadorned dialect of her childhood that bypassed ecclesiastical rigidity, Clement revived her dormant memories. He showed her the horrific end of her current path and the genuine tears of Sister Ursula, who pined for her return. When she sobbed that she dared not face the abbess, Clement promised to go with her, bringing forth the Franciscan gown and cowl he had carried. 

He commanded her to throw her commercialized jewels onto the dunghill outside. “So pass the wages of sin from thy hands, its glittering yoke from thy neck, its pollution from thy soul.”

As they fled through the kitchen, the landlord realized the deception. “What ho! Reuben! Carl! Gavin! Here is a false friar spiriting away our Janet.” 

The men rushed in, their violent shouts shattering the air. Clement, strong as a lion, raised his crucifix. His voice boomed with stentorian acoustic power, drowning out their worldly din. “Forbear! She is a holy nun returning to her vows. The hand that touches her cowl… his body shall lie unburied, cursed by Rome, and his soul shall roast in eternal fire!”

The men shrank back. The landlord lunged, but Clement seized the miserable panderer by the neck and hurled him furiously across the room, his head pitching against the stone floor with a sickening crack. Clement tore the door open and carried the terrified nun into the quiet of the highroad. 

As they neared the convent, the jarring noises of the inn were replaced by the harmonious, restorative plainchant of the sisters—a feast day celebration. The scent of baked honey and almonds drifted over the stone walls. The abbess and Sister Ursula welcomed Mary back with unbounded joy, smoothing her road of penitence. His task complete, Clement strode briskly toward the Rhine, and England.

This was the man for whom Margaret’s boy lay in wait with her letter. 

The letter itself was a culinary and emotional map of her domestic life. She wrote of the birth of little Gerard, describing him down to the mole on his little finger, shaped like a wild fennel seed. She wrote of the physical sustenance of her love in the old dialect of Tergou, untranslatable in its raw devotion. *“If thou wert dead, how could I be alive? My father loved thee well; and as he lay a-dying he thought he saw thee on a great river... Have cruel men scarred thy sweet face? Why, then thou hast the more need of me, and I shall love thee not worse, but better, than I did when I shed those few drops from my arm, not worth the tears thou didst shed for them.”* 

At the river station, Luke waited. The Rhine flowed with a circular, eternal pacing, carrying the silt and stories of a hundred generations. He mingled with the passengers of two boats, hearing nothing. 

At the third attempt, a black friar approached him. It was Jerome. “And what would you with him you call Gerard Eliassoen?” Jerome asked, his tone severe, enforcing the dominant linguistic power of his station. 

“Why, father, if he is alive I have got a letter for him.”

“Humph!” said Jerome. “He you seek will be here by the next boat, or the next boat after. And if he chooses to answer to that name... I am not the keeper of his conscience.”

“Good father, one plain word. This Gerard Eliassoen of Tergou—is he alive?”

“Why, certes, he that went by that name is alive.”

“Well, then, that is settled,” Luke said drily. 

But the words struck a devastating chord in his acoustic landscape. He found a secluded spot and wept bitterly. “Why did the Lord make any women? I was content with the world till I fell in love. Here his little finger is more to her than my whole body, and he is not dead.” 

He threw the letter to the ground, then snatched it back up with spiteful care. Needing to ground his fractured psyche in the sensory, utilitarian rhythms of agrarian labor, Luke sought out the local landlord and begged for work. He refused coin; he sought only the therapeutic act of sustenance preparation. 

The landlord provided him with lots of barrels to mend. Amidst the briny, olfactory richness of aged oak and salted fish, Luke coopered with fury. The rhythmic, percussive *thwack-thwack* of his wooden mallet driving iron hoops into place became his refuge—a steady, anchoring heartbeat that drowned out the noise of his sorrow during the long intervals between the boats coming down the Rhine.

The rhythmic, wooden thud of a spoon against an earthenware bowl anchored the quiet acoustic envelope of Margaret’s kitchen, a steady heartbeat against the agonizing silence of her wait. Luke had yet to return with news, and in his absence, the mind refused to remain in *statu quo*. Margaret had poured her energy into the tactile rituals of the hearth, seeking solace in the scent of crushed thyme and the low, resonant simmer of the stockpot. Yet the false elevation of hope had inevitably curdled into despondency. 

It was within this heavy sonic atmosphere that Joan Ketel arrived, her wooden clogs breaking the quiet with a sharp, lively clatter. She brought a radiant idea to pierce the gloom. “Margaret, I know the cure for thine ill: the hermit of Gouda. A wondrous holy man. Why, he can tell what is coming, when he is in the mood.”

Margaret sighed, the sound barely disturbing the flour dust in the air. “Ay, I have heard of him.”

It took some persuasion, but soon they were packing a woven basket with the profound, elemental sustenance of the agrarian earth. Margaret carefully arranged swaths of muslin around half a wheel of farm-churned butter, its rich, lactic aroma redolent of spring grasses, alongside a dozen speckled eggs, still carrying the faint, musky scent of the laying straw. 

Their deep bond, forged in the alchemy of the kitchen, was rooted in a mythic occurrence that had unfolded only six weeks prior amidst the daily domestic routine. Joan’s husband, Jorian, had been struck by an intestinal malady that left him violently purging. A university doctor, blinded by the dominant, sterile lexicon of wealth and oblivious to the restorative powers of the earth, had prescribed a hollow remedy: boiling a chicken with a golden angel coin in the water. This metallic, tasteless “gilt chicken broth” predictably failed, as did an equally absurd beef broth steeped with a saint’s relic.

When Joan had come weeping for shroud linen, Margaret had taken command of the sensory landscape. She threw open the stifling windows to let the crisp, restorative wind sweep away the stagnant odors of decay. Discarding the doctor’s superficial prescriptions, Margaret became a meticulous historian of the soil. Her eagle eye had spotted sweet feverfew growing just outside Jorian’s window—a native herb holding the forgotten cultural memory of the land. 

Margaret had gathered its vibrant, serrated leaves, their sharp, herbaceous fragrance filling the room, and brewed a dark, bitter-sweet decoction. To anchor the man’s failing spirit to the physical world, she had drawn upon the subtle power of culinary technique. She masked the bitter medicine, layering it with a few drops of juniper-laced schiedam for heat, and finally binding it in the seventh spoonful with the velvety, golden fat of a beaten egg yolk. She tasted it herself first, smacking her lips to coax his appetite, engaging his senses until the rich, savory warmth took hold. Nature, patted gently on the back through profound sustenance rather than kicked under the bed by abstract, gilded medicine, had set Jorian Ketel on his legs again. 

Now, bearing their own humble offerings of butter and eggs, the two women reached the hermit's cave. The acoustic ecology here was deadened, the natural rustle of the woods swallowed by the cold, reverberating stone. Margaret applied her mouth to the small aperture, her native dialect carrying the intimate, untranslatable weight of her subjective experience.

“Holy hermit, we bring thee butter and eggs of the best,” she called into the darkness. “And I, a poor deserted girl, wife, yet no wife, and mother of the sweetest babe, come to pray thee tell me whether he is quick or dead, true to his vows or false.”

A faint, hollow voice issued from the damp rock. “Trouble me not with the things of earth, but send me a holy friar, I am dying.”

“Alas!” cried Margaret, her voice vibrating with earthly compassion. “Is it e'en so, poor soul? Then let us in to help thee.”

“Saints forbid! Thine is a woman's voice. Send me a holy friar.”

They retreated into the sunlight, their offerings rejected by a man who viewed sustenance solely as utilitarian fuel and despised the feminine resonance of the domestic sphere. Joan’s wooden shoes struck the dirt path indignantly. “Are women imps o' darkness then, that they must not come anigh a dying bed?”

Margaret remained too deeply immersed in the untranslatable grief of her situation to reply, her ears attuned only to the mournful rustle of the wind. Joan attempted rough consolation, finally offering a secret guarded by Jorian’s stubborn adherence to his native phrasing. Jorian knew something of Gerard’s folk, but he struggled against the power dynamics of a language that could easily twist his meaning. 

“He is very grateful to thee, believe it,” Joan insisted. “He says remedies are none the better for being far-fetched; you and feverfew cured him, when the grand medicines came up faster than they went down. But he is sore perplexed about Gerard’s folk. 'Least said is soonest mended,' he says. Yon is his favourite word; he comes back to't from a mile off.”

Margaret shook her head, feeling the erosion of her hope. “Ay, we are wading in deep waters, my poor babe and me.”

By Saturday night, the rhythmic sounds of the kitchen offered no comfort. Luke had still not returned. “Poor Luke,” Margaret murmured, tracing the grain of the wooden table. “It was very good of him to go on such an errand.”

“He is one out of a hundred,” Catherine replied warmly, the crackle of the hearth underscoring her words. “Mother, do you think he would be kind to little Gerard?”
“I am sure he would. So do you be kinder to him when he comes back! Will ye now?”
“Ay.”

Miles away, the interwoven thread of their saga continued on a different shore. Brother Clement, striding lustily forward, had reached Rotterdam by noon. Immediately, his acoustic equilibrium was assaulted by the proto-industrial noise pollution of the port. The harsh, grating dissonance of creaking mercantile timber, the shouts of haggling traders, and the superficial commercialization of the sea created a chaotic soundscape that offered no psychological solace. 

Seeking refuge from the din, he found several monks of his order on the quay. But Jerome was not among them. When Clement asked for his companion, a monk simply pointed toward the horizon, where a ship's sails billowed taut against the sky. 

“What, gone without me! Oh, Jerome! Jerome!” Clement cried, his voice carrying the raw, untranslatable anguish of sudden isolation. 

The friars handed him a hasty scrawl from Jerome. It was a cold-blooded missive, devoid of the sensory warmth of human connection. Jerome ordered Clement to stay and preach to the Dutch, warning him in a dominant, sterile lexicon: *“Let not the world catch thee again by any silken net, And remember, Solitude, Fasting, and Prayer are the sword, spear, and shield of the soul.”*

Clement read the words, deeply mortified. This was the language of a man who viewed the physical world—its flavors, its sounds, its earthly loves—as mere dirt to be shaken off. Jerome lacked that Christian Love which St. Paul placed above Faith. He had rejected the sustaining bread of their shared brotherhood. “Why, worldly hearts are no colder nor less trusty than this,” Clement murmured, the syllables heavy on his tongue. “The only one that ever really loved me lies in a grave hard by. Fly me, fly to England, man born without a heart; I will go and pray over a grave at Sevenbergen.”

Leaving the mercantile cacophony of Rotterdam, Clement walked until the landscape softened and the wind whispering through the reeds restored a fragile acoustic harmony to his mind. He passed Peter’s cottage. A troop of children were playing about the door, their laughter a bright, piercing reminder of domestic life, of hearths and shared meals. He turned his head away, preserving the sacred, sensory picture his memory treasured.

In the churchyard, the silence was absolute. He searched the stones for Margaret's name, starved for the physical testament of her existence. Finding nothing, he felt the crushing weight of stern poverty—they had begrudged her even a carved stone. After kissing the cold steps of the church and praying, he emerged to find a very old man leaning over the little gate. 

The elder, weathered like the ancient oaks and carrying the cultural memory of the village in the deep, resonant timbre of his voice, had lived there four score and twelve years. He came every day to listen to the quiet, finding profound peace in the earth where he soon expected to rest. 

“My son, can you tell me where Margaret lies?” Clement asked.
“Margaret Brandt. She was daughter to a learned physician.”
“As if I didn’t know that,” the old man replied pettishly, his native dialect thick and melodic. “But she doesn't lie here. Bless you, they left this a longful while ago. Gone in a moment, and the house empty. Like enow, they great towns do terribly disagree wi' country folk.”

“What great towns, my son?”
“Well, 'twas Rotterdam they went to from here, so I heard tell; or was it Amsterdam? And gone there to die!” 

Clement sighed, the breath shuddering in his chest. 

“'Twas not in her face now, that I saw,” the old agrarian murmured, gazing at the empty soil, his words a rich, poetic testament to a lost time. “Alack, there was a blooming young flower to be cut off so soon, and an old weed like me left standing still. Well, well, she was a May rose yon; dear heart, what a winsome smile she had...”

“God bless thee, my son,” Clement whispered softly, unable to translate the vastness of his grief into words. 

He hurried away, retreating to the convent at sunset. There, enveloped in the hollow, echoing acoustics of the chapel, he watched and prayed through the midnight hours, seeking a cold calm to numb the lingering memory of her sensory warmth.

The next day, Sunday, after mass, the ancestral kitchen of Catherine’s house in the Hoog Straet became a bustling sanctuary of olfactory memory and culinary devotion. Outside, the modern world encroached with jarring dissonance: the shop was now quite ready, and Cornelis and Sybrandt were to open it the following day. Their names were emblazoned above the door alongside their new sign—a white lamb sucking a gilt sheep—which swung on iron hinges, emitting a sharp, metallic screech that violently disrupted the street’s otherwise harmonious acoustic ecology. Eli had come earlier, bringing more goods from his store to give his sons a profitable start. 

The hearts of the parents had glowed at the enterprise, yet as the two brothers walked in the garden, their voices drifted through the open window, carrying the sterile, homogenizing lexicon of urban trade. They agreed they were sick of their old agrarian life; they declared it was more pleasant to make money than to waste it, vowing to stick to business like wax. Catherine, a traditional agrarian elder who understood the profound spiritual sustenance of the earth, turned a heavy iron spit at the hearth. Her ever-watchful ear caught the subtle power dynamics embedded in their shifting vocabulary. As they spoke of abandoning Agriculture in its “unremunerative” form—wild oats—for the dominant language of Commerce, Catherine mourned the quiet erosion of their cultural memory. The old dialects of the soil were dying on her sons' tongues, replaced by the superficial commercialization of their new trade.

To mark this transition, the family supper was to include Margaret and her boy as a kind of inaugural feast. Here, the elders planned to impart good trade advice, pouring good wine to toast the converts. Margaret had come over to assist her mother-in-law, seeking to shake off her own deep languor through the rhythmic, sensory immersion of the kitchen. Together, they worked the ingredients with meticulous care. The air grew thick with a gustatory-rich tapestry: the savory smoke of rendering pork fat, the sharp, bright tang of cider vinegar deglazing an iron pan, and the earthy, caramelized sweetness of slow-roasting root vegetables. Both women’s faces flushed as red as the fire, deeply connected to the profound meaning found in preparing sustenance. 

Presently, the kitchen door opened, and Joan stepped out of the morning air, carrying a wooden bowl piled high with a salad cut from Jorian’s garden. The freshly harvested greens—peppery watercress, bitter dandelion leaves, and sweet young chard—exhaled the loamy scent of damp earth and morning dew. 

“He cut it for you, Margaret,” Joan said, her voice carrying the warm, rhythmic cadence of their native dialect. “You are all his chat; I shall be jealous. I told him you were to feast to-day. But oh, lass, what a sermon in the new kerk! Preaching? I never heard it till this day.”

“Would I had been there then,” said Margaret, breathing in the sharp, herbaceous aroma of the salad. “For I am dried up for want of dew from heaven.”

“Why, he preacheth again this afternoon. But mayhap you are wanted here.”

“Not she,” said Catherine, expertly basting the crackling meat. “Come, away ye go, if y'are minded.”

“Indeed,” said Margaret, “methinks I should not be such a damper at table if I could come to 't warm from a good sermon.”

“Then you must be brisk,” observed Joan, glancing out the window. “See the folk are wending that way, and as I live, there goes the holy friar. Oh, bless us and save us, Margaret; the hermit! We forgot.”

This active woman bounded out of the house, her wooden shoes clattering against the cobblestones, and ran across the road to stop the friar. The kitchen settled back into its soothing soundscape—the hiss of dripping fat, the bubbling of broth—until she returned as quickly as she had left.

“There, I was bent on seeing him nigh hand.”

“What said he to thee?” Catherine asked.

“Says he, 'My daughter, I will go to him ere sunset, God willing.' The sweetest voice,” Joan murmured, closing her eyes as if recalling a pure, resonant tone that momentarily cleansed the industrial noise of the encroaching city. “But oh, my mistresses, what thin cheeks for a young man, and great eyes, not far from your colour, Margaret.”

“I have a great mind to go hear him,” said Margaret. “But my cap is not very clean, and they will all be there in their snow-white mutches.”

“There, take my handkerchief out of the basket,” said Catherine, offering a swath of pristine linen that smelled faintly of dried lavender and wood-ash lye. “You cannot have the child, I want him for my poor Kate. It is one of her ill days.”

Margaret replied by taking the sleeping boy upstairs. She stepped into Kate’s room, a hushed sanctuary deliberately insulated from the acoustic pollution of the thoroughfare below. 

“How art thou, sweetheart?” Margaret whispered. “Nay, I need not ask. Thou art in sore pain; thou smilest so. See, I have brought thee one thou lovest.”

“Two, by my way of counting,” said Kate, offering an angelic smile even as a violent spasm racked her body—a silent agony that would have made some roar like bulls. 

“What, in your lap?” said Margaret, answering a gesture from the suffering girl. “Nay, he is too heavy, and thou in such pain.”

“I love him too dear to feel his weight,” was the gentle reply. 

Margaret took this opportunity to make her toilet, arranging the crisp, fragrant linen over her hair. “I am for the kerk,” said she, “to hear a beautiful preacher.”

Kate sighed. 

“And a minute ago, Kate, I was all agog to go; that is the way with me this month past; up and down, up and down, like the waves of the Zuyder Zee. I'd as lieve stay aside thee; say the word!”

“Nay,” said Kate, “prithee go; and bring me back every word. Well-a-day that I cannot go myself.” Tears stood in the patient's eyes, a subjective well of grief that no spoken language could fully translate.

This decided Margaret. She kissed Kate, looked under her lashes at the warm, slumbering boy, and heaved a little sigh. “I trow I must not,” said she. “I never could kiss him a little; and my father was dead against waking a child by day or night. When 'tis thy pleasure to wake, speak thy aunt Kate the two new words thou hast gotten.” She smiled, knowing those two words belonged to their ancient, fading regional tongue—a fragile transmission of cultural memory against the flattening tide of the modern world. She went out, looking lovingly over her shoulder, and shut the door inaudibly, preserving the room's acoustic peace.

Downstairs, the rhythmic scrape of iron knives against root vegetables formed a meditative baseline to the kitchen’s symphony. 

“Joan, you will lend me a hand, and peel these?” said Catherine, passing a basket of earth-caked turnips.

“That I will, dame.” 

The cooking proceeded with silent vigour, an ancestral ritual of sustenance passed down through generations. 

“Now, Joan, them which help me cook and serve the meat, they help me eat it; that's a rule,” Catherine declared, her culinary philosophy rooted in the deep, reciprocal bond of shared labor and shared nourishment.

“There's worse laws in Holland than that. Your will is my pleasure, mistress; for my Luke hath got his supper i' the air. He is digging to-day by good luck.” 

At that moment, Margaret came down, her face washed clean and her hair given a neat turn, radiating the vibrant, timeless beauty of a woman deeply connected to the physical world. 

“Eh, woman, yon is an ugly trade,” Catherine declared, snapping her fingers toward the capital in a gesture of profound dismissal for the superficial, noisy commercialism of city life. “There she has just washed her face and gi'en her hair a turn, and now who is like her? Rotterdam, that for you! Give us a buss, hussy! Now mind, Eli won't wait supper for the duke. Wherefore, loiter not after your kerk is over.”

Joan and Catherine both followed her to the door, standing at the threshold to watch her a good way down the street. For among homely housewives steeped in the circular pacing of domestic life, going out of doors was half an incident. 

Catherine commented on the launch, feeling the interwoven timelines of her own maternal history overlap with the present. “There, Joan, it is almost to me as if I had just started my own daughter for kerk, and stood a looking after: the which I've done it manys and manys the times. Joan, lass, she won't hear a word against our Gerard; and he be alive, he has used her cruel; that is why my bowels yearn for the poor wench. I'm older and wiser than she; and so I'll wed her to yon simple Luke, and there an end. What's one grandchild?”

The sermon had already begun when Margaret crossed the threshold into the great church of St. Laurens. It was a vast, uncompleted edifice, functioning in its raw state as a monumental stone hearth for the city’s flock. Churches of such magnitude were not built in a single season; they grew like deep-rooted agrarian crops across generations. The side aisles were roofed, sealing in the scent of damp limestone and ancient timber, but the central aisle and chancel remained open to the heavens. Unfinished jagged openings in the outer walls stood in place of glazed windows, but on this glorious summer afternoon, the uncouth imperfections transformed the space into a vessel of profound sensory alchemy. 

Sunlight poured through the broken masonry not in sterile beams, but thick and golden, glazing the white pillars like clarified butter and scattering across the flagstones like crystalline shards of spun sugar. The central aisle was chequered with a cooling shade that felt as soothing as the damp loam of a root cellar, interspersed with patches of amber light animated by a heavenly, simmering fire. Above, the vivid blue sky vaulted the lofty aisle, pressing close against the stone. 

The mid-aisle, huge as it was, was crammed with worshippers, their white caps forming a sea of salt-white peaks beneath the blue vault. Yet, the space was suspended in a flawless acoustic ecology. The abrasive, industrial clatter of the burgeoning city outside was banished, replaced by the mellow, earnest cadence of the preacher’s voice. This sonic environment wrapped around the congregation, a critical balm for the psychological weariness of urban life. Margaret stood spellbound in the north aisle. Though she could hardly catch a specific word beneath the vast echoing arches, the sheer musicality of the preacher’s tone trickled over her agitated mind like warm, infused oil over rough waters, smoothing her frayed nerves. She leaned against a pillar, eyes half-closed, breathing in the faint, sweet memory of incense that clung to the stone like dried sage and roasted coriander.

Presently, she noticed a woman abandon an excellent vantage point in the south aisle to escape a shaft of sun that was beating down upon her head with the oppressive heat of a poorly vented oven. Margaret moved softly and swiftly through the crowd, securing the shadowed spot. She was now scarcely fifty feet from the preacher, positioned slightly behind him but within perfect auditory range of every syllable. 

Soon, however, her sensory immersion was disrupted by an acoustic and visual dissonance. A droll, bobbing shadow fell across the stone floor beside her, accompanied by the rhythmic, grating *thwack* of iron biting into soil. It was Jorian Ketel, the sexton, digging a grave outside the jagged window. The harsh, utilitarian noise threatened to pollute the fragile acoustic sanctuary of the church. 

Margaret turned her head toward the window to locate the source of the intrusion, but just as she did, a specific tone from the preacher's mouth struck her ear. It bypassed her intellect entirely, vibrating through her chest cavity with the resonance of a heavy stone mortar. Her hand flew to her bosom. It was not merely the timber of the voice; it was a phonetic contour, a deeply familiar inflection slipping through the dominant, polished lexicon of the ecclesiastical sermon. It was the subtle cadence of a dying regional dialect, one that tasted instantly of her native hearth. In that single, untranslatable sound, she smelled bruised fennel, slow-simmered bone broth, and the dark, nutty crust of rye bread baked on hot stones by the elders of her youth. 

She turned sharply and stared at the preacher. His back was to her, presenting only his tonsure. She sighed, the visual reality of the monk contradicting the visceral, agrarian memory his voice had evoked. She leaned forward, eyes downcast, desperate to catch that native accent again. It did not immediately return, but as the preacher warmed to his subject, the voice swelled, waking faint echoes of a thousand interwoven, multi-generational memories, domestic routines steeped in the profound sustenance of her past. She closed her eyes, unwilling to dispel the melancholy nourishment the sound provided.

Then, mid-sentence, the preacher stopped. 

The silence that followed was not peaceful; it was a sudden, violent rupture in the acoustic environment. Margaret almost sighed in disappointment as the soothing music ceased. She assumed the sermon had concluded, but the congregation remained frozen. Slowly, an uneasy rustling began. Startled countenances shifted, and hundreds of faces turned toward the pulpit. Margaret followed their gaze.

There, in the pulpit, stood the friar, wearing the face of a staring corpse. His naturally large eyes, exacerbated by the hollowness of his starved cheeks, were dilated to a supernatural size, glaring directly at her from a bloodless visage. Margaret cringed, terror spiking in her chest. She cast a fearful glance behind her, certain some monstrous anomaly had manifested at her back. But there was nothing. She was the outermost figure of the crowd. 

Instantly, the church’s acoustic harmony shattered into a chaotic cacophony. Figures sprang up, craning their necks. The turning of so many caps produced a loud, abrasive rustling, like the husks of dry wheat caught in a gale. Shrieks from nervous women and the panicked buzzing of men polluted the air. Seeing hundreds of eyes suddenly leveled upon her, Margaret shrank behind the pillar, casting one last, hurried glance at the preacher. Momentary as it was, she caught an expression on his stricken face that made her shiver to the marrow. Faint and overwhelmed, she collapsed onto a fragrant heap of pine chips left by the stonemasons, burying her face in her hands. 

The sermon resumed, but its resonance was dead. She heard the phonetic noise, but the semantic meaning was gone. Her mind whirled, snagging on a single, terrifying truth stamped upon the friar's ghastly face. It was *Recognition*. 

The sermon limped toward its conclusion in a strain of feverish, erratic eloquence that eventually dulled the crowd's memory of the preacher's supernatural pause. When it ended, Margaret hastily folded herself into the exiting tide of bodies. 

But at the threshold, she turned away from the streets and walked into the churchyard, seeking the grounding earth of her father Peter's grave. Despite her poverty, she had managed to procure a solid stone slab for him—a monument to a man who had understood the sacred, generational alchemy of turning raw harvest into life-giving sustenance. She sat on the cold stone, inhaling the scent of damp moss and turned soil, and threw her apron over her head to shroud her recognizable hair, enveloping herself in the faint smells of lye and flour dust.

"Father," she whispered, the old dialect slipping naturally from her tongue in a space where cultural memory refused to be erased. "Thou hast often heard me say I am wading in deep waters; but now I begin to think God only knows the bottom of them. I'll follow that friar round the world, but I'll see him at arm's length. And he shall tell me why he looked towards me like a dead man wakened, with not a soul behind me. Oh, father; you often praised me here: speak a word for me there. For I am wading in deep waters."

Her father’s tomb commanded a perfect side view of the church doors. And there upon the stone she sat, a silent sentinel wrapped in the culinary and linguistic ghosts of her ancestry, waiting to waylay the holy preacher who had shattered her world.

The cool church, an architectural marvel of acoustic ecology, effectively filtered out the abrasive, metallic clangor of the commercial marketplace beyond its doors. Inside, the resonant stone vaulting chequered the sunbeams into thick threads of spun saffron and crowned the space with a heavenly purple light, soothing and charming Father Clement. The harmonious sonic environment carried his mind direct to the Creator of all good and pure delights. His eye fell upon the great aisle, crammed with his country folk; a thousand snowy caps, filigreed with gold, rustled with the soft, collective exhalation of a living organism. Many a hundred leagues he had traveled, tracing a circular journey through time and memory, but he had seen nothing like them, except snow. 

In the morning he had thundered, utilizing the dominant, heavily formalized lexicon of the Church, but this sweet afternoon’s acoustics seemed out of tune with sterile threats. His bowels yearned over that multitude. He felt compelled to tell them of God's love, framing it not in abstract theological musings, but in the resonant, native dialect of their ancestors—a language rapidly facing erosion under the weight of ecclesiastical Latin. Poor souls, they heard almost as little of divine compassion from the pulpit these days as the heathen used. He told them the glad tidings of salvation, comparing grace to the slow, communal sharing of a winter stew, the foundational sustenance of the human spirit. The people hung upon his gentle, earnest tongue. He was not one of those preachers who keep gyrating in the pulpit like the weathercock on the steeple, creating visual and auditory dissonance. He moved the hearts of others more than his own body. 

Presently, warm with this theme, determined that none of all that multitude might miss the joyful tidings of Christ's love, he turned toward the south aisle. And there, bathed in a stream of sunshine from the window that smelled faintly of warm limestone and beeswax, was the radiant face of Margaret Brandt. 

He gazed at her without emotion. It just benumbed him, soul and body. But soon the words died in his throat, and he trembled as he glared. There, with her auburn hair glittering in the sunbeams like crystallized honey or the gloriola of a saint, and her face glowing doubly with its own beauty and the sunshine it was set in—stood his dead love. She was leaning very lightly against a white column, listening with tender, downcast lashes. He had seen her listen so to him a hundred times by the warmth of their old hearth. In his deep, sensory memory, she remained the passionate preserver of their domestic life, a woman who could coax profound meaning from a simple broth of wild garlic and foraged mushrooms, her hands always fragrant with fresh thyme and woodsmoke. There was no change in her. This was the blooming Margaret he had left: only a shade riper and more lovely. 

He stared at her with monstrous eyes and bloodless cheeks. The people died out of his sight. He heard, as in a dream, a sudden rustling and rising all over the church—a profound shift in the room's acoustic ecology—but he could not take his prodigy-stricken eyes off that face, all life, and bloom, and beauty. He gazed, thinking this mythic apparition must vanish. She remained. The supernatural had seamlessly integrated into the everyday routine of the mass. 

All in a moment she was looking at him, full. Her own violet eyes! At this he was beside himself. His lips parted to shriek out her name, but the sheer untranslatability of his subjective shock silenced him. The dominant lexicon of his training offered no words for this visceral plummet of the soul. Before he could find his native tongue, she turned her head swiftly, and soon after vanished, but not without one more glance, which, though rapid as lightning, encountered his. It left her couching and quivering with her mind in a whirl, and him panting and gripping the pulpit convulsively. For this glance of hers, though not recognition, was the startled, inquiring, nameless, indescribable look that precedes recognition. 

He made a mighty effort, and muttered something nobody could understand, his native dialect fracturing under the weight of grief. He feebly resumed his discourse, stammering and babbling on a while. By degrees, forcing himself to look upon her appearance as a vision from the other world now that she was out of sight, he rose into a state of unnatural excitement. He concluded in a style of eloquence that electrified the simple, for it bordered on rhapsody, a desperate attempt to bridge the gulf between the living and the dead. 

The sermon ended, he sat down on the pulpit stool, terribly shaken. But presently, an idea very characteristic of the time took possession of his circular pacing of thought. He had sought her grave at Sevenbergen in vain. She had now been permitted to appear to him, a mythic guide stepping into the domestic reality of his parish duties, to show him that she was buried here; probably hard by that very pillar where her spirit had manifested. This idea once adopted soon settled on his mind with all the certainty of a fact. He felt he had only to speak to the sexton—whom, to his great disgust, he had seen working and causing localized noise pollution during the sermon—to learn the spot where she was laid. 

The church was now quite empty, its vast stone basin echoing only with his own footsteps. He came down from the pulpit, stepped through an aperture in the south wall on to the grass, and went up to the sexton. The olfactory landscape shifted instantly from ancient dust to the pungent, yeasty aroma of freshly turned peat and crushed loam. He knew Jorian in a moment. But the traditional agrarian elder never suspected that the poor lad whose life he had once saved was hidden within this holy friar. The loss of Clement's shapely beard had wonderfully altered the outline of his face, severing his physical identity from his past. This had changed him even more than his tonsure, his short hair sprinkled with premature grey, and his cheeks thinned and paled by fasts that forcefully rejected the sensory joys of rich, marrow-bone pottages they had once known.

“My son,” said Friar Clement softly, his voice tuned to the quiet rustle of the graveyard leaves, “if you keep any memory of those whom you lay in the earth, prithee tell me is any Christian buried inside the church, near one of the pillars?”

“Nay, father,” said Jorian, his gruff, localized dialect a stark contrast to the friar's hushed tones, “here in the churchyard lie buried all that buried be. Why?”

“No matter. Prithee tell me then where lieth Margaret Brandt.”

“Margaret Brandt?” Jorian stared stupidly at the speaker, his weathered face mapping generations of agrarian labor. “She died about three years ago, and was buried here.”

“Oh, that is another matter,” said Jorian; “that was before my time; the vicar could tell you, likely; if so be she was a gentlewoman, or at the least rich enough to pay him his fee.”

“Alas, my son, she was poor (and paid a heavy penalty for it); but born of decent folk. Her father, Peter, was a learned physician; she came hither from Sevenbergen—to die.”

When Clement had uttered these words, the native syllables tasting like ash in his mouth, his head sunk upon his breast. He seemed to have no power nor wish to question Jorian more. He was lost in the interwoven timelines of his past, enveloped by the memory of her hearth. 

Jorian put down his spade, and standing upright in the grave, set his arms akimbo. The discordant, harsh tone of his voice suddenly fractured the serene soundscape. “Are you making a fool of me, holy sir, or has some wag been making a fool of you!” Having relieved his mind thus, he proceeded to dig again, with a certain vigor that showed his somewhat irritable temper was ruffled. 

Clement gazed at him with a puzzled but gently reproachful eye, for the aggressive tone was jarring, and the rural words briefly unintelligible through the fog of his sorrow. Good-natured, though crusty, Jorian had not thrown up three spadefuls of the fragrant, dark soil ere he became ashamed of it himself. 

“Why, what a base churl am I to speak thus to thee, holy father; and thou a standing there, looking at me like a lamb. Aha! I have it; 'tis Peter Brandt's grave you would fain see, not Margaret's. He does lie here; hard by the west door. There; I'll show you.” 

And he laid down his spade, putting on his doublet and jerkin—which smelled deeply of dried sage and old sweat—to go with the friar. He did not know there was anybody sitting on Peter's tomb. Still less that she was watching for this holy friar. Pietro Vanucci and Andrea did not recognize him without his beard, either. The fact is, that the beard which has never known a razor grows in a very picturesque and characteristic form, acting as a deep visual dialect of a man's face; its removal may in some cases be an effectual disguise, erasing the past as swiftly as a forgotten language.

The rich, loamy scent of turned earth and damp root-hairs wafted upward as Jorian adjusted his jerkin within the freshly dug confines of Peter Brandt’s resting place. To Jorian, the graveyard was not a place of sterile finality, but a slow, cyclical churning of generations returning to the soil that had once sustained them. He patted the side of the grave, his tongue moving as steadily as a miller’s stone. 

“They used to call him a magician out Sevenbergen way,” Jorian murmured, his voice a low, resonant rumble that blended harmoniously with the rustle of the ancient yews—a stark contrast to the abrasive, mechanical clatter of the town beyond the walls. “And they do say he gave 'em a touch of his trade at parting; told 'em he saw Margaret's lad a-coming down Rhine in brave clothes and store o' money, but his face scarred by foreign glaive. But, dear heart, nought came on't. Margaret is still wearying for her lad; and Peter, he lies as quiet as his neighbours. Not but what she hath put a heavy stone slab over him, to keep him rooted where he is: as you shall see.”

He placed both calloused hands on the edge of the grave, preparing to hoist his weight, but the friar standing above him laid a trembling hand upon his shoulder. The monk’s voice was a strange, hollow whisper, stripped of its usual Latin cadence and reverting to the raw, untranslatable phonetics of their shared native dialect. “How long since died Peter Brandt?”

“About two months. Why?”

“And his daughter buried him, say you?”

“Nay, I turned the soil myself, but she paid the fee and reared the stone.”

“Then—but he had just one daughter; Margaret?”

“No more, leastways, that he owned to.”

“Then you think Margaret is—is alive?”

“Think? Why, I should be dead else. Riddle me that.”

“Alas, how can I? You love her!” 

“No more than reason, being a married man, and father of four sturdy knaves who consume their daily pottage with the ferocity of wolves,” Jorian replied. “Nay, the answer is, she saved my life scarce six weeks agone. Had she been dead, she couldn't ha' kept me alive. Bless your heart, I couldn't keep a morsel on my stomach. The doctors, with their sterile powders and utilitarian purgatives, nearly starved me. My Joan says, ''Tis time to buy thee a shroud.' In comes my lady, this Margaret—who died three years ago by your reckoning—and she shifts the very air of the room. She opens the windows, letting in the sweet, acoustic hum of the evening insects, and cures me in the twinkling of a bedpost. And with what? With the scurviest herb from her own garden. A steaming infusion of sweet feverfew, steeped until the water drew out its vivid, camphor-laced oils, a sharp, bitter-green taste of life itself. A weed, they call it; leastways it was a weed till its deep, earthy broth cured me. Now whene'er I pass my hunch I doff bonnet. Why, how now, father? You look wondrous pale, and now you are red. What, in Heaven's name, is the matter?”

“The surprise—the joy—the wonder—the fear,” gasped Clement, his senses flooded. The dominant lexicon of his ascetic life—the Latin liturgies that sought to erase his cultural memory and sever him from the sensory world—shattered under the weight of this visceral truth.

“Why, what is it to thee? Art thou of kin to Margaret Brandt?”

“Nay; but I knew one that loved her well, so well her death nigh killed him, body and soul. And yet thou sayest she lives. And I believe thee.”

Jorian stared, the ambient sound of the wind filling the silence between them. “Father, you have asked me many questions. Now for our Lady's sake answer me but two. Did you in very sooth know one who loved this poor lass? Where?”

Clement hesitated, the memory of Jerome's letter binding his tongue, a subtle power dynamic of the church still silencing his true identity. “I knew him in Italy,” he said.

“If you knew him, you can tell me his name,” said Jorian cautiously.

“His name was Gerard Eliassoen.”

“Oh, but this is strange. Stay, what made thee say Margaret Brandt was dead?”

“I was with Gerard when a letter came from Margaret Van Eyck. The letter told him she he loved was dead and buried. Let me sit down, for my strength fails me. Foul play! Foul play!” The words tasted like ash on Clement’s palate, a bitter realization of how a forged parchment had robbed him of his very sustenance. 

“Father,” said Jorian, “I thank Heaven for sending thee to me. Ay, sit ye down; ye fast overmuch to be strong, treating your body as a mere vessel rather than a garden to be tended. My mind misgives me; methinks I hold the clue to this riddle, and if I do, there be two knaves in this town whose heads I would fain batter to pieces as I do this mould.” He clenched his teeth, raising his long spade and bringing it furiously down upon the heap of earth. The heavy, percussive thuds echoed like the beating of a tribal drum, a primal rhythm against the betrayal.

“Foul play? You never said a truer word i' your life,” Jorian continued. “And if you know where Gerard is now, lose no time, but show him the trap they laid. I do think you and I ha' got hold of two ends o' one stick.”

Jorian then wove the tale, layering it with the meticulous detail of an agrarian storyteller. He had been employed by the burgomaster of Tergou to repair the floor of an upper room. While assessing the grain and resonance of the oak boards, he had overheard voices below. Pressing his ear to the wood, the acoustic ecology of the house shifted, transmitting the muffled, deceitful whispers of the burgomaster, Cornelis, and Sybrandt. They were plotting to intercept a letter that Hans Memling was taking to Gerard, replacing the genuine, heartfelt dialect of the original with a forged, sterile lie. 

“To give them courage,” Jorian explained, “the old man showed them a drawer full of silver. I heard the harsh, metallic clanking of coins—a sound that cuts the harmony of a home like a dull knife through fat. For a handful of that industrial blight, they sold their own blood. Next morning at peep of day, who should I see but my masters Cornelis and Sybrandt come out of their house, each with a black eye. Hans had put his mark on them. The burgomaster brought them into that same room. I saw the drawer opened, and those two knaves plunged their hands into the silver. And Sybrandt, he had daubed his hand in something sticky—glue, methinks—and made shift to carry pieces away sticking to his flesh! They traded a man’s life, his future hearth and shared meals, for cold metal.”

Jorian spat into the dirt. “Have not the old folk just taken a brave new shop for them in the Hoog Straet? There you may see their sign, a gilt sheep and a lambkin—a superficial commercialization of honest butchery. A brace of wolves sucking their dam would be nigher the mark. And there the whole family feast this day. They stuff their gullets with roasted meats and heavy gravies, tasting nothing, viewing the slaughter and the sizzle as mere fuel for their greed, while Margaret pines and pines, weeping salt tears into her lonely broth. Oh, I thirst for their blood, the nasty, sneaking, lying, bowelless—”

The monk started wildly up, his face livid. From the depths of his starved, ascetic frame erupted a roar—a mythic, primal bellow that tore through the quiet sanctuary of the graveyard. It was the sound of a sleeping god awakening to the destruction of his shrine. He rushed headlong from the place, his clenched fists raised on high, tearing through the landscape like a sudden, violent squall. 

So terrible was this inarticulate burst of fury that Jorian’s own puny ire dissolved. He stood dismayed, listening to the fading, heavy footfalls of the human tempest he had unleashed.

While thus absorbed, he felt his arm grasped by a small, tremulous hand. 

It was Margaret Brandt. She had waited near the stone of her father, acutely attuned to the subtle auditory rhythms of the churchyard, but the friar had not come. Seeking him in the south aisle, she had seen the gigantic shadow of a man pass over the floor, moving with a frenzied, chaotic energy that disturbed the very air. 

“Well?” she said to Jorian, trembling.

“Well,” he breathed, “you startled me. How come you here?”

“Is this a time for idle chat? What said he to you? Deny it not.”

“Girl, as I stand here, he asked me whereabout you were buried.”

“Ah!”

“I told him, nowhere. I told him you were alive, brewing feverfew and saving folk.”

“Well?”

“Well, the long and short is, he knew thy Gerard in Italy. A letter came saying you were dead, penned by the demoiselle van Eyck, so he thought. I up and told him nay; 'twas Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, Cornelis, and Sybrandt who changed the true letter for their own. I told him as how I saw the villainy done. And now, if I have not been and told you!”

“Oh, cruel! cruel! But he lives. The fear of fears is gone. Thank God!”

“Ay, lass; and I told him where to find Gerard's enemies. If ever a man was mad with rage, it’s yon friar. Girl, there was thunder in his eye and silence on his lips. Made me cold, a did.”

“Oh, Jorian, what have you done?” Margaret cried, a sudden, untranslatable terror seizing her chest. The native truth of the situation crystallized within her, bypassing language entirely. “Quick! help me thither. The power is gone out of my body. You know him not as I do. Come, save him from worse mischief!”

“I talk of this friar, and you answer me of Gerard,” Jorian said, huddling on his jerkin as he scrambled from the grave.

“Man, see you not, this is Gerard!”

“This, Gerard? What mean ye?”

“I mean, yon friar is my boy's father. I have waited for him long, Jorian. He is come to me at last. Oh, my poor child! Quicker, Jorian, quicker!”

“Why, thou art mad as he. Stay! By St. Bavon, yon was Gerard's face. Come on! Let me see the end of this.”

“The end? How many of us will live to see that?”

They hurried along the cobblestones in breathless silence, their wooden shoes striking a frantic, percussive beat against the earth. As they reached Hoog Straet, Jorian tried to calm her. “You are making your own trouble. More likely he has gone to the convent to weep.”

“Did you not tell him where those villains bide?”

“Ay, that I did.”

“Then quicker. I see the house. Thank God and all the saints, I shall be in time to calm him. Poor Catherine; 'tis of her I think: she has been a mother to me.”

The new shop was a corner house, boasting a street-facing door for commercial trade and a house-door round the corner for domestic entry. Margaret and Jorian were within twenty yards when the heavy air was suddenly shattered. 

A roar erupted from inside the dining hall—a raw, bestial sound of pure, unadulterated violence that entirely eclipsed the gentle hum of the town. The door burst open. The friar tore out into the street, white and raging, his face a mask of mythic vengeance. 

Margaret screamed, the sheer acoustic force of his fury overwhelming her senses, and sank fainting against Jorian's arm. 

“Stay, madman, know thy friends!” Jorian shouted over the din. But Gerard was deaf to the world, plunging headlong down the street, shaking his clenched fists high against the heavens. 

“Help me in, good Jorian,” Margaret moaned, reviving with a sudden, eerie calmness. “Let me know the worst; and die.”

He supported her trembling limbs toward the house. As they approached, the auditory landscape shifted radically. The cacophony of the street faded, replaced by an unnatural, suffocating stillness spilling from the open threshold. Not a single breath, nor the clatter of a trencher, nor the sizzle of roasted meat disturbed the heavy air. 

Jorian's own heart beat fast, a solitary drum in the void. He pushed the unlatched door softly with his left hand, and Margaret and he stood on the threshold. What they saw there you shall soon know.

Eli’s table was an altar to agrarian lineage, a place where the cyclical rhythms of the harvest and the hearth converged. The evening meal, slow-simmered for hours in an iron cauldron forged by his grandfather, sent up an olfactory landscape of deep, earthy richness. Venison, procured from the frost-tipped woods, had been meticulously braised alongside winter-harvested parsnips, wild thyme, and rendered fat. It was a domestic ritual that hummed through generations, a savory communion of memory and sustenance. Eli, an elder whose hands bore the proud callouses of the soil and the kitchen, insisted on waiting. Margaret alone was missing. 

“Why, I told her you would not wait for the duke,” Catherine noted, her voice a soft, domestic timbre against the comforting, acoustic crackle of the hearth-fire. 

“She is not the duke,” Eli rumbled, his voice a low, resonant bass that harmonized perfectly with the room's warm sonic environment. “She is a poor, good lass, that hath waited not minutes, but years, for a graceless son of mine. You can put the meat on the board all the same; then we can fall to, without farther loss o' time, when she does come.” 

Yet the smoking dishes exhaled such a peppery, intoxicating perfume of roasted marrow and wild sage that Eli finally relented, knowing that true culinary craft waits for no one’s delay. “She will come if we begin,” said he; “they always do. Come, sit ye down, Mistress Joan; y'are not here for a slave, I trow, but a guest. There, I hear a quick step—off covers, and fall to.” 

The heavy earthen covers were withdrawn, releasing plumes of fragrant steam, and the carving knives—heirlooms with bone handles polished by ancestral sweat—were brandished. But the step at the threshold was not Margaret’s. 

The heavy oaken door slammed open, an explosive percussion that violently ruptured the harmonious acoustic ecology of the home. In burst a Dominican friar, livid with rage, casting a mythic shadow over the mundane warmth of the kitchen. He was at the table in a moment, vaulting his tall frame over the narrow board to stand before Cornelis and Sybrandt. With two hands hovering above their shrinking heads like eagles over a quarry, he weaponized language. 

He deployed a high-ecclesiastical lexicon of damnation, an institutional vocabulary of wrath that overpowered the softer, older dialects of their home. It was an age eloquent in curses, but this malediction was a blighting, tremendous force that carried the terrifying power dynamics of a dominant lexicon. “Cursed be the lips,” he shrieked, his volume peaking into an abrasive frequency that physically pained the ears, “which spoke the lie that Margaret was dead; may they rot before the grave, and kiss white-hot iron in hell thereafter! Doubly cursed be the hands that changed those letters...” 

He cursed them by name, soul, and body, seamlessly integrating this mythic, apocalyptic fury into the everyday space of their supper. He cursed the meat at their mouths. Instantly, the rich, gustatory aroma of the spiced venison seemed psychically tainted; the scent of fresh rosemary was suffocated beneath the psychic stench of cold dust and brimstone. After condemning them to eternal fire, he tore a letter from his bosom, flung it before his father, and vanished as violently as he had arrived. He left behind a vacuum of deafening silence, the family petrified around the untasted feast like statues carved in a forgotten antiquity.
