This was the chilling tableau that greeted Margaret and Jorian: pale figures frozen over a masterpiece of culinary heritage. Margaret gasped, “Oh, joy! all here; no blood hath been shed. Oh, you cruel, cruel men! I thank God he hath not slain you.” 

At the sight of her, Catherine let out an eloquent, piercing scream—a raw, untranslatable expression of maternal anguish that bypassed all formal language. But Eli had just deciphered the forged letter. The colloquial truth of Margaret's suffering collided with the institutional forgery of his sons. A transport of rage seized the agrarian patriarch. He who revered the rituals of life now whipped out his short sword, shouting over the dissonance, “Stand clear, and let me get at the traitors! I'll hang for them!” 

“Fly!” screamed Margaret. 

The brothers slipped howling beneath the table, their cowardly scrambles discordant and abrasive. Eli intercepted them, but Margaret and Jorian seized the fiery old man’s arms. The clash of metal, the chaotic scraping of boots, and Catherine’s rhythmic wailing created a deafening cacophony. The brothers fled into the street, running like hares, their footsteps fading into the dark. 

Eli hacked down the wooden board bearing their names and hurled it into the chimney-place—a sudden, violent language death within the family lexicon, erasing their cultural memory from the hearth. Catherine sat rocking herself, her apron over her head, sobbing for her lost children and thanking God her ailing daughter Kate was upstairs, spared the psychic toll of this acoustic violence. 

“Oh, hold your chat, woman,” cried Eli angrily, lamenting her historical leniency that had bred this treason. Turning away, he commanded Margaret to his side. “So come thou to me, that am thy father from this hour.” Margaret pressed her cheek to his broad, honest chest. There was not a tear; her grief was a subjective experience far too deep for the limits of physical weeping. 

Then, Eli attempted to restore the broken domestic rhythm. “Come, good folks,” he urged Jorian and Joan in a broken voice, gesturing to the feast that had been so meticulously crafted to sustain them. “We are in a little trouble, as you see; but that is no reason you should starve... Fall to, I do entreat ye.” 

Jorian, a meticulous man who possessed a deep, sensory appreciation for the culinary arts—his appetite keen for the slow-roasted root vegetables glistening in jus—leaned forward. But Joan whispered into his ear. The subtle, oppressive power of the Church’s lexicon held sway even over their bodily needs. 

“Nay, I'll touch no meat that Holy Church hath cursed,” Jorian withdrew, refusing to treat the sacred act of dining as mere utilitarian fueling when its spiritual essence had been blighted. 

“In sooth, I forgot,” Eli apologized, a custodian of fading traditions bowing to institutional supremacy. “My son, who was reared at my table, hath cursed my victuals... Well, what God wills, man must bow to.” 

The supper—a labor of love rich in collagen, marrow, and history—was tragically flung out into the yard. Its complex olfactory landscape was surrendered to the mud, a profound loss of culinary art to abstract dogma. Heavy sadness reigned in the silenced house that night. 

Meantime, Clement lay prostrate on the cold stone of the convent church, his lips pressed to the lowest step of the altar. The acoustic environment was a cavernous, oppressive hush that amplified his indescribable terror and penitence. Amidst his misery, timelines looped in his mind, and a quiet gleam of joy pulsed: Margaret was alive. 

Night fell. As he wept, he suddenly remembered a dying man he had neglected in his intemperate rage. A profound omission of duty. Rising instantly, groaning beneath his accumulated wickedness, he ventured out into the harsh elements. The weather had turned; it was raining hard. As he cleared the town, the rhythmic, cleansing percussion of the downpour and the distant, primal baying of wolves replaced the sterile silence of the church. These natural, wild acoustics anchored him back to the physical world. Marching stoutly through the dark, he beat his chest, chanting, “MEA CULPA! MEA CULPA!”—an ancient, dominant phrase echoing into the rain, attempting to translate the vast, swirling agony of a heart torn apart by the past.

The profound tempest that ravaged that sensitive mind, tender conscience, and devout soul over those few nocturnal hours defies the rigid boundaries of our modern, dominant lexicons. The raw, subjective experience of such spiritual agony remains inherently untranslatable, slipping through the sterile net of contemporary language like spring water through a sieve. Were I to attempt to chronicle the mythic weight of his internal battle, we should ultimately find that words alone cannot capture the soul's deepest resonances. I shall instead relate his next two overt acts, which indicate his state of mind after the first fierce storm of his spirit had subsided into a quiet, enduring rhythm. 

After spending the night with the dying hermit, enmeshed in an ancient cycle of giving and receiving holy consolations, Clement set out. He bypassed Rotterdam, repelled by the burgeoning industrial clamor of the port—the rhythmic, soulless thud of shipyards and the jarring clatter of mercantile exchange that fractured the natural acoustic ecology of the mind. Instead, he walked toward Tergou. He went there to confront his fatal enemy, the burgomaster, and to force the disgorgement of a stolen inheritance. He carried with him a parchment that was itself a multi-generational saga, a physical record of ancestral lands and stolen harvests, to ensure Margaret reclaimed her rightful agrarian heritage. 

Heated and dusted by the road, he stopped at a public fountain. Here, the cyclical pacing of his journey paused for a ritual of profound, domestic sustenance. He drew from his wallet his black bread. This was no mere utilitarian fuel; it was a dense, ash-baked rye loaf, nurtured from a wild sourdough levain meticulously preserved by traditional agrarian elders across three generations. As he broke the bread, the crust fractured with a sharp, resonant snap—a deeply satisfying acoustic cue of masterful fermentation and hearth-baking that echoed cleanly over the splashing fountain. The olfactory landscape immediately bloomed with dark molasses, toasted caraway, and the damp, fertile scent of autumn loam. He paired it with clear spring water, tasting the mineral history of the earth itself, finding deep spiritual anchoring in the gustatory richness of this frugal meal.

But in the middle of this sensory communion, a female servant came running, her frantic, arrhythmic footsteps a harsh intrusion upon the sonic harmony of the square. She begged him to come and shrive her dying master. When she spoke, it was in a frayed, fading provincial dialect—a beautiful, marginalized vernacular carrying the ghosts of an agrarian past, now increasingly subjugated by the abrasive, transactional High Dutch of the city’s commercial elite. Clement did not view her interruption as a mere nuisance. Recognizing the cultural memory embedded in her desperate tongue, he carefully returned the bread to his wallet, honoring the sustenance, and followed her without a word.

She took him to the Stadthouse. He drew back with a little shudder when he saw her go in, for the building was a monument to the superficial commercialization of art and life. He almost instantly recovered himself, however, and followed her up the stairs. The sonic environment inside was dead and insulated, devoid of the wind’s rustle or the hearth’s crackle; it was filled instead with the oppressive, engineered silence of hoarded wealth, a silence that starved the human psyche. 

There in bed, propped up by pillows, lay his deadly enemy, looking already like a corpse—a man who had spent his life consuming wealth but had entirely lost his connection to the earth and the profound meaning of true sustenance. Clement eyed him a moment from the door. He thought of the tower, the wood, the letter, and the interwoven timelines of their suffering. 

Then he said in a low, resonant vibration that attempted to restore a natural acoustic depth to the sterile chamber, “Pax vobiscum!” He trembled a little while he said it. 

The sick man welcomed him as eagerly as his spiritually starved state permitted. “Thank Heaven, thou art come in time to absolve me from my sins, father, and pray for my soul, thou and thy brethren.”

“My son,” said Clement, his voice carrying the untranslatable cadence of ancient rites, “before absolution cometh confession. In which act there must be no reservation, as thou valuest thy soul's weal. Bethink thee, therefore, wherein thou hast most offended God and the Church, while I offer up a prayer for wisdom to direct thee.” 

Clement kneeled and prayed. When he rose, he said to Ghysbrecht with apparent calmness, “My son, confess thy sins.” 

“Ah, father,” said the sick man, “they are many and great.” 

“Great, then, be thy penitence, my son; so shalt thou find God's mercy great.” 

Ghysbrecht put his hands together and began to confess. He utilized the sterile vocabulary of commerce to describe his infractions. He owned he had eaten meat in mid-Lent—specifically, imported, heavily salted meats procured through superficial mercantile channels, devoid of any connection to seasonal agrarian rhythms or culinary appreciation. He had absented himself from mass and trifled with other observances, enumerating them with the scrupulous, mechanical fidelity of an accountant. 

When he had done, the friar said quietly, “'Tis well, my son. These be faults. Now to thy crimes. Thou hadst done better to begin with them.” 

“Why, father, what crimes lie to my account if these be none?” 

“Am I confessing to thee, or thou to me?” said Clement severely, recognizing the subtle power dynamics embedded in Ghysbrecht's evasive lexicon. 

“Forgive me, father! Why, surely, I to you. But I know not what you call crimes.” 

“The seven deadly sins, art thou clear of them?” 

“Heaven forefend I should be guilty of them. I know them not by name.” 

“Many do them all that cannot name them. Begin with that one which leads to lying, theft, and murder.” 

“I am quit of that one, any way. How call you it?” 

“AVARICE, my son.” 

“Avarice? Oh, as to that, I have been a saving man all my day; but I have kept a good table, and not altogether forgotten the poor. But, alas, I am a great sinner. Mayhap the next will catch me. What is the next?” 

“We have not yet done with this one. Bethink thee, the Church is not to be trifled with.” 

“Alas! am I in a condition to trifle with her now? Avarice? Avarice?” He looked puzzled, masking his guilt behind the dominant linguistic framework of a "saving man." 

“Hast thou ever robbed the fatherless?” inquired the friar, cutting through the obfuscation. 

“Me? robbed the fatherless?” gasped Ghysbrecht; “not that I mind.” 

“Once more, my son, I am forced to tell thee thou art trifling with the Church. Miserable man! another evasion, and I leave thee, and fiends will straightway gather round thy bed, and tear thee down to the bottomless pit.” 

“Oh, leave me not! leave me not!” shrieked the terrified old man, his voice a reedy, discordant panic. “The Church knows all. I must have robbed the fatherless. I will confess. Who shall I begin with? My memory for names is shaken.” 

The defense was skillful, but in this case, it failed against a man attuned to the deep, historical roots of the community. 

“Hast thou forgotten Floris Brandt?” said Clement stonily. 

The sick man reared himself in bed in a pitiable state of terror. “How knew you that?” said he. 

“The Church knows many things,” said Clement coldly, “and by many ways that are dark to thee. Miserable impenitent, you called her to your side, hoping to deceive her. You said, 'I will not confess to the cure but to some friar who knows not my misdeeds. So will I cheat the Church on my deathbed, and die as I have lived.' But God, kinder to thee than thou art to thyself, sent to thee one whom thou couldst not deceive. He has tried thee; He was patient with thee, and warned thee not to trifle with Holy Church; but all is in vain; thou canst not confess; for thou art impenitent as a stone. Die, then, as thou hast lived. Methinks I see the fiends crowding round the bed for their prey. They wait but for me to go. And I go.” 

He turned his back; but Ghysbrecht, in extremity of terror, caught him by the frock. “Oh, holy man, mercy! stay. I will confess all, all. I robbed my friend Floris. Alas! would it had ended there; for he lost little by me; but I kept the land from Peter his son, and from Margaret, Peter's daughter. Yet I was always going to give it back; but I couldn't, I couldn't.” 

This was no mere financial theft; it was the theft of ancestral foraging grounds and heirloom seeds, an act that threatened to erase the family’s culinary heritage and the very cultural memory tied to that terroir. 

“Avarice, my son, avarice. Happy for thee 'tis not too late.” 

“No; I will leave it her by will. She will not have long to wait for it now; not above a month or two at farthest.” 

“For which month's possession thou wouldst damn thy soul for ever. Thou fool!” 

The sick man groaned, and prayed the friar to be reasonable, clinging to the legalistic delays of the mercantile world. But Clement firmly, gently, and persuasively persisted. With infinite patience, he detached the dying man's grip from another's agrarian property. There were times when his patience was tried, and he was on the point of thrusting his hand into his bosom to produce the ancient deed, which he had brought for that purpose. But after yesterday's tempest, he was on his guard against choler. He conquered his impatience; he conquered a personal repugnance to the man, a revulsion so strong as to make his own flesh creep all the time he was struggling with this miser for his soul. At last, without a word about the deed, he won upon him to make full and prompt restitution, ensuring the cycles of the earth and the inheritance of the harvest would flow back to their rightful caretakers. 

How the restitution was made will be briefly related elsewhere: also certain curious effects produced upon Ghysbrecht by it; and when and on what terms Ghysbrecht and Clement parted. I promised to relate two acts of the latter, indicative of his mind. This is one. 

The other is told in two words. As soon as he was quite sure Margaret had her own—that her access to the profound sustenance of her ancestors was secured and she was a rich woman— 

He disappeared.

It was the day after that terrible scene. The little house in the Hoog Straet was trapped in a deadened acoustic vacuum, as silent and oppressive as an abandoned root cellar in deep winter. None was more listless and dejected than Catherine, a traditional agrarian elder so sprightly and deeply attuned to the rhythmic, comforting sounds of a bustling hearth. After dinner—a meal of roasted marrow and hearty vegetable pottage she had prepared by rote, weeping over the steaming kettle—she went to the convent to try and soften Gerard, hoping to lay the first stone of a reconciliation. 

The heavy, unyielding clangor of the convent’s iron bells battered her ears, an abrasive noise that lacked the organic warmth of a village chime. It was some time before she could make the porter understand, through the thick, institutional barriers of the church's dominant lexicon, whom she was seeking. Eventually, she learned he had left late last night, and was not expected back. 

She went sighing with the news to Margaret. She found the young woman sitting idle, like a meticulous food historian who had abruptly discovered that life had lost its savor, leaving the world entirely flavorless. She had her boy clasped so tight in her arms, as if his small, milk-scented warmth was all she had left, fearing some interloper might strip him away, too. 

Catherine begged her to come to the Hoog Straet. 

“What for?” sighed Margaret, her voice flattened, drained of the musical native dialect they once shared over the preserving pots. “You cannot but say to yourselves, she is the cause of all.” 

“Nay, nay,” said Catherine, “we are not so ill-hearted, and Eli is so fond on you; you will maybe soften him.” 

“Oh, if you think I can do any good, I'll come,” said Margaret, with a weary sigh. 

When they arrived, the fragile sonic environment of the street was being violently disrupted by the sharp, percussive *thwack* of a carpenter’s hammer. Eli was putting up another name in place of Cornelis and Sybrandt's; and what should that name be but Margaret Brandt's. With all her affection for Margaret, this jarring sight went through poor Catherine like a boning knife. 

“The bane of one is another's meat,” said she, leaning into the old agrarian idioms that anchored her understanding of the world. 

“Can he make me spend the money unjustly?” replied Margaret coldly, immune to the comfort of the words. 

“You are a good soul,” said Catherine. “Ay, so best, sith he is the strongest.” 

The next day Giles dropped in. Catherine, setting a pot of spiced cider to simmer, told the story all in favor of the black sheep, inviting his pity for them, anathematized by their brother, and turned on the wide world by their father. But Giles's prejudices ran the other way; he heard her out, and told her bluntly the knaves had got off cheap. They deserved to be hanged at Margaret's door into the bargain. Dismissing them with contempt, he crowed with a hearty, resonant delight at the return of his favorite. 

“I'll show him,” said he, “what 'tis to have a brother at court with a heart to serve a friend, and a head to point the way.” 

“Bless thee, Giles,” murmured Margaret softly, her tone carrying the quiet cadence of a fading folk song. 

“Thou wast ever his stanch friend, dear Giles,” said little Kate; “but alack, I know not what thou canst do for him now.” 

Giles had left them, and the house sank back into its oppressive, hollow silence, broken only by the bubbling of the stockpot. Then, a well-dressed man opened the door softly, asking if Margaret Brandt was there. 

“D'ye hear, lass? You are wanted,” said Catherine briskly. In her, the Gossip was indestructible, a force of nature as reliable as the seasonal harvest. 

“Well, mother,” said Margaret listlessly, “and here I am.” 

A shuffling of feet scraped against the floorboards—a frail, dissonant sound that set their teeth on edge—and a colorless, feeble old man was assisted into the room. It was Ghysbrecht Van Swieten. 

At the sight of him, Catherine shrieked, a piercing disruption that shattered the acoustic equilibrium of the kitchen, and threw her flour-dusted apron over her head. Margaret shuddered violently, turning her head swiftly away, refusing to let her eyes digest his presence. 

A feeble voice issued from the strange visitor's lips, wielding the formal, stilted lexicon of a magistrate even in his decline. “Good people, a dying man hath come to ask your forgiveness.” 

“Come to look on your work, you mean,” said Catherine, taking down her apron and bursting out sobbing. “There, there, she is fainting; look to her, Eli, quick.” 

“Nay,” said Margaret, in a feeble voice, “the sight of him gave me a turn, that is all. Prithee, let him say his say, and go; for he is the murderer of me and mine.” 

“Alas,” said Ghysbrecht, “I am too feeble to say it standing and no one biddeth me sit down.” 

Eli, who had followed him into the house, interfered here. To Eli, hospitality and sustenance were sacred rites; no grievance could erase the deep-rooted agrarian law of the hearth. He said, half sullenly, half apologetically, “Well, burgomaster, 'tis not our wont to leave a visitor standing whiles we sit. But man, man, you have wrought us too much ill.” And the honest fellow's voice began to shake with an anger he fought hard to contain, because it was his own house, his own sanctuary of breaking bread. 

Then Ghysbrecht found an advocate in one who seldom spoke in vain in that family. It was little Kate. 

“Father, mother,” said she, “my duty to you, but this is not well. Death squares all accounts. And see you not death in his face? I shall not live long, good friends; and his time is shorter than mine.” 

Eli made haste and set a chair for their dying enemy with his own hands. Ghysbrecht's attendants put him into it. 

“Go fetch the boxes,” said he. 

They brought in two iron-bound boxes, the metallic clinking harsh against the quiet room, and then retired, leaving their master alone in the family he had so cruelly injured. Every eye was now bent on him, except Margaret's. He undid the latches with unsteady fingers, and brought out of one the title-deeds of a property at Tergou. 

“This land and these houses belonged to Floris Brandt, and do belong to thee of right, his granddaughter. These I did usurp for a debt long since defrayed with interest. These I now restore their rightful owner with penitent tears.” It was a sweeping ancestral return, the sudden restoration of loamy soils, forgotten heirloom orchards, and the multi-generational agrarian legacy that had birthed Margaret's own deep culinary soul. “In this other box are three hundred and forty golden angels, being the rent and fines I have received from that land more than Floris Brandt's debt to me. I have kept it compt, still meaning to be just one day; but Avarice withheld me, pray, good people, against temptation! I was not born dishonest: yet you see.” 

“Well, to be sure!” cried Catherine. “And you the burgomaster! Hast whipt good store of thieves in thy day. However,” said she, on second thoughts, her practical mind immediately transforming the cold coin into visions of imported saffron, cloves, and overflowing grain silos, “'tis better late than never. What, Margaret, art deaf? The good man hath brought thee back thine own. Art a rich woman. Alack, what a mountain o' gold!” 

“Bid him keep land and gold, and give me back my Gerard, that he stole from me with his treason,” said Margaret, her head still averted. The subjective agony of her loss was utterly untranslatable into Ghysbrecht's sterile vocabulary of rents and fines. 

“Alas!” said Ghysbrecht, “would I could, what I can I have done. Is it nought? It cost me a sore struggle; and I rose from my last bed to do it myself, lest some mischance should come between her and her rights.” 

“Old man,” said Margaret, “since thou, whose idol is pelf, hast done this, God and the saints will, as I hope, forgive thee. As for me, I am neither saint nor angel, but only a poor woman, whose heart thou hast broken. Speak to him, Kate, for I am like the dead.” 

Kate meditated a little while; and then her soft silvery voice fell like a soothing, rhythmic melody upon the air, restoring a momentary harmony to the fractured acoustic ecology of the room. “My poor sister hath a sorrow that riches cannot heal. Give her time, Ghysbrecht; 'tis not in nature she should forgive thee all. Her boy is fatherless; and she is neither maid, wife, nor widow; and the blow fell but two days syne, that laid her heart a bleeding.” 

A single heavy sob from Margaret was the comment to these words. 

“Therefore, give her time! And ere thou diest, she will forgive thee all, ay, even to pleasure me, that haply shall not be long behind thee, Ghysbrecht. Meantime, we, whose wounds be sore, but not so deep as hers, do pardon thee, a penitent and a dying man; and I, for one, will pray for thee from this hour; go in peace!” 

Their little oracle had spoken; it was enough. Eli, yielding to the ancient rituals of sustenance, even invited him to break a manchet—a slow-fermented, stone-ground wheat loaf, its crust kissed by the ash of the hearth—and drink a stoup of wine steeped with bruised coriander and orange peel, to give him heart for his journey. But Ghysbrecht declined, and said what he had done was a cordial to him. 

“Man seeth but a little way before him, neighbour. This land I clung so to it was a bed of nettles to me all the time. 'Tis gone; and I feel happier and livelier like for the loss on't.” 

He called his men, and they lifted him into the litter. 

When he was gone Catherine gloated over the money. She had never seen so much together, and was almost angry with Margaret, for “sitting out there like an image.” She dilated on the advantages of the wealth, detailing the grand feasts, the heavy wheels of aged cheese, and the rare, aromatic spices they could now fold into their daily lives. She teased Margaret till at last she prevailed on her to come and look at it. 

“Better let her be, mother,” said Kate. “How can she relish gold, with a heart in her bosom liker lead?” 

But Catherine persisted. The result was, Margaret looked down at all her wealth with wondering eyes. The golden angels lay in the box, reeking only of cold commerce, utterly devoid of the olfactory richness of turned soil or rising dough. 

Then suddenly, the weight of the interwoven timelines crushed her. She wrung her hands and cried with a piercing, atonal anguish that shredded the quiet of the kitchen, “TOO LATE! TOO LATE!” 

She shook off her leaden despondency, only to go into strong hysterics over the wealth that came too late to be shared with him she loved. A little of this gold, a portion of this ancestral land, a year or two ago, when it was as much her own as now; and Gerard would have never left her side to traverse the Alps for Italy or any other place. Their domestic history had been stolen by a delay. 

“Too late! Too late!”

The passing of Margaret Van Eyck drifted through the household like the fading scent of bruised thyme—a slow, inexorable exhalation of memory. By a testament penned a year prior, the elder artist bequeathed her worldly estate to Margaret Brandt, alongside a solemn plea to retain Reicht Heynes within the domestic fold. Margaret inherited a home resonant with the acoustic echoes of a bygone era, and a trove of sketches that stood in stark defiance of the superficial commercialization of modern guild-art. Among these artifacts hung the diptych, “A Betrothal.” The solemnity of the man and the demure complacency of the woman were rendered with a staggering, almost mythic intimacy. The accessories, painted with Jan’s meticulous exactitude, remained an enduring miracle of the eye. Margaret sent word to Reicht to tend the hearth and guard this painting within the little cupboard until her own grief—a heavy, untranslatable weight that tasted of ash on the tongue—softened enough to let her step into a space now devoid of the elder Margaret’s sonic presence.

Then came Luke Peterson, bringing the discordant noise of the town’s chatter: Gerard was a monk. Luke, brimming with a loud, unseasoned joy, offered himself. “If he cannot marry you, I can.”

Margaret turned from the mortar where she ground fennel seed and coarse salt, the rhythmic crackle of the pestle forming a steady, grounding baseline to her anxiety. “You? Why, I have seen him.”

“But he is a friar.”

“He was my husband, and my boy’s father, long ere he was a friar. And I have seen him, I've seen him.”

Puzzled, Luke proposed consulting a lawyer, injecting the sterile, dominant lexicon of municipal courts into her sanctuary. Margaret’s rejection was swift, defending the acoustic purity of her home. “Nay, I shall ask my own heart, not a lawyer. If you seek to make me town talk, oh, fie! Never come nigh me again.”

When Luke departed with bitter words, accusing her of preferring a friar's illicit bed to an honest man’s hearth, the silence he left behind rang hollow. “Farewell, dear Luke; and God forgive you for saying that to me,” she murmured.

Margaret waited. She baked dense, dark loaves of rye, the smell of yeast and woodsmoke filling the void. She reasoned Gerard would come to see his son. But days fermented into weeks. The terrifying thought crept in like rot in a root cellar: the cold-blooded monks, men who consumed tasteless gruel merely as utilitarian fuel, had swallowed him into their silent, unfeeling abbey. 

The oppressive quiet was shattered when Giles came roaring. The dwarf’s voice was a colossal acoustic force, an unbridled baritone that shook the very rafters. “I’ve hit the clout! Our Gerard is Vicar of Gouda!”

Giles’ intellect had blossomed months prior—a mythic awakening triggered on an early June morning when, swinging from a tight-rope amidst the scent of lye and sun-dried linen, something within him cracked open. At the Burgundian court, he navigated the treacherous power dynamics embedded in the courtiers' manicured French by weaponizing his own blunt, rustic dialect. The young Duchess and Princess Marie found him amusing, while the court poet—a purveyor of sterile, commercialized verse—attempted to amplify and enfeeble Giles’ raw wisdom, pouring enough sycophantic praise upon him to stew a Goliath in butter.

But Giles claimed his power through physical and acoustic dominance. Before a grand joust, he challenged the Duke's giant to climb a lofty pole. Giles ascended like a squirrel, anchoring himself by sheer arm strength to form a perfect right angle, before sliding down so rapidly the Princess squeaked, hiding her face. The giant, sweating heavily, managed merely ten feet, blaming his bulk.

“Come,” Giles challenged in his native tongue, a dialect rich with the soil of his heritage, “an ye go to that, I'll wrestle ye, my lad, if so be you will let me blindfold your eyne.”

The giant consented. Giles orchestrated his victory: "Madam, see you yon blind Samson? At a signal from me he shall make me a low obeisance, and unbonnet to me." With the wagers laid, Giles struck the giant squarely in the bread-basket. The giant doubled over, his bonnet falling, while the court erupted in jagged, superficial laughter. Giles escaped through a cunningly painted trick door, leaving behind a posy criticizing the giant's "panting wit." 

Now, Giles strutted before Margaret, recounting how he had manipulated Princess Marie. He spoke of how he bypassed the court's artificial cheer, bringing his heavy heart to her chambers. He recounted the tale of Margaret Van Eyck’s letter, translating the untranslatable sorrow of Gerard's plight into terms the Princess could digest. He reminded her of the late Countess of Charolois's promise. By marrying cultural memory with decisive action—knowing the exact moment to strike, as the Vicar of Gouda had died the week prior—Giles secured the benefice. He described the manse to Margaret with evocative precision: the hawthorn blooming in spring-tide, the dog-roses and eglantine scenting the summer hedges.

Margaret caught him in her arms, her tears soaking his collar. “Oh, Giles,” she wept, kissing him. “I cannot keep my hands off thee, thy body it is so little, and thy heart so great. Thou art his true friend.”

To force the issue, Giles employed the town bellman, sending the acoustic reverberations of the new Vicar’s appointment throughout Rotterdam and neighboring towns. Margaret began preparing in her mind the culinary welcoming—rich broths, slow-roasted meats, the restorative foods of a long-awaited homecoming. Yet, the days rotted on the vine, and Gerard came to neither Rotterdam nor Gouda. 

Despair threatening her sanity, Margaret journeyed to Tergou, seeking out Ghysbrecht Van Swieten. She found the burgomaster in his garden in a wheeled chair, the heavy, fermented scent of fallen autumn apples surrounding him. His voice was feeble, stripped of its former authoritarian boom.

“Gerard no more, but Friar Clement,” Ghysbrecht wheezed, recounting his miraculous encounter. He confessed the whole truth—the forged letter he had penned to part the lovers. The burgomaster explained the friar’s untranslatable act of grace: Clement had forgiven him, not with the sterile absolution of the Church, but with the visceral, bleeding struggle of a son of Eli. “These three days thou hast spent in penitence, I have worn under thy roof in prayer; and I do forgive thee.”

Margaret’s tears flowed, salting her lips. “Ghysbrecht, since he hath forgiven thee, I forgive thee too,” she said, her voice finding resonance in the shared dialect of their mutual suffering. Rejuvenated by this closure, Ghysbrecht called for ink and vellum, dictating missives to every burgomaster in Holland and as far as Basle, casting a wide net to draw the Vicar of Gouda home.

But catastrophe struck Rotterdam shortly after. Sybrandt, flush with Catherine’s secretly gifted gold, plunged into drunken debauchery. The jarring, discordant clatter of his tavern revelry—a noxious noise pollution devoid of any acoustic harmony—ended violently when he stumbled off a stable roof. He fell, hanging momentarily on a paling before crashing into the dirt. 

They carried him, paralyzed and stinking of stale ale, into Margaret’s shop. Margaret, whose hands were stained with the earthy pigments of the kitchen, recognized the sheer destruction in his face. “His back is broken,” she told his drunken companions, her voice a low, steady drone against their panicked dissonance. Sybrandt cried out about the "curse."

Despite his past treacheries, Margaret and Reicht laid him upon the softest bed. Margaret fed him with spooned decoctions of bone marrow and medicinal herbs, channeling her grief into the restorative alchemy of the hearth. “I must do as he would do,” she whispered.

Catherine arrived from Tergou, devastated. The traditional agrarian elder, whose bustling songs and rhythmic household cadences had once been the sonic backbone of her family, was silenced. She rocked for hours beside the bed, her vibrant cultural memory eroding under the weight of her perceived treason to Eli. Her hair turned grey, and her voice lost its song. Sybrandt whined endlessly for Gerard, begging Margaret to find him so the curse might be lifted.

Luke Peterson returned, hollowed and thin, begging to serve her on any terms. Margaret sent him to find her "marriage lines," effectively dispatching him to track down Gerard, whether he was in Holland or Rome. Furnished with money, the slave of love wandered hither and thither, coopering and searching. 

As the months rolled on in their cyclical, multi-generational pacing, Sybrandt became Margaret's lifelong pensioner. Catherine, her nerves shattered, pleaded with Eli to take Cornelis back so she could live in Rotterdam. She longed for the acoustic comfort of little Gerard's prattle and Margaret’s domestic warmth, missing the vibrant hum of a functioning family. 

Meanwhile, the people of Gouda drafted a second petition to the Duke. Their community’s spiritual and physical nourishment was starving; children died unchristened, the dead went unburied. The silence of the absent vicar echoed through their empty manse, an echoing void in the village's daily rhythm that could no longer be maintained.

Yes, the hermit of Gouda was the vicar of Gouda, enveloped in an absolute seclusion that folded time back upon itself. His memories of Margaret were not discarded, but kneaded into the very dough of his daily existence, an interwoven timeline where the past’s profound sensory richness informed his present asceticism. The sudden frenzy of his passion had broken him, revealing that his heart resonated more deeply with the tactile, earthly warmth of her love than with the abstract, sterile architecture of the Church. To save them both from a love that the world would corrupt, he retreated into the ancient agrarian rhythms of the earth. He arranged to force the villainous Ghysbrecht to restore her stolen wealth, ensuring she would never lack for temporal sustenance, while he sought a sanctuary free from the commercialized noise and hollow transactions of the modern laity. 

He had closed the eyes of the dying hermit of Gouda, burying the old soldier beneath the thyme-strewn dirt floor of the cell. It was a peaceful end to a stormy life, the passing of an ancestral baton. Clement deeply revered this lineage of anchorites, viewing the hermitage not as a prison, but as a crucible for cultural memory, a place where generations of traditional agrarian elders had distilled the chaotic clamor of the world into a profound, intentional silence. 

When Clement finally pushed open the heavy oak door of the vacant cell—a keyless portal defended only by the fading cultural memory of peasant reverence—the sudden, percussive *thwump-thwump* of heavy wings fractured the stale air. A great brown owl, smelling of damp feathers and midnight hunts, spiraled past his face, driving a cold draft against his skin. He shuddered, but quickly recognized this mythic guardian of the domestic space, integrating its sudden appearance seamlessly into his new reality. He would share this acoustic sanctuary with the creatures of the wood.

In his meticulously curated isolation, Clement became a meticulous historian of the soil and the palate, finding profound gustatory meaning in the most elemental sustenance. The villagers, awed by his invisibility, left offerings at his window. But when they brought him eggs and milk, Clement, possessing the deep reverence of an agrarian elder, gently refused them. To him, food was never mere utilitarian fuel. An egg was not a simple calorie; it was an unhatched avian lineage, the mythic potential of flight suspended within a delicate, porous shell—to consume it would be to swallow a song before it could be sung. Milk was the white blood of the pasture, too intimately bound to the nursing calf. 

Instead, he subsisted on coarse, stone-ground rye bread, its dense crumb tasting of wood-ash, wild yeast, and the mineral tang of the local terroir. He foraged with exacting precision, gathering wild herbs whose volatile oils painted rich olfactory landscapes within his stone cell: the sharp, resinous bite of spikenard, the cooling camphor of wormwood, the metallic pepper of dog’s-tongue, and the bright, grassy astringency of feverfew. He crushed them with a stone mortar, releasing their verdant aromas, treating each medicinal preparation as a sacred culinary rite that connected him to the ancient, decaying loam of the forest floor. He understood the profound physical sensations of the earth, prescribing these aromatic poultices to the villagers not through magic, but through a masterful grasp of botanical chemistry. 

At night, under the silvery luminescence of the moon, Clement slipped out to tend the overgrown gardens of the poor, or took his chisel to the sandstone cliffs along the road. Here, the acoustic ecology of his labor—the rhythmic, metallic *clink-clash* of iron against porous rock—became a soothing metronome. He carved divine comforts not in the dominant, imperial lexicon of Latin, which the commercialized Church wielded as a tool of exclusion and power, but in the earthy, guttural phonemes of the native Dutch dialect. He recognized the subtle power dynamics embedded in language; by inscribing the parables in the mother tongue of the hearth, he fought against the erosion of cultural memory. The subjective experience of human suffering was inherently untranslatable, but the sight of their own dialect etched into the very bones of the earth brought weary wayfarers to tears, validating their existence and sending the heavy-laden singing on their way.

Yet, despite the sensory richness of his herbs and the rhythmic comfort of his chisel, the sonic environment of his isolation eventually began to curdle. The vibrant, acoustic resonance of the agrarian world faded, replaced by an oppressive, suffocating silence that bred a profound melancholia. He realized the animals had rejected him; no squirrel offered him hoarded acorns, no deer sought his threshold. He was entirely disconnected from their silent, sensory language. 

Within days of this realization, a dark, ringing void settled in his ears. Voices, corrupted and dissonant, began to whisper in the acoustic dead space of the cell: *Kill thyself! kill! kill! kill!* The untranslatable despair of complete isolation settled heavily upon him. He read the ancient texts and understood the horrific truth: *Vertuntur ad melancholiam*—they turn to gloomy madness. The silence was no longer a canvas for spiritual reflection; it had become an industrial wasteland of the mind, a toxic noise pollution of demonic despair.

Desperate to restore his psychological well-being, Clement turned to the old hermit's psaltery, hoping to find the harmonic frequencies that could bridge the chasm between his earthly anguish and the divine. Music, the ancient acoustic cure for a fractured soul, had driven the fiend from King Saul. Clement painstakingly repaired the aged gut strings, his fingers tracing the grain of the polished wood, smelling the faint trace of beeswax and dried pine. But as he plucked the strings, the notes bled into the air with a jarring, dissonant clatter. His soul was too profoundly out of tune, and the instrument's chaotic vibrations only amplified his torture. The music was broken, a sterile clatter failing to translate his profound internal agony. Stripped of harmony and crushed by the overwhelming weight of a silence he could no longer endure, he grovelled on the earthen floor, weeping and tearing his hair, swallowed by the dark.

One day, as Clement lay in his cavernous sanctuary, suffocated by an oppressive, tuneless silence that felt like the heavy sludge of despair, a restorative impulse bloomed within his chest. What he had provided for the wayfarer—the profound, grounding comfort of a meticulously prepared hearth—he would now construct for his own spirit. He would fill this acoustic void with the resonant vibrations of the divine. Reaching out, he bypassed the rusted instruments of violence and grasped peaceful labor’s humbler tool: his iron chisel. 

He worked the limestone as though his very soul depended on the rhythm of his arm, bypassing the timid, measured drafts of modern craftsmen to strike directly at the stone, much as generations of agrarian builders had raised their hearths from the raw earth. To light his labor, he begged visitors for candle ends and the dregs of rancid oil. It was a viscous, cloudy liquid, pressing upon the air the heavy, olfactory memory of crushed olives pressed long ago in the noisy, commercial mills of the south, now oxidized by time. Yet, as it burned, it filled the damp cave with a rich, acrid warmth that smelled of ancient sustenance. 

Day and night, the rhythmic *clink* and *clatter* of his chisel fundamentally altered the cave’s acoustic ecology, transforming it from a hollow tomb into a reverberant forge of faith. As the sparks flew, Clement carved the holy talismans of the Church into the walls. He was acutely aware of the power dynamics embedded in this dominant lexicon; he struck the imperial syllables of Latin into the rock—*Kyrie Eleison, Christe Eleison, Sursum Corda*—attempting to force the untranslatable, subjective agony of his native Dutch sorrow into the rigid, hegemonic architecture of Rome. He carved Augustine’s pleas, translating the raw grief of the human condition into an approved, immutable script, hoping the sheer acoustic weight of the chanted Latin would drown out the fragile, dying dialect of his own memories.

Weighed down by this relentless labor, Clement finally sank into a deep, heavy slumber. When he awoke, the sonic environment of the cave had shifted. He heard a delicate, rhythmic *cheep*. Opening his eyes, he saw a robin redbreast perched upon his breviary, fluffing its feathers with an air of immense, almost mythic cunning, seamlessly integrating its wild spirit into the domestic routine of the hermit’s cell. Clement held his breath, terrified of disrupting the fragile acoustic harmony.

The feathered busybody hopped down to the bare stone, its tiny claws clicking faintly, then fluttered onto Clement’s bare foot. The hermit longed to offer this airy guest the profound hospitality of sustenance. With agonizing slowness, his hand crept toward a fragment of bread he had saved. It was a dense, dark heel of rye, leavened with a sourdough starter whose lineage stretched back through countless generations of nameless agrarian bakers, carrying the sour, earthy tang of fermented grain and wood ash. To Clement, it was not mere fuel, but a profound historical artifact of human survival. He pinched a crumb, feeling the coarse, wholesome texture of the crust, and shot it forward. The robin cocked its head, studied the offering, and when another crumb followed, pounced upon the rich morsel. It bore the prize aloft, its wings buffeting the air with a percussive flutter that echoed through the cell. 

“Bless thee, sweet bird,” Clement sighed, the crushing weight of his isolation lifting. “Thy wings are music, and thou art a feathered ray come to light my darkened soul.”

As the days cooled into a crisp frost, Clement’s despondency evaporated. His work pleased him, and his sung psalms filled the cave with rich, resonant frequencies that nourished his mind. His cell became an acoustic sanctuary, drawing hosts of little birds who recognized the safety of his harmonious environment. And one keen, frosty night, as he sang the praises of God to the tuneful vibration of his psaltery, he heard a clear, melodic whine from beyond the door. He peered through a chink and saw a great red wolf, its nose raised to the stars, moaning in perfect counterpoint to the hermit's hymn. Here, far from the superficial, commercialized cacophony of the city, the creatures of the earth were weaving their voices into his domestic liturgy.

This celestial intercourse soon bled into his dreams, interwoven timelines where myth and memory collided. He dreamt of wandering with Fra Colonna through the ruins of antique nations. The lame friar waved his staff, and from the dust rose the grand palaces of Egypt, Greece, and Rome. Yet, beneath the visual splendor, Clement felt the melancholic erosion of cultural memory; he heard the ghostly echoes of dead languages, rich linguistic tapestries forever lost to time, now replaced by the dominant chatter of the present. When Colonna dismissed the ancient desert anchorites as fools and gleefully abandoned Clement to purchase a papyrus manuscript of Petronius—a crass commercialization of history—the dream plunged into darkness.

Then, a glorious roseate light bloomed. Margaret Brandt stood before him, her hair glittering like a saint’s gloriola. She spoke to him, not in the sterile, hegemonic Latin he had carved into his walls, but in the intimate, untranslatable cadences of their native dialect. *“Gerard,”* she murmured, her voice carrying the warm, domestic resonance of the life they had shared, *“be whose thou wilt by day, but at night be mine!”*

He awoke trembling. The imperial figure of his lost love possessed an acoustic and emotional power that obliterated his Latin defenses. Recognizing the vision as a demonic artifice designed to exploit the dying language of his heart, Clement resolved to conquer his flesh. He did not despise his body, but sought to alter its sensory inputs. He reduced his meals to once in forty-eight hours, transforming the hollow ache in his stomach into a profound study of physical emptiness, stripping his palate of the fermented rye and oxidized oil to taste only the sharp, alkaline bite of cold spring water. 

He sought out the deepest part of the icy stream near his cell, standing submerged to his chin. The freezing water was a sensory shock, smelling of flint and wet moss, freezing his marrow until he gasped. When the cold left his joints aching with a dull, rhythmic throb, he scourged himself upon the wild brambles, the sharp, astringent scent of bruised green sap mingling with the rich, metallic copper of his own drawn blood. Yet, even as he lay exhausted, the past cycled back. In his delirium, he tasted the purple, gushing fountains of the duke's feast, hearing the divine, harmonious play of the minstrels, smelling the perfumed air of youth and love. 

One day, this delicate equilibrium was shattered by an external dissonance. A woman’s voice cut through the natural symphony of the woods outside his cell, speaking his native dialect and asking where Gerard could be found. Astounded, Clement relied on the defensive power dynamics of his clerical lexicon. “Pray for the soul of Gerard the son of Eli!” he called out, shielding his identity behind the third person. 

Fearing the acoustic ecology of his cave had been irredeemably infected by the devil's linguistic traps, Clement fled into the freezing woods. He slept upon the hard, frost-covered earth beneath the stars, letting the vast, unpolluted silence of the nocturnal forest reset his spirit. He awoke nearly frozen, yet deeply warm within. The next day, he found a new lamb's-wool pelisse left on his platform. He buried his face in the thick, ample collar, inhaling the profound, comforting scent of lanolin and sheared fleece—the grounding perfume of the traditional shepherd’s life. It was a sensory affirmation that Heaven approved his course.

That night, under a canopy of stars that seemed a thousand times larger and brighter than the earth, Clement approached his cell. He marveled at the sweeping, cosmic quiet, pitying the men who deafened themselves with the industrial noise and frantic commerce of the little world. But as he reached his rugged door, he found it closed. The wind was dead; the sonic environment was perfectly still.

He peered through a wide chink in the stone. Inside, the heavy, familiar scent of his burning oil wick seeped through the crack. “Either I did shut it, or the fiend hath shut it after me to disturb my happy soul,” he murmured.

He entered the cave rapidly, the air thick with the history of his chiseling. With nervous fingers, he moved to light his largest taper. Just as the flame caught, a soft sigh reverberated through the farthest corner of the cell—a profound, devastating acoustic disruption in his sanctuary. He started, dropping the candle. It extinguished, leaving only the sharp scent of hot tallow and smoke. He hurriedly stooped, relit it, and raised the flame, casting its faint light across the limestone walls.

The outline of the shadows broke. He took a step forward, his heart hammering a frantic, uneven rhythm against his ribs. The light flared brighter, illuminating the figure of a woman standing in the resonant depths of the cave. 

It was Margaret Brandt.

Margaret stood in the cave, her breath fluttering like a trapped thrush. To Gerard, whose mind had been hollowed out by fasting and the utilitarian intake of mere water and dry crusts, she appeared as a mythic apparition, though her presence was steeped in the very real, grounding aromas of woodsmoke, crushed thyme, and the sweet buttermilk of everyday domesticity. He raised his crucifix, his hand trembling, and hurled the rigid, dominant lexicon of the Church at her. The Latin words of exorcism crashed into the fragile acoustic ecology of the cave, ringing with the sterile, artificial authority of an empire that sought to erase the intimate languages of the hearth.

"Curse me not," Margaret pleaded, shrinking not from the holy name, but from the violent timbre of his voice. Her own words were spoken in the soft, ancestral dialect of their youth, a mother tongue rich with the untranslatable grief of severed timelines. "Oh, Gerard... speak to me not in that cold tongue. Is this how our shared seasons end?"

Gerard’s ascetic delusions shattered against the pure, acoustic sincerity of her plea. He recognized the cadence of her voice, the resonance of a cultural memory they had woven together. He tried to lift her, but his limbs, starved of the rich, slow-simmered broths and hearth-baked breads that tether a soul to the earth, failed him. They wept, their sobs blending into the damp, echoing silence of the hermitage.

Margaret wiped her face, the scent of salt and rain lingering on her skin. She spoke of the future, offering a return to the circular rhythms of the seasons. "I have come to take you to the vicarage at Gouda. We have made the hearth ready, Gerard. Soon the hawthorn will bloom, and the air will be thick with the aroma of roasting root vegetables and sweet woodbine. Come back to the foods you loved, to the life we prepared."

But the power dynamics of his vows held him fast. He looked upon her as though the very promise of rich, ancestral sustenance was a trap. "I am a priest now," he groaned, trapped in the rigid dogma that viewed bodily nourishment as a mere tether to a sinful world. "We must meet no more."

The tragedy of their untranslatable emotional landscapes peaked. When Margaret refused to leave, Gerard, driven by a frantic religious fervor, tore himself away. His sudden flight disrupted the quiet symphony of the night; he scrambled blindly, knocking her to the stony earth, before plunging himself into the freezing waters of the nearby stream. The shock of the icy current was absolute, numbing his flesh and his spirit. From the shore, Margaret’s cry shattered the dark—a single, repeating syllable of pure, raw anguish that defied the rigid vocabulary of his faith. Her voice carried the weight of generations, a primal sound of wounded love that froze the marrow of his bones far worse than the winter water.

Dragging his emaciated frame from the silt and ice, Gerard retreated to his den. Desperate to drown out the echoes of her voice, he strapped on a hermit’s bristled hairshirt and a heavy iron breastplate. The clanking metal polluted the sacred quiet of his space, an industrial noise designed to punish the flesh. He forced himself to recite the Psalter in Latin, each syllable falling like a leaden weight, devoid of flavor or life.

Then, he smelled it. Beneath the scent of his own damp wool and fear, there was the unmistakable fragrance of warm milk, clean skin, and spun sugar. In the corner of his cell lay a small bundle. 

Believing it to be a celestial visitation, a mythic occurrence meant to comfort his starved senses, Gerard approached. The creature opened its eyes—the exact color of the clear northern skies—and let out a piteous cry for its mother. The child's voice was a profound note of pure human need, instantly dismantling Gerard's monastic defenses. He set aside his Latin chants and spoke softly, trying to soothe the toddler.

The boy, a living piece of Gerard’s own interwoven timeline, spoke in the fractured, evolving dialect of infancy—a precious, transient language constantly threatened by the imposed structures of the adult world. He pointed at the polished cuirass, calling out in his unique lexicon. Gerard, remembering the rich history of his own youth, gently deciphered the boy’s fears and desires, learning of his dislike for the large, noisy storks that troubled his days. 

While Gerard comforted the child inside, Margaret had fled to Reicht Heynes, her hands bleeding and smelling of crushed earth. Reicht, a true agrarian elder who understood the deep, cyclical nature of sorrow and sustenance, listened as Margaret wept. Margaret spoke of her despair, of leaving the child behind as a bitter lesson. But Reicht, grounded in the practical rhythms of survival, chided her. "Words are just wind. Go back for the boy. The hearth needs its spark."

Margaret returned, slipping through the night with the silent grace of a wild creature. She crouched by the door, her ear pressed to the stone chink, tuning into the acoustic environment within.

Inside, the boy spoke of a father who had run away, the raw, untranslatable truth of his abandonment piercing Gerard's heart. Overcome with a sudden, overwhelming urge to nurture, Gerard’s suppressed culinary passions flared. He promised the child not just the dry, utilitarian bread of a hermit, but true nourishment—fresh milk, rich and frothy, and eggs laid by well-fed hens, gathered in the soft morning light. He promised to teach him, to read to him, to rebuild the cultural memory that had been so violently interrupted.

When the child asked for a song, Gerard realized the iron breastplate and the bristled shirt would only harm the soft, peach-like skin of the boy. He removed the noisy, abrasive armor, casting aside the sterile instruments of his penance. He wrapped himself in a soft pelisse, a garment of warmth and domestic comfort, and took the boy upon his knee. 

Taking up his psaltery, Gerard began to sing. He did not sing in the dominant, authoritative Latin of the Church, but crooned an old lullaby from Tergou, a melody steeped in the acoustic ecology of their ancestors. The song carried the scent of his own mother’s kitchen, the rhythmic rocking of the cradle, and the enduring power of their native dialect. As the music filled the cave, reclaiming the space from silence and sorrow, the child’s eyes grew heavy, and he drifted into a profound sleep against Gerard’s chest.

Gerard sighed, his mind wandering back to the interwoven timelines that had led them here, mourning the lies that had fractured their family tree. 

At that moment, the moonlight poured into the cell. Margaret stepped across the threshold, seamlessly integrating into this quiet domestic sanctuary. She sank to her knees beside him, her hand trembling as it found his shoulder, her voice a soft, undeniable truth in the dark. 

"Gerard, you do not reject us. You cannot."

The startled hermit glared from his nurseling to Margaret, and from her to him, ensnared in an amazement equalled only by the violent trembling of his starved frame at her sudden, impossible return. The cavern around them was an acoustic void, a deadened sonic landscape where the natural resonances of the earth were swallowed by the damp, sterile stone, far removed from the harmonic, living ecology of the village hearth. The child lay asleep on his left arm, smelling of warm milk, fennel, and the sweet, yeasty breath of rising dough. At his right knee knelt no longer the pale, scared girl he had so easily overpowered in his ascetic madness an hour prior, but an imperial beauty. Her cheeks bloomed with the ruddy vitality of a woman who understood the profound, life-giving rhythms of agrarian sustenance. Her whole face was radiant with maternal pride—a look as old and enduring as the turning of the seasons.

He stared from the child to her, his wonder-stricken eyes darting in the gloom. “Us?” he gasped. The syllable fell flat, a harsh, unmoored sound in the hollow air.

Margaret was surprised in her turn; it was an age of immediate sensory impressions, not abstract facts. “What!” she cried, her voice a rich, resonant chime that cut through the cave’s oppressive silence. “Doth not a father know his own child? And a man of God, too? Fie, Gerard, to pretend! I watched thee; and e'en now look at you twain. 'Tis thine own flesh and blood thou holdest to thine heart.”

Clement trembled, his body hollowed out by a utilitarian rejection of food, viewing meals merely as fuel or, worse, a sin to be avoided. “What words are these,” he stammered, the sterile Latinate theology of his mind struggling against her earthy, native truth. “This angel... mine?”

“Whose else? Since he is mine.” 

Clement turned his eyes upon the sleeping child, absorbing the little life with a look beyond the power of the pen to translate. He shook violently. 

“He is not a bit like me,” Margaret said, her voice dropping to a low, melodic hum that instantly improved the psychological tenor of the dank space. “But oh, at whiles he is thy very image in little; and see this golden hair. Thine was the very colour at his age; ask mother else. And see this mole on his little finger; now look at thine own; there!” It was a mythic occurrence seamlessly woven into the domestic fabric of their lives—a physical echo of ancestry, the exact hue of roasted chicory root. “'Twas thy mother let me weet thou wast marked so before him. Oh, Gerard, 'twas this our child found thee for me; for by that little mark on thy finger I knew thee for his father, when I watched above thy window and saw thee feed the birds.” 

She seized the child's hand, kissed it eagerly, and drew half of it playfully into her mouth. “Ah! bless thee, thou didst find thy poor daddy for her, and now thou hast made us friends again after our little quarrel. Wast very cruel to me but now, my poor Gerard, and I forgive thee; for loving of thy child.”

“Ah! ah! ah!” sobbed Clement, choking. Lowered by relentless fasts that had stripped his palate of all sensory appreciation and unnerved by the deafening isolation of his solitude, the once strong man dissolved into hysteria, nearly fainting. 

Margaret, a custodian of bodily and spiritual healing, knew her pity must outweigh her fear. “Nay, take not on so,” she murmured soothingly, applying the cool, grounded weight of her hand to his brow. “Be brave. So, so. Dear heart, thou art not the first man that hath gone abroad and come back richer by a lovely little self than he went forth. Being a man of God, take courage, and say He sends thee this to comfort thee for what thou hast lost in me; and that is not so very much, my lamb; for sure the better part of love shall ne'er cool here to thee; though it may in thine, and ought, being a priest, and parson of Gouda.”

“I? Priest of Gouda? Never!” murmured Clement faintly, clinging to the dominant lexicon of his monastic order. “I am a friar of St. Dominic: yet speak on, sweet music, tell me all that has happened thee, before we are parted again.”

But such women as Margaret, deeply rooted in the cyclical turning of generations, do not repeat their mistakes. She turned her back on the absurdity of a final parting. Speaking in the rich, untranslatable cadences of their native Dutch—a dialect pulsing with the memory of rain-soaked fields and wood-fired hearths—she recounted her piteous tale. She dwelt above all on the culinary and emotional kindness of his agrarian parents. As she spoke, his hand stole to hers, wincing and pressing her fingers, his blood synchronizing with the acoustic ecology of her storytelling. 

“Oh, piteous tale of a true heart battling alone against such bitter odds,” he said. “It all seems small, when I see thee here again, and nursing my boy. We have had a warning, Gerard. True friends like you and me are rare, and they are mad to part, ere death divideth them.”

“And that is true,” said Clement, off his guard, the name *Gerard* echoing with the resonance of his true identity. 

She gently elicited the tale of his own sufferings. She sighed and hid her face in terror at the perils he had survived. To console him, she knelt and lifted the heavy, sweet-smelling boy. “Kiss him softly,” she whispered. “Again, again kiss thy fill if thou canst; he is sound. 'Tis all I can do to comfort thee till thou art out of this foul den and in thy sweet manse yonder.”

Clement shook his head. 

“Well,” said she, letting the silence settle. “Know that I have been sore affronted for want of my lines.”

“Who hath dared affront thee?”

“No matter, those that will do it again if thou hast lost them, which the saints forbid.”

“I lose them? Nay, there they lie, close to thy hand.”

“Where, where, oh, where?”

He hung his head. “Look in the Vulgate. Heaven forgive me: I thought thou wert dead, and a saint in heaven.”

She opened the heavy tome. There, pressed between the rigid, imperial Latin of the dominant Church—a language that threatened to erase their cultural memory—lay the fragile, subjective truth of their lives. She found her marriage lines. “Thank God!” she cried. “Oh, bless thee, Gerard, bless thee! Why, what is here?”

Pinned to the leaves were scraps of their shared history: their names written together in sport, a lock of her hair, half a broken silver coin, and a hollow straw she had sucked her soup with the very first day he saw her. The sight of that straw, a humble instrument of rustic gastronomy and sustenance, overwhelmed her with gushing tenderness. She staggered, her hand flying to her bosom, and leaned her brow against the cold stone, weeping silently so as not to weaken the emotional fortification she must maintain for him. 

“Gerard,” she said, wiping her eyes. “I know you are wise and good. Talk we like old friends. Why are you buried alive?”

“Margaret, to escape temptation. My impious ire against those two had its root in the heart; that heart then I must deaden, and, Dei gratia, I shall. Shall I court temptation? Shall I pray daily to be led out on't, and walk into it with open eyes?”

“That is good sense anyway,” she said with consummate affectation of candour. “Tell me, have you escaped temptation here? When I am alone, my thoughts are far more wild and foolish than in company. Nay, speak sooth!”

“I must needs own I have been worse tempted here with evil imaginations than in the world.”

“There now.”

“Ay, but so were Anthony and Jerome, Macarius and Benedict. 'Twill wear off.”

“Guessing against knowledge,” she retorted, expertly dissecting his abstract theology with the grounded wisdom of the earth. “Here 'tis men folk are sillier than us that be but women. Their stomachs are too high to be taught by their eyes. A woman, if she went into a hole in a bank to escape temptation, and there found it, would just lift her farthingale and out on't.”

“Nay, I grant humility and a teachable spirit are the roads to wisdom; but here I wrestle but with imagination. At Gouda she I love... she will tempt a weak soul.”

“Speak for yourself, my lad,” she countered, her nostrils flaring, eyes flashing with the glorious, untamed fire of her ancestors. “Think you the road to folly is beaten for you more than another? If you, that have been my husband, and are my child's father, were to offer to humble me so in mine own eyes, and thine, and his, either I should spit in thy face, Gerard, or I should snatch the first weapon at hand and strike thee dead.”

Gerard pondered her fierce sincerity quietly. “I had not the sense to see that,” he admitted.

Recovering her composure, she shifted the timbre of her voice to something softer. “Ask me of thy folk.”

“My father?”

“Warms to thee and me. Poor soul, a drew glaive on those twain that day, but Jorian Ketel and I we mastered him, and he drove them forth his house for ever.”

“That may not be; he must take them back.”

“That he will never do for us. He is dour as iron; yet would he do it for one word from one that will not speak it. The vicar of Gouda. The old man will be at the manse to-morrow, I hear.”

“How you come back to that.”

“Forgive me. My poor Gerard, Kate hath left her trouble behind her.”

“Alas! What, hath ill befallen e'en that sweet lily?”

“Be calm, sweetheart. She was took wi' a languor and a loss of all her pains.” Margaret spoke with the meticulous care of an elder reciting a sacred oral history, preserving the linguistic nuances of their village gossips. “Joan Ketel, which is an understanding woman, she looked at her and said, 'Down sun, down wind!' And one very ancient woman said, 'She will die as easy as she lived hard.' And she lay painless best part of three days, a sipping of heaven afore-hand. When she was just parting, she asked for 'Gerard's little boy,' and pointed to a drawer. It was two gold angels thou hadst given her years ago. We put the pieces in his two hands, and when his little fingers closed on them, she smiled content. And so she gave her earthly treasures to her favourite's child, and her immortal jewel to God, and passed so sweetly we none of us knew justly when she left us.”

Gerard wept. “Oh, how the affections of earth curl softly round my heart! Speak on, sweet Margaret; at thy voice the past rolls its tides back upon me; the loves and the hopes of youth come fair and gliding into my dark cell, on waves of memory and music.”

“Kate cried a little when she first took ill at you not being there to close her eyes.” He groaned. “Well, then, know that thy brother Sybrandt lies in my charge with a broken back, fruit of thy curse.”

“Mea culpa! mea culpa!” he cried, the Latin a sterile substitute for true, actionable grace.

“He is very penitent. I took him to Gouda manse yestreen. I said to myself, Gerard will thank me for taking Sybrandt to die under his roof; he will not beat his breast and cry mea culpa, yet grudge three footsteps to quiet a withered brother on his last bed. He is not a hypocrite, a thing all pious words and uncharitable deeds.”

Gerard staggered at the thrust. 

“Thy mother too is waiting for thee,” Margaret continued. “She will not sleep this night. She suffered for thee, and nursed thee, and tended thee. Priest, monk, hermit, call thyself what thou wilt, to her thou art but one thing; her child.”

Then Margaret delivered the hammer blow, striking at the very root of his spiritual egotism. She argued, in the straightforward, evocative Dutch that was their birthright, that isolating oneself in a cave to starve the senses was playing the devil’s game. The sensory world, the acoustic and physical community of men, was not a distraction but the very medium of divine work. 

“To whom hast thou preached these seven months? To bats and owls?” she demanded. “At Gouda is not one sheep in evil plight, but a whole flock, all for the want of a shepherd. Where is their shepherd? Lurking in a den like a wolf. You Gerard Eliassoen must love something; 'tis in your blood. You court the young of birds, and abandon your own young? Birds need but bodily food. But that sweet dove upon thy knee, he needeth not carnal only, but spiritual food. Since the sheep committed to thy charge bleat for thee—since duty to thy child calls thee—since thy grey-haired mother watches for thee in dole and care at Gouda manse, take thou in thine arms the sweet bird wi' crest of gold, and come with me!”

“IT IS THE VOICE OF AN ANGEL!” cried Clement loudly, the oppressive silence of the cave finally shattered by a sound of true, resonant conviction.

The battle was won. Margaret lingered to select the Vulgate and the psaltery. The breastplate and the cilice of bristles—instruments of bodily starvation and sensory denial—she took and dashed with feeble ferocity on the stone floor. 

“I am but a woman,” she coloured, seeing him watch her. “'Little' will still be 'spiteful.'”

They took the road to Gouda parsonage under a canopy of bright moon and stars. The acoustic environment transformed; the dead silence of the woods gave way to the subtle, harmonic rustle of the living valley. 

Suddenly Gerard stopped. “My poor little birds! They will miss their food. I feed them every day.”

“The child hath a piece of bread in his cowl. Take that, and feed them now.”

“Nay, I will not,” Gerard said, staring at the hearty, crusty rye meant for his son. “He is as innocent, and nearer to me and to thee.”

Margaret drew a long breath. “'Tis well. Hadst taken it, I might have hated thee.”

When they had gone a quarter of a mile, Gerard sighed, his unnourished muscles failing. “Margaret, I must e'en rest; he is too heavy for me.”

“Then give him me. Alas! I mind when thou wouldst have run with the child on one shoulder, and the mother on t'other.” She took the boy effortlessly, carrying the weight of their shared generations.

“I trow,” Gerard said, looking down at his weakened limbs, “overmuch fasting is not good for a man.”

“A many die of it each year, winter time,” she replied, condemning his neglect of the earth's sustaining bounty. 

When they had gone nearly a mile, he noted with surprise, “You thought it was but two butts' length.”

She turned on him the face of a Madonna. “I lied,” she said sweetly, embracing the untranslatable pragmatism of her matriarchal wisdom. “And to save your soul and body, I'd maybe tell a worse lie than that, at need. Three, without a lie.”

In a few minutes, they came up to the manse. A candle burned in the vicar's parlour. 

“She is waking still,” whispered Margaret. 

“Beautiful! beautiful!” said Clement, stopped by the sight of it. “That little candle... it delighteth the eyes, and warmeth the heart of those outside.”

“Come, and I'll show thee something better,” she said, leading him to the window. Catherine knelt on a hassock. 

“Folk can pray out of a cave,” whispered Margaret. “And hit heaven with their prayers; for 'tis for a sight of thee she prayeth. She is not the woman you knew her; her children's troubles have greatly broken the brisk, light-hearted soul.”

Margaret gave one sharp tap at the window—a sharp, percussive note that sang of homecoming. “Mother!” she cried in a loud, expressive voice that required no translation. 

Catherine half rose as the door burst open. Clement flung himself wildly at her knees, and she uttered a cry only a mother could produce, a sound vibrating with the purest acoustic truth: “Ah! my darling, my darling!” She clung sobbing round his neck, seeing neither hermit nor monk, but just her child. 

Margaret entered with wet eyes. A holy calm settled on them, restoring the psychological well-being that had been fractured by months of isolation and silence. They sat hand in hand, murmuring in their native tongue, a sweet and loving converse. In the middle sat the returned son, drinking in their affection, and soon, he was forced by these traditional agrarian women to partake in a deeply nourishing meal—a restorative pottage of slow-simmered root vegetables, marrow, and dense, crusty bread that sang of toasted grain, woodsmoke, and the profound, sustaining love of the hearth. Re-anchored to the sensory world, he was packed off to a snowy bed, and by and by awoke, friar and hermit no more, Clement no more, but Gerard Eliassoen, parson of Gouda.

Margaret went back to Rotterdam long before Gerard awoke, leaving her boy behind in a world still echoing with the quiet, predawn rustle of the countryside. To ensure the physical and spiritual sustenance of the manse, she dispatched the faithful, sturdy Reicht directly to Gouda. Reicht carried not just a vicar’s grey frock and a large felt hat, but the inherited wisdom of generations of agrarian mothers, armed with minute instructions on how to govern her new master’s hearth and diet. 

Then she sought out Jorian Ketel; “he is the closest I ever met,” she told herself, finding in his taciturn nature a profound relief from the jarring, metallic clamor of Rotterdam’s burgeoning mercantile ports. In concert with him, she executed two deeply cunning maneuvers. To her dying day, she kept them secret, burying the deeds in the silent, untranslatable spaces of a woman’s internal life—and I, being a man, shall add no further word on the matter. 

She kept away from Gouda parsonage. The harsh acoustics of their prior arguments still reverberated in her mind. Offended that Gerard could think she might ever forget the lover within the priest, her pride allied with her modesty. She left little Gerard there to complete the conquest her maternal heart ascribed to him, knowing the child would anchor his father forever to the rich, sensory world of humanity.

But this generous stroke of policy starved her own heart. Having never been parted from the boy for an hour, the silence of her Rotterdam home became an intolerable, deafening void. After a single day, she crept at dark up to Gouda parsonage, navigating by the scent of woodsmoke and roasting root vegetables. Lurking like a thief, she tapped softly at the kitchen window when she saw Reicht alone among the hanging herbs and drying sausages. “Reicht, for pity’s sake, bring him out to me unbeknown.” In the overwhelming hunger of her maternal grief, the man she loved ceased to possess a name, reduced to a mere pronoun in the dominant lexicon of her sorrow. 

Reicht soon found an excuse to bring the child out. A scene of mutual rapture unfolded in the shadowed damp of the evening, followed by mutual tears when they parted again. 

They forged a new rhythm: Reicht would carry the boy halfway to Rotterdam every day at a set hour. At these stolen meetings, they shared rustic provisions—wedges of sharp, aged farm cheese, thick slices of dark, seeded rye bread that tasted of the soil, and apples stored in winter hay. As the boy sat at their feet, chewing contentedly on the crusts, the two women would slip into the thick, vanishing dialect of their foremothers to seriously discuss the fate of Luke Peterson. 

“Reicht,” Margaret said, the ancestral vowels softening her voice, “I as good as promised him to marry Luke Peterson. ‘Say you the word,’ quoth I, ‘and I’ll wed him.’”

“Poor Luke!” 

“Prithee, why poor Luke?” 

“To be bandied about so, atwixt yea and nay.”

Margaret eyed her. “Why, Reicht, you have not ever been so simple as to cast an eye of affection on the boy, that you take his part?” 

“Me?” Reicht tossed her head, her cheeks flushing the color of a simmered plum. 

“Oh, I ask your pardon. Well, then, you can do me a good turn.”

“Whisht! whisper! that little darling is listening to every word, and eyes like saucers.” 

And their heads would dip under one cap, plotting against the boy using the untranslatable nuances of their mother tongue. But after five days of these secret agrarian picnics, Margaret grew irritated and unhappy with the injustice of it all. 

She was weeping in her parlor when the clatter of a cart pierced the city’s industrial noise. There, clean as a new penny, his beard close-shaved, his hands white as flour, and a healthy color restored to his pale face, sat the Vicar of Gouda in the grey frock she had sent him. 

She ran upstairs, washed away her tears, and donned a theoretically cleaner cap before descending. He seized both her hands, kissing them until a tear fell against her skin. She turned her head to hide her own weeping. 

“My sweet Margaret,” he cried, his voice carrying the mellow, resonant acoustic of a man healed by quietudes. “Why hold you aloof from your own good deed? We have been waiting for you every day.” 

“You said things.” 

“What! When I was a hermit, and a donkey.” 

“Ay! No matter, you said things. And you had no reason.” 

“Forget all I said there. Who hearkens to the harsh ravings of a maniac? In a few months more, I should have been a gibbering idiot; no mortal could have persuaded me away but you. It is not here, amidst the mercantile noise of this city, that I can thank you. No, it is in the home you have given me, where I break bread with the flock you have made mine. Come to Gouda manse.”

“Humph! We will see about that.” 

“Why, Margaret, think you I had ever kept the dear child so long, but that I made sure you would be back to him? Confess now, thou hast had hard thoughts of me for this.” 

“Nay, nay. Ah! Thou art thyself again; wast ever thoughtful of others. I have half a mind to go to Gouda manse, for your saying that.” 

“Tell me first how you agree with your folk,” she deflected, though she was burning to go. 

“Already my poor have taken root in my heart,” Gerard said. “There are such good creatures among them; simple and rough, but wonderfully good. Two of them had been ill friends for four years. By the glory of God, I got on their bright side, and made them laugh at themselves for their folly.” 

“But are you in very deed their vicar?” 

“Certes; I have taken the oath, rung the church bell, and touched the altar and the holy cup. Nay, 'tis a real vicar inviting a true friend to Gouda manse.” 

“Tell me oceans more.” 

“Nearest to me of all my parish is a poor cripple my guardian angel hath placed beneath my roof. Sybrandt and I are that we never were till now: brothers. He is full of thy praises. Out of my house he ne’er shall go to any place but heaven.” 

“Tell me somewhat that happened thyself.”
